## Preface

In late August 1988, I was invited to the United States for an academic visit. I spent six months there, travelling to more than thirty cities, visiting nearly twenty universities, and looking into the work of dozens of government offices and private organizations. I talked about America with Americans and with people from many other countries. Each day, I wrote down what I had seen, heard and discussed. Those notes became this book.

I went as an observer, not as someone conducting a formal inspection. But I certainly had a purpose: I wanted a fuller, closer and more truthful understanding of the world's leading capitalist country. To understand America, we must see it as a country with its own history, culture, people and institutions—not reduce it to a handful of slogans. In my 1987 book *Comparative Political Analysis*, I proposed studying politics through the particular history, social life and culture of each society. There, I had only sketched the broad approach. In America, I wanted to put it to work.

So the point of this book is not simply to describe America's astonishing variety: its sights, its people, its many different activities. My deeper interest is in how the country is governed and how its society organizes itself. I have tried to look widely, but this question runs through everything that follows. The way a society develops is closely tied to the way it runs its political and everyday affairs. To understand one, we need to understand the other. Beneath all my observations lies a deceptively simple question: **Why is there an America like this?** It is a much larger question than I can possibly answer, and I know it.

I do not think we can understand a country by taking one part of it in isolation. History, politics, culture and ordinary social life are woven together in countless ways. Pull out a single strand and pretend it explains the whole, and we will almost certainly miss what matters.

That is why this book contains far more descriptions and observations than abstract arguments. Ideas have to begin with things we can actually see and examine. An argument is convincing only when the evidence beneath it is convincing. Sometimes the most useful thing a writer can offer is not a final answer, but an opportunity to think. Of course my observations are shaped by my own judgments. Even so, I hope readers will be able to think through what they mean for themselves.

America deserves this kind of attention because, like China, it presents one of the great puzzles of the twentieth century.

China's puzzle is this: how did a civilization with more than two thousand years of history decline in the modern age and fall behind other nations? Generations of Chinese thinkers and reformers have struggled with that question.

America presents almost the opposite puzzle. How did a nation with only about two hundred years of history become one of the most advanced and powerful countries in the world?

Scholars living in this century have a responsibility to examine both. Chinese scholars have a particular responsibility to understand the puzzle of China and the puzzle of America. Only by doing so can we better understand ourselves and the wider world, and find a path toward a stronger China.

*America Against America* may strike readers as an odd, even confusing title. I chose it because America is not a single, simple thing that we can sum up in a sentence.

In the past, some people followed rigid political ideas and saw America as nothing more than the exploitation of workers and the rule of the capitalist class. They could find nothing good in it. But the real America contradicts that imaginary America.

Today we see the opposite extreme. Some people imagine America as a glittering paradise where everything is perfect. The real America contradicts that dream just as firmly.

And within American society itself, forces that help the country grow exist alongside forces that hold it back or work against it. Wherever we find one, we can usually find the other. That is the meaning of the title.

This is true of human societies in general. No society is one solid, unchanging block. None can be squeezed into a single ready-made description. I want to discover what produces these opposing forces inside America. If someone asks us to describe America in one sentence, perhaps all we can say is: **America is against America.**

The more closely we study the country, the more clearly these contradictions appear. To study a society seriously, we cannot paint everything rosy or cover everything in soot. We need to see both its strengths and its failings. A willingness to question existing arrangements is one of the forces that makes progress possible. That is the attitude I take toward America.

We should also remember that American institutions, good and bad alike, grew out of America's own history and culture. They work within that setting. We cannot assume they will work in the same way if we simply transplant them somewhere else. Countries cannot learn from one another by copying blindly.

For every picture of America, it is possible to find a picture that seems to say the opposite.

Take wealth. America is undeniably rich. There are people here who own private jets. Most families live comfortably and have a car and other modern conveniences. But that is only one America.

At the University of California, Berkeley, there is a place called People's Park. It was once an open piece of university land, but homeless people came to live there. When I visited, roughly a hundred people spent the night there each day. Some put up makeshift tents from scraps of cloth. Others spread newspapers on the ground and slept in the open. Their ragged clothes, exhaustion and poor conditions seemed to belong to a completely different picture of America. Every morning, a church charity brought breakfast. The university sometimes opened its swimming pool so that people could wash.

On the night George Bush became the forty-first president, I saw homeless people sleeping in the entrances of tall buildings along Bush Street in San Francisco. Was this America? Was it not America? Neither yes nor no is enough.

Or take democracy. America is widely regarded as a model Western democracy, and Americans take pride in that. The Constitution, elections, the division of government power among different branches, and citizens' political rights all support this view. But can ordinary people really decide how their country is run? My observations suggest that powerful groups often stand above them. In a capitalist society, private wealth places important limits on political equality.

The American thinker Sidney Hook argued that when differences in economic power are so great that one group can determine the fate of another without using political office, democracy cannot work properly. Genuine political democracy, he maintained, must give people the power, through their representatives, to shape economic policy (*Reason, Social Myths, and Democracy*, p. 286). In America, major economic decisions are largely in the hands of private business interests. Is this democracy? Is it undemocratic? Again, a one-word answer will not do.

Examples like these are everywhere. America is immensely wealthy, yet its streets are full of people who are poor. It is a leading democracy, yet it has serious limits on democratic power. Its education system is advanced, yet it faces many educational problems. It celebrates equality, yet people remain unequal. It is stable and prosperous, yet it is troubled by deep problems.

I want to set the America that actually exists against the America people imagine.

America is only one capitalist country; it cannot stand for every capitalist society in the West. By studying this particular case, I hope to improve our understanding of capitalism—and, indirectly, of socialism as well.

Nearly a century and a half ago, Marx and Engels argued in *The Communist Manifesto* that modern industry would undermine the foundations of the capitalist class and that capitalism would produce the very people who would eventually overthrow it (*Selected Works of Marx and Engels*, vol. 1, p. 263). Early in this century, Lenin described imperialism as dying and decaying.

Yet all these years later, capitalism has continued to develop, and its strength cannot be dismissed. I believe the broad historical approach of Marxism has value, but the changes it describes depend on real conditions becoming ripe. When those conditions have not developed, wishing history forward—or announcing what must happen before it does—is not a serious way of thinking about history.

For years, political prejudice led us to reject capitalism completely. Rigid formulas prevented people from examining capitalist societies as they really were. So did an extreme left-wing habit of explaining everything through class struggle. Both narrowed our view of the world and kept us from learning from other countries' achievements. Worse still, they made it harder to see capitalism's genuine failings clearly.

Every society, whatever its political system, has conflicts, competing needs and difficult choices. There is much to learn from how different societies meet people's needs and try to ease their conflicts. If this study helps China develop and improve, it will have served the purpose for which I wrote it.

The first task in understanding anything is to see it accurately. Only then should we judge it. That is the principle behind this book.

The eleven chapters cover the following questions:

1. Why different parts of America develop at different speeds, and what those differences look like.
2. The beliefs that guide American politics, and how those beliefs change.
3. The many sides of American national character, and how they shape society.
4. The written rules and unwritten habits that organize people's lives.
5. The political forces active in American society and how they compete or cooperate.
6. The democratic and undemocratic sides of election campaigns.
7. How political power works from the top down.
8. How organizations outside government help manage society.
9. How education passes on a society's culture, values and institutions.
10. How ideas shape a country's development and government.
11. The hidden problems that could threaten America's future.

Even with eleven chapters, I can examine only a small part of a country as large and varied as America. The book has two important limitations.

First, I cannot hope to cover every side of American life. I have tried to understand something of the whole from a few revealing details. The trouble is knowing whether I have chosen the right details. I think I have found some, but not nearly enough. Other writers and sources can help fill the gaps.

Second, I have written as an observer rather than as someone carrying out a thorough scientific survey. Some visits were brief; some figures and accounts, though drawn from sources, would not pass the strictest tests of statistical research. Some of my judgments may be subjective or simply wrong. Please read this as a broad study of society, not as the kind of minute examination a scientist might give a specimen under a microscope.

I have also tried not to torment readers with endless theoretical disputes and references to scholars. That is not what this book is for.

Many people and institutions made it possible, and I want to thank them.

I am grateful to the American Political Science Association, which invited the delegation from the Chinese Political Science Association and arranged an exceptionally rich programme, particularly on the practical workings of American politics. I also thank the Chinese Political Science Association for making the visit possible; the Committee on Scholarly Communication with the People's Republic of China, which invited me to remain for three months as a visiting scholar and covered the costs; and Fudan University, which gave me the time away from teaching to pursue this work.

The University of Iowa's Department of Political Science welcomed me for three months and provided everything I needed to study and work. The University of Michigan's centre for Chinese studies, Miami University's Department of Political Science in Ohio, and the School of International Relations at the University of California, San Diego, arranged shorter visits. I received a warm welcome during three weeks at the Institute of East Asian Studies at the University of California, Berkeley. I also thank the Faculty of Law at Keio University in Japan. A week there helped me form the impressions described in the section called “The Empire of the Sun.”

There are too many individuals to thank properly. Virginia Yan of the Committee on Scholarly Communication with the People's Republic of China took great care in arranging my visit. Professor Stevis and his family offered me extraordinary help during my three months in Iowa, as did Professor Douglas Madsen, head of the Political Science Department, and the department's entire staff.

I am also grateful to Professor Lucian Pye, president of the American Political Science Association; Professor Arnold of Miami University; Professors Oksenberg and Li Peiru at Michigan; Professor Rosenbloom at Syracuse; Professor Mal Kalle at the University of California, San Diego; Professors Robert Scalapino and Gao Ruosi at Berkeley; and Professor Yoshinari Kokubun of Keio University. Their help was invaluable.

My Chinese friends who had lived in America for years also taught me a great deal. I thank Zheng Shiping, Shen Yi, Chen Feng, Gong Ting, Zhou Xueguang, Yu Xu, Wang Renli, Wu Danli, Lin Zhimin, Xu Huahua, Jin Ping, Gai Zheya, He Yufan, Yang Rijun, Huang Geng and Zhu Chen. They lived in different parts of the country, but wherever I visited them, I enjoyed hospitality of the most wonderfully Chinese kind.

I am especially grateful to Associate Professor Ni Shixiong, whose friendship and generous help meant so much during our time together at Berkeley.

When I was putting the final manuscript together, Ren Xiao, Qiu Kaiming, Yao Qin, Guo Dingping and Ding Chunfang helped organize the drafts. Ding Chunfang carefully read through the entire manuscript. My thanks also go to everyone in our department's library and office, who were always willing to help.

Finally, I thank my wife, Zhou Qi. Nobody else could have given me the help she did.

**Wang Huning**
Department of International Politics, Fudan University
April 1, 1989

# Chapter 1: An Uneven Land

## 1. The Puzzle of America

Flight CA985 stopped in Tokyo before making the long journey across the Pacific to San Francisco. We arrived late, just as several flights from Europe and Japan were coming in. The baggage and immigration halls, normally so spacious, were packed with people. The noise was tremendous.

Everyone seemed excited to have reached America, wherever they had come from. One plane must have arrived from France, because I could hear French being spoken all around me.

Then came the wait. We stood in line for more than an hour, and people began complaining, just as they would in China. Many had imagined that long queues were something you left behind when you came to the West. Yet here we were, barely off the plane, waiting in one of the longest lines imaginable.

At San Francisco airport, arriving passengers were divided into three groups: American citizens, foreign visitors, and new immigrants. Two of the counters on the left were reserved for Americans. Hardly anyone was waiting there. Being an American citizen clearly had its advantages. The lines for everyone else were much longer, and it was hard not to feel that we had already been placed a rung lower.

There were large groups of Japanese travellers in the immigration hall. Japanese tourists and businesspeople could now be found all over the world, often travelling in groups. Their presence was a visible sign of how wealthy and powerful Japan had become. Foreigners might not always find the Japanese particularly likeable, but their country's economic achievements commanded respect.

I heard that the American government was considering allowing Japanese visitors to enter without visas. There were so many of them that this single change, according to one estimate, could speed up the processing of arriving passengers by a third. But such arrangements usually require both countries to offer the same privilege, and Japan apparently had little desire to waive its visa requirements for Americans.

Japan's experience suggests two things. A country's economic strength is one of the main forces shaping its standing and reputation in the world. Yet wealth alone does not determine how other countries see it.

America is so far ahead in so many areas that arriving here can feel like stepping into the future. The experience leaves people with very different questions.

Some wonder how best to enjoy everything America has to offer. Others ask a more difficult question: **How did America become what it is?**

I put this question to several friends.

One pointed to America's extraordinary natural resources and the opportunities it gives talented people to compete and succeed. Another agreed, though he added that things might only look that way when you first arrive.

Then I asked G., a friend studying for his doctorate at Stanford University.

His answer was just one word: *tradition*.

Of all the answers I heard, this was the most abstract, but perhaps also the most revealing.

No society becomes prosperous through economic forces alone. Nor can a country reach America's level of wealth simply by changing its policies or persuading people to behave differently for a few years. Success on this scale cannot be produced by government orders.

Something deeper must be at work.

What makes generation after generation of Americans willing to strive, work, build, and improve?

We might point to their inventiveness, determination, or willingness to save and work hard. But what matters most is whether these qualities become part of the culture—habits and expectations passed from one generation to the next.

A useful habit cannot shape an entire society unless it takes root. And for that to happen, it usually takes several generations.

Americans talk constantly about innovation, yet tradition has an enormous hold on their society.

The Chinese also talk constantly about innovation, but our old traditions have a habit of disappearing and then returning.

Someone once made an interesting observation: when Americans talk about innovation, they are not necessarily talking about breaking with tradition. In America, tradition and innovation are almost two sides of the same thing. What counts as tradition today may have been a bold new idea only thirty or fifty years ago.

In China, by contrast, innovation often means a deliberate break with the past. But traditions that have survived for a thousand or two thousand years are not so easily swept aside.

The enormous numbers of people now coming to America should give us plenty to think about.

Just before leaving China, I had followed a lively debate among Chinese scholars about how we should understand socialism and capitalism. The reason for the debate was plain enough. After decades of socialist development, China still lagged behind the leading capitalist countries in economic prosperity.

This raised difficult questions.

Was Marx right about the course human societies would follow? Would socialism eventually overcome capitalism, as he had predicted? Or was history proving more complicated than expected?

These questions occupied many minds.

I remembered the film *The Red Guards of Honghu Lake*, which had been shown aboard the Boeing 747 on our way to America. It brought to mind the Chinese Revolution and the great struggles through which the Chinese people had overthrown the old forces that oppressed them.

But winning a revolution is not the same as securing a country's future.

Had the revolution, by removing those old burdens, also ensured that Chinese society would develop successfully? And what were the long-term consequences of a revolution that began in the countryside, surrounded the cities, and drew most of its strength from the rural population?

These were questions worth asking.

America's achievements have created a puzzle for the entire world. Its wealth, political system, way of life, and international standing are so remarkable that they force people to think.

But not everyone sees the same puzzle.

People in wealthy countries look at the extraordinary progress of technology and modern living and wonder whether humanity has gone too far. Have we begun to live in ways that go against our own nature? Will we exhaust the planet's resources? Could our relentless pursuit of progress eventually destroy us?

These are the fears raised by thinkers such as those associated with the Club of Rome, who have long warned about the dangers of unchecked growth.

People in developing countries ask almost the opposite questions.

How did this astonishing world of wealth and technology come into being? What kind of political and economic system made it possible? Did America become so prosperous by chance, or were there deeper reasons for its success?

Such questions can lead people to doubt the American system. But they can just as easily make them question their own.

Either way, America leaves us with a puzzle.

You encounter it when you visit the country. You encounter it even if you never set foot there.

And there is something curious about this puzzle: getting caught up in it is easy. Finding your way to an answer is much harder.

## 2. Manhattan and Chinatown

Our plane reached New York. After landing I collected my luggage, but the friend who was supposed to meet me was nowhere to be seen. The other passengers disappeared one by one until I was left waiting alone.

I began to get nervous. Friends had warned me that New York could feel frightening, especially because of its high crime rate. I wondered whether I might run into trouble before I had even left the airport. Half an hour later my friend finally arrived, and I could relax.

We went straight from the airport to the United Nations headquarters. The building is magnificent. We visited the Security Council chamber and took photographs, then went into the General Assembly hall. I found the Chinese delegation's seats and had my picture taken there. We each posed at the speaker's lectern too, as though we were addressing the nations of the world.

During the tour I saw the fine and often expensive gifts countries had given the United Nations: an ivory carving from China, a royal barge from Thailand, a painting from the Soviet Union, and many others. Every country seems to want to be seen and respected at the UN. But how many really live by the principles they praise there?

It reminded me of a group whose members proudly declare their loyalty while repeatedly breaking its rules. Nations today are divided by conflicting interests and deeply held political beliefs, and new disputes keep erupting. The UN has certainly not achieved everything it ought to have achieved. Still, its contribution should not be dismissed, especially in economic, social and cultural development. The ceasefire between Iran and Iraq was a notable success in the effort to end conflict.

The world plainly still needs humanity to find ways of controlling its own violence. Outside the UN stands a sculpture of a man beating a sword into a ploughshare. Looking at it, I wondered how people might truly turn their weapons into tools for peaceful life. History offers plenty of examples of people trying to end war through war. So far, striking one sword with another has never turned either into a plough.

New York's Chinatown felt familiar at once. Compared with surrounding streets, it looked a little dirtier and more crowded. I had heard that it was a complicated neighbourhood, home to many of the habits and problems Chinese people recognize in their own communities. It brought to mind Bo Yang's book *The Ugly Chinaman*.

I did not spend much time walking around. From what I saw, small businesses were everywhere. Chinese signs filled the busy streets, making me think of China, so far away.

The contrast with the rest of Manhattan was striking. Chinatown spoke of Chinese culture; the streets beyond it spoke of Western culture.

When Chinese scholars and students meet in America, we nearly always end up talking about China and the United States. And within that endless conversation, two subjects keep returning: economic development and political democracy.

America's economic and technological achievements in this century are plain to see. No other country has matched them. Japan is advancing rapidly and challenging America's lead, but economic competition is one thing. In military power, culture and natural resources, Japan still cannot compare with the United States.

What we need to understand is the relationship between America's economic success and its political development. The world pays attention to America in both areas. China, meanwhile, has a great deal of catching up to do in both. That is why the two questions dominate our discussions about modernization.

How can China build a modern economy? More particularly, can we do it while keeping public ownership of the main means of production? Most of the world's wealthy countries do not have that kind of economy. This fact presents us with a serious challenge to think through.

And how should political democracy develop? Must it advance alongside economic growth, or can the two move at different speeds?

There are arguments on both sides. Some say a country cannot modernize its economy without political democracy. Others point to Hong Kong, Taiwan, Singapore and South Korea. During their periods of rapid economic growth, none was a fully democratic political system: Hong Kong was under colonial rule, Taiwan under one-party rule, and South Korea under strong military influence.

Another argument is that economic development creates the conditions for democracy. Supporters point to movements toward democracy in newly industrialized countries and territories. But critics reply that Western countries established democratic or republican institutions after their earlier political revolutions, long before they were nearly as wealthy as some developing countries are today.

Chinese thinkers will have to wrestle seriously with these questions.

Economic growth by itself does not settle the argument. Both centralized government and democratic government can help or hinder development. What matters is which changes brought about by economic growth make democracy more likely to develop.

There is another side to the relationship between wealthy and poorer countries.

Countries that have not yet developed strong economies must often depend on advanced countries for technology, equipment and precision machinery. Their needs differ according to their circumstances. In parts of Africa, the most urgent need is simply enough food to keep people alive.

Whatever they need, poorer countries often face the same bargain: to obtain goods from wealthier countries, they must sell their own best products. As a result, their people may be unable to enjoy the finest things their own country produces. Even the second-best products may be reserved for foreign visitors.

Look at an American shop and you will find goods from around the world: China, Taiwan, South Korea, Japan, Jamaica, Mexico, and many other places. Countries send their best products into the American market because they want dollars. Competition for those dollars is fierce, so quality tends to be high and prices low.

This helps make American shops astonishingly well supplied. It is partly the result of competition in the market. America also benefits from the international standing given to the dollar by the Bretton Woods agreements after the Second World War.

But there is another side here too. The flood of foreign goods puts pressure on American factories. Some close, and workers lose their jobs. That is why supporters of free trade and those who want to protect American industries remain locked in a struggle.

## 3. The Four Cs

After living in America for a short time, I began to notice four things that seemed to be everywhere. They may look like ordinary features of daily life, but together they tell us a great deal about how this society works.

They all happen to begin with the letter C in English: **cars, calls, computers and cards.**

**Cars.** They fill the streets of big cities and small towns, the freeways and the country roads. There are so many makes and models that the variety is almost bewildering.

While we were in Washington, Antony King, vice-chancellor of the University of Essex in Britain, pointed to the traffic and said, “Ten years ago, most of the cars I saw in America were made here. Now you see every kind, especially Japanese cars.”

The struggle between American and Japanese carmakers is well known. One reason Japanese manufacturers have been so successful here is simply that the American car market is enormous. A car matters tremendously to everyday life. In much of America, **not having a car is like not having legs.**

The highway system is highly developed. A typical freeway might have three lanes in each direction, used by vehicles travelling at different speeds. Some roads have as many as seven lanes on each side. Traffic streams along them day and night.

Most families own a car, and many own more than one. At Stanford I visited a professor whose family had three. The number of privately owned cars in America must be among the highest in the world.

**Calls.** Telephones are everywhere. Every home and office has one, and many homes have several extensions. Public places are full of payphones, where you put coins into a machine to make a call. Newer machines take telephone cards instead. At airports, you see whole rows of them.

The Sheraton and Hilton hotels where I stayed in Washington both had rows of public phones in their lobbies. Universities have them in libraries, dining halls, meeting rooms, computer centres and language centres.

What is impressive is not just how many telephones there are, but how easily they work. Pick up a receiver, dial a number anywhere in the country, and you are usually connected within half a minute. International calls are easy too. Americans conduct an astonishing amount of business over the phone.

They conduct their romances that way as well. Several parents complained to me that their children would talk to someone they were dating for two hours at a stretch, tying up the family line and running up huge bills. Of course, the social importance of a good telephone system goes far beyond romance.

**Computers.** Wherever you go in America, somebody seems to be using one. During a visit to the National Security Council, I noticed that people there worked with computers. University professors have them in their offices and often at home. Hotels use them to manage bookings and services. Applications for driving licences are entered into computers. Shops and dining halls use them to take payments; libraries use them to find books and records; banks use them to manage deposits.

Factories, government departments, the armed forces, aviation—all depend heavily on computers. They make work faster, more accurate and easier to check.

Computers can also connect to networks that span the country or even the world. At Yale University, the head of the East Asian library entered my name into a computer. Within seconds he found copies of my books *National Sovereignty* and *Comparative Political Analysis* in two other universities. Computers can even send messages to one another. If you know someone's network address—what the source calls a “Bignet ID”—you can type a message and send it to them.

**Cards.** These are not the paper ration cards Chinese people once used to obtain coal, eggs, vegetables or other daily necessities. American cards are usually plastic, with a magnetic strip that stores information.

They come in seemingly endless varieties: credit cards, bank cards, library cards, telephone cards, identity cards, and many more. New kinds keep appearing. I saw a newspaper report about an airline offering certain passengers the use of first-class facilities through a special card. My United Airlines ticket from San Francisco to Iowa was itself much like one.

Cards work together with computers. Slip a bank card into a machine and you can withdraw money, deposit it, or check your balance without asking a bank employee for help. Almost anyone with a job seems to carry a wallet stuffed with cards. Even the design of wallets has changed: they now have little pockets made especially for them. Many wallets manufactured in China might not sell well in America, simply because they have nowhere to put the cards.

These are the four Cs. What do they tell us?

Cars allow people and goods to move across the country. But they carry more than physical things. As people travel, ideas, attitudes and habits travel with them.

Telephones create an extraordinary network of communication. Voices carry facts, feelings and personal relationships from place to place.

Computers help different organizations work together and manage information on a huge scale. They can also make the handling of information more consistent and impartial.

Cards turn many everyday dealings into something that can be handled through a simple recorded sign or number. Instead of dealing directly with every person and every object, an organization can often deal with the information represented on a card.

Together, these developments help a society pass on its rules, habits and shared beliefs. They give those beliefs a physical place in everyday life, making the larger system more familiar and more firmly established.

Yet none of this comes free. Modern progress has costs, for people and for nature alike.

More cars mean more pollution, more road accidents and more waste.

More telephones mean more interruptions and more opportunities for eavesdropping.

More computers can leave an entire communications system vulnerable. If a machine fails or a computer virus spreads, the damage may reach a great many people, and valuable records can disappear.

More cards bring more theft, lost cards and opportunities for fraud.

This raises a larger question: how does a society make its institutions last?

Writing rules and creating organizations are not enough. Institutions become truly secure only when they are woven into the ordinary lives of the people who depend on them.
## 4. Everything Has a Price

Another striking feature of American society is the sheer number of things that can be bought and sold. This is capitalism in full colour. Walk into almost any shop and you are met with such a dazzling range of goods that it is hard to know where to look first.

But the market goes well beyond the things on shop shelves. In America, almost anything can be turned into something for sale: people's bodies, clean air, ideas, and all manner of useful objects and services. When Marx described the commodity—the thing made or supplied for sale—as the basic building block of capitalism, he saw something important. In today's capitalist societies, buying and selling have spread into more corners of life than ever before, even if they take different forms in different places.

The clearest example is that human labour itself can be bought and sold. People offer their time and abilities in return for wages. This far-reaching system is part of how capitalism develops. It does not depend simply on what particular people happen to want.

But what interests me most is not how this system came about or just how far it has spread. I want to ask a different question: **What does it do for the way society is organized and run?**

Think of the extraordinary demands modern life places on a country. People need homes, food, transport, jobs and education. They also expect entertainment, healthcare, culture, art and a thousand other services. All these activities are connected, and as society grows more complicated, keeping them going becomes an enormous task.

Can a government really take direct responsibility for everything?

In theory and in practice, I doubt that any government has the ability. Even if it tries, there are limits to what it can reach and control. In a small society, the government may be able to handle a large share of these tasks itself. Singapore, Hong Kong and South Korea suggest some possibilities. But in a country with hundreds of millions of people, it becomes much harder to imagine the government directly managing every part of life.

History gives us examples of countries that organized themselves this way and then struggled to meet basic needs. Their governments took on more than they could manage, and the problems accumulated.

There is another approach. Let different parts of society develop their own ways of doing things and meeting people's needs. The government can then supervise and set boundaries instead of trying to run every activity itself.

This is one of the important things that buying and selling make possible. A market gives people and organizations reasons to supply what others need, without waiting for an official instruction.

Consider how this works in a few everyday areas.

**Housing.** In many developing countries, finding decent homes for everyone is a huge problem. Governments work tirelessly to provide housing and still cannot keep up. In America, most families are reasonably well housed. Homeless people certainly exist, and in the larger cities they are impossible to miss. Yet many households have spacious homes, sometimes with seven or eight rooms occupied by only one or two people. New houses continue to be built.

Housing is largely handled through the market. If you want a home, you buy or rent one. Property is a major part of the economy, and prices change from place to place. A house that costs around $100,000 in a small Midwestern city might be impossible to find for even $200,000 in New York or San Francisco. An apartment in Manhattan might rent for $400 or $500 a month, while a comparable place in a small town might cost only $200 or $300.

Because people can earn money by building or owning property, there is a continuing reason to build more. Year after year, the number of homes grows. The government's main job is to make sure the activity stays within the law, not to build, assign and repair every home itself. Public housing for the poor accounts for only a small part of the total.

**Food.** Feeding a population is another problem that troubles governments around the world. Some countries cannot provide enough to keep everyone properly fed. Others have achieved that much, but still struggle to offer a good variety of foods. Officials can spend all their time dealing with shortages.

In America the abundance is startling. There is so much food, and so much choice, that it can seem wasteful. Production and distribution are handled largely through buying and selling. Farmers and food producers make what they believe people want. Distributors and shops bring it to customers. Each stage has a chance to earn a profit.

That chance to make money is one of the strongest reasons the system keeps working. Of course, markets alone do not explain the abundance. Modern science and advanced methods of production are essential too.

**Transport.** As a country develops, people and goods need to move more freely. Business, education, cultural life and personal opportunity all depend on that movement. If transport cannot keep pace, growth in many other areas suffers.

In America, much of transport is run as a business. Airlines such as United, Delta, Northwest and TWA must cover their costs and compete for customers. Air travel is so extensive that it has become one of the forces behind the country's economic growth. On the roads, companies such as Greyhound connect towns and cities across the nation.

The prospect of profit encourages companies to offer services. Competition then gives them a reason to improve those services and reach new customers.

**Jobs.** Modern society faces a difficult contradiction. New technology allows more work to be done with fewer people, yet more and more people need employment to earn a living.

In America, labour is bought and sold like other things. The government does not normally assign people to jobs. It can guide employment policy and help create opportunities, but individual employers choose whom to hire, and individuals seek work wherever they wish. At least, that is how the system is meant to operate. It certainly does not mean that everyone can get the job they want.

In a society where almost everything can be purchased, the pay attached to a job can matter more than the kind of work it involves. People may accept work because the wages allow them to buy the things they want. Once they have earned money, they can return to the market for goods and services.

The government therefore does not have to arrange every appointment or match every worker with every employer. Businesses and individuals settle much of this among themselves.

These examples show how American society is organized on two levels. Businesses and other organizations handle countless practical tasks. The political system sets rules and helps keep these organizations working together.

We should not imagine that the market makes everything perfect. It does not. What it offers is a way for government to avoid being overwhelmed by the details of daily life. A government tangled in every small decision will struggle to do its job well. Once it steps back, it still needs to supervise, but it can do so more indirectly.

The market has astonishing reach. Wherever there is money to be made, somebody is likely to turn up with goods or a service. Kentucky Fried Chicken, hamburger chains, Coca-Cola, Pepsi, Crest toothpaste, Marlboro cigarettes, and United Parcel Service can be found in places that seem remarkably remote.

The prospect of earning even a modest amount is enough to draw businesses into places they might otherwise ignore. It also encourages them to offer new kinds of products and enter fields that nobody has yet developed. This happens with small everyday goods, and it happens with advanced technology.

But we must look at the other side too.

The power to turn things into products for sale can be disturbing. Bodies, sex, knowledge, politics, power and even the law may become opportunities for profit. The pornography industry around Forty-Second Street in New York is one example. In many ways the spread of buying and selling can damage society and create serious problems. Those problems then become another burden for government.

So a market can lighten government's workload only on one condition: **government must still regulate the market.** The point is not that literally everything should be for sale. The point is to make sure the buying and selling that do take place work within reasonable rules.

Prices and profits help coordinate economic life, but public authorities have a part to play as well. Once the major areas of daily life have developed their own organizations, government can leave direct management to them and concentrate on oversight.

This change does not happen overnight. Even after years of development, there will be disputes, obstacles, conflicts and disorder. Yet it is easier for a government to supervise a few dozen large areas of activity than to manage tens of thousands of separate jobs itself. That difference can make the government stronger and more effective.

## 5. A Different World Next Door

When I speak of America's “remote regions,” I do not mean places that are far away on the map. In fact, some are only a few minutes from modern cities. Their remoteness is of another kind.

I am thinking of the religious communities the Chinese text calls *Armenians*. They are an unusual people, largely rejecting modern technology and preserving a way of life that resembles Europe in the seventeenth or eighteenth century. That is what makes them so fascinating. Modern Western life has spread across the world, changing societies thousands of miles away. Yet here, close to the heart of modern America, is a community that has kept it at arm's length.

Only a short drive from Iowa City—perhaps ten or fifteen minutes—is a settlement called Kolona. As you approach, you begin to see horse-drawn buggies on the road. They look like vehicles from an old European painting: a horse pulling a small enclosed carriage, often with people dressed in black sitting inside.

Even the road makes room for them. Separate lanes run alongside the car lanes because these residents do not use modern automobiles. Officials spent considerable effort persuading them that sharing the highway with fast-moving cars was dangerous and that they should put a bright red warning sign on the backs of their buggies.

Who are these people? Where did they come from, and why did they settle in America? Their history offers a clue.

Their community traces its beginnings to the religious upheavals of sixteenth-century Europe. They had beliefs of their own, and established churches persecuted them harshly. I was told that people were burned, executed or imprisoned for their faith. Beginning around 1750, some groups wandered through Germany, France, the Netherlands and Russia in search of somewhere safe to live. Eventually they turned toward the New World.

In America, they could find land and own it. Land matters enormously to them. They see it as a visible gift from God, something precious that people should not abandon. This belief helps explain why they cling so firmly to farming and to their old way of life.

There are said to be about 112 of these settlements across America. Not all follow exactly the same rules. Some groups farm with tractors and combine harvesters. But, despite such differences, they retain much of their older spirit and outlook.

What does that outlook mean in practice?

Above all, they ask whether something is useful rather than whether it is fashionable or impressive. Clothes should keep you warm, not show off your wealth. At one time they even rejected metal buttons because these seemed like unnecessary luxuries and signs of status. You will not find bright, showy pictures hanging on their walls. Instead, there may be a plain calendar with an unremarkable picture.

Their ideal is a simple, natural life in which a community can provide for itself.

Some of their choices are hard for modern people to imagine. In the strictest settlements, there is no electricity. They refuse to bring it into their villages because they believe in being self-reliant. Centuries of persecution taught them not to depend too heavily on the outside world.

They do much of their farming with horses rather than tractors or combines. Women wear old-fashioned dresses, not jeans. American homes almost always have telephones, but theirs do not. Some settlements accept washing machines and refrigerators, though they generate their own electricity with diesel engines rather than drawing it from outside power lines.

For everyday travel they use buggies. Some residents have never visited Iowa City, even though it is only ten minutes away by car.

To the modern eye, they can appear isolated and backward. They may know relatively little about what is happening in the wider world. Yet within their own way of life they seem content and at ease. Their choices are also part of their religious faith.

They follow traditions that many outsiders find puzzling. In winter, men gather to cut blocks of ice from a frozen river, then store them for the summer. They are so good at keeping the ice that it may last until the following September. The ice does the work of a refrigerator. Ancient China knew similar ways of storing ice.

They do not necessarily need to keep doing this today, but the work helps preserve the life they believe in.

At home they speak a German dialect rather than English. They run their own schools, but many children do not continue into higher education. The education available within the community is limited, and they are reluctant to send their young people to large cities.

Their religious life has its own routines. About once every two weeks they hold a gathering at a different family's home. The hosts prepare carefully and provide a meal. The society is strongly patriarchal: men hold authority, while women have little say. Every boy and girl learns to handle horses, because that skill is an essential part of everyday life.

Despite the changes that have come to some settlements over the years, their basic outlook has hardly changed.

How has a civilization powerful enough to transform distant countries failed to transform these communities on its doorstep?

I put this question to a writer living in Kolona, John M. Zielinski, who had written a book about these communities in America.

“Imagine growing up in a place like this,” she told me, “and being taught these beliefs strictly from childhood. They would not be easy to abandon. And these people have not had much higher education. They have not been trained for modern jobs. They know farming. This is where they feel safest.”

That final point struck me as especially important: **this is where they feel safest.**

Zielinski added another reason. The American Constitution protects their right to live and worship as they choose. The government cannot force them to change their religion or their way of life.

There is something remarkable about a community rejecting modern life while living right in the middle of the modern world.

These people are not far away in any geographical sense. Their distance is in their minds and beliefs. They have chosen to separate themselves from the modern world. And when people reject change at that level, it becomes very difficult for the outside world to make them change.

This tells us something that applies far beyond these settlements: **real modernization begins in people's minds.**

Another important factor is the feeling of safety. For these residents, their familiar way of life offers protection. Change threatens to take that protection away. They fear that adopting modern ways would mean disorder and danger.

Every attempt to modernize a society changes old relationships and ways of living. It also takes away things that once made people feel secure. When enough people fear losing that security, they may resist change with extraordinary determination.

How does American society handle the tension? In large part, it leaves them alone.

The government does not try to make everyone live alike. Nor could it easily do so. Forcing these communities to adopt modern technology would simply create more problems for public officials. Many other groups in America live in ways that do not fit the mainstream. They are allowed to continue.

Some disagreements can be eased simply by accepting that differences exist. Nobody seems embarrassed by these communities' rejection of modern life. On the contrary, many Americans see their freedom to live differently as an expression of the American spirit.

Sometimes, the most effective way to govern is to refrain from interfering.

On the return journey, I saw an elderly couple travelling along a modern road in their horse-drawn buggy. They looked entirely peaceful, comfortable and content.

Was it possible, I wondered, that modern life had something wrong with it?

Every way of life brings benefits. And every way of life has its price.
## 6. The Amana Colonies

The Amana Colonies in Iowa are a curious place. Americans sometimes describe them as an example of “socialism” or “collectivism” in the middle of the United States.

How could a community based on shared ownership survive in a country built around private business and private property? What was this community actually like? Coming from China, I was fascinated. **How did Americans go about living collectively?**

Amana has a long history. In 1714, two people in southwestern Germany began a religious movement that eventually became known as the Community of True Inspiration. The Amana community grew out of that movement.

Its members later suffered persecution because of their religion. In 1842 their ancestors crossed the Atlantic to North America. They first established six villages in New York State and two in Canada. But the area around Buffalo began to grow into a city, and the community decided to buy land farther west.

In 1854 they purchased the land in Iowa where the colonies now stand: first 25,000 acres from the government, and later another 1,000 acres. There they farmed and built their lives.

They chose to live as a religious community. Land, buildings and other property belonged to everyone collectively. A single leadership group made decisions about both religious affairs and everyday life. The arrangement lasted for more than a century, until the members voted in 1932 to separate the church from the management of the community and move toward private enterprise.

The community's original charter stated that its purpose was not worldly success or personal gain. It was to love God, serve God together, and follow God's commands. Members pledged themselves to this shared aim.

This tells us something important. From the beginning, Amana's system of shared ownership rested on a religious belief that brought people together. Perhaps a community that asks everyone to put collective needs ahead of personal interests cannot hold together for long without some strong shared belief. It is a question worth thinking about.

Guided by that faith, the colonists owned everything together: the land, crops, livestock, houses and farm tools. The community did more than share property. It took responsibility for educating children, publishing material, and looking after older people, the sick and those unable to support themselves.

Its leadership arrangements were unusual too.

Until 1883, the community was led by Christian Metz, who had helped bring its members from Europe to America. After his death, leadership passed to a thirteen-member board of trustees, chosen each year from a group of elders.

Men who had signed the community charter could vote, as could widows and women over thirty. Originally Metz had chosen the elders himself. After 1883 they were nominated by an elders' council and approved by the board.

The elders led religious affairs and met on Sunday mornings. Local councils of elders met each week and looked after local matters. The highest body, the board of trustees, met monthly and oversaw both religious and everyday affairs. Under this system the people of Amana lived collectively for many years.

Why had they chosen this way of life in the first place?

Metz explained the belief behind it. The church, he said, served God, and God blessed the community through the church. Anyone who wanted to devote themselves fully to God should therefore devote themselves fully to the church. Property should belong to the church, which would provide for everyone's needs. Anyone who could not accept the arrangement was free to leave; anyone who stayed had to live by it.

The name *Amana* came from a passage in the Bible's fourth chapter of the Song of Songs, which calls a beloved to come away from Lebanon and look down from Mount Amana.

Then came the great turning point of 1932.

The people of Amana voted to abandon their communal way of life and adopt the system of private business that surrounded them. Church and community government were separated. The elders no longer directed every aspect of daily life.

Why did they make such a dramatic change?

Was something wrong with collective living itself? Was the capitalist world outside simply too powerful? Or had the people lost faith in the religious beliefs that had held their community together?

These are not small questions. Communities based on shared living still face serious challenges. To understand Amana's experience is to understand something about why those challenges arise.

The official who received us gave an explanation that sounded remarkably familiar.

One problem, he said, was that people had little reason to take initiative or work hard. Some became lazy, knowing that the community would provide for them whether they made an effort or not.

Another problem was the younger generation. Young people began to doubt the old rules. They saw the attractions of the outside world and no longer wanted to attend religious services eight times a week.

The elders sent some young people away for higher education, training them to become doctors, teachers or businesspeople. But many others found their hopes blocked. Young men could not find the work they wanted, while young women might have little prospect beyond a job in the communal dining halls.

There was also a problem with power. The same elders stayed in charge, and leadership began to look almost hereditary, as though certain people had a permanent right to rule. That hierarchy weakened the spirit of togetherness on which the community depended.

Today Amana is organized very differently. Much of the property that once belonged to everyone has been turned into shares that people can buy. Shareholders can receive dividends, and employees earn wages. There are nearly a hundred private businesses, most of them shops and restaurants.

The remaining shared assets are managed by a board of directors. They include factories making refrigerators, textiles, dyed cloth and furniture, along with farms and hotels. People no longer live communally as they once did.

Amana's own publicity material now takes care to distinguish its early “communism” from the political communism of Russia. It explains that shared ownership was a practical answer to economic problems, not a fundamental political doctrine or belief.

But this explanation does not really fit Amana's beginnings. Its original way of life was clearly built on a belief—religious rather than political, certainly, but a belief all the same. When the church governed everyday life as well as religious matters, that distinction may not have meant very much to the people living under it.

Once we recognize how important that shared faith was, the change of 1932 becomes easier to understand.

I suspect the deepest reason was that younger people were losing interest in the beliefs that had once held the colonies together. Surrounded by a prosperous and tempting world, they began to want different things. Once a new generation turns away from a society's founding beliefs, it becomes extraordinarily difficult to preserve the old system by force or authority alone.

This is not a problem unique to Amana. Every kind of society faces it. In many countries today, young people are losing confidence in the basic ideas on which their institutions were built. If that loss of confidence is not addressed, the institutions themselves will come under pressure.

Amana provides a vivid example of what happens when two different ways of organizing society exist side by side and influence one another.

In the end, the colonies adopted the dominant values of American life. Their old system was too weak, politically, economically and culturally, to withstand the attraction and power of modern capitalism.

Today the Amana Colonies are protected as a national historic site. In a way, that is their most revealing feature.

They show how, when one set of values dominates a society, competing ways of life can gradually become things of the past.

Will America's own dominant values someday become history too? That will depend on whether another way of life emerges that proves more powerful.

## 7. Life in the Countryside

For a visitor racing through America, the country can seem like a succession of dazzling cities: skyscrapers crowded together, cars rushing past, brightly lit shops, and streets full of people from every walk of life. America's wealth is most visible in its cities, so it is easy to mistake those first impressions for a picture of the whole country.

But America is not just its cities, however important they may be.

The gap between town and countryside is considerable. Rural living standards fall well short of those in the great cities. Anyone who wants to know America must spend some time outside its urban centres. Otherwise, at best, they have seen only part of it.

Living conditions in the countryside have pushed people towards the cities for years, leaving fewer and fewer behind. Today, only a few per cent of Americans live in rural areas; more than ninety per cent live in towns and cities. One might say this is a sign of highly developed agriculture, which no longer needs an enormous farming population. America is also, as I understand it, the world's largest importer of food, although it exports a great deal too. And as people have left the land, American farms have had to rely increasingly on machines.

The great farming regions lie mostly in the middle of the country and parts of the Midwest. Flying from one coast to the other tells you very little about how life on those vast plains differs from life in a major city. To feel the difference, you have to live there for a while, or at least travel along the roads that cross them.

You need to keep your feet on the ground. Looking down from the clouds will not tell you much about country life.

In many places along the eastern and western coasts, you rarely see enormous fields stretching towards the horizon, farmhouses scattered across open land, cattle and pigs grazing at leisure, or roads that send up great clouds of dust. I saw almost no farmland on the train from New York to Philadelphia. But driving from Iowa City into Illinois or Missouri, or travelling through Ohio, I saw one immense field after another.

I would happily spend five or six hours in a car just to take in the American countryside.

What does that countryside look like? Several things struck me.

**First, the land itself.** In the farming states, fields seem to stretch on forever. This is an enormous natural advantage. Much of the land is flat or gently sloping, with few hills or rivers to obstruct large machines. Farming by machine is much harder in parts of China's Jiangsu and Zhejiang provinces, where the landscape is broken up by waterways and hills.

The soil looks extraordinarily rich—dark, almost black. One friend told me he had planted tomatoes behind his house and harvested more than he could possibly eat. Much of this American land has been farmed for less than two hundred years, and it still has plenty of life in it. In China, after more than two thousand years of cultivation, much of our farmland has become poor.

**Then there is the emptiness.** You can drive for miles without meeting another person. Houses appear here and there, often separated by great distances. Most farms belong to individual families. Their homes are generally comfortable, though not as impressive as the houses in the cities.

The quiet seems to suit the wildlife. Many roads carry signs warning drivers to watch out for deer. Once, on the way to Chicago, I saw three or four deer playing beside the road.

**The small towns are another part of the picture.** Shopping and cultural life are less developed here than in the cities. I must have passed through dozens of these places. Many looked rather old and worn. Some consisted of little more than a single street, with shops on either side. Even so, the shops usually carried all the everyday goods people needed. That is one of the remarkable things about a market economy: goods find their way into even the smallest communities.

The buildings were generally two or three storeys high, in older styles, with little of the striking modern architecture seen in the cities. There were not many buildings, and not many people either. Attitudes tended to be more conservative. People were often less open to unfamiliar ideas than city dwellers.

Country roads were much humbler than the big interstate highways. Most had only two lanes, one in each direction, and their surfaces were not especially smooth. They were little different from some of China's better long-distance roads. Still, the signs were clear and the lanes carefully marked. Smaller roads leading to farms or houses were often covered in gravel rather than asphalt. Driving along them raised a cloud of dust. You really felt you were out on the road.

**Abandoned houses were common too.** Some had collapsed and been left untouched. Others were still occupied but looked badly neglected. As more people moved away, homes fell into disrepair or stood empty. Children went to university in the cities, found work there, and never returned. When their parents died, the family home might be sold cheaply—or simply left to wind and rain.

The countryside could look shabby beside the bright lights of America's cities. Some places had lost every resident and become ghost villages.

**There were sights that reminded me of poorer countries.** You could see cattle, horses, and pigs grazing in fields, though not in anything like the great herds of Inner Mongolia. Most American farms are family-run, so their herds are limited in size. Some animals are kept inside buildings, where passers-by never see them. Others graze in fenced areas: cattle, pigs, sheep, or horses. From time to time, the smell of manure hits you full in the face. In America, that is an unusual experience.

**Finally, rural poverty was impossible to miss.** Country people generally lived less comfortably than city people, and many worried constantly about money. Some had little education and struggled to find good jobs. They could not simply move to a city and start again. Others survived by running tiny businesses.

I visited a fisherman who lived beside the Mississippi River in Missouri. He caught fish and sold them from his home at modest prices. The house stood on a fairly large plot of land, but it was run-down and had not been repaired in years.

Some areas were much poorer still. Mr Y. told me about one of the poorest places he had visited in America. People lived in broken-down houses with hardly any furniture—perhaps just a few battered tables and chairs. The smell when you entered was unpleasant. The residents seemed weary, dispirited, and without much hope. Such conditions were not everywhere, of course, but neither were they rare.

All this poverty must be judged in context. American villages and farms look poor beside America's richest cities, but compared with rural areas in many developing countries, they are still relatively prosperous.

Every country has some difference between town and countryside. What matters is what that difference means for the country as a whole.

In America, where more than ninety per cent of the population lives in urban areas, the problems of the countryside are unlikely to become an immediate source of nationwide unrest. No satisfactory solution has been found. The difficulties may last for years and may well get worse. But for the moment, under certain conditions, they are unlikely to shake the country.

China is a very different matter. With more than eighty per cent of its people living in the countryside, the gap between rural and urban life carries entirely different political and social risks.

America's answer, in practice, has been to draw people out of the less developed areas and concentrate them in cities. As industry and other forms of production have grown, this movement has helped reduce tensions in the countryside.

But it has not solved the problems themselves. How far can this movement go? There must be a limit. If farm production ever comes under serious threat, the gap between town and countryside could become a much bigger problem. And under the present system it is hard to imagine what could reverse the flow of people into cities.

Every society has to think seriously about these differences. Unequal living standards create tensions between groups of people. Those tensions may appear sooner in one country and later in another, but sooner or later they become part of the challenge of modernization.

The real question is not simply how much the countryside has developed. It is **how far the tensions have been eased—and whether the methods used to ease them have created new problems of their own.**

## 8. The Decline of the Farm

American farms have always interested me, and the reason is simple. Only about three or four per cent of Americans work in agriculture, yet they grow enough food for more than two hundred million people and still have enormous amounts to export. I remember the Soviet Union importing large quantities of American grain.

Many other countries have far more people in the countryside than in the cities, yet still cannot feed themselves. Finding a way past this problem is essential if a country wants to become modern and prosperous.

China has nearly 1.1 billion people, more than eighty per cent of whom live in the countryside. Yet our food supply, including meat and other everyday foods, is far from plentiful.

Feeding people is perhaps humanity's oldest problem. It has troubled us for as long as we have existed, and we still have not solved it. Famines in Africa have reached appalling proportions, with countless lives at risk.

Food is a matter of life and death in the most literal sense. I remember reading in *The Winds of War* about young women in Berlin, just after the Second World War, offering their bodies in return for a little food. When people cannot meet the most basic need of staying alive, talk of dignity and rights becomes terribly difficult.

The farm I visited was just one among many, but it was a fairly typical American farm. The owner lived there with his wife. Their son had gone away to university and found work in another state.

Their house stood alone in a great open landscape. It was a beautiful home, much like the houses I had seen in cities. It had electricity, running water, a telephone, and all the ordinary comforts of modern life.

This is worth noticing. Even when you travel a long way before reaching the next house, that distant household generally has all the essential services. Companies will provide them, sometimes even when doing so cannot be especially profitable. In everyday living conditions, a farm owner need not feel worse off than anyone else.

And the word *farmer* means something different here from what it means in China. In America, the farmer usually owns both the farm and its land. A Chinese state farm might employ thousands of people. An American family farm may be run by just two or three.

The farmer I met was named Oberman. He told us he had 800 acres of land—about 4,857 Chinese *mu*, the unit commonly used to measure farmland at home. He cultivated it himself, with one regular hired worker and occasional help from his elderly father.

That was all.

Yet his output was astonishing. Besides farming those 800 acres, he raised pigs. The number almost made me gasp: **1,200 pigs at a time, with 2,400 produced over the course of a year.**

And that was not the end of his responsibilities. He sat on the board of an agricultural bank, attended to the bank's affairs when farm work was quiet, and took part in community projects and plans for local development.

How could one man do so much?

We asked him to show us his land and pig buildings so we could understand it for ourselves. He drove us around the 800 acres. Only then did we begin to grasp the scale of the place.

Next came the machinery. He owned three tractors, two of them very large, and a combine harvester as big as the machines used on the great farms of Heilongjiang in northeastern China. There was other equipment too. Almost every part of the work was done by machine.

His combine could cover as much as 80 acres in a day, he said, so managing 800 acres was not beyond him. He grew mainly corn and soybeans, and after the harvest he used highly automated equipment to dry the crops.

The geography helped enormously. Much American farmland consists of huge, unbroken plains. Big machines can travel across them without constantly meeting a hill, stream, or narrow field. In parts of China's Zhejiang province, by contrast, waterways and hills make this sort of farming much harder.

The pigs were raised with a similar reliance on technology. Oberman had four crowded pig buildings and a modern system for preparing their feed. The ingredients were mixed in carefully chosen proportions to make the animals grow quickly. After six months, the pigs were ready for market.

The pork produced under one man's care could feed hundreds of people.

But American farmers face serious problems too.

The first is money. Farm work demands enormous effort, yet the income is often modest. Oberman's farm earned between $20,000 and $30,000 a year, while the combine alone had cost $120,000, bought with a loan. That was before accounting for the other machinery. Some larger and more successful farms earn very good money, of course, but many do not.

This year, drought has struck much of the country. Farm incomes have fallen, in some cases to about half their usual level. The financial consequences are severe. We saw corn growing to only half its normal height, with thin, poorly developed ears. Compared with many other occupations, farming brings a relatively small reward. That makes the future of agriculture harder to secure.

The second problem is the work itself. A farmer works much harder physically than someone sitting in an office or an air-conditioned factory. Much of the day is spent outdoors, exposed to whatever the weather brings.

And raising pigs is filthy, smelly work. Automation cannot change that. Pigs have no particular interest in cleanliness. When we entered the pig buildings, the stench was so strong that I wanted to turn back. Oberman could not turn back. He told us he often needed several showers after work before the smell finally came off him.

I doubt many Americans would willingly take on that life.

The third problem is the younger generation. With farming so demanding, and city life offering far more entertainment and cultural opportunities, young people increasingly dream of leaving the countryside.

Oberman told me that after graduating from university, his son had said he did not want to return to the farm just yet. The news had hurt him deeply. He still believed his son would eventually come back. But if he did not, what would happen to the farm? Oberman had only one son and no one else to take over.

Despite these problems, the market still pushes enough people into agriculture to keep it operating. What matters for the country is that its farms produce so much food. That abundance helps hold society together and keeps political life stable.

Imagine what would happen if Americans suddenly found themselves without enough to eat.

One thing most wealthy countries have in common is that they have solved the basic problem of supplying everyday necessities. Food and other essential goods are widely available. Many poorer countries have not managed this.

A strong farming sector helps steady a society and gives its economy room to grow. The Soviet Union has struggled with agriculture for years, and those struggles have held back both its economy and its political development. Similar problems can be found elsewhere.

In a world with a rapidly growing population, **agriculture is a lifeline.**

# Chapter 2: An Old Political Spirit

## 1. The American Spirit

H. S. Commager is a well-known American historian and commentator. His books can be found on the shelves of most university libraries in the United States, and he is regarded as one of the great interpreters of American life. His *Growth of the American Republic* is highly respected among Chinese scholars. Another of his books, which I will call *The American Spirit*, appeared in 1950, was warmly received, and has been reprinted more than twenty times.

I brought a Chinese translation of it all the way from Shanghai. That was quite a journey for a book, but it was worth making. Anyone who wants to understand the American spirit should read it.

Commager writes with confidence and force. He notices things other people overlook and has a gift for drawing big conclusions from them. He says he was not trying to write a conventional history. He wanted to explain, rather than merely record, the country he was studying. Above all, he wanted to catch hold of something hard to pin down: the distinctive way Americans think, behave, and see the world.

He concentrates on the period from the mid-1880s to the 1940s, a time he regards as a turning point in American history and thought. It is a large book—about half a million Chinese characters in the edition I read—but its writing is clear and lively. The wealth of historical detail and the originality of its arguments are impressive.

What, then, does Commager mean by the American spirit?

He begins with the idea that Americans have developed a recognizable character and a way of thinking about life. Both are products of what they inherited and the conditions in which they have lived.

Their inheritance includes European traditions, some reaching back two thousand years to ancient Greece, Rome, and Palestine. American family life, religion, institutions, and values all carry traces of that older world. But Americans have never accepted everything they inherited. They have selected some traditions and left others behind, partly in response to their new surroundings.

For two centuries, their basic political and legal institutions have changed relatively little. Yet the way society is organized has been transformed, and people's attitudes have changed enormously. The American setting has also helped reduce some of the differences between people arriving from very different backgrounds.

Commager argues that American character comes from the country's overall environment, not simply from one region or another. He has in mind its vast territory, the freedom to move from place to place, its atmosphere of independence, and its optimism and drive.

In Europe, he argues, old class structures and powerful national and local traditions have often made particular communities more important than the whole. America developed differently. As it grew during the Industrial Revolution, old local loyalties had less power to hold the nation back. A wider American identity came to matter more.

This leads to a striking puzzle. Americans come from extraordinarily mixed backgrounds and live under very different climates and conditions. Yet they have developed a national character that seems both distinct and remarkably persistent. History alone does not easily explain it. Anyone trying to understand America must be prepared to think about that puzzle.

Commager offers a long description of the qualities he sees in Americans. Here are some of the main ones, in roughly his own order.

1. **An unusual optimism.** America seems full of opportunity, blessed with favourable natural conditions. Anyone with ambition and a little luck may become wealthy. Optimism grows out of things people see happening around them: wilderness turns into farmland, villages become cities, and ambitious plans become real.
