2. **A belief that almost anything is possible.** Americans are reluctant to give up before they have succeeded completely. They look towards the future more than the past, sometimes treating family history as a concern for people who have nothing to look forward to. They can imagine any barefoot boy growing up to be president or a millionaire. They invest hope in children, encourage them, and give them room to grow.

3. **A taste for big ideas.** Their imagination ranges across an entire continent. They dislike fussing over small matters, hesitation, and timidity. Grand projects and daring enterprises appeal to them.

4. **A focus on material comfort.** Americans expect a comfortable life and often regard it as their natural right. They may also look down on people whose living standards are lower than their own.

5. **A determination to overcome obstacles.** They believe that persistence, hard work, intelligence, and a little luck will eventually pay off. Work is a virtue; idleness is almost a sin. Anything that legitimately increases wealth seems worth doing.

6. **A love of numbers.** Americans measure almost everything: population, skyscraper heights, miles of railway, production records, and much else besides. To describe their enthusiasm for scale and quantity, Commager suggests, we might need a new vocabulary—or even a new kind of mathematics.

7. **A practical turn of mind.** Americans want politics, religion, culture, and science to be useful, though they can be surprisingly romantic about business. When a new problem appears, they look for a new tool or method. They are willing to experiment, and their enthusiasm for inventions—great and small—is legendary. American universities were among the first to teach technical subjects.

8. **A dislike of abstract theories.** Americans tend to avoid difficult arguments about the meaning of politics or human behaviour, rather as a healthy person avoids taking medicine. They have little patience for ideas that seem to float above common sense. Their closest thing to a philosophy is the belief that a useful idea is a good one. They sometimes suspect that only the unhappy or confused need complicated theories. They do not see themselves that way.

9. **A practical attitude towards religion.** Their religious roots include Calvinism, a branch of Protestant Christianity that emphasized discipline and work. But religion, for many, has become less about elaborate beliefs concerning salvation and more about the conviction that honest work matters. They belong to different denominations—different branches of the church—without necessarily caring much about their doctrinal differences. To many Americans, the divide between Methodists and Presbyterians is no clearer than the supposed difference in principle between Democrats and Republicans.

10. **A dislike of political grandstanding.** Americans have little patience for grand theories and politicians who talk without saying anything useful. If someone mocks them for lacking a political philosophy, they may take it as a compliment. Despite being a relatively young nation, they have developed considerable political skill. Their institutions can be as adaptable and efficient as the machines they invent.

11. **An uneasy relationship with high culture.** Americans can be suspicious of art and culture when they distract people from what seems more important, yet tolerant of them as leisure activities. Commager even describes them, in the language of his time, as pastimes for women. More generally, Americans want culture to have some use. They care about education and willingly spend money on it, but they expect it to produce people who can do practical things.

12. **A powerful sense of superiority.** They can dismiss other countries and peoples almost without noticing they are doing it. They believe their country is better, and sometimes that they themselves are better too. This can become a feeling that America has a special mission: to offer the world's greatest hope.

13. **A willingness to experiment.** America is itself an enormous experiment. Americans often see every new venture as a gamble and an opportunity. They are happy to move house, leave old places behind, and try something nobody has attempted before. Challenges attract them more than familiar routines.

14. **A strong belief in democracy and equality.** Ability and good fortune have room to flourish, but the equality Commager describes is mainly social rather than economic. Ideas of equality shape American manners, work, entertainment, language, literature, religion, and politics. Real differences in wealth remain, even while people speak and behave as though everyone stands on equal ground. This social equality can make everyday relationships more open and friendly.

15. **An easy, sociable manner.** Americans are often good-natured, generous, welcoming, and eager to make friends. They enjoy company and group activities. They are informal in speech, dress, eating, and personal dealings—so informal that others sometimes find them rude or lacking in restraint.

16. **A suspicion of authority.** Rules can feel like a personal insult or a challenge. Schools may appear undisciplined; parents exercise little control over children, who show little outward deference in return. Yet family life can be happy. Soldiers may seem relaxed about discipline and still fight effectively. Americans may not look respectful of individual laws, but they respect the idea that everyone, including those in power, must answer to the law. Above all stands the Constitution.

Commager's point is that such beliefs must have practical effects. They cannot help shaping the way Americans behave every day.

He gathers these observations in a chapter about nineteenth-century Americans. Some of what he describes still seems true today, although any account of a whole nation invites objections. For almost every example supporting one of his claims, someone could probably find an example pointing the other way.

Still, he is an American scholar writing about his own people, and his account deserves a hearing. There is also a certain amount of self-congratulation in it. Most of his qualities sound like virtues, and Americans certainly have their faults as well.

Even so, traces of this spirit are easy to spot: the space shuttle and the Strategic Defense Initiative, nicknamed *Star Wars*; the endless stream of new devices; the mountain of statistical reports; the insistence on practical results; the noisy American classroom; and the way people respond to scandals in government.

Perhaps a society has no single spirit until someone notices one. Or perhaps that spirit has always been there, waiting to be discovered.

The philosopher Hegel believed he had discovered what he called the “Absolute Spirit,” an ultimate force behind the unfolding of history. The curious thing about the American spirit is that it seems to have no such grand, all-embracing spirit at its centre.

## 2. The Mayflower

Massachusetts might be called America's state of political history. The historic sites here are young by European standards, but they are central to the country's political development.

Europeans often make fun of Americans for having no history. Some Chinese people say the same thing. But the joke points towards a serious question: **How did a society with only about two hundred years of modern history become one of the world's leading powers?**

Much of America's founding history is preserved in two places: Philadelphia, Pennsylvania, and the area around Boston, Massachusetts.

In Philadelphia, I visited several historic sites: places where important meetings were held during the struggle for independence, the building where the Constitution was drafted, and the Liberty Bell. Crowds of Americans and visitors from abroad were there too.

Everything was carefully preserved. These places were more than old buildings or monuments. They were history lessons you could walk into.

Americans are especially good at creating such living textbooks. People sometimes say that American society provides little political education. Yet its political traditions are deeply rooted in the public mind. Places like these help explain why. The libraries established in honour of former presidents serve much the same purpose.

Some of the earliest chapters of this living history are in Massachusetts.

In 1620, the *Mayflower*, a large wooden sailing ship, brought a group of early European settlers across the Atlantic. Americans often treat the voyage as the beginning of their country's modern story. Today visitors can see a replica of the ship, said to resemble the original closely.

Inside, everything has been arranged to suggest life aboard the vessel. Guides dressed in seventeenth-century clothing tell visitors about the hardships of the crossing, speaking in old-fashioned accents and expressions. They take their roles seriously and pay attention to the smallest details.

Not far from the ship stands a large rock, traditionally said to mark the place where the passengers first stepped ashore—the symbolic beginning of America.

Americans often trace their national spirit back to the *Mayflower*. Some even say that the principles governing American society were already established when the ship arrived, by the sixty-odd people aboard the ship.

There may be something in that view, but it is easy to take it too far.

One fact is important: many Europeans who crossed the Atlantic in that era had experienced religious persecution or political exclusion. In 1620, the Stuart monarchy in England was becoming increasingly authoritarian. About twenty years later, England would be plunged into revolution.

Think of the people prepared to leave Europe under such circumstances. They wanted to escape persecution and arbitrary rule, and to find freedom and a chance to prosper. That longing helped drive them towards the New World.

The land they entered had no single, fully established political system of the kind they had known in Europe. That gave them room to put their ideas into practice. Had they arrived somewhere with a complete and firmly rooted political order, a few dozen newcomers could hardly have remade it. They would have had to adjust to the society already there, just as immigrants to America today must largely live within its existing institutions.

The beliefs carried by the *Mayflower* settlers took root, in this account, because the settlers found political room in which to build something new.

But there is a crucial qualification. The freedom they sought was freedom for people like themselves—for white settlers. For a very long time, white Americans denied Black people and Indigenous Americans the full standing of fellow human beings.

One explanation for the political system that later developed in America is often overlooked. My former graduate-school adviser, Professor Chen Qiren, drew attention to a distinction between two kinds of colonies.

In one kind, settlers arrived from the ruling country, occupied land, and worked it themselves. The United States, New Zealand, and Australia belong to this category. In the other, a relatively small number of colonial rulers governed and exploited a much larger existing population. He gave examples from Africa, India, and Indochina.

These two forms of colonial rule, he argued, gave rise to very different political systems.

His explanation began with the question of whether conquerors could profit by forcing the existing population to work for them. In much of Asia, he argued, established systems of production made that possible for European colonizers. In North America, he believed, Indigenous communities lived under different economic conditions and could not be exploited in the same way for the same return.

European settlers therefore concentrated on taking Indigenous people's land. Had the societies they encountered been organized differently, he asked, would the course of American history have changed?

This reasoning also reveals the brutality of colonial attitudes. Indigenous Americans were long dismissed as “savages,” and colonizers sometimes treated people they regarded as less “civilized” as though they were not human at all. The very language of civilization could become a way of denying other people their humanity.

The *Mayflower* spirit survived not simply because somebody made a deliberate effort to protect it. It survived because the settlers' daily lives came to depend upon the rules and beliefs they had brought with them.

I also visited Plimouth Plantation, a recreation of a seventeenth-century settlement near the sea. From the village, you can look out over a vast sweep of water. The wooden houses and everyday tools are those of a poor farming community from centuries ago.

The place is best described in two words: poor and undeveloped.

That is hardly surprising. People who had crossed an ocean and arrived with almost nothing had to start from scratch. Villages with similarly harsh conditions can still be found in many parts of the world. But what did those conditions mean for the settlers who lived here?

Precisely because they had built a life out of so little, they valued their property and independence deeply. People born into wealth may never feel that attachment so strongly.

When Britain tried to draw more wealth from its colonies, the settlers rose to defend what they had made. In 1775, fighting broke out between colonists and British troops. I visited a site associated with the first shot of the War of Independence. A monument stands there; another, across the river beside the road, marks where a British soldier fell.

The Americans eventually won.

Their struggle had been shaped by political ideas circulating in Europe, including those associated with the revolutions that challenged inherited privilege and monarchy. Britain's own earlier revolution mattered too. Many of the main principles later written into the American Constitution came from English and French thinkers.

The American founders also faced a particular advantage. They did not have to dismantle a powerful old political order within their own society on the scale confronted by revolutionaries in Britain or France. They could adopt institutions that protected the interests of the growing business-owning class with relatively little resistance from an entrenched aristocracy.

Think, by contrast, of Germany in the middle of the nineteenth century, where the landowning Junker aristocracy remained strong enough to obstruct a comparable political revolution.

Americans devote enormous care to protecting the physical reminders of their past. Historic sites have staff to maintain them and facilities to make visiting easy: parking, shops, restaurants, explanations, and often a room showing films or slides made especially for the site.

Objects that might not even count as historic treasures in China are preserved with great care here.

In time, Americans will have a long history to look back on, precisely because they are taking such pains to preserve the history they have now. They know their national past is relatively short, and they treasure almost anything with historical significance. In countries with far richer histories, things of much greater value are sometimes left to decay.

But preserving these places is only half the achievement. Americans also know how to turn them into lessons that anyone can understand. They open them to the public and make sure visitors have the help they need to appreciate what they are seeing.

This is how the American spirit is passed on. It is a kind of political education woven into everyday society, rather than delivered only through formal instruction.

Any society that hopes to remain stable over time must find ways to pass on its central values. Different societies will choose different methods. **Much of the result depends on how they do it.**

## 3. The Beliefs That Hold America Together

One reason Europeans sometimes look down on America is that they see it as a country without a long cultural history: a nation of newly rich upstarts who have little civilization of their own.

That attitude also appears in discussions of politics. Europe produced thinkers such as Aristotle, Plato, Hobbes, Montesquieu, Locke, Rousseau, Mill, and Hegel. Their writings helped people understand political life, and many ideas that now guide Western politics first took shape in their work.

America has had only two hundred years to develop its own political thought. Its founders drew heavily on ideas already developed in Europe and contributed relatively little that was entirely new. American scholars themselves readily acknowledge this.

But it would be a serious mistake to conclude that Americans have no political beliefs—or that such beliefs play only a small part in American life.

The Harvard political scholar Samuel P. Huntington explored this question in his 1981 book *American Politics: The Promise of Disharmony*. He argued that anyone who thinks political ideas matter less in America than in Europe has misunderstood the country.

According to Huntington, Americans began forming a shared set of political beliefs during the struggle for independence. By the late eighteenth and early nineteenth centuries, these beliefs had become something we might call the *American Creed*: a collection of ideas about how political life should work.

The ideas themselves came largely from European thinkers who challenged monarchy and inherited privilege, and some had roots as far back as ancient Greece. But in America they became widely accepted and, over time, part of the way people understood themselves as Americans.

Their meaning has shifted over two centuries, yet their central place in American life has remained surprisingly steady.

Nobody needs to spend every waking hour preaching these beliefs. They are taught and reinforced through universities, courts, government, and the media. They also shape the things people do without thinking about them. To a considerable extent, they guide the country.

Look at the presidential race between Michael Dukakis and George Bush. Both campaigns argued about who represented America's political *mainstream*—the beliefs and expectations shared by most voters—and who had moved away from it. Neither candidate wanted to be seen as an outsider to that mainstream. That could cost votes.

What exactly are the beliefs in the American Creed?

Huntington names five: **freedom, equality, individualism, democracy, and the rule of law**. Individualism means giving people room to choose their own lives; the rule of law means that government and citizens alike are bound by laws rather than by the personal wishes of those in power.

The list looks simple. But getting an entire society to accept even one idea is extraordinarily difficult. It takes the work of several generations. And, of course, what Americans believe is not always what they do.

Where did these values come from?

You can point to the Declaration of Independence, which used them as arguments against British colonial rule. You can also point to European political thought, where the same ideas had been discussed long before the American Revolution.

For the early Americans, the attraction of these beliefs was often emotional as much as intellectual. They felt them deeply. But they did not necessarily have a worked-out theory explaining how the beliefs fitted together.

In fact, the ideals can pull against one another.

As Huntington points out, government by the majority can threaten the rights of a minority. Putting law above everything may weaken the idea that political power belongs to the people. Strong individualism can work against the cooperation that democracy requires.

These are not just problems that appear in books. The tensions have repeatedly emerged in American political life. They help explain some of its most stubborn difficulties.

Americans take pride in their democracy. But beyond voting in elections, how much can an ordinary citizen really do to shape national politics? Perhaps every organized society runs into some version of this problem.

Most Americans nevertheless share these broad beliefs. One explanation is that the colonies did not develop the same deeply entrenched ranks and inherited social divisions found in Europe. There was no equally elaborate set of competing ideas tied to an ancient social order. People could adopt a common set of values as part of building a new country.

As American society changed, different groups and classes began interpreting those values in different ways. The struggle between the two major political parties is one clear example.

It would be an exaggeration to say everybody accepts the American Creed today. Some immigrant communities—including people with Chinese, Latin American, and Japanese backgrounds—have inherited different cultural traditions and may not identify fully with the country's dominant beliefs. Still, a mainstream exists.

One curious advantage of these shared words is that they are so open to interpretation. People can use *freedom* and *equality* to defend quite different positions. Their very lack of a single fixed meaning may help them survive.

About 140 years ago, the French writer Alexis de Tocqueville observed that Americans generally believed power came from the people, that freedom and equality mattered, and that ordinary citizens should have political rights.

James Bryce later described a similar set of convictions: individuals possess fundamental rights; authority comes from the people; governments must obey both law and the public; local government should take priority over distant national government; the judgment of the many deserves more weight than that of the few; and the best government is one that interferes as little as possible.

All these principles can be understood in more than one way. Most of the time, Americans do not feel much need to explain them. Everybody assumes they already know what the words mean. But when a crisis comes, very different interpretations can emerge.

Huntington points to four periods when this happened: the revolutionary era, the age of Thomas Jefferson, the Civil War, and the upheavals of the 1960s.

Each saw widespread dissatisfaction, fierce arguments about political ideals, unrest, growing hostility towards those in power, pressure for reform, and more people becoming involved in politics.

Behind these disturbances lay a deeper problem: everyday political life had fallen short of the country's professed beliefs. Tensions built up until they erupted, like pressure escaping from a volcano, and institutions had to change.

But because most people still believed in the same basic ideals, their first instinct was not to abandon those ideals. It was to demand that the country live up to them.

That gives American development an important thread of continuity. The country keeps trying to realize roughly the same principles, rather than replacing them with a new set every time a crisis occurs. Such continuity is a powerful advantage.

Many political scholars speak of a gap between *ideas and institutions*: the distance between what a country says it believes and how its political system actually works.

America has that gap too. Its daily political life still falls far short of the ideals of freedom, equality, individualism, democracy, and the rule of law. These are unfinished projects, even if America has carried them further than some other societies.

The important question is not when the country will finally achieve them perfectly. It is when society can create the conditions that bring them closer to everyday reality.

Some people may dislike the dominant beliefs, but the political mainstream makes it difficult to oppose them openly. In ordinary times, most people make their peace with the way things are. When circumstances demand it, they appeal to the ideals again.

There is a lesson here for any society. Winning agreement on a set of values is hard enough. Living by them is harder still.

A society must first make its central beliefs widely accepted, then work to turn them into reality. Without that shared starting point, the second task becomes even more difficult.

**What a country says it believes tells us something important. It does not, by itself, tell us what that country has actually achieved.**

## 4. Freedom or Equality?

Every society has a few beliefs that matter more than the rest. They shape the direction the country takes, influence the way people think, and give people standards for judging both public affairs and their private lives.

So what is the most important belief in American society?

Americans disagree sharply about the answer. In recent years, it has become a subject of heated debate.

Those who call themselves liberals often put **equality** first. After all, the country's founding principles declare that people are created equal. The new conservatives, on the other hand, tend to place **freedom** above equality. To them, freedom has always been the guiding American principle.

During a visit to a university in Ohio, I asked an experienced professor of American politics what really separated the Republican and Democratic parties.

He thought for a moment before answering. “Their views of freedom and equality. Republicans put more weight on freedom; Democrats put more weight on equality.”

So even these two basic ideas, both central to Western political history, do not mean quite the same thing to everyone.

My own impression is that most Americans today put freedom ahead of equality. Freedom has become the stronger force in American thinking and culture.

That does not mean people agree on what freedom means. Quite the opposite. Different groups interpret it in whatever way best serves their own interests. *Freedom* is a particularly flexible word. It can be used to defend many different positions. *Equality* is somewhat harder to stretch in that way.

To understand why, we need to look at how Americans came to think about these ideas.

Nearly 150 years ago, Alexis de Tocqueville travelled through the United States and wrote *Democracy in America*. It became a classic and is still essential reading for anyone studying American politics. That is quite an achievement in a country where people are not especially inclined to treat political theorists as unquestionable authorities.

One of Tocqueville's most important claims was that the defining feature of American life was an unusual **equality of conditions**. By this he meant that people did not begin life trapped in the same rigid ranks and inherited social positions that had long existed in Europe. He believed this relative equality shaped almost every other American value.

There is something right about that argument. But there is something wrong with it too.

After independence, America formally adopted the principle that people were born equal. It did not inherit the same entrenched feudal aristocracy found in European countries. There were fewer ancient fortunes and fewer families whose political standing had been built up over centuries. That made it easier to establish a society in which people could think of themselves as equals.

Britain and France faced a much harder task. Their class divisions had deep historical roots. A new law could declare everyone equal, but it could not simply wipe away centuries of differences in wealth, status, habits, and culture. Even if it removed material privileges, it might leave old assumptions about superiority and inferiority untouched.

America had an advantage in this respect. Coming from a Europe shaped by aristocratic traditions, Tocqueville naturally found American society more equal. If he had travelled in the opposite direction, from America to post-revolutionary France, he might have reached a different conclusion.

But his account also missed something essential. Much of the equality he admired existed on paper, not in people's lives.

Even today, can anyone seriously say America has achieved equality for all its citizens? American politicians and scholars themselves regularly attack the country's inequalities.

When Tocqueville visited, slavery remained widespread in the South. The Civil War had not yet happened. Women did not enjoy equal social standing or political rights. Indigenous Americans were even further outside the circle of people treated as equals. And economic inequality existed then just as it does now.

Even if we talk only about *equal opportunities*, rather than *equal outcomes*, it is difficult to say that Americans of Tocqueville's day enjoyed truly equal conditions. I suspect his judgment reflected, above all, the experience of someone who had grown up in a far more unequal society.

That does not mean there was no difference between America and Europe. The movement towards equality was real progress. It also matters that people were striving for equality: they demanded it precisely because they had not yet achieved it.

There is another complication. Anyone who wants genuine freedom must first accept some degree of equality. Without a basic equality of rights and opportunities, freedom cannot be fully enjoyed.

Since the days of the English thinker John Locke, freedom and equality have been two of the central ideals of Western political life. But they do not always fit comfortably together.

When a society has little of either, the conflict between them may be hard to see. Both seem worth pursuing. But as each ideal develops, the tensions become clearer.

If we insist on giving every person the widest possible freedom, efforts to create equality may begin to look like restrictions. Equal conditions require limits on the advantages some people can enjoy over others. The conflict becomes sharper if we want equal *outcomes*—not just a fair chance to succeed, but a more equal share of the results.

On the other hand, if equality is our first concern, then everybody must enjoy the same freedom. Nobody can be allowed more liberty than anyone else. As the thinker Auguste Comte suggested, that is a demanding standard, and it is difficult to realize in practice.

People also disagree about what the words themselves mean. That adds still more room for conflict. The further a society advances towards both freedom and equality, the more likely it becomes that new tensions will appear between them.

Americans in Tocqueville's time certainly spoke about equality. But it was often a distant principle, and progress towards it was painfully slow.

Women began to gain voting rights in the latter half of the nineteenth century, but nationwide voting rights did not come until the 1920s. Black Americans' struggle for equal rights became a central national issue in the 1960s, while Indigenous people's rights continued to be violated.

Even Abraham Lincoln, who fought against slavery, did not necessarily imagine freedom for Black Americans as full equality with white Americans. He said he had never promised that Black people would hold government office or marry white people, and spoke of the tensions that made it difficult for the two groups to live together.

This is why some of today's new conservatives argue that Lincoln gave Black Americans freedom without giving them equality.

At heart, I think, most Americans prefer freedom. They demand equality most strongly when they feel their freedom is being restricted. Once the restriction is lifted, freedom usually becomes more attractive than further equality.

Why?

Because the equality guaranteed by Western political systems is mainly **political equality**: the right to vote, equal treatment under the law, and the same formal political rights. It is not the same as equality in wealth, social position, or everyday opportunity.

Western scholars have long recognized the difference. Granting people the same legal and political rights is one thing. Asking them to share economic advantages more equally is another. The second can threaten interests that people are determined to protect.

After political equality is established, people pursue their own work, business, and cultural lives. Over two centuries, America has grown enormously richer, but the benefits have been distributed very unevenly. The gap between rich and poor has become one of the basic divisions in society.

In a prosperous country, only a minority are extremely rich or extremely poor. Many people in the middle believe they are already comfortable—or that they might become wealthy one day.

They tend to think their prosperity depends on having freedom in economic life. If the government limits that freedom in the name of equality, they fear it will hurt them.

They argue that business and private property should not be treated like political rights. The government should not take one person's money merely to give it to another. People who own wealth, or hope to own it, are especially likely to agree.

This argument reflects the interests of those with property. They do not want restrictions placed on what they can do with their capital, and they do not want to share the deeper advantages that come with wealth.

America has changed greatly since Tocqueville's visit. After the Second World War, the Black civil rights movement and the women's movement made major gains towards political equality, though the work remains unfinished. But economic equality has advanced much less.

The government has tried anti-poverty programmes, welfare measures, and *progressive taxation*—a tax system in which people with higher incomes pay a larger share. These measures were meant to make society more equal. Yet they have also provoked widespread resentment.

Policies influenced by the economist John Maynard Keynes, who argued that governments should step in to support jobs and economic activity, came to be blamed for *stagflation*: the painful combination of rising prices and a stagnant economy. As these difficulties grew, Keynesian approaches lost much of their popularity. Large welfare programmes became unpopular, and repeated Democratic election defeats seemed to reflect that shift.

Welfare programmes need public money. Paying for them usually means higher taxes, and nobody enjoys seeing more of their income taken away.

Americans may say they want equality. But when equality becomes closely tied to higher taxes, many pull back. If somebody continues to demand a more equal distribution of wealth, others answer that such measures would violate their freedom.

There are deep economic and social reasons, then, for freedom's position at the centre of American life. The argument is also a struggle between groups with different interests.

Looking at American history, one could say that people have sometimes fought for freedom by demanding equality. Once they have won the rights they wanted, however, it is freedom they are most eager to preserve.

That does not mean nobody values equality. Many do. But for a great number of Americans, the equality they want stops at equal political rights. They find it much harder to accept demands that go further.

The War of Independence carried the principle of equality to a new stage, and later struggles gradually widened political rights. But progress towards economic and social equality has been slow because those areas are treated as matters of personal freedom—especially the freedom to own and use private property.

Americans, in this view, are willing to accept **equality of opportunity**, but not **equality of results**. Once they believe everyone has been given a fair chance, they see what happens afterwards as a matter for individual freedom. If people ask for still more equality, they regard it as an attempt to make everybody end up in the same position.

That helps explain why freedom, rather than equality, has become the country's leading value.

**In an age when individualism is so powerful, equality has little chance of taking first place.**

## 5. Two Hundred Years of the Constitution

Philadelphia and Washington are linked by American history. Philadelphia was a centre of the struggle for independence, and it was there, in 1787, that delegates met to write the United States Constitution. The document has remained in force ever since, making it one of the oldest written constitutions in the world.

Washington, as the national capital, is where much of the system created by that document operates today.

Americans are immensely proud of their Constitution. In 1988, as the country celebrated two hundred years of the Constitution, the government held a grand ceremony by the Statue of Liberty, and President Reagan gave a speech.

In Philadelphia, I went to see the building where the Constitution was written, but the crowds were so large that I could only look at it from outside. Later, in Annapolis, I saw a handwritten copy of the document.

A few sheets of yellowing paper, and they have had such extraordinary power over a country! Clearly, the real foundations of that power lie far beyond the paper itself.

The Constitution grew out of the War of Independence and the political struggles surrounding it. The main changes took place over roughly twenty years, from 1770 to 1790.

At first there were thirteen separate colonies under British rule. In 1774, representatives from the colonies met in Philadelphia at the First Continental Congress to discuss their shared concerns. Fighting with Britain broke out the following year, in what is now Massachusetts.

In 1776, Thomas Paine published *Common Sense*, making the case for independence. By 1782 the war was drawing to a close, and negotiations in Paris led to an agreement. The Constitution was drafted in 1787. Enough states approved it for it to take effect in 1788. George Washington became the country's first president in 1789, and in 1791 the Bill of Rights was added to the Constitution.

These were the main steps in the country's early political development. They show that the Constitution was born from a struggle for independence—and that its rules were also intended to protect particular interests.

That was very much on the minds of the men who wrote it.

The thirteen states sent fifty-five delegates to Philadelphia. Writing a constitution for a whole country was a huge task, yet they finished the draft in a single summer. Perhaps that was possible partly because political and social life was still less complicated than it would later become. Attempting the same job today might take two years or more.

Among those involved were George Washington, Alexander Hamilton, James Madison, and Benjamin Franklin. They had read the great European thinkers of the Enlightenment, a movement that placed great faith in reason and challenged old forms of authority. They knew works such as John Locke's *Two Treatises of Government*, James Harrington's *The Commonwealth of Oceana*, and Montesquieu's *The Spirit of the Laws*.

But these men were not just readers of political theory. Many had already helped run governments. According to one account, twenty had worked on state constitutions, while thirty had served in state legislatures. They understood the practical difficulties of governing and the ways public office could be misused.

Another feature of their situation deserves attention. They were not trying to invent an entirely new set of political principles. In many ways, they were carrying forward principles already familiar from Britain, while changing the institutions that put those principles into practice.

Britain had undergone its Glorious Revolution about a century earlier. By the time of the American Revolution, its political order was no longer simply the old system of feudal rule. American colonists could therefore borrow from the political ideas of the very country they were breaking away from.

It was almost as though the people who had been ruled wanted to govern themselves in the way their former rulers did.

Other revolutions faced a harder problem. Revolutionaries in Britain, France, Russia, and China had to challenge the basic principles of their existing political systems. They wanted to create forms of government whose practical workings had yet to be tested. Powerful surviving interests from the old order also made building the new one more difficult.

Because of their own experience, the American founders were especially concerned with two things: **the authority of government and the freedom of the individual**.

The way Britain had governed the thirteen colonies separately also shaped the result. No single colony was in a position to command the others. The states had to bargain and compromise. Each delegate had a state behind him and reasons to protect its interests. None wanted another state to gain enough power to dominate it.

That encouraged a constitution designed to prevent any single part of the union from becoming too strong.

In their book introducing American government, Kenneth Prewitt and Sidney Verba argue that the Constitution rests on a particular view of human nature. They quote John Adams:

> Human desires, passions, prejudices, and self-interest will never be conquered by kindness and knowledge. You say, “The love of liberty is rooted in the human soul.” Well, it is rooted in the soul of a wolf too. I doubt one person is necessarily more reasonable, more tolerant, or easier to live with than another. We cannot rely on people's love of liberty alone. We must have political institutions that help that love defend itself against its enemies.

Prewitt and Verba draw a straightforward conclusion: **people cannot be trusted with unchecked political power**.

The Constitution therefore reflects a cautious, even pessimistic, view of human nature. It assumes that power must be limited because people are fallible. This differs from some traditions of Eastern thought and may help explain certain differences between Western and Eastern political development.

From this starting point, the Constitution established three broad principles:

1. **Government by elected representatives.** The country rejected inherited titles of nobility, opened public office more widely, and established regular elections so that representatives could act on the public's behalf.

2. **Power divided between different bodies.** Some powers belong to the national government and others to the states. This is the federal system, and at the time it was probably the only workable compromise. Power is also divided within the national government among the lawmaking, executive, and judicial branches—Congress, the president, and the courts—so that no one part controls everything.

3. **Limits on what government may do.** The government cannot simply interfere with people's religion, speech, writing, or right to gather together. It must follow laws rather than the wishes of particular rulers: a government of laws, not of men. The courts exist in part to uphold that principle.

We should not imagine that the founders wrote all this out of concern for every ordinary working person. Their first priority was to safeguard the interests of their own newly powerful group. Only much later did the Constitution's promises begin to reach the wider population.

Black Americans were still fighting, sometimes at the cost of their lives, to secure basic rights in the 1960s. They continue that struggle today, though it no longer so often involves bloodshed.

One of the most remarkable questions in modern politics is why this Constitution has lasted for two hundred years, when written constitutions elsewhere have so often been replaced.

Prewitt and Verba suggest two reasons. First, it addresses political conflicts that have never disappeared. The arguments of the 1780s still exist, though they take new forms—for example, the struggle over the balance of power between the national government and the states. Second, government by elected representatives, divided powers, and limits on authority were attractive principles in the founding era and remain attractive today.

There is another important reason: the Constitution has been able to change.

Had it remained exactly the same in meaning for two centuries, it is hard to imagine it surviving. The remarkable thing is that its substance has changed while its outward form has endured.

Its language is often broad enough to allow new interpretations. The Supreme Court can apply old provisions to new circumstances. Rights that were not originally spelled out can come to be recognized. Formal amendments can also change the text.

The essential point is that these changes take place through the Constitution's own rules and procedures. People revise their understanding of the document without discarding the document itself.

Nothing in the world stays unchanged. Americans interpret their Constitution very differently from the way its authors did two hundred years ago. Yet they prefer to say that they are still following the same ancient document.

There is a practical reason for this. It can be difficult to persuade people to accept what someone alive today has decided. It is sometimes much easier to win agreement by appealing to words handed down from the past.

Ultimately, no force in American political life seems strong enough to sweep the Constitution aside. Maintaining it and interpreting it have become the only realistic choices.

Perhaps there is a general lesson here. **If some group can still overturn a country's constitution and basic political order whenever it becomes powerful enough, that country has not yet achieved a stable system of government.**

## 6. The Roots of American Politics

Not far from Boston is a quiet little town called Belmont. It is said to have preserved something of America's earliest political system. Local people still have a strong tradition of governing themselves, and in that tradition we may find some of the roots of American politics.

I think of these roots as political genes: the small, basic habits from which a country's political life grows. Professor Lucian Pye, who was president of the American Political Science Association in 1988, particularly recommended Belmont. He accompanied us on our visit.

Belmont is about half an hour's drive from Boston and has a population of 26,000. The first person to welcome us was the town clerk, herself an elected official.

She began by telling us that Belmont was run by friends and neighbours looking after their own affairs. That was what local self-government meant here. Then she explained that both the system and the spirit behind it had come from Britain. The earliest settlers brought these traditions across the Atlantic, and people had kept them alive ever since. Many towns in the eastern United States, especially in Massachusetts, still have something of this character.

Belmont's government is worth looking at more closely.

The town has its own basic rules, known as the *General By-laws*. They serve much the same purpose as a constitution. Under these rules, ultimate authority belongs to the townspeople. Every adult citizen who lives there has the right to vote.

There are two kinds of officials: those chosen by the voters and those appointed to carry out particular jobs.

The town's chief executives are called *selectmen*. There are three of them, each with equal authority. Together, they handle everyday town business. One seat comes up for election each year, and a selectman can be elected again.

Other important posts are filled by election too. Voters choose people to serve on the electricity, health, school and water boards. They also elect the town treasurer and the moderator, who presides over meetings. These officials do not owe their positions to the three selectmen. Each is answerable to the voters.

That is the point. No single town leader can simply take charge of everything.

Belmont also has an elected town meeting, which serves as a body for discussing public business. But the source of authority, in the town's own understanding, remains the citizens as a whole.

Beneath the elected leadership is a network of appointed officials: an executive secretary, advisory bodies and committees that help with the town's daily work. Other staff look after community development, services for older people, finance, firefighting, roads, policing and help for veterans. The moderator works with appointed committees concerned with legal matters, spending authority and budgets.

The details may sound complicated, but the principle is straightforward. The people who lead the most important parts of the town government are chosen by the public. That gives residents a direct way to watch over them and call them to account.

Belmont may be small, but it handles a great deal of money. In the 1986–87 financial year, its spending came to $31,709,621. How to spend such a sum is the town's biggest political question.

Its arrangements are designed to make sure that public money is used properly. Financial decisions must be made openly and approved at the town meeting, which normally gathers once a year to settle the most important local business.

The meeting in 1987 took place on April 27 in the high school auditorium, the largest meeting space in town. Residents received the proposed decisions in advance. The relevant committee had already studied each item and prepared its advice.

There were forty-seven matters to vote on. Most concerned the practical business of living together: how money should be spent, which public facilities needed expanding and how local services could be improved.

A few involved the town's political rules. Item 2 dealt with giving the selectmen authority to settle disputes; items 35 and 37 concerned changes to the by-laws. But most were much more ordinary. Should officials receive higher pay? Should the water pipes be extended? Should travel costs be reimbursed? Should the town buy parking meters, a large snowplough, heavy trucks or photocopiers? Should a road be repaired?

Even decisions like these required public approval. Officials could not simply spend the money because they thought it necessary.

The financial reports presented to the meeting set out the salaries and allowances of officials and committee members in detail. Everything was open to inspection. Under such conditions, it becomes very difficult to use public office for private gain. That does not make corruption impossible, but it gives corruption very little room to hide.

There is something here of the citizens' assemblies of ancient Rome. We can see an old feature of Western political life surviving in an ordinary American town.

Of course, Belmont is not an independent country. Its own rules must fit within the laws of Massachusetts and the United States. But one of the striking things about American government is that towns and other small local authorities do not all work in the same way.

Many have kept their older customs. Americans have not tried to make every local government an exact copy of every other. Instead, they have allowed different arrangements to survive and develop. That is unusual in the world.

The country has a common political framework, but the actual work of government can look very different from place to place. Each community has made room for its own history, habits and needs.

I believe that this flexibility matters enormously. A political system works best when it fits the people who live under it. In America, we can see this fit most clearly not in Washington or even in the state governments, but in counties, towns and smaller communities.

Many countries have no comparable arrangement. Experience suggests that the more completely a country forces its local governments into one standard pattern, the less able those governments are to respond to local conditions.

Every country faces the same difficult question: how can it hold together as one political community while still allowing different places to govern themselves in ways that suit them?

This brings us back to Belmont's political genes.

The early settlers who crossed from Britain had often suffered religious or political persecution. They wanted protection for their rights and were deeply suspicious of unchecked power. They also carried with them ideas drawn from older Western traditions of self-government, including those of ancient Greece and the towns of medieval Europe.

They travelled so far because they wanted to escape one way of political life and build another. That helps explain why ideas of self-government took root so early among them.

We must remember, however, who counted as a member of that political community. As in the slaveholding societies of the ancient world, these freedoms were initially shared among the settlers themselves. They were not extended to Indigenous peoples, or later to Black Americans.

Yet there is a larger lesson here. Anyone who wants political rights for himself must eventually be willing to share them with others. Otherwise, he may find that his own rights cannot be protected either. The histories of many countries, including modern China, show us why.

When the American Revolution came, the colonists sought to protect and strengthen habits they had already developed. The nature of the earliest settlers helped those habits turn into lasting political institutions.

Imagine, instead, that the first people to arrive had been British princes and aristocrats determined to preserve their privileges. What kind of America might have emerged then?

The different experiences of countries in Latin America and Africa offer plenty of examples worth thinking about.

## 7. The Rules That Keep Politics Running

January 20, 1989, was inauguration day in Washington. George Bush, elected as America's forty-first president, was to take the oath of office at the Capitol. His vice president, Dan Quayle, would be sworn in as well.

The leading figures of American politics were all expected to attend: officials from the White House, both houses of Congress and the Supreme Court.

The ceremony marked two things at once. Bush would become president, and Ronald Reagan would stop being president. It was a major national occasion, but it was also something more ordinary and more important: a rule of political life, repeated so often that everyone knew what was expected.

The ritual places limits on both the incoming and outgoing presidents. It also shows the public how their system is meant to work. The new president gives a speech setting out his plans, while the old president leaves office.

**Passing power from one leader to another is one of the hardest problems in politics.** Many countries have never found a reliable way to do it, and the result is repeated instability.

The most important fact about an inauguration is not that a new president gains power. It is that the previous president loses it. The man who once held the highest office returns to private life.

The Constitution does not spell out every detail of how one president should hand over power to the next. But Americans have carried out the ceremony for so long that it has become an accepted rule, with a familiar order and carefully worked-out customs.

On the morning of January 20, the open-air stage in front of the Capitol was ready. A raised platform stood before the building. Behind it, a doorway was hung with deep-red curtains, and a red carpet led forward to the lectern and microphone.

The important guests arrived one after another. Reagan and other officials travelled from the White House to the Capitol. Each person or group entered the platform in a fixed order, escorted by attendants and separated by a minute or two.

First came the Supreme Court justices. Then the wives of Reagan, Bush and Quayle entered by another passage. Reagan took his seat, followed by Quayle. Bush arrived last.

A presiding officer representing the two houses of Congress led the proceedings. A minister opened with prayers for America and for the new president, vice president and their families. A children's choir sang a hymn, followed by a Black singer performing a song of praise.

Quayle took the oath first. Supreme Court Justice Sandra Day O'Connor read each line, and he repeated it. His wife held their family Bible while he rested his left hand upon it.

Then came Bush. Chief Justice William H. Rehnquist administered his oath. Barbara Bush held two Bibles: one belonging to the family and another said to have been used by George Washington, the first president, at his inauguration. Bush placed his hand on them and repeated the words of the oath.

The same singer then led the national anthem, and the crowd joined in. Afterward, Bush delivered his inaugural address.

He praised Reagan, saying that the former president had earned an important place in America's heart and history, and thanked him on behalf of the nation.

He prayed for the strength to do God's work and spoke of a simple duty: power should be used to help people, not to serve the private ambitions of those who hold it.

He called for a renewal of the American spirit. Americans, he said, should want to leave their children something more than larger cars and bigger bank accounts. They should pass on an understanding of what it means to be a faithful friend, a loving parent and a good citizen. A country without high moral aims would not truly be America. The task was to make the nation kinder and the world better.

He called for an end to crimes connected with drug abuse. He asked Congress to work with him, saying the country needed action, compromise and harmony, and offering his hand in cooperation.

And he expressed confidence in the future. America's problems were great, he said, but its determination was greater; its challenges were great, but its will was greater still. Human failings might seem endless, but so was God's love.

When the speech was over, Bush said goodbye to Ronald and Nancy Reagan. The Reagans were leaving Washington and the White House for good. A helicopter would take them to Andrews Air Force Base, where a plane would carry them home to Los Angeles.

Bush, Quayle and their wives accompanied them to the helicopter. With its departure, the Reagan years were over.

Bush then returned to an office inside the Capitol to sign his first documents as president, including appointments to his cabinet. After a congressional luncheon, he made his way back to the White House, a journey of about 1.6 miles that is sometimes made on foot. A new presidency had begun.

Everything about the transfer was carefully arranged. It was also astonishingly expensive.

Estimates put the cost of the full week of inauguration events at around $30 million, counting both public and private spending. A complete set of tickets to the various ceremonies and banquets cost $25,000. The official celebrations alone were expected to cost about $20 million. Reagan's inauguration in 1981 had cost $16 million, paid for by private contributions.

The public paid a substantial amount too. Congress budgeted $775,000 for the ceremony. The Washington city government set aside $2.3 million for police, firefighters and medical services. The Defense Department spent about $2.5 million on troops, ceremonial guards and related arrangements.

This kind of spending is itself a feature of American political life. Much of the money came from private donors. These gifts were not supposed to carry political obligations, but whether they were truly free of such expectations is another question.

Even so, when we compare the expense with the cost of a transfer of power marked by disorder—or bloodshed—it does not seem so high.

The whole event took place in public view. Newspapers and television covered it closely, and television and radio carried the ceremony live. Public attention helped make the handover orderly. Everyone could see that power was changing hands according to an accepted procedure.

Any political system that hopes to last must solve the problem of succession. America has spent generations building customs that everyone in political life is expected to obey. A leader who breaks them risks losing public acceptance and the right to govern.

It would be almost impossible to imagine an outgoing president simply refusing to leave the White House.

On the day Bush was sworn in, Reagan moved out. The Oval Office stood empty. *The New York Times* published a photograph of Reagan taking one last look at the bare room. Soon Bush would be working there instead.

Reagan's staff had to leave as well. While he travelled to the ceremony, moving trucks began arriving at the White House. After the oath, he flew directly to Los Angeles. He was once again a private citizen.

An ex-president still has much more influence than an ordinary person, of course. But his time in power is over.

This is the force of political custom.

The ceremony draws on words and objects that Americans associate with their founding. The oath goes back two hundred years. So does the Bible attributed here to Washington's inauguration. The whole occasion is steeped in tradition.

That may seem strange in a country so devoted to individual freedom and new ideas. Why should Americans, of all people, care so deeply about old customs?

Perhaps the answer is that **tradition becomes especially important where no single person has the authority to decide everything.** People need something they can all accept. Rules inherited from the past can command agreement where a rule invented by a living politician might not.

At every level of American government, from the nation to the states and towns, customs matter. They are especially powerful when the question is who gets to rule next. Countries that have experienced great political upheaval without developing settled habits of succession often struggle with that question for years.

Looking deeper, these customs also help maintain a balance of power among rival groups within the ruling class. They give competing interests a way to manage their relationships without destroying the system they share.

Such habits grow out of a society's political, economic and cultural life. They cannot simply be created overnight. They reflect how the whole society has learned to work.

Sometimes **customs are stronger than laws**. Laws are written on paper; customs live in what people believe and expect.

One sign of political maturity is that a society has turned its political principles into habits so deeply rooted that people follow them almost without thinking.

## 8. A Third American Republic?

Most people have heard of France's Fourth and Fifth Republics. But a *Third American Republic*? That sounds unfamiliar.

The idea was put forward as early as 1969 by the political scholar Theodore J. Lowi in his book *The End of Liberalism: The Second Republic of the United States*. Lowi traced changes in the ideas that shaped American government and asked where those changes might lead. His argument is a useful way to understand America.

During the fierce presidential campaign of 1988, the Republicans found an effective weapon against their Democratic opponent: they called him a *liberal*.

The remarkable thing was that the label had become an insult.

Political scholar Seymour Martin Lipset discussed this change in *The New York Times* on October 27. Americans had long celebrated liberal ideals. The struggle for independence had been driven by the desire for freedom and equality, two ideas at the heart of Western political thought. Yet now, being called a liberal could almost seem shameful.

How had that happened?

The change took hold after the Second World War, especially during the 1960s. Since then, public support for liberalism had weakened. To understand why, we need to look at another set of ideas, associated with the British economist John Maynard Keynes.

The older picture of capitalism was built around what Adam Smith called the *invisible hand*: people pursuing their own interests in the marketplace could help keep the economy running without officials directing everything. The accompanying principle was *laissez-faire*—roughly speaking, let businesses and markets get on with it.

But capitalism developed problems that this approach did not seem able to solve. As production grew larger and more closely connected, the need to coordinate economic life grew too. By the early twentieth century, unemployment, crises and other pressures had become serious threats to Western societies.

Scholars began looking for a different approach. Keynes's ideas offered one.

Instead of leaving everything to the invisible hand, Keynes argued that government should use its own visible hand to steady the economy. It could encourage spending and investment, fund public works and try to make sure that people who wanted jobs could find them.

The great economic crises of the 1920s and 1930s gave these ideas an opening. Without such severe trouble, Keynesian policies would have found it much harder to win support in the West.

People often refuse to change course until they run into a wall. Governments are much the same. And, as we shall see, Keynesian policies would eventually face troubles of their own.

The turning point came during Franklin Roosevelt's presidency. Keynesian thinking became closely associated with American liberalism. If a conservative Republican had happened to be president during that crisis, I suspect he might have turned to similar measures. Circumstances often determine which ideas succeed.

There was also a deeper reason for the connection. Liberalism tended to trust people's ability to make reasonable choices, provided they had the right opportunities. Conservatism placed less faith in human judgment and more emphasis on rules and restraints.

One might therefore expect conservatives to favour a stronger government. Yet liberals wanted government to create the conditions in which people could do better, and they also needed to win elections. In practice, the two aims came together: use public action to create a better environment for individual lives.

Roosevelt's New Deal greatly expanded federal power. The government became involved in economic and social matters on a scale that would have seemed foreign to the older capitalist ideal. Its responsibilities grew at once and dramatically.

Lowi believed this change produced two powerful trends.

The first was a growing belief in the importance of state action. The second was what political scholars call *pluralism*: a system in which many organized groups compete to influence decisions.

In practice, Lowi argued, this became *interest-group pluralism*. Trade associations, businesses, unions and other organized groups all tried to win favourable treatment from government. He saw a new mixture of state power, capitalism and bargaining between organized interests, which he called *interest-group liberalism*.

His argument runs something like this. Once organized groups become the main players, competition is no longer perfectly open. Instead of everyone competing on equal terms, powerful groups negotiate with one another. Bargaining takes the place of direct coercion or violence. If the system remains peaceful and stable, these competing groups may be able to work out their differences among themselves.

But there is a danger. The process can end up serving groups with the greatest influence rather than the public as a whole.

This, for Lowi, was America's *Second Republic*. He argued that every Congress and administration since 1961 had followed this approach. Government agencies had slipped further away from public control, while old privileges were protected and new ones appeared. Worse still, the system could not deal indefinitely with the deeper problems of capitalism.

Lowi's complaint was that such a government could bargain but could not plan. Planning means making choices, setting priorities and using authority to carry them out. Interest-group politics replaces those hard decisions with endless negotiation.

Government could take on more and more responsibilities without being able to bring them together into a workable whole. Meanwhile, the economic policies associated with Keynes helped create another problem: government spending that continually exceeded government income.

By the end of the 1960s, these strains were becoming visible in economic trouble, public deficits, social conflict, declining confidence and fears of moral decay. What had promised a brighter future now looked to many Americans like a source of disorder.

It was against this background that Bush could use the word *liberal* against Michael Dukakis in 1988.

Lowi divided modern American government into periods. His *First Republic* lasted from 1787 into the 1930s. It was marked by limited federal authority and considerable power for the states.

His *Second Republic* began in the 1930s, when the federal government greatly increased its power to regulate economic activity and redistribute income through taxes, benefits and public spending. This made possible a society of higher wages, more benefits and greater consumption, but also, in his account, larger deficits and mounting public anger.

What would a *Third Republic* look like?

For Lowi, it would mean finding a way out of the problems that had reached a peak in the 1960s. Organized interests had weakened traditional democracy and left government looking ineffective and untrustworthy. His proposed answer was *judicial democracy*: stronger, clearer legal procedures for making and reviewing decisions in every part of public life.

Whether or not one accepts his solution, the problem he identified remains.

After the great expansion of government, how can America change the system it has built? How can it reduce the responsibilities government has accumulated?

Once a person has put on a great deal of weight, slimming down is no easy matter. Government faces the same problem—and so do the people who depend on it.

The liberal era brought extensive public benefits. Many Americans complain about them and see them as a heavy burden on government. Yet the benefits cannot simply be thrown away. They have become one of the supports holding society together.

After living with the troubles of big government, Americans want smaller government. But cutting government back too far could leave society without the support it needs.

Modern life calls for more coordination, not less. Technology has given us better ways to coordinate complex activities, yet Americans remain uneasy about any attempt to manage society too completely. Their beliefs and feelings still pull them towards the older promise of individual freedom.

America has not found an easy way through this contradiction. I suspect it will be struggling with it for many years.

# Chapter 3: The Many Sides of American Character

## 1. Citizens of the World—Whether They Like It or Not

Americans are becoming citizens of the world, though many of them have no particular wish to be.

Their economy and their country's international influence are drawing them into an increasingly connected world. Yet the average American, in my experience, often knows less about other countries than people in nations Americans might consider less developed.

American scholars have repeatedly expressed concern about how little the younger generation knows of the wider world. Their knowledge seems especially limited for people who belong to such a powerful nation.

In poorer countries, the situation can be quite different. People look abroad because they want better lives and hope to learn how other societies have become prosperous. Their interest in the world can be intense.

For many Americans, by contrast, international life comes to them whether they seek it or not. Commerce, technology and political events leave them little choice. The older desire to keep America apart from foreign affairs, once associated with the Monroe Doctrine, would be difficult to revive except under very unusual circumstances.

The cowboy of the Western films, travelling on horseback with a dusty face and a broad hat, has given way to an American who flies around the world in a Boeing 747—or travels with an aircraft carrier or nuclear submarine.

Yet ordinary Americans have lived in comfort for so long that many show little curiosity about the world beyond their doorstep. A number of scholars and public officials understand the danger. They have therefore devoted great effort to helping people learn about other countries.

Two kinds of institution do much of this work: schools and universities, and research centres.

America may have more organizations studying foreign countries than almost anywhere else. Universities divide the world into countries and regions, establish special programmes and bring together experts. Independent research centres do much the same. More and more attention is given to teaching students to look beyond America's borders.

Several features stand out.

**First, the research is specific.** Universities do not merely teach broad subjects such as international relations or comparative politics—the study of how different governments work. They also build teams that concentrate on particular places.

Iowa State University has programmes on China and Japan. The University of Michigan is known for its China studies. Syracuse has strengths in the study of India, while the University of Washington in Seattle has its own China programme. Michigan also has a centre devoted specifically to contemporary Chinese culture.

Specialized programmes bring people who know a region together and help them deepen that knowledge.

**Second, education has a clear purpose.** A country cannot expect one generation after another to understand the world by accident. It has to teach them.

Professors I met in the Midwest told me that people there were often conservative, less open to unfamiliar ideas and less interested in foreign affairs. They believed that learning about the wider world was necessary for the region's future development.

On the East Coast, professors gave a different explanation. They described international awareness—especially knowledge of Europe—as part of their region's longstanding culture. Eastern universities already had considerable experience and resources in international research.

In the West, the emphasis was different again. Professors told me that the twenty-first century would belong increasingly to the Asia-Pacific region. Facing the Pacific Ocean, the West Coast had every reason to develop a deeper understanding of its neighbours across the water. Universities there had therefore invested heavily in Asian and Pacific studies.

**Third, government gives this work strong support.** A country's future depends on the knowledge and abilities of its people. Its position in the world, and its ability to compete, are shaped by what its citizens know and can do.

Because America is a federal country, state governments have considerable influence over their public universities. Many use that influence to encourage international education.

Consider the University of California at San Diego. Not long ago it was a relatively modest institution, but it has been growing rapidly. Construction seems to be under way everywhere on campus.

The university has established a graduate school devoted to international relations and Pacific studies, intended in part to prepare people for California's expanding ties with the Asia-Pacific region. It plans to recruit thirty-five professors—a substantial number by American standards—and is offering generous salaries to attract established scholars from other universities.

A leading expert on Japan has reportedly been recruited from Berkeley with a salary of about $150,000 a year.

A new building for the school is going up beside the ocean. With the Pacific stretching out before its windows, what better place could there be to encourage people to think about the world?

The school was established by the state government in 1986. It is described as the only centre in the nine-campus University of California system devoted primarily to international education about the Pacific region.

Its stated purpose is clear: to prepare students interested in Pacific countries for leadership in business, diplomacy, public service and related fields, and to become a centre for research into the region's economic, political, social, technological and security issues.

This is only one example. Government is not supporting such programmes merely so students can collect more knowledge. The larger goal is to strengthen the country's economic, political and cultural position. But that larger goal is impossible unless people are educated first.

**Fourth, American institutions actively exchange ideas and people with other countries.** They do not simply ask American professors to study foreign societies from a distance. They invite scholars from those societies to work alongside them.

Americans take a practical approach to expertise. If a person can do the job, where they were born need not matter. I have seen groups of visiting researchers from many countries at university centres. American universities also employ professors of Chinese, Korean, Japanese, Indian, Canadian, French, Hungarian and many other backgrounds.

The logic is simple enough. Someone who grew up in a country, speaks its language and understands its customs may have knowledge that an outsider lacks. Why not make use of it?

The Institute of East Asian Studies at the University of California, Berkeley, is a good example. It has relatively few permanent researchers and a small administrative staff, yet dozens of visiting scholars from China, South Korea, Japan, Nepal and other countries work there.

Its director, Robert A. Scalapino, argues that the Asia-Pacific region matters to the whole world. It is home to more than half the world's population, contains major political and military powers, and has enormous natural resources.

The institute works to bring American and Asian scholars together. And the benefit does not end when the visitors go home. They return to their own countries as colleagues with whom American researchers can continue to exchange information and ideas.

Those relationships spread America's academic influence far beyond the people physically present at Berkeley. There may also be effects beyond academic life, which hardly need spelling out.

So there are two sides to America's growing involvement in the world.

Ordinary people are often pulled into international life by forces they do not control. Competition from Europe, Japan and other parts of Asia makes it impossible for them to ignore the world economy. Government decisions about places such as the Middle East and Central America bring foreign affairs into their lives too.

But political leaders, scholars and research institutions are doing something more deliberate. They are preparing people for a world in which America's position cannot be taken for granted. Their programmes have long-term aims and are carefully organized.

This effort has been part of America's development, and it will shape its future. Yet the people directing it are anxious that younger Americans know too little about other countries.

Their concern comes down to a difficult question: can America remain a world power if its people do not really understand the world? Will they be able to compete with other countries that are every bit as determined and increasingly capable?
