No country can prosper indefinitely without understanding the strengths and aims of others. Today's nations both cooperate and compete, but the competition is fierce—over politics, economics, culture, military power and even ways of life. In recent history, countries that fell badly behind often paid for it in poverty and weakness. Now the competition is becoming still more intense, and not always fair.

The decisive factor is people: what they know, how they think and what they are prepared to learn.

Imagine two people arriving on the same world stage. One has spent his life driving an ox cart through a quiet rural landscape. The other travels by jet and lives amid the constant movement of a modern city. Faced with unfamiliar countries and complicated institutions, which is better prepared to take part?

But the real dividing line is not geography. It is what happens inside a person's mind.

Someone living in a great modern city can still have a narrow view of the world. Someone living in the countryside can be deeply interested in international affairs.

Education is one of the strongest ways to break down that mental barrier. Only when people truly look beyond it can their country take a full and effective part in world affairs.

That is what it means to become citizens of the world.

## 2. The Urge to Do Something Different

In many ways, Americans are still a remarkably conservative people.

Ideas and ways of life associated with sexual freedom, rock music, hippies, homosexuality, unconventional behaviour and racial equality have by no means won everyone's acceptance. Many people still hold firmly to traditional beliefs. In politics, especially, old standards remain powerful.

The Republicans' repeated presidential victories seem to reflect this tendency. Americans still judge political leaders by rather conventional expectations of personal behaviour. The Democrat Gary Hart had to withdraw from the presidential race after a scandal involving a woman.

Dan Quayle became vice president largely because he was Bush's running mate. Many Americans shook their heads when they spoke of him. They said he had done poorly at school, served only in the National Guard, lacked experience and owed his success to a wealthy father.

People in Asia sometimes assume that sexual relationships cannot cause much trouble in a country as sexually open as America. Yet in politics, they can cause enormous trouble. The same is true elsewhere in the West.

Americans continue to look back to the beliefs of their country's founders. Their political institutions help preserve those beliefs and resist competing ones. In that sense, Americans are conservative.

And yet the very same people are among the world's most enthusiastic inventors.

Here is the puzzle. Americans can be devoted to things that are old and familiar while eagerly welcoming things that are strange and completely new. Their society produces an astonishing range of inventions and seems unusually willing to entertain daring ideas.

They have launched space shuttles, proposed the Strategic Defense Initiative—the plan popularly known as *Star Wars*—and, at the end of 1988, displayed the remarkable new B-2 bomber. Their talent for small inventions is just as striking. Walk through a big department store and you will find devices for almost every imaginable purpose.

How can a society be so attached to tradition and so determined to break new ground?

Part of the answer is that the two impulses operate in different areas of life. Americans often hold conservative views about morality and public values. But in technology, they welcome the boldest experiments.

Some Americans have even built a simulated space settlement in a remote area, with plans for volunteers to live inside it, cut off from the outside world, for two years. The project moved ahead with remarkable speed.

If someone proposed building a motorway across the Atlantic, linking America to Europe, or across the Pacific to Asia, I suspect Americans would be more likely to call it a magnificent idea than to dismiss its inventor as mad.

Using human skill to master the natural world has long been part of the American tradition. So inventing something new can actually be a way of honouring something old.

The freedom to dream on a grand scale is especially strong where machines, buildings and material things are concerned. America's history and technological achievements have encouraged people to believe that almost anything can be attempted.

I found myself thinking about this during a visit to the Gateway Arch in St. Louis.

The arch is one of the most extraordinary structures of its kind. It rises about 630 feet—nearly 200 metres—and is made entirely of stainless steel. Against a clear blue sky, it gleams in the sunlight like an enormous silver rainbow beside the Mississippi River. Its span is more than 200 metres.

At its base is a museum commemorating the westward expansion associated with President Thomas Jefferson in the early nineteenth century. Inside the arch are lifts that carry visitors up through its two curved sides to the top. There, a passage about ten metres long has windows offering a sweeping view of St. Louis.

The idea is extraordinary. The design, by Finnish-American architect Fero Saarinean, won a national competition in 1947. Construction began in 1963 and was completed in 1965.

Even the building process was unusual. There was no great tower of scaffolding around it. Engineers began at the bottom of both legs, mounting cranes on the rising structure and moving them higher as work progressed. The two halves stretched up into the air, curving towards each other according to careful calculations, until they finally met.

From the first drawing to the last piece of steel, it was a bold undertaking. And Americans did not merely admire the idea. They built it.

Surely somebody must have asked whether it was useful. Would it make money? Why not put up an ordinary monument instead? What if the two halves failed to meet?

Another Missouri building reveals the same taste for the unexpected: the church at Westminster College in Fulton.

Fulton is a small, unremarkable place that became known around the world. In 1946, shortly after the Second World War, Winston Churchill delivered his famous speech there about an *iron curtain* dividing East and West. The phrase became a common Western description of the Soviet Union and the countries of Eastern Europe, and the speech came to mark the beginning of the Cold War.

The church looks ordinary enough from outside. What makes it remarkable is where its stones came from.

They were brought all the way from Britain.

The building had originally been a British church, first built in the twelfth century and completely rebuilt in 1677. It suffered severe damage during the Second World War. Only ruins, loose stones and twelve stone pillars were left standing.

For years the site remained a ruin. Then the Westminster College memorial committee proposed moving the stones across the Atlantic and rebuilding the church in Missouri, where it would also serve as a memorial to Churchill.

Work began in 1965. Some seven hundred tons of stone crossed the ocean, at a cost of about $3 million. President Harry Truman laid the foundation stone, and the project was completed in 1969.

Surely people asked questions here too. Why not use local stone? Why spend so much more? Were there really no stones in America? Why ship a heap of broken rock halfway around the world?

These are only two examples. There are countless others.

In Iowa City, a basketball arena can hold more than ten thousand spectators, yet from outside it appears barely one storey high. Most of the building lies underground. The University of Iowa's law school has an exposed concrete-and-steel structure, a great dome and small windows. It looks rather like a military fortification. I doubt it would be easy to storm in a battle.

Americans can imagine carving the enormous faces of five presidents into a mountainside. In Washington, a monument to unknown soldiers rises like a bare Egyptian column. The Vietnam War memorial is a wall of black stone engraved with the names of the dead.

Even beds can be inventive. Furniture shops sell waterbeds, with mattresses nearly a foot deep filled with water. They are wonderfully soft, and the water can be heated or cooled.

American films are full of unusual ideas: *E.T.*, *Star Wars*, *Superman* and *Close Encounters of the Third Kind*. The newly displayed B-2 bomber looks nothing like a conventional aircraft. With no tail, it seems to be made almost entirely of wings.

Clothing can be stranger still. People pay high prices for new garments that have been deliberately made to look old and torn. Apparently, making them look so worn takes considerable skill.

And in science and technology, the examples are too numerous to count.

Of course, novelty can go too far. Those expensive, pre-damaged clothes are one example. There are enormous luxury cars fitted with kitchens, swimming pools, places to play golf, telephones and televisions—pleasures available to only a handful of people.

On the street you sometimes see hair standing straight up like an explosion, or heads shaved in startling patterns. In public places, someone may hang a few battered sheets of metal and call the result modern sculpture. Some forms of modern art are more than many people can comfortably appreciate.

Still, the urge to invent and stand apart is a major driving force in American development. It helps move technology and the economy forward.

So why does a society that holds so tightly to traditional values also make so much room for new ideas? I see several reasons.

**First, Americans draw a line between public values and practical invention.** Matters of morality and shared beliefs concern everyone, and people tend to expect broad agreement about them. But machines, products and technical ideas are largely treated as matters of personal initiative.

In a society without the old European barriers of aristocratic birth and inherited rank, people are expected to make a name for themselves through achievement. Standing out becomes a way of gaining recognition. Paradoxically, the stability of America's public values gives people room to experiment with material things without overturning the whole social order.

**Second, conservative values do not usually place heavy limits on technical progress.** The settlers who came from Europe had to struggle with the natural world and make a living in difficult conditions. Overcoming those obstacles became part of their tradition. Today, a new invention can be seen as another expression of that same old determination.

Americans often treat a technical change as just that: a new way to make or do something. It does not automatically require them to abandon their moral beliefs.

Other societies do not always make such a clear distinction. If every new machine or material change is treated as a challenge to the whole way of life, invention becomes harder. American ideals such as freedom, equality and the pursuit of happiness are broad enough to make room for innovation. Creating something new can be seen as an exercise of freedom; welcoming it can be seen as a sign of openness and equality.

**Third, the way American society works pushes people to invent.** If you want to succeed, you cannot simply do what everyone else is doing.

Money creates one pressure. Businesses and individuals who want to earn more have to offer something different, something that catches the public's attention. Prosperity creates another. When many people already live comfortably, it takes something genuinely unusual to stand out.

As standards rise, people keep trying to reach higher. They invent to earn money, to prove themselves and to win recognition. Everyone is trying to get one step ahead.

**Fourth, national pride plays a part.** I would call it the vanity of a great power. That is not always an admirable quality, but it can encourage invention.

Americans grow up hearing that their country is number one. Many believe it. In advanced technology, the desire to be first becomes especially strong. This confidence has helped produce achievements admired around the world.

But it can also lead to illusions. The belief that America must always come out on top has already suffered some painful shocks. Its usefulness as a spur to invention does not make it an infallible view of the world.

**Fifth, individualism encourages originality.** A new idea usually begins when somebody decides to do something unlike what others have done. That takes a willingness to think independently and sometimes to ignore other people's opinions.

Large inventions may involve many people, but they are still made up of individual contributions. A culture that values individuality makes it easier for people to attempt something different. In cultures that place more pressure on people to conform, standing out can be harder both for the inventor and for the community asked to accept the invention.

Individualism can make social harmony more difficult. But it can also release enormous creative energy.

**Sixth, America's democratic traditions make people receptive to new ideas.** Americans are often quick to welcome something novel. Sometimes, to put it less politely, they are quick to join the cheering.

A person who fails to praise the latest invention or unusual artwork may worry about seeming narrow-minded or uncultured. It is a little like looking at an abstract painting and being afraid to admit that you dislike it, in case everyone laughs at you.

Yet much of the enthusiasm is genuine. Americans admire success, respect people who think differently and often grant special recognition to those who break new ground.

No society can develop without invention. It needs people who dare to try new things, and it needs a public willing to give those people a chance.

But society also needs continuity. If every shared belief changes constantly, there is little to hold people together.

The real challenge is to preserve the values that give a society stability while allowing technology and material life to change—and then to let those changes strengthen and renew the values themselves.

Seen this way, a society's ability to encourage invention is not mainly a question of how many machines or scientists it has. **It depends on what its people believe, and on whether those beliefs leave room for the new.**

## 3. Nothing Is Beyond Question

America is a society with very little room for mystery. People grow up expecting that almost anything can be examined, explained or changed. This is an important part of the country's culture.

In many other societies, certain things remain surrounded by awe. Some African and Latin American cultures, and parts of Western European culture too, give mystery a much larger place.

What difference does that make? At the very least, treating something as mysterious can protect old beliefs and institutions from challenge.

The same is true of nature. Scientific progress depends on people's willingness to investigate the natural world. If a mountain, a storm or a disease is regarded as something too sacred or mysterious to examine, people may stand at a distance and pray for help instead of trying to understand it.

Americans seem to have remarkably few barriers of that kind. Even when a taboo exists, it often fails to stop them.

And the question goes beyond nature. If people treat human beings and their own societies as things that cannot be understood, they will struggle to develop their culture and institutions too.

We can see this attitude in many parts of American life.

**The heavens.** Many peoples have looked at the sky with a sense of profound mystery. Americans have strong religious feelings, but for most of them that has not made the heavens a place beyond human reach.

The Apollo Moon landings and the space shuttle programme show a willingness to explore what earlier generations could only imagine. Even the Star Wars defence programme treats outer space as a place human beings might be able to use and control.

Americans may think of heaven as God's home, but that has not prevented them from sending machines and people into space. Films such as *Star Wars*, *E.T.* and *Close Encounters of the Third Kind* are, in this sense, expressions of a desire to strip away mystery rather than surrender to it.

Yet powerful religious preachers remain an important force in American society. Their influence reminds us that religion, and a sense of the divine, have not disappeared.

**Nature.** In many cultures, the natural world has a mysterious quality. Americans tend to approach it differently. They keep exploring it, learning about it and turning what was once unknown into something familiar.

I suspect that this confidence has helped their society develop. One of the starting points of science and technology is the belief that nothing in nature is necessarily beyond human understanding or inventiveness.

**The human body.** Americans are also willing to look at themselves without much mystery.

Religious people know the Bible's account of God creating humanity. But that belief has not stopped doctors from opening chests or skulls, operating on hearts and brains, and treating such procedures as technical problems to be solved rather than forbidden acts.

There is great interest in artificial hearts. America is also among the leading countries in the use of what are called test-tube babies—children conceived with medical help outside the mother's body.

Once the human body is no longer treated as untouchable, even the idea of creating or replacing parts of human life begins to seem imaginable.

Sex education for young people belongs to the same pattern. Sex is surrounded by secrecy and mystery in many societies. In America, much of it is treated as ordinary knowledge, something people may discuss as readily as food or the other necessities of daily life.

Magazines showing naked bodies meet with opposition, but many people have become so accustomed to them that they take little special interest.

This attitude towards human beings affects not only medicine and other natural sciences. It also affects the study of society. If we can learn what human beings really are, why should any part of human life remain forever beyond explanation?

**Politics.** In some countries, political power is surrounded by ceremony and mystery. Americans are much less inclined to treat it that way.

At times they seem almost too practical, handling politics like a business and leaving little room for deeper cultural meaning. Yet this willingness to question political authority helps their system survive.

Political cartoons are a good example. Presidents and other powerful people are favourite targets.

During the 1988 election campaign, cartoonist Joe Sharpnack drew a child rolling around on a bed, crying, "I want to be vice president! I want to!" Nearby stood a figure resembling Bush, holding an American flag and saying, "All right, all right. Look, Daddy has made you a new coat!"

The joke was aimed at Bush and his running mate, Quayle. No political leader was too important to be laughed at.

The press plays a similar role. Reporters helped bring the Watergate scandal, the Iran-Contra affair and allegations of bribery at the Defense Department into public view.

Politics is treated as another human activity. Most people do not even take much interest in it. A candidate for Congress may have to drive himself from place to place, asking ordinary people for their votes.

**Society itself.** Americans do not generally regard society as something handed down from beyond human control. People created it, and if necessary they can create it differently.

Recently, a university student studying computers wrote a programme that entered the Pentagon's computer network and damaged its databases. The programme spread like a growing infection—a kind of computer germ.

The incident illustrates a larger attitude. Americans are especially unlikely to treat the things human beings have built as sacred or beyond understanding. If people managed to create something once, they can find a way to create it again—or change it.

**Children.** American upbringing also encourages this lack of fear before the mysterious.

Americans have imagined a tremendous number of ghosts and monsters in stories and films, yet they do not necessarily believe in them. At Halloween, children dress up as ghosts and other frightening creatures and go from house to house in their neighbourhoods.

The lesson seems to be that ghosts need not be feared. Human beings may be much more frightening.

In some other societies the belief is almost the reverse: people are ordinary, but ghosts are terrifying. It would be interesting to examine what these different habits of mind produce.

There are gains and losses in this American attitude. That is true of almost everything that helps a society develop.

By refusing to leave nature, humanity and social life shrouded in mystery, Americans have learned more about all three. Their willingness to question and investigate has helped drive progress.

But it also creates difficulties. When nothing is beyond challenge, it becomes harder to maintain authority, moderation, self-control and confidence in anything lasting.

If everyone believes that nothing can ever be trusted completely, the resulting spirit of questioning may become a society's greatest strength. It may also become its greatest destructive force.

There is the human problem in a nutshell: **we cannot afford to surround everything with mystery, but perhaps we cannot live without any sense of mystery at all.**

How did America develop this attitude?

The answer is too complicated to settle here, but its history offers a clue. American society grew on land that, in the experience of its European settlers, did not carry the weight of their old cultural traditions. The country had abundant resources and favourable geography. In the early years, people could see tangible rewards for practical effort, above all in the form of land.

Daily experience encouraged them to learn simple, useful truths about the world rather than stand in awe of it. The long emphasis on practical results and economic growth strengthened the habit.

We might even say that money helped strip away mystery. The desire to earn and prosper pushed people first to explore nature, then to study the human body and finally to question the workings of society itself.

Economic growth encouraged this attitude, and the attitude in turn made further growth possible.

Two other features of American culture matter here. Americans enjoy doing things differently, and they are accustomed to taking on challenges and overcoming obstacles.

In broad terms, I would describe American culture as one that pushes outward and seeks to conquer, while Chinese culture has more often sought to protect and defend.

But what exactly do I mean by *mystery* here?

I mean the belief that certain matters are beyond the ability of ordinary people to understand or change—or that such matters are none of their business.

That is different from belief in the supernatural. The supernatural concerns the relationship between human beings and powers beyond nature. The kind of mystery I am discussing also concerns the relationships between people: who may ask questions, who may act, and who is expected to stand aside.

## 4. Making Things Sacred

Americans have little patience for mystery, yet they have a remarkable talent for treating certain things as sacred. At first, that sounds like a contradiction. But there is an important difference between the two.

In some societies, people treat extraordinary events or abilities as the work of powers beyond the human world. The author of *Chariots of the Gods?* describes examples of this. On some Pacific islands, people reportedly treated the unexpected arrival of American military aircraft during the Second World War as something supernatural. They even built wooden models of planes and worshipped them. In other places, healers are surrounded by an air of mystery. America has little of this tradition. There have been extraordinary exceptions, such as the Peoples Temple, in which hundreds of members died in a mass suicide. But such cases are rare.

What I am talking about is something different. Americans are not especially inclined to turn people or things into gods. Instead, they have a habit of lifting ordinary, human achievements onto a pedestal. I call this *making things sacred*.

It is not quite religious worship. It takes place in everyday life, and it does not require a priest, a church or an official campaign. A whole society can gradually give a person, an event or an ideal a status far greater than it would otherwise have. Let me explain through some of the things I have seen.

Americans can become intensely emotional, and they respond readily to the excitement of a crowd. You can see this at the national conventions of the two main political parties. People cheer, shout, wave and celebrate with extraordinary enthusiasm. When a presidential candidate appears, the applause may continue for ten minutes. Speeches are interrupted again and again by cheers.

The same thing happens as the candidates travel around the country during an election campaign. Wherever they go, enthusiastic crowds greet them.

This is curious. In a culture that places such importance on individual independence and equality, why should anyone receive this kind of devotion?

The answer, I think, is that the candidate has become more than a person. The crowd sees a symbol of the things it values and hopes to achieve. What it celebrates is not simply the individual standing on the stage. It is the larger idea that person has come to represent.

Americans do this in almost every field. Anyone who achieves something extraordinary may be lifted onto a pedestal.

In politics, Washington and the other Founding Fathers, Lincoln, Roosevelt and Kennedy have all received this treatment. When people honour them, they are honouring an idea of what America stands for.

In sport, outstanding athletes become figures of admiration. The runner Carl Lewis and the diver Greg Louganis are examples. Business produces its own heroes, sometimes even more readily. Lee Iacocca, who made his name at Ford and then attracted new attention after leaving to join Chrysler, became a celebrated figure. His book was a bestseller.

Film stars, directors and artists are obvious candidates too. Peter Ueberroth became famous for organizing the twenty-third Olympic Games and was celebrated well beyond his particular achievement. Even Lieutenant Colonel Oliver North, caught up in the Iran-Contra affair, acquired a devoted following. His admirers saw him as a man of duty and, in their eyes, an example of the American spirit.

Professors, by contrast, seem rather unlikely to receive this sort of devotion.

Popular singers certainly do. Madonna was enormously famous, and Michael Jackson was an even bigger sensation. President Reagan gave Jackson an award of some kind. The effect of his performances on his audiences is astonishing. His fans seem almost ready to die of excitement.

Inventors and businesspeople in technology can also become heroes, including the founders of Apple Computer and An Wang of Wang Laboratories.

The striking thing is that no central authority decides who will be celebrated. The public makes its own choices. In this sense, the process is open to almost everyone.

And it is not limited to individuals.

Consider American football. For many spectators, a game is much more than a contest between two teams. They come to cheer for a whole set of loyalties and beliefs. The national anthem, the ceremonies, the players' entrance, the game itself and the entertainment during the breaks all carry meanings beyond sport.

The great celebration beneath the Statue of Liberty for the country's two-hundredth anniversary had a similar quality. It was a vast public event, full of excitement and ceremony, but it was also an occasion for giving national ideals an almost sacred place in people's hearts. The government helped organize it, and governments often encourage this kind of feeling.

The armed forces are another example. Military victories are celebrated, and those who die in battle receive special honour. In Washington there is an unusual memorial to the Vietnam War, with the name of each fallen service member carved into its surface. Official funerals for the dead are solemn occasions.

The space programme receives the same treatment. After the *Challenger* shuttle exploded, the government paid extraordinary tribute to the astronauts who died. They were remembered as people who had sacrificed themselves in pursuit of the American spirit of exploration. When *Discovery* later returned to space successfully, the government celebrated it as a victory for that same spirit. The event meant much more than a technical achievement.

What is happening in all these cases? Something that people admire—an athlete, a leader, a mission, an achievement—is turned into a living example of what America is supposed to be. The public celebrates it, and in celebrating it renews its own attachment to those ideals.

American culture has a deep tradition of admiring heroes and success. Western films have long expressed it through the figure of the brave, resourceful hero. *Star Wars* expresses something similar in a very different setting. Today's heroes no longer have to look like cowboys, and the meaning of success keeps changing. But the appetite for heroes and remarkable achievements remains.

Where other cultures might direct this feeling towards gods or spirits, Americans often direct it towards human beings and what they can accomplish.

Most of the time, these feelings lie quietly beneath the surface. In a society that also prizes equality, doubts every claim to greatness and insists that opinions are relative, open displays of admiration may seem out of place. But bring people together at a stadium, a political convention or a concert, and the emotions burst out. The occasion allows people both to admire something larger than themselves and to release feelings they usually keep hidden.

This is worship of a worldly kind. It is not the worship of gods, ghosts, spirits or other supernatural powers. For many Americans, belief in God is a matter of faith and personal feeling rather than the kind of devotion I am describing here. Because their culture is so ready to question mystery, they turn their desire to admire something towards the visible world around them.

They admire courage, intelligence, adventure and success—things they can see in people they know about. The response is a complicated mixture of reason and emotion, conscious choice and instinct. Ultimately, the person being celebrated is less important than the spirit that person represents.

That spirit has become part of the country's cultural inheritance, passed from one generation to the next. Each new celebration helps keep it alive. It is hard to define precisely, but its presence is unmistakable. Henry Steele Commager's *The American Mind* offers another way of looking at it.

The larger importance of this habit is that it keeps a society's central values alive and spreads them widely. People do not merely hear about those values; they take part in celebrating them. Their feelings, beliefs and ambitions meet in a shared experience.

I am reminded of Rousseau's argument that a society needs a kind of civic religion—a set of common beliefs that binds its members together. America's habit of turning worldly achievements into objects of admiration seems to serve a similar purpose.

In a society where people are so independent and so concerned with their own lives, this may be one of the most effective ways to keep shared values alive.

No society can develop steadily without some beliefs that hold it together. The difficult questions are where those beliefs come from and how they survive. Perhaps the strongest arrangement is one in which people draw their ideals from the world around them, then choose for themselves to celebrate and preserve them.

## 5. When Technology Leads Us Astray

The space shuttle *Discovery* rose into the sky, and television networks across America carried the launch live. For Americans, this was no ordinary success.

More than two years had passed since the country had last sent a shuttle into space. In January 1986, the *Challenger* had exploded, killing its crew and shocking the world. Now, two and a half years later, *Discovery* was flying. It was a moment that many people had long dreamed of seeing.

The shuttle programme expresses an important American belief. As the historian Henry Steele Commager put it, Americans tend to believe that nothing is impossible—and that you should never give up until you have won.

Space exploration is a magnificent example. Building, launching and controlling a shuttle requires an astonishing combination of skills. A glance at the hundreds of computers in a mission-control centre is enough to suggest the complexity of the task.

After the *Challenger* disaster, the space programme spent two and a half years making improvements. More than four hundred technical changes were introduced. The people involved were convinced that solutions could be found, so they kept working until they found them.

The same confidence lies behind America's boldest projects, from the shuttle to the Star Wars defence programme. It also encourages much smaller inventions: machines that open envelopes or cans, electric pencil sharpeners and countless other devices that make ordinary tasks easier.

This belief in the power to solve problems has helped make America what it is.

But it carries a danger.

As Americans invent more and better ways to overcome difficulties, they may gradually begin to believe that technology itself solves problems. People build the machines, yet come to act as if the machines are the real source of power. Technology stops being a tool in human hands and begins to rule the people who created it.

I discussed this with a professor who shared my concern. He agreed that the habit has become widespread.

When Americans face a complicated problem involving human relationships, culture or society, they often treat it as a technical problem—or as something that can be solved with enough money. They are less ready to ask whether the real difficulty lies in people's beliefs, feelings or decisions.

You can see this in politics and foreign affairs. The Soviet Union grows stronger, so America races to develop superior weapons, eventually proposing the Star Wars programme. There is terrorism, so the answer is more advanced means of attack. Shipping lanes are threatened, so the answer is a powerful, well-equipped navy. A foreign government is disliked, so opponents of that government are supplied with sophisticated weapons.

Even the equipment designed to help people with disabilities shows the pattern. There are wheelchairs that guide themselves, bedside devices that respond to commands and glasses that help users find their way. These inventions can make movement and everyday tasks much easier. Yet they do not necessarily resolve the deeper difficulties a disabled person may face in society.

The same is true in international affairs. A technical solution can help with a human problem without reaching its roots.

At the same time, technology has become a form of politics.

After the successful launch of *Discovery*, Forrest McCartney, director of the Kennedy Space Center, said that every American had reason to hold their head high that day. President Reagan watched the launch on television in Washington and declared that America was back in space.

Space exploration had been political from the beginning. In the 1960s, the Soviet Union succeeded in landing on the Moon, stirring anger in America. President Kennedy called for an enormous national effort, and the Apollo Moon landing eventually put the United States ahead in that contest.

Behind the contest in technology was a contest for political power and national standing. Political competition drove technological competition, while technological success strengthened political power.

This growing link between politics and technology is one of the great developments of the twentieth century. Politics without advanced technology cannot become truly powerful. But technology, too, needs political support if it is to reach the height of its powers.

The danger is that the relationship can begin to turn things upside down. Instead of people controlling technology, technology seems to control them. In America, this tendency is especially visible.

If another country wants to surpass the United States, it must surpass it in science and technology. With many other countries, that would not be enough. Their strength also depends on cultural habits, public attitudes and the way society is organized.

Americans have been accustomed to being on top for a very long time. The country's leading position began to emerge after the First World War. Seventy years have passed, long enough for several generations to grow up with that idea. People born after the Second World War are now entering middle age, and many have known nothing but the belief that America is number one.

That creates a national weakness: Americans find it difficult to accept defeat.

Pride in technical achievement gradually becomes a belief in national superiority. People struggle to imagine that another country might outdo them.

Japan has provided an uncomfortable lesson. In the decades since the war, it has advanced at extraordinary speed. In some areas, including electronics and cars, it has overtaken American producers. Japanese products have flooded into American markets, and Japanese investment has followed.

I have heard that Japanese buyers have purchased so much property in Hawaii that prices have soared. Americans do not like this development. When some speak about the Japanese, I hear a dismissive or even contemptuous tone. For a long time, Americans seemed unwilling to recognize Japan's success.

Harvard professor Ezra Vogel put considerable effort into getting them to see it. His book *Japan as Number One* helped awaken Americans to a reality they had preferred to ignore. I suspect similar surprises lie ahead.

The shuttle programme shows how tightly politics and technology have become entangled. Some scholars have begun to criticize the way this can distort people's priorities. A physics professor named Allen argued that after *Challenger* was lost, NASA became so concerned with restoring its reputation and achieving a political success that getting a shuttle back into space became its highest priority.

That is why I speak of the shuttle leading people astray. The shuttle is only an example. What worries me is something much broader: **the belief that technology can answer questions that are, in the end, questions about human beings.**

It may take Americans several generations to recognize the limits of that belief.

## 6. Why Americans Work So Hard

Americans do not all approach work in the same way. Far from it. Some people, by their own choice, live on government assistance rather than look for jobs. Others find work but do not keep at it. Many taxpayers are furious about this. Why, they ask, should the government take money from people who work and give it to people who sit around doing nothing? Complaints of this kind are especially common among the middle class.

But look at the people who are working, and a different picture emerges. Most do their jobs energetically and conscientiously. There are several ways to explain this, but one thing is clear: **a society's willingness to work is one of its greatest resources.** When people take their work seriously, the whole country benefits. When they do not, the country loses something precious.

The rapid economic growth of Japan, Singapore, Hong Kong and South Korea has been closely connected with people's attitude towards work.

Different societies encourage hard work in different ways. In East Asian societies such as Japan, South Korea, Singapore and Hong Kong, strict supervision may be an important part of the answer. In America, where personal independence is so highly prized, the question is more interesting. What keeps people working so hard when they do not want others controlling their lives?

Whatever the answer, Americans generally strike me as diligent, and some display an astonishing commitment to their jobs.

In government offices, airports, shops, railway stations, hotels and other places I have visited, most employees are competent, helpful and ready to take the initiative. They know their work and get on with it. I have visited many public and private organizations, sometimes to carry out research and sometimes simply because I needed something done. In most places, I found people willing to help.

At Congressman Negel's local office, two employees carefully explained everything I asked about and searched through drawers and files to find useful material. The three secretaries in the political science department where I stayed worked quietly at their desks, seldom talking to one another. They were occupied with their own tasks. In most American workplaces I have visited, people are busy. It is unusual to see anyone sitting around without work to do.

The service industries are especially good at making life convenient. Waiters are friendly and attentive. Shop assistants answer questions and often ask whether you need help before you approach them. Bus drivers look out for their passengers. After I had taken a particular bus for some time, the driver learned where I lived and began stopping at the right corner without my having to ask.

Airlines also take care of passengers when something goes wrong. The parents of a friend of mine visited America without knowing any English. Their flight was delayed, but the airline arranged everything, including a hotel and transport. And if you feel like eating pizza without leaving the house, you can simply telephone a company and have one delivered.

These services make everyday life easy. They also make a newcomer feel less like a stranger.

Many professionals work extraordinarily hard. A young teaching assistant I know comes to the office almost every Saturday and Sunday while finishing his doctoral thesis. Most professors turn up at the department every day and spend hours writing at their computers. I have heard that some become less industrious after reaching the highest professorial rank, but there are plenty of exceptions.

One professor's wife works with lasers. Her determination is remarkable. She spends nearly every evening and Sunday in the laboratory.

The Japanese may go even further. Working deep into the night is common there. There is even a grim saying about working until your urine turns red. Americans work hard too, but their habits are not quite the same. They have a special word for people who cannot stop working: *workaholics*. It combines *work* with *alcoholic*, as if devotion to work were a kind of addiction.

But what makes people do it? In a capitalist society that celebrates independence, why should so many people be willing to work so hard?

If a country needed a huge government department simply to make sure that everybody took their job seriously, the cost of running the economy would become unbearable. So there must be other forces at work.

One common explanation is the *work ethic*: the belief that working hard is a good thing in itself. Sometimes this is called the Puritan or Protestant work ethic. Early European settlers brought with them the religious idea that material success was a sign of God's favour, and that those who prospered through their efforts might be among the people God had chosen for salvation. The German thinker Max Weber wrote a well-known study of the connection between these religious beliefs and the growth of capitalism.

But religion no longer explains very much of what we see. That older outlook survives in some circles, yet its influence has weakened greatly. Older people may still believe in it. Many younger Americans would hardly recognize the religious thinking behind it.

Another explanation is the American Dream.

Anyone who has watched the television series *Ellis Island* will recognize the familiar story: immigrants arrive in America, work hard, earn money and perhaps become rich. The dream is not only to survive, but to rise in society—to make something of yourself.

This still matters. Money is one of the main reasons people work, even though it is rarely their only reason. Not everybody wants to become a millionaire. Many people would be perfectly content with a comfortable life.

There is also a psychological explanation. Work can give people satisfaction, a sense of purpose and a feeling that their lives matter. The psychologist Abraham Maslow explored such needs in his work. America's many volunteers offer a good example. They work without pay, yet they gain companionship, recognition and relief from loneliness. Volunteering helps them feel useful and connected to others.

Still, these explanations do not tell the whole story.

I think the strongest reason is that people can achieve the kind of life they want only by working for it. Two simple facts make that possible.

First, how comfortably you live depends largely on how much money you earn. Money is essential not only for getting by, but for improving your life.

Second, most jobs are provided by private employers. Both private businesses and public organizations have broad powers to hire and dismiss employees. Jobs are not guaranteed for life.

Put those facts together and a powerful pressure emerges. You have to work to live. And if you want to keep your job or earn more, you have to work well. Otherwise, you may lose it.

This, I believe, is what drives most Americans to work conscientiously today.

Other conditions help it work. American shops are filled with goods and services. If you have money, you can generally buy what is offered, whatever your family background or social standing. People therefore have a strong reason to earn money rather than depend on rank or personal connections.

Money also has a way of making different kinds of work seem more alike. A dollar earned by cleaning streets can buy the same things as a dollar earned in an office. The money does not carry the smell of the job that produced it. Once people want the things money can buy, the nature or status of the work may matter less than the reward.

This system is effective, but it is hardly gentle. It brings people into the capitalist economy and obliges them to follow its rules. You might call this the pressure that comes with private ownership and the need to earn a living. Those who refuse to take part can find themselves pushed to the margins.

Workers earn wages; business owners earn profits. That is one of the basic arrangements that keeps capitalism running.

And notice something important: it works largely outside politics. Government does not have to stand over every worker to make sure they are diligent. The need for wages and the possibility of dismissal do much of that work on their own. This leaves government with a lighter burden.

Of course, that is only the broad picture. There are people who oppose the system, and others who prefer to live outside it altogether.

Americans can also be surprisingly good at relaxing. Their saying is *work hard and play hard*. They throw themselves into their jobs, then throw themselves just as enthusiastically into having fun.

The Japanese, it seems to me, often practise only the first half. Japan has recently been discussing *karoshi*, death brought on by overwork, including cases of middle-aged people who have worked themselves to exhaustion. In America, by contrast, people plan ambitious weekends and holidays, often preparing to spend a great deal of money. Students can be especially wild at weekends. Perhaps all that strictness and pressure at work creates a need to let loose afterwards.

There is a simple lesson here for every society that wants its people to work well. **People need to feel that they are working for themselves, not merely for someone else.**

Different societies can create that feeling in different ways. Money alone is not enough; culture and politics matter too. In reality, almost no organized society allows everybody to work entirely for themselves. If it did, it would scarcely be a society at all.

The important thing is that people believe their efforts serve a purpose of their own. That belief can be enormously powerful.

## 7. The Sexual Revolution

To many people in the East, the West's sexual revolution seems both fascinating and hard to imagine. Stories about sexual freedom in Western countries have travelled far. But how much can a visitor really learn about it?

It is not easy to answer that question after only a short time in America. A tourist can see cinemas showing adult films. In New York, prostitutes can be seen on certain streets. Television sometimes shows nudity or sexual activity. Shops sell magazines such as *Playgirl*, *Playboy* and *Penthouse*.

But these are the commercial signs of sexual freedom. They tell us little about what ordinary Americans actually believe or how they live. I have not had the opportunity to enter deeply into people's private lives, so what follows rests more on ideas and published material than on close personal observation. I remember that *The Glory and the Dream* gives a particularly vivid account of these changes.

One fact is clear. Western attitudes towards sex are very different from what they were in the eighteenth century, or even before the two world wars.

Traditional European culture placed great importance on women's chastity and respectable behaviour. There were strict expectations about how women should act, and old European customs were particularly demanding.

Those attitudes have changed enormously. As Elizabeth Janeway and others have argued, sex need not be understood only as physical pleasure or only as a sign of a deep, lasting union between two people. Traditionally, when sex was defined in these ways, religion, morality and society set firm limits on where it belonged.

In medieval Europe, religious life encouraged people to restrain their desires and devote themselves to God. *The Decameron* offers a glimpse of the longings people experienced beneath those restraints. Sexual activity belonged to the body, while religious devotion was supposed to raise the soul above bodily needs. The faithful were expected to control their instincts.

These deeply rooted beliefs shaped Western attitudes for centuries. After the Second World War, they began to change rapidly.

Americans sometimes call the older outlook the rigid sexual standards of the Victorian age. Under those standards, sex belonged within marriage, prostitution was condemned, pornography was a sign of moral decline, and homosexuality was treated as evil. All of these ideas have been challenged. That does not mean they have disappeared; many people still hold traditional views.

Some figures from the period illustrate the scale of change. According to statistics attributed to the Department of Commerce, unmarried couples living together accounted for fifteen percent in 1980. That year, 660,000 children were born outside marriage. In the early 1980s, one estimate put the number of people working in prostitution at roughly two million, including 600,000 girls aged eighteen or younger. The pornography business was said to earn about four billion dollars a year.

Homosexual people were also gaining greater public recognition. In some places, according to the accounts I encountered, same-sex couples could officially register a marriage and live together. People marched in public to demand legal rights.

Television and films show the change in a more immediate way: sexual scenes appear so often that they can begin to seem routine. Of course, the finest films are not great because they contain such scenes. Their artistic achievements lie elsewhere.

What do Americans themselves think about all this? A book called *Sexual Values: Opposing Viewpoints*, by Bruno Leone and M. Teresa O'Neill, offers a useful way into the debate. It brings together sharply different views on several questions.

**Is sex outside marriage acceptable?**

Some people believe that the main purpose of sex is pleasure. From that point of view, whether someone is married matters less than whether the experience is enjoyable. Others insist that sex should involve love, and that love means a commitment to a particular person. Still others look at the question mainly in scientific or practical terms.

Supporters of greater sexual freedom say it can relieve tension, help people understand themselves, provide experience, help them choose a marriage partner, reduce jealousy, save time, weaken discrimination between men and women, or reduce demand for prostitution. Critics emphasize the need for trust, personal understanding and a good relationship between partners. They also argue that guilt and emotional difficulties can make sex harmful or unhappy.

The argument extends to government. One side says that regulating private sexual behaviour is an intrusion into people's personal lives. The other says that some rules can help families grow stronger.

**Should children receive sex education?**

Sex education is widespread in American schools, but it remains controversial. Critics fear that explaining sex to children will encourage them to experiment, leading to more teenage pregnancies. Supporters argue that young people need knowledge if they are to behave responsibly. They point to figures suggesting that, in a given year, around ten percent of girls aged fifteen to nineteen become pregnant.

In a society where sexual material is already so visible, young people will hear about sex whether adults want them to or not. In that sense, schools are being pushed into teaching the subject. A third position holds that parents should have the right to decide, by voting or other means, what kind of sex education their children receive—or whether they receive any at all.

**Should homosexuality be accepted?**

The answers range from strong opposition to full support. Some conservatives argue that homosexuality harms society and weakens traditional ideas about masculinity and family life. Some even distinguish between relationships between women and relationships between men, claiming the latter to be more damaging because they believe they threaten the family.

Others reject these claims entirely. They argue that homosexuality is not an illness, that same-sex relationships bring people together and that gay people contribute to society. Some believe that such relationships should remain private. Others insist that people have every right to live openly, without being forced to hide. For them, this is a question of basic human rights.

**Is pornography harmful?**

In 1968, President Nixon established a commission to examine the issue. Eight members studied adult magazines and films and reached different conclusions. The majority report argued that such material was not inherently harmful. It suggested that it could help adults understand one another and that the public would not accept broad legal bans.

A minority report took the opposite view. It argued that pornography damaged public morality, family attitudes, culture and the values by which people live.

Those who favour bans link pornography to sexual violence, problems among young people and the breakdown of families. Those who oppose bans say it may provide a harmless outlet for people's feelings and that freedom should include the right to make personal choices about such material. The question of whether pornography encourages violence is also fiercely disputed.

**Should prostitution be a crime?**

Here, too, the disagreement is direct. Some argue that selling sexual services must be treated as criminal behaviour. Others say it should not be a crime at all. Neither side is easily persuaded.

All these arguments belong to the wider sexual revolution. Questions that previous generations believed they had settled are now being asked again. The old certainties have given way to disagreement.

American culture has become unusually willing to tolerate different ways of life. That is one of its strengths, and perhaps also one of its weaknesses. A society this open can make room for some of humanity's finest achievements. It can also make room for things many people consider deeply harmful.

The sexual revolution has had a particular effect on the young. Many parents raising children today came of age during the upheavals of the 1960s. They are generally more relaxed about sex than earlier generations were.

For a high-school student to have a boyfriend or girlfriend is perfectly ordinary. It is even more common among older students. Young people often think about sexual pleasure separately from marriage or starting a family. Their parents have largely grown used to this distinction, and some explain contraception and responsible sexual behaviour to their children in the hope of preventing unwanted pregnancies.

A professor told me that her children did not need her advice. Whenever she tried to explain something, they told her they had learned it three years earlier. Sexual relationships among university students are also common and often casual.

To someone from the East, some of these attitudes remain difficult to accept.

What, then, does *sexual liberation* actually mean to the people who support it?

A document called the *New Charter of Sexual Rights and Responsibilities*, issued in February 1976, gives an especially clear statement of the idea. It argues that bodily pleasure has a value equal to moral concerns, and that traditional religious and social beliefs have too often treated pleasure as sinful. Such beliefs, it says, damage human relationships. The charter points to research claiming that denying people bodily pleasure, especially while they are growing up, can contribute to broken families, child abuse, crime, violence, alcoholism and other harmful behaviour. It declares physical pleasure within human relationships to be an important part of life.

That is the revolution in a nutshell: a change from fearing bodily pleasure to actively seeking it.

In one sense, American sexual freedom seems to have gone almost as far as it can. Adult magazines reveal practically every part of the male and female body. There is little left to expose without turning to an anatomy lesson. Large colour photographs show the body in minute detail, including the sexual organs. Many states allow these magazines to be sold openly, and adult films show sexual acts directly.

In such an environment, it is hardly surprising that public arguments become heated.

The greatest concern is the younger generation. Schools have introduced courses to help students understand a world in which sex is so visible. There is no shortage of handbooks for teenagers or for parents, and libraries carry them in large numbers. Students learn about the male and female body, the changes of puberty, contraception and other practical matters. Such education is widespread.

Yet sexual liberation has not erased everyone's personal standards. One friend told me that Americans are not nearly as casual about sex as films suggest. He certainly took the matter seriously.

Political life offers evidence of the limits. An affair outside marriage can still become a scandal. Gary Hart, who had hoped to run for president in 1988, was forced out of the race after reports of such a relationship. Similar issues can cause trouble in schools and other institutions. Dan Quayle, the Republican vice-presidential candidate, was caught up in rumours of this kind, though the particular allegation came to nothing.

So the moral rules that help hold American society together have not vanished. They continue to exert real influence.

Why, then, did the great shift towards sexual freedom take place after the 1960s rather than earlier? Was it connected to the extraordinary growth of material wealth?

I think there must be a connection.

Freud argued that civilized life requires people to restrain their instincts. Herbert Marcuse called this *necessary repression*: the limits people accept so that organized society can exist. But once a society becomes wealthy enough that most people no longer have to devote all their energies to surviving and struggling against nature, those instincts may find more room for expression.

Rising prosperity can therefore bring a revolution in moral attitudes. And this creates a new problem for government and society. Once the struggle to produce enough food, housing and other necessities becomes less urgent, more attention turns to how people choose to behave.

Any society that reaches a certain level of wealth may eventually face this question.

Marcuse saw sexual liberation as one way human beings might free themselves from the pressures of capitalism's enormous industrial system. He gave it a political meaning. So far, though, that political meaning is not particularly obvious to me.

What I can say is that the social importance of sexual liberation is not simply that individuals enjoy more pleasure. It is that people's beliefs about right and wrong are changing. And changing beliefs have enormous consequences for how society is organized.

Sexual liberation has also brought new problems. When sex was tightly restricted, frustration and conflict over those restrictions created difficulties for families and society. Greater freedom has reduced some of those old tensions. In that respect, families and public institutions have been relieved of part of their burden.

But they have taken on another burden instead.

Maintaining an old set of rules becomes harder the longer those rules have been losing their hold. Maintaining a new set of rules is difficult for a different reason: it takes time before people become accustomed to them. The older the order, the harder it may be to preserve. The newer the order, the harder it may be to make it work.

That is a real dilemma. Sexual liberation provides one of the clearest examples. America is already living with the new problems it has created, and I expect those problems to become more visible in the years ahead.

## 8. Lonely Hearts

A recent book called *The Lonely American* has caused quite a stir. Its central argument is that loneliness has become a serious part of life in the United States. It affects people of every kind: single and married, young and old.

Loneliness is more than an unpleasant private feeling. People need affection, companionship and some sense that they belong. When those needs go unmet, they may feel lost, anxious, restless, unhappy or painfully alone. These feelings can change the way they act, and that means they also affect the wider society. What begins in individual hearts can become a problem for families, communities and government.

But are Americans really so lonely?

Not everybody thinks so. Some insist that Americans are not lonely at all, and many probably do not feel lonely themselves. We certainly cannot say that every American suffers from it. But there are enough lonely people to make the question worth asking.

**Children can be lonely, even at home.**

There are many possible reasons. Large numbers of children live with only one parent, often because of divorce or because their mother was not married when they were born. In some of these homes, the child may lack the warmth and attention they need. Over time, that absence can become a deep sense of being alone.

Yet children who live with both parents are not necessarily protected from loneliness. If their mother and father spend most of their time working, they may have little energy left for the children.

American parents also place great importance on teaching independence. Children are often given their own rooms while still very young and encouraged to manage on their own. There are clear advantages to this. It can help a child grow in confidence and learn to be self-reliant.

But mixed with other difficulties, it may also encourage isolation. I would not claim that raising children this way must produce lonely adults. Still, the impression it leaves on a child's mind is not necessarily one of warmth and togetherness. Psychologists tell us that what happens in childhood can leave marks that last throughout life.

**Older people often feel loneliness much more sharply.**

A child who has always known solitude may hardly recognize it as loneliness. An older person who remembers a fuller family life may find the emptiness much harder to bear.

Loneliness among the elderly has become a serious problem in America. Adult children usually live apart from their parents and have their own affairs to attend to. They may come to visit during holidays, but such visits can be brief and hurried. In everyday life, they rarely interfere with their parents' decisions. They regard those decisions as private—and, to be candid, may not wish to become involved.

Many older Americans live in retirement or nursing homes. Moving into such a home at a certain age has become a familiar part of life.

The film *On Golden Pond*, starring Henry Fonda and winner of Academy Awards, captures this loneliness beautifully. It shows an elderly couple facing the sadness of growing old, often with humour. The story struck a chord with a great many people.

Older people also form organizations to help one another and relieve their isolation. There are even groups that arrange funeral and burial matters for elderly members. The very existence of such organizations tells us something about the problem.

**People between childhood and old age can be lonely too.**

Work might seem an obvious place to find company, but it often does not provide the kind of human connection people need. Much work is organized around practical tasks and fixed goals. People may spend their days together without exchanging much affection or sharing anything personal.

Of course, some workplaces are warm and friendly. But close relationships are not easy to build when everyone is concerned mainly with their own responsibilities. Americans' strong sense of personal independence can make the difficulty greater.

People also change jobs frequently. If someone works in one place for only three or five days before moving on, there is hardly time to form a friendship.

Marriage might offer protection from loneliness, but not always. Some husbands and wives struggle to communicate what they really feel. Divorce makes the problem more obvious. America has an exceptionally high divorce rate, and many people find themselves living alone after their marriages end.

Loneliness takes many forms. Sometimes it barely touches the surface of a person's life. Sometimes it goes much deeper.

It is one of the troubles that seem to grow alongside modern prosperity.

A friend once told me a story that brought this home with painful clarity.

It concerned a woman approaching fifty. She had grown up in a wealthy family and married a university professor, but the marriage had ended in divorce. Her children were now adults, living independent lives elsewhere.

She lived alone in a house. She had no steady job and earned what she could from occasional work. She was deeply unhappy and often drank too much. When she was drunk, she would lose control, crying and making a scene.

Her behaviour seemed to come from a profound sense of loneliness.

But what struck me most was not simply her situation. It was the way the people around her responded.

The friend who told me the story was staying in her house. He was worried about her and wanted to help.

He telephoned her children.

Their response was that this was their mother's own business. They had no right to interfere. If she thought she needed to go to hospital or call the police, that was a decision she would have to make for herself. Her children could not make it for her.

In effect, they were saying that there was nothing they could do.

He then called her closest friend.

The answer was much the same. She felt powerless to intervene. Even the closest friendship, it seemed, did not give one person the right to take charge of another person's life.

And so my friend was left watching the woman drink, cry, and fall apart, unable to do anything.

Her closest friend offered a revealing explanation.

"She wants me to be her mother," she said. "She wants someone she can behave like a child with."

Yet even this friend seemed, deep down, to look down on her.

Why had the woman reached such a state?

The answer, I think, lay in a loneliness that went far beyond the ordinary experience of being alone.

Her isolation was not simply something she felt inside herself. It was also reflected in the way the people around her treated her.

She had become cut off from society, and society had, in its own way, cut itself off from her.

This was the predicament of one lonely American.

But what makes Americans lonely?

There is no simple answer.

We could point to the economic system. Money occupies such a central place in American life that it can shape the way people relate to one another. Human relationships become tangled up with financial concerns, while warmth and personal affection are pushed aside.

Even a small act of kindness can come to involve money.

We could also point to individualism, one of America's most deeply held values.

Individualism means that people should be free to make their own choices, live their own lives, and take responsibility for themselves.

But that freedom has another side.

The more people insist on standing alone, the easier it becomes for them to find themselves alone.

Tocqueville argued that the American Revolution helped individualism take root. Yet individualism also brings with it the possibility of isolation. Perhaps a society cannot fully embrace one without accepting some measure of the other.

The extraordinary importance Americans place on personal independence may therefore help explain why so many struggle with loneliness.

Or we could look at the way Americans guard their privacy.

People do not want others intruding into their lives. They want to decide for themselves what happens within their own homes, families, and personal relationships.

That is understandable. Privacy is a form of freedom.

But protecting privacy can also mean protecting loneliness.

Perhaps Americans actually want a certain amount of solitude. There is nothing wrong with that. Most people need time and space for themselves.

The question is whether they are prepared for something more.

What happens when the solitude they choose becomes an isolation they can no longer bear?

A society may create ways of life that give everyone the personal space they want. Yet when millions of people live by those same rules, the combined result can be something nobody intended: a loneliness far greater than any individual wished for.

And once that happens, what began as a private difficulty becomes a problem for society as a whole.

Loneliness is not simply an accident that happens to particular individuals. It can be produced by the very way a society is organized.

As long as that way of life remains unchanged, finding a lasting solution will be difficult.

One of the hardest problems any society faces is dealing with the troubles created by its own success.

The author of *Megatrends* argues that the more advanced technology becomes, the more people long for genuine human warmth and connection.

There is an important lesson here.

Whenever a society pursues one goal too single-mindedly, it risks creating new problems that eventually demand public attention.

Loneliness has already become one of those problems in America, and it is likely to remain so.

Look closely at many government policies, and beneath the practical concerns you will often find something deeper: the quiet, persistent effects of loneliness on millions of individual lives.

## 9. Tomorrow's World

The American way of thinking is a complicated mixture of habits, and some of them seem to contradict one another.

Americans are widely known for being practical. Ever since thinkers such as William James and Charles Peirce helped develop the philosophy of *pragmatism*, this has seemed one of the country's defining qualities. In everyday language, pragmatism means judging ideas by what they actually achieve rather than by how impressive they sound.

You can see this habit almost everywhere. Americans want things to be useful. They value results they can see and measure. They have little patience for beautiful-sounding ideals that never lead anywhere.

In contemporary America, this practical spirit often takes a very simple form: money. If something can *make money*, people are inclined to regard it as worthwhile. The ability to earn a profit has become, for many, the clearest test of whether an idea is useful.

There are still people who work for political, moral, religious, social or philosophical ideals. But a great many of those employed in these fields have more ordinary reasons for doing what they do.

During an election, people work long hours for the two main parties. Some are passionate believers. Others are simply paid campaign workers. They have been hired to do a job, so they do it.

Government offices are filled with people whose everyday responsibilities rarely involve grand thoughts about the ideals of the American nation. They work because it is their job, not necessarily because they feel personally responsible for the moral future of the country.

Staff in welfare organizations help poor people and people with disabilities. Many are undoubtedly compassionate, but not everyone is motivated chiefly by sympathy. They are also employees, paid to provide a service.

University professors write books, lecture with great enthusiasm, criticize government and call for reform. Yet many regard this as professional work rather than a personal mission to save society. They are doing their jobs. Only some feel that the concerns of their household, their country and the entire world must all become their own concerns.

I could give many more examples. The point is not that Americans have no ideals. It is that practical rewards shape a remarkable amount of what people do, especially in a society where money carries such weight.

And yet another powerful tendency runs through America. I will call it *thinking about the future*.

This is the curious part. In a society so concerned with immediate reward, people are also willing to devote extraordinary resources to things that bring no clear benefit today but might matter greatly tomorrow. The future may mean a new machine, a scientific idea, a different political situation or some other possibility that does not yet exist.

At first glance, these two habits point in opposite directions. One asks, "What use is this now?" The other asks, "What might it become years from now?"

Both are deeply rooted in American life.

We can see this concern for the future in several areas.

**Politics.** During the 1988 presidential election, George Bush and Michael Dukakis spoke repeatedly about America in the twenty-first century. Would the country keep its place in the world? How should it respond to Japan and Europe, or to the possibility of growing competition from China? What about the Soviet Union and the countries of Eastern Europe?

Both parties tried to persuade voters that their policies would ensure that the twenty-first century remained an American century, just as they believed the twentieth had been. Some commentators were already predicting a Japanese century or even a Chinese century. Bush repeatedly insisted that the coming century would belong to America.

That promise was politically powerful.

Richard Nixon's 1987 book *1999: Victory Without War* returned again and again to the same questions: what threats would America face, how could it respond, and how could it succeed in the world of the future? It quickly became a bestseller.

That tells us something important. It is not only politicians who care about the future. The public cares too. Otherwise, promising to defend America's place in the next century would hardly be such a useful way of winning votes.

**The military.** America spends heavily on research into future wars, weapons and strategy. The Star Wars defence programme, with costs running into tens of billions of dollars, is one striking example. To many people it sounds almost fantastical. Yet its supporters are determined to make it real because they fear a new round of competition in advanced weapons.

The programme has provoked fierce debate. Beneath the arguments lies a conflict between immediate practicality and long-term ambition. Why spend so much on something uncertain? And how can the country afford not to prepare for possible dangers ahead?

In military planning, the second argument often wins. Arms manufacturers have reason to support it too: preparing for tomorrow can bring them profits today, even if they are thoroughly practical businesspeople.

The newly unveiled B-2 bomber expresses the same outlook. So do many American responses to unrest and conflict in Nicaragua, the Philippines and the Middle East. They may be presented as reactions to immediate events, but they are often driven by plans for the future.

**Science and technology.** Here the concern with tomorrow is even stronger. Large sums go into basic research in physics, astronomy, biology, chemistry and other fields whose discoveries may have little immediate use.

People often say that science departments in American universities have more money than any others. Much of it comes from foundations and organizations outside the universities. Why are those institutions willing to invest so heavily? Because they believe that knowledge discovered now may be enormously valuable later.

America has recently announced plans for a gigantic particle collider, eighty kilometres in length. The project sounds almost unbelievable. But the government is prepared to support it because of what it may teach scientists in years to come.

Computer companies likewise spend heavily on new generations of machines, long before today's machines have become useless.

Environmental protection is another example. The issue has attracted exceptional public attention, and concern about it now has a strong influence on government policy. Without a willingness to think ahead, it would be much harder for people to agree that environmental damage must be prevented before its full effects are felt.

**Cities.** Long-term thinking can be seen in the way American towns and cities are planned, whether in a small place such as Iowa City or a huge metropolis such as New York.

An important test of any proposed road, subway, building or housing development is what will happen to it in the decades ahead. Will it help a city grow, or become an obstacle to further development?

Consider New York's World Trade Center. Its towers rise impressively above the city, but the world beneath them is extraordinary too.
