The vast underground areas connect to subways and trains serving New York and neighbouring states. The designers were looking beyond the immediate needs of the buildings to the future movement of people around the city.

Many American towns also contain houses built fifty or even a hundred years ago. They are still comfortable and attractive, especially after renovation. These old homes remain a valuable resource.

Imagine if every generation had built houses that became unusable after five or twenty years. America could never have accumulated its present stock of housing. Good planning means building for people who have not yet been born.

**Education.** Americans understand that the future will belong to today's children and young people. Can they meet the challenges that await them? Will they have the knowledge and confidence to deal with a changing world?

The phrase "a paradise for children" reflects the attention American society gives to preparing the young. Universities play an equally important role. America's present achievements and world position cannot be separated from its system of higher education.

Education is one of the strongest forces that keeps a society going and allows it to develop. Whatever a country's political system, it cannot remain strong for long if it fails to educate the next generation. American governments and universities devote enormous effort to this task.

So it would be a mistake to describe American society as practical and interested in nothing else. Practical thinking is certainly powerful. But the pull of the future is also unmistakable.

Why has it become so strong? And how can it live alongside America's desire for immediate results?

The practical spirit has deep historical roots. Early European settlers arrived in unfamiliar surroundings and had to build lives for themselves. They did not have vast inherited wealth or the luxury of spending all their time debating philosophy. They needed food, shelter and a way to survive. Useful action mattered more than impressive theories. As the country expanded, this habit was passed from one generation to the next.

Thinking about the future grew especially powerful for a different reason. During the twentieth century, America became increasingly involved in world affairs and rose to a leading position. After the Second World War, it stood as the strongest power.

Decades of success taught Americans to think, "We are number one." Keeping that position became a shared national ambition. But in a world of intense competition, a country that wishes to stay ahead must keep looking forward. It cannot simply rely on what it has already achieved.

The link between national pride and long-term ambition is difficult to measure, but I suspect it is strong. A leading country that fails to prepare for others to catch up will eventually be overtaken.

There may also be a more personal reason.

For many Americans, the future is uncertain. Few jobs, marriages, educational opportunities or social arrangements come with a lifetime guarantee. The government rarely promises to take care of a person forever. Even social insurance generally depends on having worked and paid into the system.

People cannot assume that their families, parents, friends, employers or government will always protect them. They must hope that tomorrow's society will offer opportunities at least as good as today's. They want a future in which they can still earn a living and, if possible, live better than before.

That uncertainty gives individuals a powerful reason to care about the future, quite apart from any national ambition.

Now the relationship between the two American habits becomes clearer. Sometimes immediate interests and long-term plans collide. At other times they support one another. People may invest in the future because it seems the most practical thing to do. Or they may make a practical choice today because they are trying to protect tomorrow.

Many of America's public arguments arise from these competing ways of thinking, even when the deeper disagreement is not immediately obvious.

America's development cannot be separated from its concern for what lies ahead. People may look to the future for very different reasons. Some want their country to dominate the world. Others want to improve the world. Others are concerned mainly with their own lives.

But whatever the motive, the habit of thinking beyond the present becomes a force in its own right. It gives a society energy that no other kind of motivation can quite replace.

In the broadest sense, every country needs to think seriously about the world to come and the place it hopes to have in it. Only then can its people find a path forward—and learn to see beyond the limits of the world they already know.

## 10. People Are Still People

It is often said that relationships between people are simpler in America. Life is not so tangled up in personal connections. People get by on their abilities, knowledge and money, rather than on whom they know or which family they come from. This is commonly seen as one of the great differences between Eastern and Western societies.

In Eastern cultures, especially those shaped by Confucian traditions, great importance is placed on social position, duties, manners and family ties. Western cultures tend to put more weight on ability, law, practical advantage and recognized authority. As a broad comparison—and only as a broad comparison—there is some truth in it.

But it would be a mistake to imagine that America always works this way. People in Eastern societies do not rely on personal connections in every situation. Nor do people in the West always manage without them.

America's wealth makes some problems easier to solve, without complicated arrangements or favours. A person can spend the day doing exhausting work—repairing roads, cleaning buildings or carrying heavy loads—and then use the wages to buy anything sold in a shop. They can stay in an expensive hotel or eat in a fine restaurant. If they have the money, they can enjoy many of the things that wealthy people enjoy.

This is part of what Herbert Marcuse meant by the integration of society. He was critical of it. In his view, workers were being absorbed into the capitalist system and losing sight of the exploitation and oppression they still experienced. The differences between classes seemed less obvious, even though they had not disappeared.

Such a development is possible because goods are so plentiful. An abundant market can ease tensions within society. In many countries, after all, one of the strongest sources of conflict is a shortage of things people need. Most ordinary people do not spend their days wrestling with grand theories. They think first about food, clothing, housing and getting around. A society can often do more to bring people together by meeting those everyday needs than by offering the most sophisticated explanation of a political ideal.

American relationships may be less complicated, but they are often less close as well. Much of American life is organized around protecting one's private world. People generally dislike others entering too far into their affairs, and hesitate to intrude on someone else's. This too is quite different from many Eastern traditions.

Even the meaning of *friend* is different. An American may call someone a friend because they met at school, in church, at a sports ground or at work. The relationship may be friendly without being particularly deep. The kind of friendship in which two people understand and trust each other completely is harder to find.

In America, it is often easy to become acquainted and difficult to become truly close. In China, it may be harder to establish a friendship at first, but easier to build a lasting bond once it begins.

Mobility is one reason. About one in five American families, according to the figure I heard, moves home each year. Material prosperity makes that freedom possible. But moving so often has two effects. People must learn to make new friends quickly. At the same time, frequent moves make it harder to build relationships that cannot easily be broken.

Our strongest friendships outside the family often begin in childhood or youth. After starting a family, people have less time, money and energy to devote to new friendships. Cultural habits may make things harder too. A child who grows up in one place for ten or fifteen years has a chance to share a whole period of life with the same companions. At university, the pattern changes.

American universities do not organize students into fixed classes in the way Chinese schools do. You attend one class for one subject, another class for another, and meet an entirely different group the following term. In this sense, university students can be rather lonely. Good housing, though comfortable, also gives people separate spaces. It can isolate them, on campus and beyond.

Long experience of living independently may leave Americans unsure whether to approach a stranger. They can seem reserved or passive, especially with visitors from other countries. Of course, *foreigner* is an awkward category in America: Americans themselves often cannot tell who was born in the country and who was not. Usually it is the visitor who is conscious of being foreign. But when a clearly identified visitor takes the initiative, Americans are often extremely warm.

Some people believe these habits are good for development. Society is not weighed down by an elaborate network of personal obligations, so people can make their way through their own abilities. Others believe the opposite: relationships lack depth, people are too detached from one another, and social life loses some of its warmth.

Those are broad judgments. Look more closely, however, and personal relationships in America turn out to matter enormously. People are still people. How could any society continue without relationships between them?

In many ways, what I found resembled the patterns familiar in Eastern societies. One professor put it neatly: in America, you can live very well without connections, but you can live even better if you have them. That strikes me as a fairly balanced assessment.

The Chinese writer Bo Yang wrote about *The Ugly Chinaman*. But the weaknesses he described are not peculiar to one nation. They appear in different degrees wherever human beings live, shaped by culture and economic circumstances.

A few examples may help explain what I mean.

Personal connections are particularly visible in politics. One of the arguments surrounding the 1988 presidential election concerned Dan Quayle, the Republican candidate for vice president. Critics said he was not especially outstanding and had not risen through his own efforts. His family's income was reportedly two million dollars a year. Questions were also raised about his poor academic record and his circumstances during military recruitment.

Whatever the details, the point was plain: family background still matters in America. People without influential families can succeed, but those who come from them may find the road much easier. The Kennedy family is an obvious example.

After George Bush became president, he could appoint the people who would fill a great many political offices. Old friends of his received powerful, well-paid positions, even when parts of the public were unimpressed by their qualifications. I will return to these political matters elsewhere; there is no need to pursue them here.

An academic example is just as revealing. A professor who studied Africa told me that proper research required visiting countries and regions across East Africa. To do that, he had to be away from the department instead of teaching his usual classes. His department chair gave him permission.

Other professors were jealous. They said little openly, but complained behind his back: here was a colleague who could travel abroad, avoid teaching and still collect his salary. What a wonderful arrangement for him—and what a poor bargain for everyone else!

I asked how he had secured the chair's agreement. He told me they were on excellent terms. In fact, he was arranging for the chair to visit an African country, enjoy a distinguished reception and give some lectures along the way. This professor was a thoroughly decent man. He also understood how to get things done.

At a university on the East Coast, a friend described equally complicated relations within a department. Professors competed, schemed and exchanged both open criticism and private attacks. Sometimes arguments broke out during classes.

One professor, for example, dismissed an entire school of thought as lacking theory and relying on vague, empty data. Another professor who happened to be present immediately demanded that he explain himself more precisely. The apparent academic dispute was really an expression of a conflict between the two men. Scholars working in the same field may see each other as rivals. Of course, other colleagues get along perfectly well.

Outside universities, relationships can matter even more. How well a worker gets on with an employer or a supervisor can directly affect that person's position and future. If personal ties carry weight even in higher education, why would we expect other workplaces to be different?

Within universities, full professors who hold permanent appointments often exercise considerable power. Other teachers have little say by comparison. Senior professors can influence who is promoted and who is allowed to stay. That division of power shapes relationships throughout the department. It is hardly surprising that younger or less secure teachers want to remain on good terms with those above them. I am not saying that such relationships are necessarily bad. I am pointing out that they exist.

One scene I witnessed made the point especially vivid. A delegation from Japanese business and academic institutions came to speak at an American university. By then, Japan's wealth was famous around the world. At the reception, many Americans treated the visitors with exaggerated politeness.

A woman who held an office in the local government clasped a Japanese visitor's hand for several minutes, smiling broadly and repeatedly praising his tie. Watching her made me uncomfortable. Her real purpose was to attract Japanese investment to her area. Many Americans look down on the Japanese, yet the way they behave toward Japanese visitors may be quite different from what they privately think.

Examples of this kind are everywhere. What differs from Eastern societies is the way personal connections operate, and how much depends on them.

The American system gives people an important escape: if things become intolerable, they can leave. In an economy built largely around private employers, moving from one organization to another is ordinary. In societies where that option is much more limited, personal relationships may hold far greater power over a person's life.

America's institutions, material abundance and cultural habits therefore give relationships a different place than they have in Eastern societies. But to claim that Americans do not value connections, or do not need them, would be to imagine an ideal world rather than the real one.

People are still people. They remember kindness and repay it. They remember injuries too, and may seek revenge. Human beings have feelings and needs of their own.

How far can modernization really change human nature? That is a question worth studying carefully.

# Chapter 4: The Many Ways Society Runs Itself

## 1. The Invisible Hand

Western economies and societies are often said to operate through the *invisible hand*: prices, markets and other economic forces guide activity without anyone directing every step. Since Adam Smith set out the principles of economic freedom, Western countries have seen the rise of Keynesian policies, which give governments a larger economic role, and of the welfare state. Yet the invisible hand remains a central principle of how their societies work.

At times, the invisible hand has given way to a *visible hand*—the government directly managing economic and social affairs. Western governments now regulate economic activity much more than they did in the past. Even so, markets still do most of the day-to-day work of bringing people and businesses together.

Both approaches involve government, but in different ways. The choice affects what government is expected to do and how much work it must carry. In general, relying on markets lightens that burden; directing economic life adds to it. In modern society, politics and economics are so closely connected that changing the way an economy is controlled also changes the shape and duties of government.

Under the invisible-hand approach, government controls the economy mainly from a distance, through taxes, finance and law. Individual businesses must then find their own way in the vast sea of the marketplace. That brings them into competition with one another.

A business that wants to survive, let alone make large profits, must win customers away from its rivals. It cannot simply turn to a government office for help. Legally and in the way they are organized, government and private business belong to quite different worlds. A firm that fails to compete or is badly managed may go bankrupt.

Sometimes the cause is inside the firm: poor decisions or wasteful management. Sometimes it comes from outside: rivals improve, customers change their minds or the wider circumstances shift. To avoid defeat, every business must keep improving.

The most important contest, in the end, is over the quality of what customers receive. Whether a company sells an object or provides a service, what matters is how well it meets people's needs. Without that quality, customers leave. Without customers, there is no market. Without a market, there are no profits, and the business disappears. The pressure is relentless. It drives firms to improve what they offer and to fight for their share of the market.

None of this means that government does nothing. It uses laws, taxes and financial rules to shape business activity, but usually does not run businesses itself. Government plays a part when a company opens, while it operates and when it closes.

There is a useful difference between *regulating* an activity and *managing* it. Regulation broadly tells people what they must not do. Management tells them what to do and how to do it. That is a simplified distinction, of course. But it captures an important idea. The government's task is not to take every business by the hand and tell it how to operate. It is to keep businesses within the rules and encourage their activities to serve society's wider development.

This arrangement is far from perfect. If the invisible hand takes the lead, government may have little influence over some parts of economic life—or may find intervention very difficult. Officials can also use that as an excuse to avoid responsibility and stand by while things go wrong.

Consider a small but revealing example: the travel agencies in San Francisco's Chinatown.

I cannot say exactly how many there are. Perhaps dozens. Walk through Chinatown and the signs seem to be everywhere. Open a local Chinese-language newspaper and the pages are full of travel advertisements. Most of these agencies are run by Chinese businesspeople. They arrange group tours and airline tickets, sell gifts, and offer services that allow customers to pay abroad and collect goods in China, among other things.

Their services are broadly similar, so they compete fiercely for the same customers.

Here are just some of the names I came across: Pan-Wei Travel, Lida Travel, Huiya Travel Agency, Your Travel Agency, Shunfeng Travel Agency, Cathay Travel, Golden Mountain Travel Agency, Lishan Travel Consultants, Feifei Travel Company, Jiehang, Taikoo Express, Dongfeng Company, Seven Seas Travel, Flying Boat Travel Agency, Lizhi Travel Agency, Wing On Travel and Enhua Travel Agency.

That is only a small selection, and it covers only Chinatown, where the businesses mainly serve overseas Chinese and Chinese-speaking customers. Add the agencies elsewhere in San Francisco and the scale of the competition becomes obvious.

These firms book trips throughout the world—to Europe, Asia and Latin America—as well as tours within the United States. To attract customers, each must find something new or appealing to offer: cheaper tickets, better routes, hotel bookings, car rentals or some other useful service.

Prices vary. For a flight from San Francisco through Japan to Shanghai, one agency might ask $480, another $460 and another $440. Competition of that kind puts pressure on agencies to offer sensible prices. If an agency charges too much, people take their business elsewhere.

But it cannot simply charge less than everyone else, regardless of its costs. If the fare brings in no profit, the firm may fail. The contest is therefore not just about prices. It is also about management, the abilities of employees and the way a business is run. That is how the invisible hand works in practice.

Competition also pushes agencies to look after their customers. I arranged my return trip from San Francisco to Shanghai through one of them. I telephoned to ask about the price, and the employee booked the flight for me on a computer. When I came to collect the ticket a few days later, everything was ready. She asked whether I wanted to change the date or flight; when I said no, she issued the ticket.

She also reminded me that international flights had to be confirmed seventy-two hours before departure. The agency would take care of the San Francisco departure, she said, but I must remember to confirm the connection in Japan myself. That was beyond their reach.

Then she handed me the forms for a Japanese visa. I knew little about the application process, so she immediately called the visa section of the Japanese Consulate General in San Francisco and asked for the details. A few days later the agency called again: the flight from Tokyo to Shanghai had been cancelled. Which alternative date would suit me? It was unusually attentive service.

Another professor from our department was leaving San Francisco for Japan, Hong Kong and Singapore. His arrangements were more complicated. The flights had to connect properly, and the visas had to be ready in time for each destination. After considering his options, he chose an agency that worked out the cheapest combination and helped with the visas. A Hong Kong visa had to be arranged in Los Angeles, an inconvenient journey to make personally, so the agency handled it for him.

Customers can ask for all sorts of help, and these businesses try hard to meet their requests. They often anticipate needs before a customer has thought of them.

Why are they so attentive? The answer is simple. Good service helps a business live; poor service can put it out of business. In a crowded market, companies have little choice.

But the invisible hand needs the right conditions before it can work well. One is an abundant supply of resources and services. Imagine all these travel agencies competing for customers when only one or two airlines could supply tickets. The airlines could charge monopoly prices and would have little reason to persuade agencies to sell for them.

When airlines compete too, travel agencies have more choices. Each part of the economy must face some competition if the whole system is to work this way. If the wider economy is not competitive, a few businesses cannot create meaningful competition on their own. The invisible hand will be too weak and the visible hand too strong.

A competitive economy is built over time. It cannot appear overnight. America took hundreds of years to develop these arrangements.

Their importance extends far beyond commerce. They free the political and administrative system from much of the burden of running daily life. Transport, communications, food, housing, ordinary goods and many other services are supplied and organized largely by private firms competing with one another. Government works mainly through indirect oversight.

So the invisible hand reduces government's workload, while the visible hand increases it.

The invisible hand has weaknesses of its own, precisely because no one can see it clearly. People often discover where its influence lies only after events have moved a long way. By the time everyone realizes what has happened, serious problems may already have developed in the economy or society. The experience of Western countries, especially since the First World War, provides many examples of this pattern.

What interests me here is less the conduct of business than the effect different economic arrangements have on government.

Think of politics and economics as railway engines and carriages. When markets lead, the political engine does not have to pull a long train of economic carriages. The economy has an engine of its own. Each engine carries a lighter load and may move faster. But the two engines are harder to keep moving together in the same direction.

When government directly manages the economy, the political engine pulls that entire train. Its load is much heavier, and it may be slower. On the other hand, because there is only one engine, it may be easier to keep all the carriages moving together along a planned route.

That comparison concerns the practical organization of economic life. Once we bring wider political questions into it, we might reach different conclusions.

No one can understand a country's politics without understanding its economy. Political institutions do not float above the economic world in which they operate. America's political system—its structure and its character—makes little sense unless we also examine the economic arrangements beneath it.

## 2. How Money Runs Society

There is a Chinese saying: “Money can even make the devil work for you.” In America, a classic capitalist society, it seems we could add another line: money can make the gods work for you too.

You can feel the power of the green dollar bill almost everywhere. Because goods and services are so widely bought and sold, money gives people a kind of equality: if something is available for purchase and you have enough money, you can generally buy it. No separate social rank needs to give you permission.

The pursuit of money is one of the visible forces driving the American economy and society forward. Many people work primarily to earn it. A professor's teaching is a job; it need not be inspired by a deep urge to save the nation. Politicians are not all idealists either. Work done for a party or an election campaign may also have financial rewards in view. In a society that measures so much through money, living without it is nearly impossible. To a considerable extent, American life is organized around it.

Once we recognize this, an interesting pattern appears. I think of it as *money running society*.

Money is not merely something people earn and spend. It is one of the main tools through which society is organized. People manage money, and then use systems built around money to manage one another. Because money is so important, enormous effort goes into inventing methods, rules and technologies for handling it.

The result is a vast system of control and coordination. Capital uses it to obtain profits from other people's work—what Marx called *surplus value*. Much of this system operates outside government and apart from the political institutions. It handles complicated tasks that officials do not need to direct themselves.

Marx observed that in a capitalist market, dealings appear to be between things, but beneath the surface they are relationships between people. It is a powerful observation. What looks like the management of money is often the management of human behaviour. These arrangements shape how people think, feel and act, without requiring a government order.

For the individual, using money in America can be wonderfully convenient. Behind that convenience, however, lies a complicated machinery of control.

Banks are at the centre of it. They are everywhere, and there are many different kinds; state-chartered banks are often among the stronger ones. Banking does far more than keep money safe. Employers, for example, can send wages directly into each worker's account. They do not need to hand everyone bundles of cash and leave each employee to deposit the money later. The bank handles the whole process.

Carrying large amounts of cash is both inconvenient and unsafe. Imagine taking a bag stuffed with banknotes to buy a refrigerator or a colour television. This helps explain the extraordinary importance of cheques in America and other Western countries.

Banks supply different types of cheque books. Once you open an account, you can choose the kind you want. The cheques are printed with your name and address, usually within two weeks. In principle, cheques can be used across much of America and in many other places.

To pay for something, you write the name of the person or business receiving the money, enter the amount and sign. The shop or hotel sends the cheque through its bank, and the sum is taken from your account. Each month, your bank sends you a statement and returns the cheques it has processed. You do not have to concern yourself with handing over actual cash.

There are risks. A lost cheque book can create trouble, because the signature is the main barrier to someone else's use. People may also write cheques after their accounts have run out of money. That is why businesses sometimes ask for identification or record a telephone number before accepting a cheque.

Travellers can use travellers' cheques, which are no use to someone else until properly signed. Even these can be lost and must then be reported missing.

Banks also offer cards that let customers withdraw money from machines. The card is plastic, with a magnetic strip on the back. Each customer has a secret number. Enter it into the computerized machine, and the machine recognizes the account and supplies the requested cash. Many machines serve a local area, though some belong to wider national systems. They can also accept deposits: enter the number, put the money into a supplied envelope and feed it into the deposit slot.

Credit cards are another everyday convenience. They can be used in much of the Western world and in many other countries. Some Chinese shops and hotels already accept them.

A credit card must be applied for. Like a cash card, it is made of plastic and has a magnetic strip, together with the holder's name and identifying number. In shops equipped with the necessary technology, the card can be checked electronically. The screen tells the seller whether it is valid, whether it has been reported lost and whether the account is in good standing.

A machine then presses the card's name and number onto a payment slip, which the customer signs. No cash changes hands. The credit card company sends a bill each month, and the customer must pay it by the required date.

There are many cards. Visa, MasterCard, American Express and Discover are among the best known. They have different levels, often based on how much a person may spend at one time—perhaps $1,500, perhaps considerably more.

Credit cards are certainly convenient. But everyone who uses one also accepts a degree of control by the company that issues it.

Paying the company may be as simple as writing a cheque at home and mailing it. Mail and cheques allow people to settle a remarkable range of affairs without visiting anyone in person. You can send a cheque to pay taxes, order goods, have a roll of film developed or repay a personal debt. The banks on the two sides take care of transferring the money.

Borrowing is another part of this system. Banks lend to businesses for investment, something that also happens in socialist countries. American businesses, however, are privately owned, so business borrowing ultimately involves private owners. Banks also lend directly to individuals buying houses or cars.

Personal borrowers repay part of the loan each month, together with interest. Banks therefore earn interest from households as well as businesses, a notable difference from the Chinese experience of the time. A loan ties the individual to the bank. If payments stop, the bank can claim property to cover the debt, with the law and the police behind its rights.

Debt collection does not end at the bank. Suppose a person receives expensive treatment in a hospital and cannot pay. The hospital may hand the case to a business devoted entirely to collecting debts. Such firms understand the relevant law. They may arrange a repayment plan, with regular monthly payments. If that fails, they can take the matter to court.

For all their conveniences, banks and financial companies maintain strict controls. Anyone who wants their services must submit to those controls.

Look at what can be required when applying for a credit card. An applicant may have to give their name, date of birth, Social Security number, address and home telephone number. The form may ask how long they have lived locally, whether they own or rent their home, their previous addresses and how long they stayed at each one. It may demand the names and addresses of close relatives and friends who live elsewhere, and even the mother's name before marriage.

Then come the details of employment: the employer's name and address, the applicant's work, position, length of service and telephone number. Someone employed for less than three years may have to identify earlier employers. The form can ask about annual pay, other income and its sources, a contact who can confirm the information, and bank accounts, including account numbers and branch details.

The company checks these answers before issuing the card. In the process, a system designed to handle money creates a remarkably detailed file about a human being. Loan applications demand similar information.

Whole businesses specialize in assessing a person's reliability with money. These are *credit-reporting companies*. They are private firms, subject to law, that collect personal records, especially financial ones rather than political information.

Some operate across the country; others are local. Almost anyone who has dealt with a bank or financial institution has a record somewhere. Banks and lenders can ask these companies about their customers. A loan officer may use a computer terminal to look up how reliably an applicant has paid previous bills. A poor record can lead to a refusal.

The network is extremely powerful. Banks supply information about their customers, while credit-reporting companies may charge cooperating banks less for access to their files. The exchange makes the system stronger. Anyone who uses money—and who in modern society can avoid it?—comes within its reach.

Good credit, on the other hand, opens the door to many useful services. The same system that controls people also rewards those who comply. Being shut out can make ordinary life very difficult.

And the rewards can extend far beyond banking. American Express, for example, advertises more than 1,400 travel offices in a thousand cities around the world, ready to help customers plan journeys or deal with emergencies.

These arrangements show how capital can exercise enormous influence over ordinary people, and through them over society as a whole. Most of the institutions are private, yet together they manage a substantial part of everyday life.

Their power rests less on orders or force than on people's willingness—or practical need—to take part. Some join gladly; others are pushed toward the system by the circumstances of life. Either way, they come forward and accept its rules.

Americans dislike being told what to do, but they are very fond of money. The logic of money leads them to accept a great deal of supervision. Because the system touches the basic conditions of living, that supervision is effective.

Perhaps giving money this power to organize people is one important way of reducing the burden on government. Money connects individuals with individuals and organizations with organizations. It is astonishingly energetic. Government remains outside much of the process and influences it through law. Meanwhile, the people and firms inside the system pursue their own interests and, in doing so, help keep it operating.

Simple bank deposits alone would probably never have created so extensive a network. The decisive question is how far money becomes the centre of a system on which people depend. Americans prize freedom and do not want to depend on other institutions. Yet they rely deeply on this one.

No society can be effectively run without some central set of arrangements on which almost everyone depends. Different stages of development may give that role to different institutions. In a highly developed economy, perhaps the main organizing force gradually shifts from political institutions to economic ones.

## 3. Putting People to the Test

Modern societies need common standards. America has developed them to a considerable degree. Many kinds of work and activity are governed by clear requirements. This is particularly striking when it comes to judging what people know and what they can do.

America is a society where people move around a great deal. There is no fixed household-registration system like China's, and people do not carry a single official employment file from job to job. Their education may have taken place in very different institutions and under different systems.

So how can a university or an employer know whether someone has the knowledge and skills needed for a particular place or position? One important answer is a common examination. Schools use test results in deciding whom to admit, and employers often consult them when deciding whom to hire. Chinese students are familiar with examples such as the TOEFL and GRE.

The interesting point is that these exams are often not designed and administered by government departments. Private, specialized organizations do much of the work. A government may ask one of them to create an examination, or specify that a person must pass a particular test before taking up a profession. Lawyers, for instance, have to pass the appropriate examinations.

The organizations set the questions, arrange the tests and analyze the results. Seen from the standpoint of how society is run, they perform an important public function without being government offices.

One such organization is ACT, the American College Testing Program. I visited its offices and spoke with its director, Richard L. Ferguson.

ACT is independent and does not operate for profit. It serves students, parents, high schools and colleges, as well as professional associations and government agencies. Founded in 1959, it became widely known in the 1960s for organizing and designing standardized college-entrance examinations.

Since then, its work has expanded into education, admissions, vocational training and professional qualifications. Its basic purpose is to provide information that can help raise the quality of education and improve decisions about students and careers.

ACT's headquarters are in Iowa City. It also has offices in Washington, D.C., California, Denver, Atlanta, Austin and Albany. Each office covers several states, creating a network across the country.

Its leadership has two parts. There is the ACT Corporation, made up of representatives elected by the states. They meet once a year to review policy. Then there is a fifteen-member board of directors, which meets every three months to review the organization's work. The board appoints the chief executive, who is responsible for everyday operations.

ACT offers a remarkable range of services. A few are worth examining.

**The ACT Assessment Program.** This is a broad programme serving universities, high schools and students hoping to enter college. Students complete educational and personal-background questionnaires, four examinations—English, mathematics, social studies and natural sciences—and a survey of their interests.

ACT analyzes the responses and produces reports for students, schools and colleges. These can help students choose careers, schools plan courses and universities decide whom to admit.

According to the figures I was given, roughly a million junior-high and high-school students take part each year. The results are used by some 2,900 universities, colleges, academic organizations and state education systems. In other words, a private nationwide body arranges an important part of college admissions testing, rather than a government ministry doing it directly. The educational institutions recognize its results as authoritative.

**Educational Opportunity Service.** ACT uses computers to examine information about the students who take its tests: their educational plans, interests, strengths and aims.

**Enrollment Information Service.** This has two main parts, concerned with analyzing the market for education and the likely returns from different approaches to student enrollment. Both draw on data collected through the examinations.

**Professional testing programmes.** ACT has more than forty standardized tests that can help people without formal specialist training earn proof of what they have learned through work or independent study. The aim is to show that they have reached a college-level standard in a particular field. Subjects include arts, science, business, education and nursing. Organizations use the results to award qualifications and design courses.

There are many other services: career planning, computer analysis of data and financial analysis, among them.

Another major part of ACT's business is work for professional associations. American professional bodies are generally independent of government. But if they want the public to trust them, they must show that their members meet high standards. A professional association with no reputation for quality will have little influence.

No one may order these groups to examine their members. Often they decide to do so themselves—and ask a specialist organization to carry out the tests. ACT provides such services to about eighty bodies, including unions and government agencies.

The tests help decide whether someone has the knowledge needed for a profession, a scholarship, a certificate, a licence or admission to further training.

To give an idea of the range, ACT has worked with organizations such as:

- The American Academy of Ophthalmology
- The American Academy of Otolaryngology
- The Association of American Business Schools
- The American Association of Teachers of German
- The American Society for Microbiology
- The American Council on Education
- The American Dental Association
- The Association of American Medical Colleges
- The Emergency Nurses Association
- The Law School Admission Council
- The National Pharmacology Association
- The United States Department of Defense

And many others.

ACT is not the only organization of its kind. Several other large companies work nationwide in the same field. They compete for business and must protect their reputations. Each spends considerable effort asking how examinations can be improved, how candidates can be assessed more fairly and how suitable standards can be chosen.

They must also prevent questions and answer papers from leaking. Their reputation is essential. If a company were found cheating, it could lose customers and collapse.

Even a non-profit organization has salaries and operating expenses to pay. ACT, for example, employs nearly five hundred full-time and hourly workers. At American wage levels, that is a large bill. Government does not simply pay the organization's costs. It must meet its own expenses through the services it provides.

Organizations like ACT exist because society needs consistent standards. Without that demand, they would have no reason to exist. A modern society is also, to a considerable degree, a society of standardized procedures.

Two arrangements help these testing organizations succeed. First, government may decide that anyone entering a certain occupation must pass an examination administered by a named organization. Second, universities, professional associations and other independent bodies may impose their own requirements.

Anyone wanting to enter the relevant occupation, school or programme must meet those requirements. This has two results. Government avoids the cost of creating large examination departments of its own, while fees paid to independent organizations support the testing system. And common standards can be applied across society.

There is a further safeguard. Government need not remain loyal to one testing organization. If it doubts the quality of an examination, it can recognize a different company's test instead. Universities and professional bodies can make similar choices. Everyone has a reason to protect their own credibility, so each organization helps keep the others in check.

But none of this works unless the wider society truly wants common standards. That desire grows when individual organizations must take responsibility for their own success or failure.

If a school, business or association has no interest in the quality of its work—if it can rely on someone else to protect it, however badly it performs—why would it worry about choosing people carefully? Only when admitting or employing the wrong person can harm an organization's own interests does it have a strong reason to judge qualifications reliably.

The movement of people provides another powerful reason. In a highly mobile society, individuals need to prove their abilities wherever they go. Employers and institutions need dependable evidence of those abilities.

A certificate recognized only in one town may not be enough. Even a broad qualification such as a university degree may say too little about someone's ability to do a particular professional job.

The more freely people move, the greater the demand for widely accepted proof of what they know. To obtain that proof, people must meet standards recognized by others. Both sides reinforce one another, pushing society toward more common measures of individual ability.

In a sense, a qualification is like the label or certificate attached to a product. When people buy goods, they look at their purpose, quality and size. As the importance and complexity of human skills grow, some common way of identifying them becomes almost unavoidable.

## 4. A Culture of Rules

Every society needs rules to guide relationships between people. Its political system and culture may differ from those of other societies, but it cannot do without some way of settling disagreements and limiting behaviour.

Rules come in layers. A constitution sits at the highest level, followed by laws and then more detailed regulations. They are one means of dealing with conflict. But their importance varies from culture to culture, as does the form they take. Some societies write them into law. Others express them through inherited customs and taboos, or through religious duties.

America is a society overflowing with rules. Almost every area of life is governed by them. How did such a strong *culture of rules* develop? A few examples may help us understand.

The greatest American rule is the Constitution. But here the Constitution is much more than the foundation of politics and law. It is also a central part of the culture.

I remember an American professor who came to lecture at Fudan University. A Chinese student asked him, “America has no fixed ideology. What do Americans use to bring their thinking together?”

It was a very Chinese question. The professor was thoroughly puzzled. After someone explained what the student meant, he thought for a moment and replied, “The Constitution.”

The answer seemed almost beside the point. A constitution is law, while an ideology is a set of beliefs. The latter does not carry the force of law. Yet his response showed how deeply the American Constitution is embedded in people's thinking.

The Constitution expresses much of the dominant political outlook of the Western world and gives those beliefs legal force. Over more than two hundred years, it has entered American public consciousness as the highest authority to which disputes can be brought. Americans do not think about it every moment of the day. But when serious conflicts arise, they tend to see it as the one shared foundation everyone can trust. I discussed this earlier in “Two Hundred Years of the Constitution.”

Of course, the Constitution is far too broad to guide every ordinary action. Everyday life is governed by countless more specific rules. To someone brought up in an Eastern culture, many of them may seem excessively strict. Living under them can feel rather less comfortable and free than one might expect.

**Alcohol.** Strict rules govern its sale and use. People below the legal age are not permitted to drink. Shops and restaurants must obtain a government licence before selling alcohol. In Iowa City, supermarkets do not sell it before noon on Sundays. The explanation I heard was that people ought to be in church at that time. That is not necessarily the government's intention; it reflects religious influence and tradition.

**Food.** Here too the rules are demanding. There are limits on the chemical substances permitted in fruit and vegetables, and standards that meat must meet before being sold. Food that fails these tests cannot go on the market. Breaking the rules can bring severe penalties and heavy fines.

**Cars.** Their numbers are astonishing. Nearly every household has one, and many have two. Managing all this traffic is a major problem, so strict rules govern how people drive and park.

A person must pass a test to get a driving licence. At high speeds, ignoring the rules can easily mean a serious crash or death. Moving traffic presents one set of problems; stopped cars present another. Parking is a major concern. Signs along the streets tell drivers where they may leave their cars and where they may not.

Park illegally and you may find a ticket on your windscreen. Refuse to pay and a court may summon you, or the unpaid fine may cause trouble when you next renew your licence.

Highways may be called *freeways* or expressways, but they have strict speed limits, generally between fifty and sixty-five miles an hour. Police officers patrol the roads or wait for speeding drivers. Breaking the limit can bring a fine of several dozen dollars.

Drivers and front-seat passengers must also wear seat belts, though the precise rules vary by state. In some places the requirement applies on highways, while in others it also covers ordinary city streets.

**Taxes.** The rules are perhaps more detailed than anywhere else. That may be because taxes provide the government's income. The instructions can take an ordinary person a very long time to study. Almost every part of the process is covered. Mistakes or refusal to comply may bring harsh penalties, so Americans have to be extremely careful in their dealings with the tax authorities.

If someone cannot or will not pay property taxes, the government can seize and sell the property to recover what is owed. Everyone is required to report their income. In this area, Americans may be less free than in almost any other. I will return to taxation later, and also to the way public decisions are conducted in the open.

**Education.** Schools and universities have detailed rules covering admission, examinations, tuition fees, course credits, the duties of teaching assistants, health insurance and much else. These rules govern the institution's work. A student who breaks them may have to leave.

Factories, government departments, schools, military institutions and other organizations each have their own detailed requirements.

Yet human beings do not naturally enjoy following rules. We would often prefer to live as we please, without restrictions. Rules impose limits on that desire. Nevertheless, people in America generally follow many of them, even when nobody appears to be watching.

Parking spaces reserved for people with disabilities are usually left alone by other drivers. Where parking meters stand beside the road, most people put coins in and respect the time limit. Traffic rules are widely observed. Even with no cars coming, pedestrians often wait for the crossing signal. People avoid smoking where signs forbid it.

On buses, passengers commonly pay their fares by dropping coins into a machine rather than handing them to a conductor. Sometimes the driver is not even present when passengers board at the end of a route; people still put in their fares. There is an even stronger commitment to obeying the law itself.

Why does this happen?

The historian H. S. Commager offered an intriguing explanation. He suggested that Americans take being ordered to follow a rule almost as an insult or a challenge. That fits the human desire to be free from restraint. Yet he also observed something apparently contradictory about their attitude toward law.

At first glance, Americans may seem ready to break rules. Minor violations are common. At the same time, they hold the law in unusually high regard.

Commager argued that Americans see their nation's history as a story of resistance to inherited traditions and authority. Regulations can stand for tradition, and discipline can stand for authority. People are suspicious of both. Yet they have learned from the country's experience that government and the Constitution can work, and that attacks on their basic authority cannot simply be tolerated.

The result is a respect for law alongside an instinct to challenge restrictions. And because so many detailed modern rules are passed by legislatures and given the force of law, the two impulses can be partly brought together.

America can fairly be called a culture of rules, even though crime and lawbreaking remain serious and worrying problems. Rules shape the broad movement of society. They must have acquired this power for a reason. I cannot claim to know the whole answer, but there are other possibilities worth considering alongside Commager's.

One is America's history as a relatively young nation. Unlike many European and Eastern societies, it did not develop the same elaborate structure of inherited social ranks.

In older societies, traditions and authority often become closely attached to particular people and groups. Some groups embody custom; others represent recognized power. They hold established places in society, and when interests clash, they may step in to settle disputes.

In other words, the power to bring people together and resolve conflicts is often carried by *people*: those who possess learning, wealth, status, political influence or inherited standing. Where that network is very strong, written rules may find it difficult to become the main source of authority. Unless the relationships and institutions built around particular people change, the law may have only a limited independent role.

When America emerged as a new country, it lacked many of those deeply established personal structures. But every society needs some way of settling conflicts. Rules made and recognized in common were therefore able to take a firmer place.

People could pursue their own interests under those rules while trusting that others would have to respect them as well. If nobody obeyed common rules, and there were no accepted personal authorities to step in instead, everyone's interests would become less secure.

Shared rules were meant to protect general interests rather than simply favour one powerful person. That helped lay the foundation of America's culture of law.

Of course, it does not follow that the rules have always protected every member of society equally. Once a legal system develops, different interests shape it, and some people benefit more than others. My point is narrower: American history helped create conditions in which common rules could take the place occupied elsewhere by influential people and established social groups.

A second reason may be the extraordinary growth of science and technology since the Second World War. Modern inventions have entered ordinary homes and forced people to learn new habits. Without rules, many of these devices would be useless—or dangerous.

Cars travel faster, so safe driving requires agreed procedures. Highway networks grow more complicated, so drivers must observe the system or traffic breaks down.

And consider the machines people use at home: televisions, video recorders, cameras, washing machines, rice cookers, microwave ovens, stereos, vacuum cleaners, lawnmowers, egg beaters, food slicers, letter openers, can openers, massagers, electric toothbrushes, electric razors, video games and countless others. Using them properly demands more detailed knowledge than working with simple traditional tools.

The same is true of equipment at work: computers, laser printers, fax machines and photocopiers. Ignore their operating instructions and they may simply refuse to work.

In public places, people use payphones, newspaper machines, automatic ticket sellers, vending machines, photo booths, currency-changing machines, stamp machines and bank machines that accept deposits and dispense cash. Every one has its own procedure. Fail to follow it and the machine will not do what you want.

Financial life adds another set of procedures—for cheques, credit cards, loans and shares.

Americans grow up surrounded by systems that quietly insist on correct behaviour. To take part in everyday life, they must learn how each one works. Following rules becomes more than a practical habit; it becomes part of the culture.

A small contrast makes this vivid. Americans often cook by carefully following a recipe, measuring the ingredients with special tools and trying not to get the quantities wrong. Chinese cooks are much more likely to judge by eye, throwing in a handful of this and a pinch of that. As technology supplies ever more specialized products, people who use them become accustomed to following instructions.

Rules do not always help society develop. Whether they help or hinder depends on the circumstances. But whenever people live together and their interests clash, they need some way to settle disputes. Rules agreed in common may be imperfect, yet they are among the forms of coordination most likely to win broad acceptance.

When the habit of following rules joins with the careful, consistent methods encouraged by modern technology, its influence spreads even further. Most people do not learn complicated scientific reasoning directly. But once the products of science become part of ordinary life, their habits of order and precision begin to shape people almost without their noticing.

## 5. The Tax System

America's tax system is extraordinarily complicated. It is hard to make sense of, and many Americans do not understand it very well either. A professor of political science told me that he once found himself unable to understand a tax document sent out by the government. He telephoned the responsible office and said, "I have a doctorate in political science, and I still can't understand your forms."

Taxes are a major political issue. One of the central arguments in a presidential election is whether taxes should go up or down. Newspapers reported that Bush had persuaded many voters that Dukakis's policies would raise their taxes. That was one reason Bush had pulled so far ahead in the opinion polls.

People care deeply about taxes because they want to know how the government is spending their money. Indeed, taxation lies at the very foundation of the United States. One important cause of the War of Independence was the British government's heavy taxation of its thirteen North American colonies and the resistance it provoked. In a modern country, taxes are never merely an economic matter. They are political through and through.

The system is too complicated to describe in full, so I will concentrate on the main points.

How do people pay taxes? And how much do they pay? These are important questions about the way a society is run.

Some taxes are collected by the federal government, through the Treasury Department. Each state also collects its own taxes. A family that owns a home must pay property tax as well. Then there is sales tax. When people buy things in a shop, tax is added to the listed price of most goods at the cash register. It is generally between four and eight percent. Those are the main kinds of tax an ordinary person encounters.

Consider federal income tax. Toward the end of each year, the federal tax authorities send out documents and forms. Individuals must fill them in carefully and return them, together with any tax they still owe, before April 15. The forms are far from straightforward. People must report all their income: wages, dividends from shares, payments for writing, and anything else they have earned.

Could someone simply leave something out?

That would be asking for trouble. It would also be against the law. Serious breaches of tax law can lead to prison. Sometimes people make careless mistakes and fail to report income, with painful consequences. Once an omission has been found, it may be difficult to prove whether it was accidental or deliberate.

The government already knows about much of the money its citizens receive. America has a highly developed computer system, and every citizen has a Social Security number. Companies and other organizations report payments under the recipient's number. Wages, fees for writing and dividends can therefore be checked against the information the person puts on the form. The authorities need only consult their computer records to see whether the figures agree. That does not mean everyone pays what they owe. Tax evasion is widespread enough, and quite a few senior officials have been caught up in scandals over it.

In most jobs, the employer has already taken a substantial amount of federal and state income tax out of a worker's wages. The money goes straight to the relevant governments; the worker never receives it. The employer also deducts Social Security contributions, recorded under the employee's number to support their retirement in later years. By the time people collect their pay, a sizeable share has already been deducted.

Even deciding what belongs on a tax form involves many rules. Are you married? Do you have children? Do your parents depend on your income? Different circumstances are treated differently, and each taxpayer has to give the details.

There are also careful distinctions between money that must be reported and money that need not be. The list of income the source describes as exempt from reporting includes disability payments to veterans, some payments to workers with disabilities, benefits for children, gifts and inherited property, certain welfare payments, compensation for disasters, and some scholarships. But research and teaching grants, wages, profits from selling personal property, prizes, and money earned outside the United States must be reported. Failure to do so breaks the law.

Reporting income is only the beginning. Certain amounts can be left out of the final taxable total under the rules—for example, some retirement income and deductions available to older people, blind people or people with children. There are further, complicated provisions for investments, official duties and office expenses. Everything else is subject to tax.

Because the government has already taken money from people's pay, they must work out whether it has taken too much or too little. That is where the painstaking calculation begins.

In addition to the allowances already mentioned, some medical and dental costs can be deducted. So can certain state and local taxes, under arrangements between governments; property taxes; some interest payments and borrowing costs; charitable gifts and donations; and other expenses. The source also mentions a deduction described as 65 percent of personal interest income. It distinguishes allowable donations from items such as political contributions, membership fees for clubs and learned societies, and blood donations, which it says cannot be deducted.

Some costs of doing a job can also qualify: safety equipment, small tools, necessary work clothes, protective clothing, medical examinations, fees paid to professional organizations, and subscriptions to professional journals. Every category comes with its own conditions.

Only after sorting through all this paperwork can the taxpayer calculate the income on which federal tax is due. The booklet sent out by the government includes tables showing how much tax is owed at different income levels. Compare that figure with what has already been withheld from wages, and the result becomes clear: either the government returns the excess, or the taxpayer pays the difference.

How much does an ordinary household actually pay? Let us look at some examples.

After the permitted deductions, the remaining income is taxable. There are four categories: single people; married couples filing one return together; married people filing separately; and heads of households. For many American families, the annual amount left after deductions falls somewhere between $30,000 and $50,000. Here are the figures in the 1988 tax tables, as given in the source:

| Taxable income (US dollars) | Single | Married, filing jointly | Married, filing separately | Head of household |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| $30,950–$31,000 | $6,695 | $4,913 | $7,386 | $5,583 |
| $40,000–$40,050 | $9,863 | $7,447 | $10,554 | $8,259 |
| $49,950–$50,000 | $13,345 | $10,581 | $14,210 | $11,741 |

These are substantial amounts of federal tax. The figures make it easier to see why taxes loom so large in American politics.

Federal taxes are not the end of the story. People must pay state income tax as well. The procedure is similar. The state sends out forms; taxpayers report their income and calculate deductions. Some medical and dental bills, taxes already paid, interest payments and donations may qualify. The rules broadly resemble those for federal tax. According to the example I saw, a person with an annual taxable income between $29,950 and $30,000 would owe $1,837 in state tax. As with federal tax, much of it has already been withheld from wages, so the final return determines whether the person owes more or receives a refund.

Homeowners also pay property tax to local government. In Iowa City, the bill on a house is around $1,000 a year. Local authorities assess the value of the property and use the tax to maintain roads and public facilities, including libraries.

Put these three taxes together, and a married couple filing jointly with about $30,000 of taxable income pays more than $7,000 in tax—roughly a quarter of that amount.

America's tax system is highly developed, and on the whole it works well. Tax laws are strict. People who conceal income, underreport it or deliberately avoid paying what they owe face penalties that vary with the seriousness of the offence.

Dealing with taxes is an important annual task for Americans. They keep piles of bills, receipts and other documents to support their claims for deductions. By the end of a year, the paperwork can be enormous. It is also highly private. A family's tax return reveals its true income, which is one of its most closely guarded secrets.

Taxation is a central issue in American politics, as it is in other capitalist countries. In socialist countries, people do not generally pay a regular, universal personal income tax of this kind. Does the Western income-tax system serve a wider social or political purpose? That question deserves careful thought.

At least one belief is firmly planted in the American mind: **the money the government spends is money the people have paid in taxes.** Americans therefore want officials to spend it properly. They pay attention to government policies and decisions because those decisions concern their own money.

Without a visible system of personal taxation, people may feel less concerned about what their government proposes. In reality, government spending affects them just as much, but the connection is less obvious. A tax system makes that connection direct. It is one of the foundations of Western political life.

When people hand a share of their earnings to the government, they feel entitled—and obliged—to watch how the money is used. Officials must continually explain their policies, not least to show that they are not wasting taxpayers' money. Voters pay attention to elections because the choice of officeholders affects their immediate interests. It matters who becomes president, senator, representative, governor or local official.

Through taxation, a kind of agreement takes shape between public officials and the people. The public gives the government money and expects something in return. The system encourages a sense of responsibility, even if that responsibility comes about partly because people have been made to pay. Governments in countries without this kind of tax system still need money, of course; it simply comes through other channels. But people there may feel a much weaker personal responsibility for watching what government does.

Taxes have another important function: they bind the whole society into a closely connected system.

With computers and Social Security numbers, the tax authorities link individuals, employers, organizations and government offices in a vast network. A person may be free of direct supervision by almost everyone else, yet still be answerable to the tax authorities. One sign of that power is the requirement to disclose a family's most private financial information. Small wonder that some people say a tax official is more frightening than a security official.

## 6. When Technology Organizes People

America is a country where individualism is held in the highest regard. Individual freedom seems almost sacred, beyond the reach of any outside power. That is the impression most visitors receive, and plenty of Americans themselves worry that the country has taken individualism too far. People from other cultures, after spending some time with Americans, may find some of their strongly individual habits obvious—and occasionally hard to accept.

But there is a puzzle here. If personal freedom matters so much, and everyone guards the privacy of their own life, what makes more than two hundred million people work together? How do they each take their place in the enormous machine we call society, allowing it to keep running?

A large society needs cooperation and collective action. Yet the values Americans celebrate put individuals and their private lives first. How can both things be true?

Many forces help bring them together: political institutions, laws, shared interests, money and the need to earn a living. But one other force is easily overlooked.

It is science and technology.

Technological progress works in two directions at once.

First, advanced technology divides work into more and more specialized tasks. Every person is given a clearly defined job. In a curious way, this supports the ideal of individual independence. Computers and automated systems allow people to do their particular work without depending directly on another person or having to take orders from a superior. Instead, they depend on machines and follow the instructions those machines impose.

This is one aspect of what the philosopher Herbert Marcuse described as the way people can become estranged from their own work and lives.

Modern technology cannot be put to work without breaking a complicated production process into a great many steps. Each step requires somebody who knows how to perform it. The task may seem tiny, but a person without the right training cannot easily take over. The more advanced the technology, the more this is true.

In the seventeenth century, a craftsman might make an entire product from beginning to end. Today the situation is completely different. Technology has made people's roles more specialized, their places in society more clearly defined, and their sense of individual responsibility stronger.

Where production relies mainly on traditional methods, a person's role may be less clear. Workers can often exchange tasks more easily. That flexibility has advantages, but it can also leave organizations less stable and make confusion more likely. This is not just an economic question. It has political consequences as well. If a large part of a society does not know what its members are supposed to do, the society may find itself in a state of lasting disorder.

Technology's second effect seems to point in the opposite direction. The same inventions that give each person a separate task also demand extremely close organization.

A production process is broken into countless little parts. Yet in the end, all those parts must be brought back together and made to work as a whole. That need creates an immensely powerful system of coordination. It does not depend on political authority or the law, but it can be just as commanding.

Technology persuades people to follow strict rules because the rules have a practical logic. A machine will not work simply because someone wishes it to. Each person must do the right thing, in the right order, at the right time. In this way, technology also places limits on the ideal of unrestricted personal freedom.

Think of a space shuttle. How many people must work on it? How carefully must their tasks be fitted together? Or consider IBM, which has to organize thousands of individuals into one working enterprise. The orders involved are not political commands. They are what the economist John Kenneth Galbraith called the *imperatives of technology*—demands created by the work itself.

One of the strange features of modern life is that people will accept instructions from technology far more readily than they accept instructions from politicians or the law. Before taking medicine, most people will study the directions carefully and follow them with great caution. But it is much harder to persuade people to obey political instructions or to accept a political appeal to end racial discrimination.

A society with advanced technology is generally highly organized. Where technology is less developed, coordination often remains looser and daily activities less orderly. In America, much of the organizing work is carried out by companies that bring technological methods into practice. The same contrast can be seen in less prosperous countries: a technologically advanced factory or workplace may be far more tightly organized than the society around it.

Scholars have examined this process. In *The New Industrial State*, Galbraith argued that the spread of technology brings six important changes:

1. More time passes between the start of a task and its completion.
2. Production requires more capital.
3. More time and money must be committed in advance to particular work.
4. Specialized workers become essential.
5. Organizations must become larger and more tightly coordinated.
6. The long commitments of time and money, the need for large organizations, and the conditions of modern markets make planning necessary.

Galbraith's central point is that technology inevitably makes society more organized. Large economic and technical systems are increasingly run by professional managers, who make the decisions needed to keep the whole operation working. He called this managerial system the *technostructure*.

In other words, the growth of technology creates groups of people whose job is to organize and direct others. They exercise a kind of authority without being politicians. And that can greatly reduce the burden placed on government. One of government's tasks is to coordinate people's behaviour. If another system already keeps much of that behaviour within an orderly framework, governing the wider society becomes easier.

John Naisbitt, in *Megatrends: The New Directions Transforming Our Lives*, described a similar process, but put more emphasis on what it feels like to be human. He used the expressions *high tech* and *high touch*: the more technology enters our lives, the more we may crave direct human contact.

He also spoke of *forced technology*, the feeling that people are increasingly compelled to live within technical systems they did not choose. People become tied to the workings of machines and procedures. Some resist electronic funds transfers or automatic systems for retrieving information. Naisbitt saw that resistance as a response to the loss of warmth and direct contact with other people.

There is, I think, another side to the matter. Technology has developed its power to organize people to such a degree that it may no longer stop at work or the practical details of daily life. It can reach into people's thoughts and their most private affairs.

In America, almost nothing seems strong enough to break through the barriers people put around their personal independence and privacy. Yet technology can cross those barriers. It also promises material rewards, and that gives people another reason to accept it. Their desire for greater human contact is, in part, a protest against being managed by technology. The protest itself shows how powerful technology has become.

This result was almost entirely unexpected. There is an old Chinese saying about a person who carefully plants flowers that refuse to grow, while a casually planted willow flourishes. Technology's power to manage people is something like that. Those who first championed technological progress were not setting out to build a new way of governing human behaviour. Yet the practical use of technology has become one of the strongest forces doing exactly that.

To a considerable extent, American society is organized by technical procedures. People obey technology more readily than they obey politics. Technological development divides society into many small but connected fields. Each person occupies one of them, and special skills are needed to enter. The education system is largely built around supplying those skills. Education, in turn, becomes part of the same process, producing ever more people and habits suited to a world organized by technology.

Radical thinkers criticize this as a loss of human freedom and independence. From the point of view of human experience, they have a case. But no society can do without technology, and technology brings these demands with it.

Choosing to develop, admire and apply technology is therefore not merely a decision about factories, economic growth or technical skill. It affects the way a society is organized and the way people live. That makes the questions we must ask about technological progress far more complicated than they first appear.

Everything brings benefits and costs. What matters is the historical situation in which a society makes its choices—and how it handles the problems those choices create.

## 7. Even Cats and Dogs Aren't Free

Earlier in this chapter, in "A Culture of Rules," I discussed the extraordinary number of rules and regulations in American society. There are several reasons for this.
