I suggested that people are more willing to follow rules when they are meant to apply equally to everyone, and that the spread of advanced technology also encourages a habit of doing things according to set procedures.

But there is another reason worth considering: the American national character.

I do not believe Americans are naturally mild people who dislike disagreement. They may look that way at first. People rarely make loud scenes in the street or quarrel in public vehicles. You do not often see fierce arguments at work either. Yet this calm surface is, to a large extent, the result of rules that have already settled how different interests should be handled.

Why are there so many rules in the first place? Because society needs them. And why does society need them? Because people disagree. Without disagreements, there would be much less reason to write rules.

Americans are, in fact, quite ready to argue over their rights. When the rules are unclear, disputes are quick to arise. Part of this tendency comes from the country's history. As settlers moved into vast stretches of land, people could claim property for themselves. Those who never asserted a claim, or refused to defend one, could end up with nothing. Western films are full of dramatic versions of these conflicts.

Modern individualism creates conflicts of its own. A society that gives great importance to the individual needs rules not only to settle arguments, but to keep one person's freedom from colliding with another's. It is therefore natural for the rules to grow into an elaborate system.

In cultures that put greater emphasis on the group, formal rules may be less clearly spelled out. Japanese people, for example, are much less likely to go to court than Americans, who seem ready to sue over almost anything.

This helps explain why American law reaches into so many parts of daily life. There are rules about land for building houses, where cars may be parked, clearing snow, raising children and caring for elderly parents.

To see just how detailed the rules can be, consider the regulations issued by one city for people who keep domestic animals.

The introduction says, in effect: "The law protects people, and it also protects pets. Although keeping an animal is regarded as an individual's right, some animals cause problems and must be controlled. These rules are intended to make the public healthier and safer. Citizens should read them and understand why both they and their animals must obey the law."

Animals almost seem to be given rights alongside human beings. Many Americans treat their pets as equal members of the family. Any rules about animals must take their owners' feelings seriously rather than simply brushing them aside. But feelings do not mean the pets are free to do as they please.

Here are some of the regulations:

- **Rabies vaccinations.** All domestic animals, cats included, must be vaccinated against rabies once they are over six months old. Wild animals can pass this deadly disease to pets, and pets can pass it to humans. Owners must keep proof of vaccination.
- **Leashes.** Animals of any age must be on a leash when outside their owners' house or land. Allowing a pet to roam freely breaks the rules. A lost animal can be impounded, and the owner fined.
- **Licences.** Animals over six months old must be licensed by the city. The owner must produce a rabies vaccination certificate. Fees vary, and the licence has to be renewed each year. People who have not applied by March 1 must pay another $15. All licences expire on December 31.
- **Fines and impounding.** Animal-control officers may confine wandering pets and charge their owners. For a dog, the fee is $10 for the first offence, $20 for the second, $30 for the third and $40 for the fourth, increasing in the same way thereafter. For a cat, the corresponding charges are $5, $10, $15 and $20. If an owner does not collect the animal within twenty-four hours, a daily charge is added: $8 for a dog and $5 for a cat. Owners must show the vaccination certificate and licence when collecting their pets. After four days, they lose their claim to the animal, which may be offered for adoption or put to sleep. Any citizen may claim a stray animal. If a complaint is made, the owner may have to go to court, where a judge decides the penalty. People can telephone the authorities at any time to report an animal that is breaking the rules.
- **Other offences.** These include abandoning a pet within the city; allowing it to bark or otherwise disturb people; tying it to equipment or fixtures in a public place; leaving it without suitable food or water; keeping it somewhere harmful to its health; and allowing it to interfere with other people's lives.

These are only the main points. The full regulations are more detailed still.

Why does a city need so many rules just to deal with cats and dogs? Because people's interests differ. Some Americans adore animals. Others cannot stand them. The regulations are meant to keep those very different feelings from turning into open conflict.

I saw a small example myself. A family's dog wandered onto a neighbour's lawn to play. The neighbour came out and shouted angrily. The dog's owner hurried outside and brought it home. Without rules everyone could refer to, scenes like this could easily grow into serious quarrels.

The regulations try to anticipate nearly every situation in which people might disagree. When an argument arises, people can look up the rule and see what should happen. Otherwise, every family member might have a different story about who was right, with the father, mother, daughter-in-law and son all insisting that their own version was the only fair one.

So, despite its reputation, American society is not quite as "free" as people sometimes imagine. People cannot simply do whatever they want. Even cats and dogs have their freedom restricted. Their owners become tied down as well, although they have chosen that responsibility for themselves.

Some people love dogs as though they were their own children, and there is an entire business built around pets: doghouses, carriers, clothes, food and almost anything else one can imagine. Yet the pets themselves live under all kinds of restrictions. If cats and dogs understood what was happening, they might march on Washington to demand "cats' rights" and "dogs' rights."

There is a larger lesson here.

As a society develops, differences in people's interests give rise to more disputes. More disputes create a greater need for ways to settle them. Different cultures can choose different approaches, but every society must keep looking for new areas of possible conflict and finding fair, workable ways to handle them.

Broadly speaking, there are two approaches. One relies on people themselves—personal judgment, relationships and negotiation. The other relies on established rules. Either can work. What matters is that each likely dispute has some accepted way of being resolved, and that the steps are clear enough to be followed again.

Every time a conflict occurs, society should aim to do more than settle that particular quarrel. It should learn from it and develop a procedure for dealing with similar cases in the future.

A modern society is, among other things, a society that has learned to settle its disagreements without falling into disorder.

# Chapter 5: Competing Political Forces

## 1. Government by Donkey and Elephant

The donkey is the symbol of the Democratic Party; the elephant represents the Republican Party. People sometimes describe American elections as a battle between the donkey and the elephant. In reality, both parties represent the classes that hold power in society, and between them they dominate politics. So we might just as well speak of government by donkey and elephant.

The two parties have an enormous influence on elections. Anyone who wants to understand American politics—and how the country is governed—must understand their role. Yet there is something strange about them. Although they seem to control politics between them, neither looks much like a political party in the usual sense.

They are more like loose collections of people. Compared with parties in most other countries, East or West, they can almost look like a disorganized crowd. What makes them so different?

**First, neither party has a formal system for joining.** There are registration procedures in some places, but there is no clearly defined body of party members in the strict sense. Voters decide for themselves which party they belong to. Someone can vote for a party's candidate and then regard themselves as a member of that party. Nobody needs to admit them, and nobody has to approve their claim. They can simply say, "I'm a Republican" or "I'm a Democrat."

Even the parties' leading figures may never have thought of themselves as formally "joining" anything. The doors are wide open. People drift in and out as freely as visitors to a fair.

**Second, neither party has a single, coherent political theory.** Democrats and Republicans disagree on many issues, but there are also divisions within each party. Both contain people on the left, the right and in the middle, and almost any opinion can be heard among their supporters. Senators and representatives elected under a party's name are free to take their own positions. They can even oppose proposals made by their own side.

**Third, neither party has a complete, binding programme.** Anyone searching for an authoritative set of instructions explaining exactly what the Democrats or Republicans intend to do will be disappointed. There are broadly two kinds of statements about their aims: the platform adopted at a national convention, which does not bind individual politicians, and the speeches of a presidential candidate or president.

Neither tells the public precisely what the party will do in every circumstance. At election time, the parties suggest reforms to domestic and foreign policy, but these are not fixed, long-term political programmes.

**Fourth, neither party has a tightly organized structure.** You hardly ever hear about local party branches meeting regularly or holding ordinary membership meetings. American parties come alive mainly at election time. Between elections, even their supposed members may barely think about belonging to them. It is like a country fair: people appear when the occasion arrives and then discover what needs doing.

The federal system makes close party organization even more difficult. States have their own authority, clearly separated from that of the national government. In practice, every state has something like its own Democratic Party and its own Republican Party. The national organization may have little idea what the local branches are doing during an election. In the end, what matters is whether the candidate carrying the party label wins.

So American parties are very different from the organizations normally meant by the word *party*. Yet we should not underestimate them. Sometimes their loose structure is the very source of their power.

They do have formal organizations. These simply tend to matter much less between elections.

There are permanent party bodies and temporary election organizations. At the lowest level is the *precinct committee*, often run by only one or two people. Town committees operate at a similar level. Above them are county committees, usually made up of people from the precincts. These are more important. Between county and state level there may also be a committee for a congressional district, responsible for putting forward candidates for Congress.

Next comes the state committee. Because the states have different laws, the way these committees are chosen varies widely. A state committee is a permanent party organization, responsible for publicity and for keeping the party strong within its state. Its chair is often a prominent political figure.

At the top sits the national committee, made up of representatives from the states. It is loose and has relatively little power. Neither party has anything resembling a tightly controlled central committee or an inner standing committee. The national chair helps run the election headquarters, promotes candidates and raises donations. The role is less that of a supreme leader than of a manager keeping things in order.

When elections approach, the parties also open campaign headquarters at national, state and county levels.

On paper, all this looks like a reasonably complete organization running from top to bottom. In practice, there is often little connection between the levels. Without a common theory, a binding programme or a fixed membership, what exactly is there to keep them closely tied together?

The national and state committees maintain some contact. Below that, the links become much weaker. National campaign headquarters cannot really direct every local office. Local organizers use the national party's name while pursuing their own plans. They are there to win an election, not to secure the sorts of personal rewards that might keep people attached to a party organization—better housing, cheaper goods or a handsome official car.

People who join a campaign know what they are there to do. That makes the system workable. If something might help the candidate win, local organizers are free to try it. The national committee usually sees little reason to interfere.

Indeed, both parties are delighted when someone else wants to use their name. Meet a few basic conditions, carry the right label, and much of what you do is up to you.

It reminds me of the chain businesses that can be found all over the country: McDonald's, Hardee's and Kentucky Fried Chicken. Each outlet sells products under a familiar name, but the people at the top do not control every detail of its daily life. American politics and American business seem to follow a similar pattern. The two major parties operate like nationwide chains. Their local branches use their own methods to sell the party's product.

There are other parties, of course—the Socialist Workers Party, the American Party, the People's Party, the Communist Party USA and others. But none has developed enough strength to challenge the Republicans or Democrats seriously. One important reason is that the major parties have no sharp boundaries. They can take in people with very different beliefs. Dissidents have little need to abandon the party and build a rival of their own when there is room for them inside it.

It may seem almost unbelievable that two such loosely organized bodies can dominate the political life of a great country. Yet much of their strength lies precisely in their looseness.

There are several reasons.

**To begin with, tightly controlled parties would not fit American culture very well.** Americans would find it difficult to accept an organization that demanded strict obedience or expected them to follow a political programme written to last twenty years. They are practical-minded. They want answers to the problems they encounter in daily life. Their strong sense of personal independence makes them reluctant to give themselves completely to any organization, especially one that asks them to set aside their own interests. They choose a party because they believe it may serve those interests.

**The second reason is that almost anyone can join or leave.** Ordinary supporters are not subject to strict party discipline. In unflattering language, the two parties are a jumble of different people and opinions. In kinder language, they are open and inclusive. That openness is one of the conditions that has allowed them to survive for so long.

They have little reason to exclude anybody willing to support them. Instead, their loose structure helps them absorb people who might otherwise become opponents. Because the boundary between belonging and not belonging is so unclear, opposition from within often changes very little. Ordinary people may make a noise, but the real strength of the parties remains in the hands of a small circle of leaders, far beyond the reach of most supporters.

**Finally, the parties exist above all to win elections.** They do not have a single fixed political objective. They do whatever they believe will win votes. That pushes them to pay attention to what voters want and to adjust their proposals as public concerns change. Their policies often begin as summaries of the problems people are talking about, followed by promises to deal with them.

This ability to adapt has helped both parties hold on to their positions. At the same time, party leaders can work the interests of major business groups and particular social classes into the proposals they present to voters.

And once the election has been won, those leaders may forget their promises and take a different course altogether. By then, voters have few immediate ways to stop them.

## 2. The Rewards of Victory

In theory, the old system of handing government jobs to the winning political party has been greatly restricted. Since the introduction of the civil service system at the end of the nineteenth century, public officials and employees have fallen into two broad groups. Some hold political offices filled through elections or appointments. Most belong to a permanent civil service and are chosen through open, competitive procedures. Their jobs do not depend on which party wins an election. They do not enter and leave office with the party in power. I will return to this subject in Chapter 7, Section 7, "Choosing a Career in Government."

In practice, however, winning a presidential election still brings a great many rewards. The system of distributing positions among political allies has by no means disappeared at the top.

According to figures published in *The New York Times*, the president can appoint roughly 8,000 people to jobs of different kinds. Some are full-time, some part-time; some exercise real power, while others hold mainly honorary positions. The article estimated that there were about two million federal employees in all, although some estimates run much higher. Against that total, 8,000 may sound insignificant.

But it is not. These positions matter, first because they carry power and standing, and second because many are well paid. When a party wins, it gains control of a valuable collection of offices. People who helped secure the victory expect to be rewarded with appointments.

Soon after Bush was elected the forty-first president, Reagan asked the people holding these appointed positions to resign, making room for the new president to assemble his own team.

To see why these offices are attractive, consider some of the posts and salaries quoted in the source:

- Fourteen cabinet secretaries, earning $99,800 a year.
- Their deputies, earning between $75,500 and $89,500.
- Thirteen ambassadors abroad, earning $75,500.
- The heads of various regulatory commissions, earning $82,500.
- Lawyers and doctors working at the White House, earning $89,500.

And those are only examples. Taken together, the 8,000 positions are quite a prize.

A comparison with university pay gives some sense of the amounts. A few exceptionally distinguished professors, especially in engineering, science or medicine, can earn more than $200,000 a year. Professors in the humanities generally earn less. A full professor might receive between $40,000 and $60,000; an associate professor less than $40,000; and an assistant professor still less, even with a doctorate.

Of course, not all 8,000 appointed officials receive such generous salaries. And some people have no interest in a government career because private business can pay so much more. Chrysler's chief executive, Lee Iacocca, received $7 million in 1987. One professor remarked, "Nobody is worth that much money."

Public office generally pays less than business. But it offers something business salaries alone cannot: political authority—the right to exercise the power of government.

Once a party has won the presidency, it has to decide how to share out these rewards. This is a difficult and delicate task. The people who worked hardest for the campaign must feel recognized, but the new administration also has to function well. If the same party has won another term, there is the added question of what to do with officials already in place.

Consider two of Bush's early choices.

**James Baker**, appointed secretary of state, was fifty-eight. A lawyer from Houston, he had chaired four of Bush's campaigns and had done a great deal to help him. He had also served as Reagan's White House chief of staff and as secretary of the Treasury. He was close to Bush personally as well as politically—one of his most trusted friends.

**John Sununu**, chosen as White House chief of staff, was forty-nine. He was the governor of New Hampshire and held a doctorate in engineering from the Massachusetts Institute of Technology. His great service to Bush had come during the New Hampshire primary. Bush had been in danger of losing to another Republican, Bob Dole. As governor, Sununu threw his political organization into the effort to rescue Bush's campaign.

In choosing Sununu, Bush passed over Craig Fuller, who had worked loyally as his chief of staff since 1985. Fuller had served him faithfully, but Sununu's contribution to the election was simply too important to overlook.

Bush continued to announce appointments. Before the election, supporters had given money, time and energy. Now that victory had come, those who had helped could look forward to government positions. The winners celebrated; the defeated candidates and the many people who had worked on their campaigns could only watch as the desirable jobs went elsewhere.

There are checks on this process. That is hardly surprising when so much is at stake. Of the offices the president can fill, about 700 require the Senate's approval, including ambassadorships. Members of the White House staff and the president's advisers do not need that approval. The requirement forces the president to consider whether certain choices can survive examination by another part of government.

Thus the American civil service system has greatly reduced the practice of distributing government jobs as political prizes, but it has not removed it. The same practice exists outside presidential appointments. A newly elected senator or member of the House of Representatives can assemble a staff, rewarding those who helped the campaign.

These are official jobs, and official jobs carry power. Power means the ability to make decisions. People in important positions can influence where government money goes, so many more people may benefit from an election victory than the individuals who receive formal appointments.

A senior appointment also offers advantages that may last long after the official has left government. If the party loses the next election, or the official is dismissed or decides to resign, large companies and other organizations may compete to offer them well-paid jobs. Many former senior officials now work in major businesses. Having served in the White House or Washington is itself a valuable qualification.

What part does this arrangement play in governing society? Why has a portion of the old system survived?

There are several reasons.

**First, distributing appointments allows an elected president to form a team that can actually govern.** The old practice was reformed at the end of the nineteenth century because it encouraged favouritism, inefficiency and corruption. It also made government unstable. Officials appointed for political reasons might expect to be replaced after the next election. With so little time, some were tempted to enrich themselves before they had to leave. Constant changes of personnel prevented government work from continuing smoothly.

Yet appointments at the highest levels remained. They let a president put trusted people in the most important positions and build a government whose different parts can act together. Such a team can do good things or bad things, but from the point of view of directing a government, it is difficult to imagine how the president could manage without one.

Suppose a president had no power to choose colleagues, and every senior official was selected by somebody else. How would the government function? Rival authorities would constantly obstruct one another. That is one reason this limited form of political appointment has survived. Every incoming president wants the power to assemble a team, so no president has been eager to give it up.

**Second, the president's freedom to appoint is real, but it is not unlimited.** Congress can supervise government officials, including the president. Nixon was forced to resign over Watergate, and many other senior officials have had to leave office because of their actions.

The greatest limit is the presidential election every four years. When a new president takes office—especially one from the other party—the appointed officials must be reappointed if they want to remain. Otherwise, they must leave. A change of party in the White House normally means that the outgoing party's appointees pack their bags.

If an administration loses public support and then loses the election, its leading officials lose their jobs as well. So, even with the many restrictions on presidential authority, winning the White House still allows a president to share the rewards of office with political allies. Within the federal executive, this can amount to government by one party. The difference is that its term is limited, it must face elections, and Congress can hold it in check.

The rule is simple: the winner takes the offices; the loser goes without. It is one of the enduring truths of American political life.

**Third, political appointments are a gamble for the people who accept them.** A job won through a party's victory may disappear when the party is defeated. What does an appointed official do then?

Permanent civil servants do not face the same problem. They can remain in their careers until retirement. Political appointees cannot count on holding office for life. When the political wind changes, they have to make other plans.

The most senior officials may have little trouble finding positions, but the problem can be very real for people further down the ladder. American society, however, has arrangements that make this circulation of officials possible.

People are generally expected to find their own work. Government does not assign everybody a position. Private companies and organizations, as well as universities and other institutions, hire people through competition. Those with useful abilities and experience can find another job. Someone who has held a senior position in Washington is likely to be more attractive to employers than an ordinary applicant. Some appointees already own businesses; others go back to the jobs they held before entering government.

The wider society can therefore absorb people leaving political office without turning every change of administration into a serious social crisis. Many appointees understand from the beginning that their time in Washington may last only a few years. They prepare their next move well before they have to leave. Nobody forces them to take an appointment. They enter knowing the risks.

Without those opportunities to return to ordinary work, imagine the consequences of replacing so many important officials every time the government changed hands.

The distribution of political jobs clearly has disadvantages. The question is how to supervise it and keep it within limits. Nor could every society adopt the same arrangement. It works only where the wider social system can support it.

For all its problems, this limited form of rewarding the winning party helps create a united and effective administration. It also keeps the leading groups within the ruling class working in step. That is one of the basic mechanisms of American government.

## 3. Interest Groups

Interest groups, also called pressure groups, are a major feature of American politics and society. To understand how American government works, we have to understand them. But their importance goes beyond formal politics. They also help explain how American society organizes itself.

What is a pressure group? Broadly speaking, it is a group of people who join forces to influence political decisions. Usually they have interests of their own to protect. They try to change government policy either to safeguard what they already have or to gain more. A small number are driven chiefly by ideals. Most are driven by their own interests.

Government decisions help determine who gets what in society. Policies affect how benefits, opportunities and resources are shared. People who want to defend or enlarge their share therefore have a strong reason to influence those decisions. This is the foundation of interest-group activity.

Interest groups are also widely seen as a foundation of political pluralism—the idea that power should be influenced by many different organizations rather than controlled from a single centre. If groups outside government can shape decisions, political power has many voices. If nobody outside government can influence policy, power is concentrated in one place.

Groups use various means to make themselves heard. One of the most important is *lobbying*: approaching officials or lawmakers and trying to persuade them. Many of the lobbyists active in Washington work on behalf of particular interest groups.

These groups are characteristic of capitalist societies, although they can also be found in some developing countries. Their role can be unusual because they connect economic power with political power.

Under capitalism, the government does not directly run most economic activity. In principle, it is supposed to settle disputes over economic interests through laws and public policy. But different groups work to influence those laws and policies, trying to make them serve their own needs as fully as possible. Through this process, organizations with economic power become linked to organizations with political power. Other kinds of groups can build similar connections.

Interest groups are, in this sense, a natural result of capitalism. When property is privately owned, the boundaries of individual interests are usually clear. People have strong reasons to defend what is theirs and to seek more. It is only natural that individual interests should be brought together into larger groups, capable of gaining more influence than a person acting alone.

There are groups of every kind: economic, linguistic, cultural, religious, racial and professional. One traditional feature of capitalism is the belief that government should remain limited—the old claim that the best government is the one that governs least. But when people want government to protect or expand their interests, a single person's voice may carry little weight. Working together can make the difference.

A habit of forming independent groups is therefore an important part of America's social organization.

According to estimates quoted by some scholars, six out of ten American adults belong to an organization of some kind. Some groups are openly political, such as Republican and Democratic clubs or organizations formed to campaign on particular issues. Others are astonishingly varied: school-service organizations, youth groups, veterans' associations, farmers' groups, racial and ethnic organizations, churches, charitable associations, professional societies, labour unions and sports clubs.

These are independent organizations. They are not government departments, and they do not operate under government command. If they want their interests taken into account, they must find ways to influence the political process from outside.

Among the most powerful are business associations, labour organizations and farmers' groups. Business groups have substantial financial resources. They can hire experts to speak for them and work on political decisions. The National Association of Manufacturers and the U.S. Chamber of Commerce are examples. Unions have a different source of strength: large memberships. Numbers can give them considerable influence.

But interest groups do not all have equal power. Small organizations cannot begin to match the resources of the largest ones. They may be free to campaign, yet they often achieve little. The groups that most frequently shape political decisions are the large, well-established ones.

To be fair, a political system cannot give every person and every small group an equal hand in every decision without becoming impossible to manage. If everyone could pull government in a different direction at once, the political process would lose its ability to act together. But the result of this arrangement is that the largest groups—whether economic or political—come to exercise much of the real power.

We can see this inequality in the resources a group needs to influence government. No interest group can simply march into the president's office or a senator's office and order them about. It must persuade them. To do that effectively, it needs several things.

**First, money.** An organization needs enough funds to employ people who work for it full-time, including lobbyists operating at the federal, state and local levels.

**Second, members.** A handful of people usually cannot accomplish much on their own. There is strength in numbers. There is, however, a different argument worth noting. Because people can sometimes enjoy the benefits of a group's success without helping to pay for them—a problem known as *free riding*—very large groups can actually be harder to organize effectively than small or medium-sized ones. The benefits they seek are often shared by everyone, including people who do nothing to help obtain them.

**Third, expertise.** An interest group cannot simply demand a law in its favour and expect it to appear. Laws must be drafted, discussed and voted on. Groups therefore need strong, well-supported and convincing arguments. They must understand their subjects in depth and be able to explain why a proposal makes sense. Underhanded deals certainly occur, but politics also puts obstacles in their way: rival groups watch one another closely. Expert knowledge becomes another way to win influence.

**Fourth, connections.** Knowing the right people matters. Having a friend or contact inside government can make all the difference between getting a hearing and being ignored.

Large, wealthy groups naturally find it easier to satisfy all four conditions. So although American politics may be called pluralist, we should be careful not to exaggerate how widely power is actually shared.

Some of the larger or more influential interest groups exercise powers that look almost governmental. They have been called *quasi-governments*—organizations that are not part of the state but perform some similar functions.

Professional associations, for example, may control entry into an occupation by setting standards and rules. Private organizations may distribute public funds that have been supplied by government. The government establishes the broad conditions, but the organization making the day-to-day decisions has a great deal of influence over who receives the money. Other groups are so powerful in a particular industry that government officials have to consider their views before taking action.

The private ownership of economic life helps explain why these groups exist. The capitalist approach asks government not to direct the economy itself. Yet government still needs to guide economic activity, win cooperation and sometimes regulate it. It cannot simply do whatever it wants. It has to take account of the major groups affected by its decisions.

The groups, in turn, cannot act entirely as they please. They must operate within laws and public policies and pay attention to government. Each side limits and influences the other. That two-way relationship is one of the distinctive ways American political and social life is managed.

There are two ways to judge the role of interest groups.

On one hand, they gather people's demands, bring them together, and carry them into the political system. They create channels between government and a society too large and varied for officials to understand on their own. Many groups perform that service even when it is not their conscious aim.

On the other hand, they often speak only for a particular set of interests. Ordinary people who lack wealth, influence or organization may not be able to form powerful groups at all.

Both things are true.

If the best-funded, best-organized groups have the greatest say in political decisions, who speaks for ordinary citizens? Is American democracy as democratic as it claims to be? There is good reason to ask.

As I have argued, interest groups grow out of America's social system, political institutions and culture together. Within that combination, it is difficult to imagine a completely different way of bringing society and government into contact. The important question is when the benefits and weaknesses of this arrangement might fall so badly out of balance that the system enters a crisis.

Every government needs ways to gather, compare and understand the many things people want. Its resources are limited. It can rarely satisfy every demand. The best policy is not one that promises to give everybody everything; such a policy is impossible. It is one that uses the resources available to meet as many needs as possible.

To do that, a government must first know what those needs are. It needs as many reliable channels into society as it can develop.

## 4. Lobbyists

I have used a Chinese expression that sounds a little like the English word *lobbyist* while suggesting someone who enjoys the art of persuasion. Others translate the term as "corridor legislator" or simply "persuader." Whatever name we give them, lobbyists are an important part of American political life. We cannot understand American government without looking at what they do.

What is lobbying, and who are the people who do it?

According to Karen Sagstetter's book on the subject, lobbyists are professional persuaders. They work around city councils, state legislatures and federal institutions. Wherever government makes decisions, lobbyists can be found. Their aim is to influence policies and laws on behalf of particular public or private interests. They want government or a legislature either to take a certain action—or to leave something alone.

There is nothing inherently shameful about being a lobbyist in America. It is not necessarily a secret occupation carried out behind closed doors. Lobbyists are visible, sometimes prominent, and can have a great deal of influence.

Frank Cummings, in his *Capitol Hill Manual*, makes this clear to people working in Congress. Historical stories, he explains, sometimes give the impression that "lobbying" is a dirty word. But there is nothing automatically wrong with it. Indeed, he argues, lobbying is necessary.

Why? Because no member of Congress can be an expert on every subject. Even lawmakers who specialize in a particular field cannot know everything about it. Nor can they automatically know what a particular group thinks about a particular issue. Lobbyists can bring them information and convey the concerns of the people they represent.

From the point of view of making laws and governing society, that is the useful side of lobbying: it carries information and interests into government.

But lobbyists always have a purpose. Most do not work for the good of society as a whole. They work for specific interests. When a lobbying campaign succeeds, a particular group may gain a substantial advantage.

Imagine, for example, that a suit made in China costs only $50, while the higher cost of American labour means a similar American suit sells for $100. Buyers may naturally choose the cheaper Chinese suit. American clothing manufacturers would then face falling sales and profits. Their lobbyists might go to Congress and ask for laws imposing extra taxes on imports or limiting the number of foreign suits allowed into the country. If Congress passed such legislation, the American manufacturers would benefit greatly.

That example is hypothetical, but it shows the basic idea.

Lobbyists work for all sorts of clients. Many represent private businesses and commercial groups. Others work for trade unions, environmental organizations, public-welfare groups and similar bodies.

Quite a few work for foreign governments. Other countries want favourable treatment from American laws or public decisions, and some pay large sums to hire people who know how to influence Washington. Governments send experienced representatives to meet officials, explain requests and argue their cases. In 1969–70, for example, South Korea sent a substantial delegation to Washington. It worked for seven months in an effort to obtain an additional $50 million in American aid.

Even the American president needs people to lobby. Because power is divided among different parts of government, a president must persuade Congress to support proposed policies. Much of this work is done by officials in the administration, sometimes through teams established specifically to deal with Congress.

The clients and methods vary, but the essential task stays the same: to influence legislation or government decisions.

Lobbyists use whatever means they consider effective. They meet lawmakers, prepare documents, and arrange lunches and conversations. But the work is not as easy as it may appear. Effective lobbying calls for particular skills and knowledge.

The main activities include:

1. **Research.** Studying a subject or proposed law in detail so that arguments can be supported with facts.
2. **Giving evidence.** Appearing before legislative hearings to explain a position.
3. **Working with lawmakers.** Helping prepare speeches, reports, draft bills and letters in response to constituents.

There are less direct methods as well. *Grassroots lobbying* tries to change the views of voters in a legislator's own district, so that those voters will put pressure on their representative. *Cross-lobbying* brings several groups or lobbyists together to work toward the same goal. Groups may also publish lawmakers' voting records to influence public opinion or encourage voters to reward or punish particular decisions.

Members of Congress need lobbyists, too. When they prepare to speak or vote on a bill, they need information. They do not always sit and wait for somebody to approach them; sometimes they actively seek out the lobbyists who know most about the issue.

Of course, attitudes toward lobbying differ. Some politicians welcome it; others dislike it. But there is one thing they cannot forget. Every lobbyist has an objective. A lobbyist who makes a lawmaker's life easier is usually doing so for a reason. As the *Capitol Hill Manual* warns, there is danger in that relationship.

Lobbying has become a recognized part of political life, and that is reflected in the law. In 1946 the United States passed an important law regulating lobbying. Under its provisions, people paid to influence legislation were required to register. The rule could apply to individuals, associations and companies.

They were supposed to provide information about their work: their names and business addresses; who employed them; how long they had been lobbying; their salaries and who paid them; their day-to-day expenses; which laws concerned them; who received money from their activities; and what the money was spent on.

On paper, this looks like a reasonable system of oversight. In practice, it has serious gaps. Some people clearly influence legislation while insisting that they are not lobbyists and have no particular interest in the legislative process. Washington is said to have around 20,000 lobbyists busily at work.

Lobbying can be remarkably effective. The organizations lobbying on behalf of Israel, for example, are powerful and have achieved important results. There are roughly six million Jewish people in the United States, and, according to the figures discussed here, their average educational level, social standing and involvement in politics are relatively high. A leading pro-Israel lobbying organization, AIPAC, was described as having seventy-five members and a budget of $5.7 million in 1985. Arab lobbying organizations also possess considerable resources.

The book *Washington Lobby* gives many examples intended to demonstrate how effective such efforts can be. Ordinary people, needless to say, rarely have professional lobbyists of their own.

One striking feature of American society is the way it turns the political arena into something resembling a marketplace. Politics becomes a place where people exchange things, promote their interests and bargain over outcomes, much as they do in business.

Groups try to sell their "products" or buy the products offered by others. Politicians negotiate, compete, argue and look for an advantage.

This marketplace operates under legal rules, but those rules begin by accepting that politics will be conducted in this way. The arrangement has both strengths and weaknesses.

On the positive side, competition can reward proposals that are better argued or more convincing. Anyone who wants support has an incentive to make a strong case and offer something people will value.

On the negative side, large, wealthy organizations can dominate the market. They can promote weak proposals or advance their own interests at everyone else's expense, much as a powerful company can earn excessive profits by controlling a commercial market. It may be difficult for one group to take over the whole political system, but money gives large groups a clear advantage.

This way of thinking is deeply rooted in American political culture. It has been shaped by the country's highly developed market economy. In business, people seek the best customers, promote their products, find the most effective methods and negotiate the most favourable terms. In politics, lobbyists, lawmakers and officials often meet one another in much the same spirit. Each is, in a sense, a buyer or a seller.

There is another cultural change at work here: politics has become an ordinary, worldly activity.

People speak of religion becoming less separate from everyday life. Politics can undergo a similar change. Americans often approach political activity much as they would approach business, research or the task of earning money. They do not necessarily regard politics as a sacred activity standing above everything else.

That does not mean everyone has equal power. Political authority is still largely held by people at the top of society. But even those people have to conduct politics through everyday interests, negotiations and practical exchanges. In America, it would be difficult to do otherwise.

## 5. Radical Organizations

Passing through the city centre, I noticed a young woman selling books from a stall in a busy spot. Hardly anyone was stopping. I happened to glance at the books as I walked by, and what I saw made me stop for a closer look. There was *The Communist Manifesto*, a collection of Fidel Castro's speeches, and another of Trotsky's writings. It was fairly obvious that she belonged to a radical left-wing group.

I was told that America has several hundred small, sometimes tiny, organizations of this kind. They are often remarkably active, but they have little political influence. Most Americans pay them no attention.

The woman was also selling her group's newspaper, *The Militant*. Under the title, it described itself as a socialist weekly newspaper published in the interests of working people. It was about the same size as Shanghai's *Xinmin Evening News*, ran to sixteen pages, and cost a dollar—more than an ordinary newspaper.

I looked through it and found several stories worth thinking about.

The largest amount of space was devoted to defending a man named Mark Curtis, an active member of the organization who had been accused of sexual assault. According to the account, on March 4, 1988, a young woman named Morris was watching television at home when someone called for her parents sometime after eight in the evening. She said they were out. Ten minutes later, someone knocked on the door. When she opened it, the man assaulted and struck her. *The Militant* printed a detailed account of Curtis's movements that evening, intended to show that he could not have committed the attack, and urged readers to support him.

Other stories criticized Prime Minister Margaret Thatcher for expelling Cuba's ambassador to Britain and a third secretary at the Cuban embassy. The ambassador, Oscar Fernández Mell, was described as one of the founders of the Cuban Communist Party, a member of its first Central Committee, and a man who had once helped Che Guevara support an anti-imperialist struggle in Zaire.

The woman selling the paper had told me that Cuba might be the only socialist country left in the world.

There was a lengthy report on political developments in Burma; another about a meeting of 250 people in Zimbabwe to discuss Che Guevara and the Cuban Revolution; and an article reflecting on a miners' strike in Pennsylvania.

One article deserves particular attention because it gives a good sense of what the organization believes. Entitled “One Caribbean, One Destiny,” it reported on a meeting in New York about the future of the struggle against imperialism. The meeting had been organized by the Caribbean Unity Alliance, made up of thirty-eight political organizations from twenty-three countries.

The main speaker was a former press secretary to the prime minister of Grenada. His words are worth considering:

“We are here to discuss how to bring our alliance together against theirs. Ours is an alliance of workers, farmers, fishermen, young people, students, and those who suffer. Theirs is an alliance of people with power, privilege, capital, and wealth.”

There are still plenty of people in the world who speak this way. Many at the meeting condemned American intervention in Cuba and Nicaragua as aggression and argued that it stood in the way of people's access to housing, education, bread, and other necessities of life.

More interesting still, the newspaper carried a notice about the Socialist Workers Party's participation in the 1988 presidential election. Its presidential candidate was Warren, and its vice-presidential candidate was Mickells. It even had a campaign committee. Candidates like these were unlikely to make much noise. They would probably be drowned out altogether by the uproar surrounding Bush and Dukakis.

*The Militant* also urged people to subscribe. Its advertisement promised to tell the truth about the wars the United States was waging against peoples around the world. If readers relied only on the major news organizations, it said, they would know nothing about America's wars against the people of El Salvador, Guatemala, and Nicaragua.

All this makes clear that we are dealing with a radical organization. There are countless groups like it in America and other Western societies, but most have little influence. Some of the problems they discuss are real. And some of their proposals may even help society move forward. Yet organizations that call themselves socialist have an especially weak position in America.

We cannot understand this simply by examining their programmes or activities. Their position reflects something much larger: the relationship between the world's two great competing sets of political beliefs.

Since the Second World War, socialist countries have experienced one difficulty after another. Their economies have fallen far behind those of capitalist countries. As a result, socialism has not been attractive enough to people looking at the two systems from the outside. Meanwhile, the economic development of capitalist countries has created an almost overwhelming appeal.

Most people do not judge a society by carefully weighing its institutions, structure, values, spiritual life, or understanding of human nature. They judge it by everyday life—by what they can eat and how they can live. They make their judgments with their stomachs rather than their heads.

Given the huge differences in economic and social development, movements calling for the reform of capitalism are unlikely to gain much influence in Western societies. American society can therefore afford to leave them alone, letting them flourish or disappear as they will.

But imagine that the economic balance between East and West were reversed one day. I suspect these groups would then face controls. In fact, socialist countries might not even need to overtake capitalist ones. If their living standards simply caught up, the battle of ideas could return with renewed force.

A friend agreed. He added that perhaps even a few more serious economic recessions would be enough to create an audience for the radicals.

## 6. Rule by the Many—or by the Few?

Many people describe America as a society in which power is shared among many different groups. They say its political system works the same way. One of the earliest scholars to put forward this view was David Truman, who described its basic workings in *The Governmental Process*. Another was Robert Dahl, who examined American politics in *Who Governs?*

I have read Dahl's *Modern Political Analysis*, so I am familiar with his approach. Strictly speaking, the term he uses is *polyarchy*, rather than *pluralism*. But whatever the difference between the two terms, the basic idea is similar: power in American society is shared. Many competing interests struggle to gain influence. No single interest can take all the power, and no group is completely shut out. Power passes among different social organizations through this competition.

This view of politics rests on the importance of interest groups.

Its central claim is that different groups in society can all influence how political power is used. But is that really possible? Those who support this view say it is.

They offer several reasons.

First, America is a country of immigrants, filled with different ethnic, language, and religious communities. Differences between occupations also remain clear. America is what John Naisbitt calls a “melting pot society.”

Second, Americans have a strong habit of forming groups to pursue shared interests. That much is certainly true. Groups of every description are everywhere. Because people generally believe that government should do as little as possible, anyone who wants a problem dealt with has two choices: organize with others and do something about it, or organize with others to influence the government. Elections encourage the habit. Americans learn from them that once enough people join together, they become a force that others must take seriously.

Third, the way government divides power also helps. Different branches of government restrain one another, so no single part can hold all the power. Different interests therefore compete to influence decisions. They bargain, negotiate, and compromise, while government serves as a mediator—although government has interests of its own, too.

Other scholars reject this account. They argue that American democracy is, in practice, government by a small group of powerful people. Most citizens cannot really exercise political power. The groups that can compete effectively are large, well-organized ones. Small groups have much less influence. And for the many people who belong to no organization at all, the promise of shared power means very little.

Large groups can exercise far more control than small ones. Even organizations that claim to speak for an entire occupation or community do not include everyone in it. Each has its own particular interests. And the government is not simply a puppet waiting for groups to pull its strings. It has substantial powers of its own and can use them to carry out its own policies and purposes.

Those who argue for rule by a powerful few say that ordinary citizens, elected representatives, and interest groups do not really hold the decisive power. That power belongs to a small circle of people—the *power elite*.

Among scholars associated with this argument are Grant McConnell, author of *Private Power and American Democracy* (1970); Theodore Lowi, author of *The End of Liberalism* (1969); C. Wright Mills, author of *The Power Elite* (1956); and Ralph Miliband, author of *The State in Capitalist Society* (1969).

Mills has been especially influential. He argued that a small group commands the central organizations of modern society. Its members run the machinery of the state, lead military organizations, and occupy the positions from which the most important decisions are made.

In his account, American power comes chiefly from three places: large corporations, the military, and political leaders.

A few hundred major companies dominate the economy and help determine its direction. Political authority has become increasingly concentrated in the federal government, while the president's powers have expanded. The military has become an immense organization, taking up a major share of government spending.

The leaders of these three worlds form the power elite. They make decisions that may affect life and death. The wider public can do little but watch.

Mills went further. He believed these three centres of power were closely connected and worked together. A retired general might become the manager of a defence contractor. A defeated presidential candidate might take a job advising a major corporation. A company's chairman or senior manager might become a government official.

Thomas R. Dye explored a similar question in *Who Rules America?* His answer was that the country was governed by around five thousand powerful people.

Another of Dye's books is particularly interesting: *The Irony of Democracy: An Uncommon Introduction to American Politics*. Its central argument is that America is ruled by an elite, and has been since the country was founded.

According to Dye, the consequences are a population separated from real power, a governing elite firmly in control, and waves of public protest. These are the difficulties at the heart of American politics.

Democracy is supposed to mean government *of* the people, *by* the people, and *for* the people. Yet in the everyday workings of politics, rule by a small group of powerful people appears almost unavoidable.

The larger point is this: whether the people at the top number five thousand or fifty thousand, they are still only a small minority. They hold the main powers through which society is governed. Rule by such groups is a common feature of capitalist societies.

That presents a difficult question for the future of human society. Is democracy in its strictest sense really suited to the inner demands of a large, complex society?

Americans defend democracy in principle, and they defend the democratic system set out in their Constitution. But in their feelings and culture, they also have an extraordinary admiration for exceptional individuals. Few peoples celebrate outstanding talent and achievement as strongly as Americans do.

Many Americans appear casual or indifferent about all sorts of things, yet hero worship runs deep in their culture. Perhaps that has something to do with their history and the way the country developed. This cultural habit makes it easier for them to accept rule by a small, talented, powerful group. They speak in favour of democracy for everyone, but in practice accept government by the few. (See Chapter 3, Section 4, “Making Things Sacred.”)

This is both a curious feature of the American system and something Americans find difficult to explain. Many scholars recognize that rule by an elite conflicts with the principle of government by the people.

Yet the elite's power arises from democratic institutions themselves. People use those institutions to gain influence, and they cannot simply be ordered to stop. Forcefully abolishing or restricting that influence would itself violate democratic principles.

America will have to find some way through this dilemma as its system develops. For now, it is impossible to say which direction that change will take.

## 7. Democracy Through Participation?

In recent years, a number of scholars have been talking about what they see as the latest development in capitalist republican government: *participatory democracy*. Some have praised it as “the democracy of the twenty-first century.” Alvin Toffler's *The Third Wave* and John Naisbitt's *Megatrends* are among the best-known works advancing this idea. Both argue that allowing more people to take part in decisions is bringing about a revolutionary change. Harlan Cleveland, director of the Hubert H. Humphrey Institute of Public Affairs at the University of Minnesota, has written that in an information society, participation may become the newest definition of democracy itself.

America is a classic example of a democratic republic. Its system was not created out of thin air. It developed through a particular historical process, driven in large part by the rapid growth of capitalism in the thirteen British colonies.

Not long after these colonies were founded, a free-enterprise economy began to take shape. Woollen textiles and metalworking were among the most developed industries. Colonial metalworking even competed with production in Britain. But Britain's ruling class placed political and economic restrictions on the colonies, and tensions grew steadily worse. The colonists wanted a government of their own, one that would protect their growing economy and help it develop. This created the historical demand for a democratic republic.

As capitalism took hold, producing and exchanging goods became the leading forms of economic activity. In my view, the very nature of that exchange creates a demand for political equality.

Here is the reasoning. When goods are bought and sold, their value is measured by the human work involved in producing them. Different kinds of work are treated as comparable, and goods can therefore be exchanged according to their value. The amount of work society normally requires to make something is worked out through everyday production—under ordinary conditions, with average levels of skill and effort. That process assumes a kind of equality. In the marketplace, one producer's goods must be compared with another's without treating the producer as naturally high or low in rank.

The democratic republic turns this economic principle into political institutions and laws. As the saying goes, “Goods are natural champions of equality.”

Over the past twenty years, advances in production and technology, along with changing social conflicts, have brought new developments to republican government. More citizens now take part in political decisions and lawmaking. The range and depth of democratic activity have expanded rapidly.

Some scholars say that the political institutions of the industrial age are worn out, and that we are moving into an era of “participatory democracy” or “semi-direct democracy”—a system in which people vote on more decisions themselves rather than always leaving them to elected representatives.

Toffler argues in *The Third Wave* that the new society poses a revolutionary challenge to the institutions of the industrial age. It cannot be met simply by revising a few rules, because it strikes at one of the foundations of industrial-age politics: the idea that citizens must govern through representatives.

Naisbitt likewise describes a movement “from representative democracy to participatory democracy.”

American writers offer many descriptions of this change, but their arguments can be grouped into three main ideas.

**First, the people who actually exercise executive power should be chosen directly by as many citizens as possible.** This would replace older forms of indirect election. Some scholars point to presidential elections as an example. They argue that the Electoral College is becoming little more than a formality and that, in practice, the president is increasingly chosen directly by the voters. Perhaps political parties themselves will become less necessary.

**Second, citizens, states, cities, and towns should be able to take a much greater part in making laws and public decisions.** This is even more important than the first change.

America's founding principles placed great weight on freeing the individual, including freedom of speech and keeping government interference in private life to a minimum. But poor communications made it difficult to put these principles fully into practice. Modern communications equipment is changing that. Citizens can now join discussions and express their wishes far more easily—even by sitting at home and pressing buttons.

The “electronic town hall” in Columbus, Ohio, which uses television to discuss political questions, is a good example. Naisbitt also points to figures showing that the 1970s saw a surge in initiatives and referendums. These allow citizens to propose measures or vote directly on them. He concludes that Americans and their states are now directly shaping a large share of their laws.

**Third, these developments should lead to political power being spread more widely.** If citizens can make decisions, help write laws, and solve their own problems, it matters less who becomes president or who wins a seat in the legislature. They no longer need representatives for everything.

Why, then, do people still elect representatives? Supporters of this argument give two reasons: it is what we have always done, and it remains politically convenient. People do not want to vote on every little matter. They want a direct say in decisions that genuinely affect their lives.

From this perspective, concentrating power at the centre no longer works. Democracy is supposedly moving away from central control and toward decisions made by people closer to the problems themselves.

There really has been an expansion of democracy in its outward forms. But I believe that, at the same time, real power is becoming more concentrated. These two trends work upon one another in a complicated process.

Breakthroughs in electronics, information technology, energy, computers, and other fields have transformed production. They have encouraged more participation in managing how goods are produced while also making it easier to concentrate control over people. Two economic developments are especially important here: the wider spread of company shares and the growing number of small and medium-sized businesses.

In contemporary America, shares in a major company may be held by thousands or even tens of thousands of people. Workers, too, may own a small amount of stock. Their holdings give them a stake in the company, however modest, and they naturally begin to demand some voice in its decisions.

At the same time, large companies with monopoly power depend on smaller businesses as sources of profit. They cannot earn all their advantages entirely from within their own operations. As big corporations grow, so does the number of smaller firms that serve them. The owners of those firms increasingly want a say in decisions affecting the wider economy, so they can create better conditions for their own businesses.

All this strengthens the idea of equality rooted in the exchange of goods. People begin to demand political arrangements that look democratic and allow their interests to be heard.

The demand appears first in the management of public affairs. Public services and shared resources are closely connected to economic activity. Their scale and importance keep growing. They can obstruct production, but they can also protect it and help it develop. People who already take part in managing businesses therefore want a say in how public affairs are managed as well. They want decisions that will help their own work and enterprises prosper.

Yet there is another side to this story.

A worker with a handful of shares cannot determine what a company does. The money invested by these small shareholders is effectively under the control of the people who own large blocks of shares. When ownership is scattered among thousands of investors, someone holding only two or three percent of a company's shares may, in some circumstances, exercise control over the whole enterprise.

Big capital thus gains command of money contributed by people across society. Rather than spreading real economic power, widely scattered ownership can help concentrate it more firmly.

The same is true of small and medium-sized businesses. In this account, many exist because larger corporations can draw profits from them. Their owners' efforts to influence public decisions may help their firms develop, but they also expand the sources of profit available to big business. In the end, these efforts can strengthen the concentration of capitalist production.

We arrive at a contradiction: democracy in appearance, concentrated power in substance.

On the surface, people have more democratic rights. They can take part in managing production and public affairs. But when we look at where the real power lies, their participation may merely lead them—often willingly—to act in ways that promote the further concentration of capital. Many separate actions come to serve a single controlling purpose.

Herbert Marcuse of the Frankfurt School, who studied America's economic and political system, offered a thought-provoking observation: the world of modern technology has invaded and reduced private space, while mass production and mass distribution demand ever more of the individual.

Such a process is bound to affect politics as well. It can produce a level of coordination and uniformity far beyond anything a skilled craftsman or orchestra conductor could imagine.

Naisbitt gives participatory democracy a simple guiding rule: people whose lives will be affected by a decision ought to have a part in making it. We have seen that there are deep economic reasons for this demand. But the participation it produces remains quite limited.

When Naisbitt discusses American politics, he first confines the new practices largely to *local* politics. Initiatives and referendums may give citizens new ways to make decisions, but mainly about local matters. They have little effect on what the national government does.

And the decisions themselves often concern narrow questions: whether herbicides may be sprayed from aircraft, where smoking should be forbidden, whether beer bottles should be returned for reuse, or what colour streetlights should be. These methods have much less effect on the major questions at the centre of political and economic power.

In the late 1970s, California passed Proposition 13, a measure to cut taxes. It set off a tax-cutting movement, and other states proposed plans of their own. Yet within a year, the movement had largely faded.

Meanwhile, political power continues to gather at the centre. Modern technology gives government new practical means to extend that control.

Many American thinkers recognize the trend. Even Toffler, while describing the Third Wave, senses the problem. He argues that one unusual principle of the new government is the growing power of minorities. The old industrial-age principle of majority rule, once central to the legitimacy of politics, is becoming less suited to reality. Politics can no longer be understood simply as the majority having its way.

Behind the appearance of participatory democracy, then, the concentration of power in America is speeding up. According to the figures cited here, roughly one in six employed Americans works for the federal government or bodies connected with it. Since 1930, the federal workforce is said to have grown by five hundred percent. In 1976, federal and local governments together employed more than fifteen million people and paid a combined wage bill of $167 billion.

Robert J. Ringer expressed his frustration in vivid terms. He pictured the world struggling in the hands of an artificial monster called government: a creature growing larger and harder to control, slowly reaching into every corner of our lives and trampling on people's rights as though that were the most natural thing in the world.

In 1976, the Harvard professor Daniel Bell observed that a central problem in Western political systems was the relationship between people's desire to participate widely and the growth of rule by government officials.

Participatory democracy offers a temporary way of handling that contradiction. By expanding one side of the state's activity—formal opportunities for citizens to participate—it can strengthen the other side: the state's power to govern.

This is a more indirect, and potentially more effective, way of exercising control. It also fits more comfortably with the equality associated with producing and exchanging goods.

Universal voting rights have been supplemented by initiatives and referendums, turning some decisions once made only through elected officials into matters on which citizens can vote directly. Scientific and technological progress has made this expansion possible.

But these changes in the outward forms of democracy have not altered the basic character of the American political system.

# Chapter 6: Democracy's Imperfect Elections

## 1. The Race for the White House

1988 is a presidential election year. The contest held every four years is unusually lively—one of the most exciting and colourful parts of American political life. Each time, the two major parties fight fiercely for the right to occupy the White House.

This year the Democrats have nominated Massachusetts governor Michael Dukakis. The Republicans have chosen the sitting vice-president, George Bush. Both men, along with their running mates, are rushing from place to place, giving speeches and asking for votes.

One force shapes everything about a campaign: the voter's ballot. Without votes, nothing else matters. A candidate must win enough voters' support to take power and enter the White House. That pressure forces both candidates to speak about the matters ordinary people care about.

In fact, the two parties have no fundamental disagreement over their guiding political beliefs. They disagree about broad policies and practical measures *within* a shared set of beliefs. Much of their argument is about what position to take on problems both sides recognize.

Consider the differences between Dukakis and Bush on several major issues, as reported in American newspapers:

| Issue | Michael Dukakis | George Bush |
|---|---|---|
| **Foreign policy: Nicaragua** | Opposes aid to the armed groups fighting the Nicaraguan government. | Supports military and humanitarian aid to those groups. |
| **Foreign policy: South Africa** | Wants tougher economic sanctions, possibly a more complete embargo. | Opposes further sanctions. |
| **Foreign policy: Middle East** | Wants stronger strategic ties with Israel, opposes arms sales that could threaten Israeli security, and encourages direct talks between Israel and Jordan. | Wants America to help arrange Middle East negotiations while making clear that it will never abandon Israel. |
| **Foreign policy: Persian Gulf** | Calls for an international arms embargo and international naval protection of freedom of navigation. | No position given in this comparison. |
| **Budget: income tax** | Would consider raising income tax only as a last resort. | Promises not to raise income tax under any circumstances. |
| **Budget: capital gains** | Opposes cutting taxes on profits from selling investments. | Wants to lower the capital-gains tax, with a figure of 15–28% given in the comparison. |
| **Budget: line-item veto** | Supports giving the president the power to reject individual spending items. | Also supports it. |
| **Budget: deficit** | Wants to reduce the deficit but gives no detailed plan; says military spending will remain at its present level. | Calls for a flexible spending freeze but refuses to specify which programmes would be cut. |
| **Health care: coverage** | Supports a broad health-care plan and wants employers to pay for workers' insurance. | Proposes changes involving life-insurance arrangements, individual retirement accounts, and other long-term protection plans; the source wording is unclear. |
| **Health care: AIDS** | Wants more money for AIDS research and compulsory testing in the armed forces and for immigrants from countries with high rates of AIDS. | Wants to encourage group insurance, leave AIDS education under local control, and test prisoners and immigrants for AIDS. |
| **Environment: acid rain** | Wants national limits on the release of sulphur dioxide and nitrogen dioxide, substances that contribute to acid rain. | Supports developing ways to clean coal before it is burned. |
| **Environment: drilling** | Opposes oil drilling in important protected areas and fishing grounds. | No position given here. |
| **Environment: nuclear energy** | Opposes building new commercial nuclear reactors until safety problems are solved. | Supports building nuclear reactors. |
| **Employment: jobs** | Makes full employment his first goal. | Calls for thirty million new jobs over eight years, though he later considers that target too optimistic. |
| **Employment: minimum wage** | Wants to raise it to $4.25 an hour. | Opposes an increase. |
| **Arms control: “Star Wars”** | Opposes the proposed space-based missile-defence programme. | Supports research and experiments in space. |
| **Arms control: nuclear testing** | Wants negotiations for a complete ban on nuclear tests. | Opposes a comprehensive nuclear test-ban treaty. |
| **Arms control: anti-satellite weapons** | Wants negotiations to end testing and development. | Supports continued testing and development. |
| **Social issues: death penalty** | Opposes it. | Supports it. |
| **Social issues: help for families** | Supports a $2.5 billion measure to give states funds to help poor and middle-income families. | Opposes federal relief payments; supports a new $1,000 tax credit for each child in families earning less than $20,000 a year. |
| **Social issues: abortion** | Opposes amending the Constitution to legalize abortion; supports federal funding. | No position given here. |
| **Social issues: guns** | Supports the right to own weapons. | Opposes federal gun registration and licensing; favours easing gun-control laws. |
| **Drugs: leadership** | Wants a cabinet-level body to coordinate all anti-drug programmes. | Also wants a cabinet-level body to lead the fight against drugs. |
| **Drugs: foreign policy** | Wants more help for other countries to stop drug production and for governments willing to block drugs from entering America. | Supports the death penalty for drug offences resulting in death. |
| **Drugs: further measures** | Promises more support for local drug enforcement and rehabilitation. | Wants Latin American countries to discuss how to end economic dependence on drugs; proposes taking driving licences and federal student loans away from repeat offenders. |
