| **Education: teachers** | Wants a national fund to recruit teachers. | Plans a $500 million fund to improve secondary schools and students' education. |
| **Education: schools and skills** | Would encourage companies to let employees teach for three to five years. | Wants more testing of pupils and more checks of teachers' abilities. |
| **Education: retired people** | Supports hiring retired people with useful expertise. | No position given here. |
| **Education: college costs** | Would create an education investment plan to help students repay tuition loans. | Would create savings bonds for higher education, with tax relief if the money is used for college. |
| **Education: families** | Would create a college opportunity fund, with government guaranteeing tuition when parents set money aside for their children's education. | No position given here. |

This comparison makes one thing clear. Both candidates spend their time on the issues that concern the wider public. They do so because they want votes. Foreign policy, budgets, health, the environment, jobs, drugs, and education are all subjects of constant discussion among scholars, journalists, politicians, and ordinary citizens.

Many of these problems are headaches for the whole country. Anyone who wants to win the presidency has to promise to deal with them.

Of course, the candidates themselves cannot know whether they will be able to solve these problems. But to win, they must offer plans. In most cases, the plans already exist in society, often in several different forms. The parties' job is to choose—or perhaps simply adopt—the proposals most likely to win sympathy and support.

Even “choose” may not be quite the right word. From the party's point of view, it is putting a product—the candidate—on the market. From the voter's point of view, it is shopping among the products already on offer.

This gives elections a strange logic. Do parties choose voters, or do voters choose parties?

Quite often, I suspect, it is the parties that choose the voters. The result is that parties need no single fixed programme. Party members can change their minds, and voters can support the left today and the right tomorrow. That is the power of elections. (See Chapter 5, Section 1, “Government by Donkey and Elephant.”)

There is another problem. During a campaign, nobody can really test whether a party *can* carry out its programme, or whether it even intends to. By the time voters can judge its performance, the election is over and the winners are in office. There is little they can do until the next election, when the same cycle may begin again.

During the campaign, the important thing is to make attractive promises.

Scholars have studied this problem extensively. Two interpretations are common.

One sees an election victory as a *mandate*: voters authorize a president to carry out the promises made during the campaign. The other treats an election as a judgment on the performance of the previous government. Voters decide whether they want a change. In this second view, the main question is whom to replace, not necessarily what the replacement will do.

Either way, candidates do whatever they think will help them capture the White House. The system demands extraordinary political flexibility and quick reactions. Candidates must be sensitive to changes in public opinion, like a barometer responding to the weather. Otherwise they have little hope of becoming president.

## 2. The Road to the Presidency

By late October 1988, the presidential campaign is in full swing. The two parties' presidential and vice-presidential candidates have already taken part in three televised debates. Opinion polls fill the television screen. Candidates travel from state to state, making speeches. In just a few weeks, the country will vote.

The American president may be the most powerful head of government and head of state in the world. Backed by the country's extraordinary strength, a president can easily begin to believe that he has the power to shape the course of history.

Listen to the candidates and you hear the same theme from both: each promises to keep America in first place in the world.

Richard Nixon's recent book, *1999: Victory Without War*, has become extremely popular. One passage reveals the outlook of someone who has occupied the presidency. Nixon argues that peace and freedom cannot survive unless America plays a central role in international affairs. He recalls André Malraux saying that America was the first country in history to become a world power without deliberately seeking that position. But Nixon adds that if America fails to lead the free world, there will soon be no free world left to lead.

It takes a remarkable opinion of one's own country to speak in such sweeping terms.

Where do American presidents come from? At first, that sounds like a strange question. Under the American political system, voters elect the president. In principle, anyone who wins enough votes can walk into the Oval Office of the White House. And people do cast real ballots, choosing between the Democratic and Republican candidates. A candidate who fails to win sufficient support cannot become president.

But it would be a serious mistake to conclude that everyone has a realistic chance of reaching that office.

In the abstract, any citizen who meets the legal requirements can be president. In practice, over the two centuries since the country was founded, only a little over forty people have been so fortunate. The rest may dream about the job, but that is usually as far as it goes.

Long before the day of the national vote, the process that produces a president has travelled a long road. Much of that process is not part of the formal machinery of government, but it is central to American political life.

Candidates from small parties generally disappear without making much impression. After all their effort and noise, they may gain a little name recognition, or perhaps improve their physical fitness from all the travelling, but little else. Only the Republican and Democratic candidates have a serious chance of winning the final contest.

That means anyone who wants to be president must first secure the nomination of one of those parties. This alone excludes a huge number of people. And within each party, the people able to decide the nomination form a relatively small circle.

Small groups of influential party members meet in what Americans call *caucuses*. They put forward candidates for public office, including state positions and the presidency. These meetings operate at different levels. At state level, influential members of state legislatures may play the central part. National contests have their own caucuses and procedures. The Iowa caucuses have been especially important in the 1988 election.

Then come the *primaries*, the party's internal elections, followed by the party conventions. Delegates come from state and local organizations, giving these later stages a broader base than the small caucuses. A limited field of people from the same party is put forward, and the party eventually settles on the candidate it considers best suited to compete in the national election.

Look at this process and it becomes clear why ordinary people seldom reach the nomination stage. The opportunity belongs mainly to those the two parties are prepared to accept.

But there is something else worth noticing. Much of the difficult work of organizing the candidates and narrowing down the field is done by the parties, not by the government.

Any large democratic election needs some way to reduce the number of candidates to a manageable size. Imagine asking the government to decide who is entitled to stand and who must be excluded. That would almost certainly violate democratic principles. The parties take on a task the government could not easily perform. Every democratic society has to consider what kind of arrangement can do this work.

The national party conventions are major events. Hundreds or thousands of activists gather together, while television networks carry proceedings live. Each convention does two main things: it draws up the party's platform—its statement of policies—and chooses its presidential nominee.

All the other voters can then choose from the nominees the parties have selected, or refuse to support them.

So the delegates matter enormously. States choose them in different ways. Some elect delegates; some give them a degree of freedom in deciding whom to support; some allow delegates to make up their minds entirely; and some elect part of the delegation while county and state party organizations appoint others.

To win the nomination, a hopeful candidate must therefore begin by influencing who becomes a convention delegate. This variety of methods fits America's generally varied political arrangements. It is one of the country's striking features.

Nor can just anyone become a delegate. Many are already politicians or officials: members of Congress, governors, mayors, state legislators, and other important party figures. Ordinary citizens may find themselves watching from the sidelines.

The delegates' job is to decide which personalities voters will eventually be offered. The two parties choose one presidential candidate each. For most voters, the final election is a choice between those two people. Independent candidates may appear in individual places, but they rarely gain a national platform.

Once the nominations are settled, the presidential campaign begins in earnest. This is American democracy on display—but it is a democratic contest between two people, or at most a handful.

I call it an *incomplete contest*, much as economists talk about markets in which competition is limited.

Without the parties' earlier, less democratic work of narrowing the field, the democratic stage might be impossible. What would happen if several hundred people ran serious nationwide campaigns at once?

The final campaign is nevertheless important and genuine. The two nominees race around the country, make speeches, and appeal for support. Their victory depends on how citizens vote. Voters have little choice of candidates, but the choice they *do* have is enough to keep each candidate anxious: they can always vote for the other person.

There is another complication. When Americans vote for president, they do not directly elect the president. They choose members of the *Electoral College*.

Each state has a certain number of electoral votes, equal to its representation in Congress. In the figures used here, California has forty-seven and Iowa has eight. The presidential candidate who wins a state takes all its electoral votes. This makes large and populous states especially important.

That is why the second debate between Bush and Dukakis in 1988 was held in Los Angeles. California's forty-seven votes were worth fighting for. California and New York together accounted for eighty-three votes, while 270 were needed to win the presidency.

The parties select the electors through state conventions, party committees, and other procedures. The electors are expected to support their party's official nominee. On election day, voters are therefore choosing the electors rather than voting directly for the president.

Only the voters on the winning side in each state see their preference reflected in the state's electoral votes. Everyone else's choice is effectively overridden by the majority in that state.

On the first Monday after the second Wednesday in December, the winning electors meet in their state capitals, submit their certificates, and complete the formalities. By then, the result is already known. The meeting has largely become a ceremony.

Money creates another enormous obstacle for an ordinary person who dreams of becoming president. Campaign spending has reached staggering levels. In 1972, the officially reported campaign expenditure was $10 million—never mind money that was not formally reported. The 1988 total has yet to be announced.

Beyond a certain point, the sums make no practical difference to an ordinary citizen: they are simply impossible to raise. Presidential campaigns usually depend on donations and gifts. Those who give money have reasons for doing so, and those who receive it understand that perfectly well. Rockefeller alone spent more than $3 million on various election expenses. How could an ordinary person enjoy the same political opportunity?

My purpose is not to argue that the American political system fails to work. I want to raise a different question: if a large society must eventually choose a single political leader, how far can democracy truly extend?

America's elections show how hard that question is. Governing a society and running its politics require some process that brings many competing views toward a single decision. If democracy were complete and unlimited at every stage, America might not even be able to elect a president.

The parties bring that concentration about outside the formal government system. They gather support and narrow choices through methods that are not especially democratic. Only *after* that has happened do voters make their selection. The public gets the second turn.

Why, then, does the whole process still appear democratic? I see two main reasons.

First, the parties carry out this work outside the institutions of the state. They cannot simply use government offices to carry out their plans. Because their activity takes place outside the formal political system, it does not count as part of the system's official exercise of power.

The more tightly controlled stage is treated as social and party activity rather than government activity. Parties influence politics chiefly by getting their candidates elected. Once those candidates take office, they can carry out their aims using lawful government procedures.

Every society needs two kinds of political activity: a process that concentrates decisions enough to produce leaders, and a process that allows those leaders to govern through arrangements with wider public participation. America's formal political system chiefly handles the second task. The parties handle much of the first.

Second, the parties themselves have developed fairly regular ways of doing things, though the procedures of the Democrats and Republicans are not identical. Their meetings and conventions are influenced by party leaders, but they still leave room for disagreement. Different candidates from the same party can compete.

This helps conceal how much power is ultimately concentrated in the final decision. It also gives the parties a way to manage disagreements among their own members. I will return to the political parties elsewhere.

There is something remarkable about the American political system. You cannot simply call it undemocratic. But you cannot simply call it democratic either.

That is why I call the American election an *incomplete contest*.

## 3. A Picnic with the Local Democrats

Looking at the parties' role in a presidential election gives us a sense of how they operate at the national level. But a party cannot function without its local organizations. Unless people at each level are active and effective, the party becomes an empty shell.

The weekend is a familiar American institution. Most people take Saturday and Sunday off. By Friday, everyone is saying, “Have a good weekend.” Even that little greeting reveals something about the way people live. Perhaps our distant ancestors used to greet one another with “Catch a rabbit!”

On September 16, 1988, I met a few university students campaigning for Dukakis and local Democratic candidates at the University of Iowa's student activity centre. They had set up a little table outside the cafeteria: two young women and a young man. The women seemed rather shy, hardly the sort of people one would immediately associate with politics. The young man, however, was full of energy.

When he heard that I taught political science and wanted to learn how local politics worked, he was delighted. He immediately invited me to a weekend Democratic Party dinner, or *picnic*, organized at what the source calls the party's “third level.”

“Invited” does not quite mean invited for free. There was a charge. These gatherings are held largely to raise money for candidates.

We arrived at about five in the afternoon. The meeting place lay in a quiet area outside Iowa City, perhaps a farm or a park. The facilities were simple: a large building rather like the meeting hall where I had worked during my years at a cadre school, except that this one had a proper floor. Outside stood a wooden shelter.

Two young men were selling drinks beneath it. Since the whole purpose of the event was to raise money, there was no question of giving everyone unlimited free refreshments. Several young women were selling T-shirts printed with campaign slogans or candidates' names. That, too, was a way of spreading the message.

There was also a Polaroid camera and a life-size picture of Dukakis. You could stand beside the picture and have your photograph taken, as though you had met the candidate himself. It looked surprisingly convincing.

But the main way of raising money was through the meal charge. It was quite expensive: eight dollars for one person, twenty-four for a family, and six for a child. No one was forced to come. It was a case of putting out the fishing line and waiting for volunteers to bite.

Most of those who attended were active Democrats, so they were happy to contribute. Their enthusiasm was not entirely detached from their own interests. Among them were members of Congress, state legislators, senators, and other prominent local people. They had needed Democratic Party support to win their positions, so they naturally took the party's gatherings seriously. Indeed, they could hardly afford not to.

This creates a network of personal relationships. Without it, a politician loses access to many useful resources.

You might expect people in a society so devoted to individual freedom and private life not to care much about such networks. What I found was the opposite. People cared a great deal. Those who were not part of the circle worked hard to get in, and not everyone arrived with entirely innocent motives.

The leading local Democrats were all there. Their presence mattered to the gathering, but the gathering also mattered to them. On one side, the organizers needed important visitors to make the event look impressive. On the other, the politicians needed to show their faces. It was both an obligation and part of creating a favourable public image.

One of the most prominent people was a member of Congress named Nagel. He wore an apple-green T-shirt and walked around greeting everyone, looking relaxed and approachable.

Another was the Democratic candidate for governor of Iowa, John Christal—a tall man with glasses and a scholarly manner. He moved easily through the crowd. I went over to talk to him. He was friendly and asked what I thought would happen to Gorbachev's reforms.

I said Gorbachev faced many difficulties. Christal replied that the Soviet system was deeply entrenched and would be hard to change.

For someone involved in local politics to take such an interest in an international question was unusual, at least in my experience. Americans active in local affairs often have a limited view of the world, something that does not seem to match their country's position as a major power.

The conversation made me curious about Christal. Someone happened to hand me his campaign literature, so I read it.

John Christal was the son of an Iowa farmer. He graduated from the University of Iowa with a degree in economics in 1949. From 1949 to 1959 he worked in farming, before moving into banking. Between 1960 and 1984 he served as chairman of an Iowa savings bank, and in 1973 he headed a state bankers' association.

The brochure said his financial skills had saved many small farmers and agricultural banks from collapse. He had also been nominated as Iowa's “Farm Leader of the Year” in 1987.

He had taken an active part in Democratic politics since 1956. The brochure also claimed that for twenty-five years he had been among the most respected advisers on Soviet affairs. How he acquired that particular expertise I could not discover. Judging from his career, business had been his main occupation and politics a secondary one.

Many American politicians are not politicians by profession. They take public office when the opportunity or need arises. At the top, Carter had grown peanuts and Reagan had been an actor. The same pattern reaches down to officials in towns and cities. Perhaps this is one of the distinctive features of American political life.

It means that many officeholders have somewhere to return to when their political careers end. No special arrangement has to be made for them.

More important, they bring years of business experience and practical social skills into government. That can make government particularly capable of handling economic questions and problems of social development. Local government is often less like a purely political organization and more like a large company managing a range of public affairs.

Voters, after all, tend to judge politicians by the practical benefits they receive. Abstract political principles matter less. Christal's long experience with the economy was therefore a valuable qualification for public office.

I also met a state senator named Jane, whom I had interviewed in 1987 about the role and workings of the state senate. She was handing out fans printed with her name—a simple way to keep herself in people's minds. This was exactly the sort of occasion where she needed to be seen.

Several other leading Democrats from the county were there. Kerry Bowen, Nagel's representative in the county, joked that if I attended the Republican picnic, I must not tell them what I had learned here. The parties were, after all, fighting an election.

This gathering helped me understand something important. Although the Democratic and Republican parties have loose organizations and relatively little internal discipline, both have effective ways of working from the national level down to the local level.

Local activities are rarely guided by a detailed national set of political ideas. Their immediate purpose is to win votes in a particular district—often for a local or state candidate rather than the presidential nominee. Yet these activities strengthen people's sense of belonging to a party, whether Democratic or Republican.

A party does not exist only in its offices and committees. It exists in people's minds. Party identity is political, but also cultural and emotional. One reason the two major parties work so effectively across the country is that they have huge, energetic networks at the local level.

What holds the whole structure together is a web of interests.

At the national level, neither party has a single, clearly defined interest shared by everyone who supports it. When states back a presidential platform, they may genuinely be responding to beliefs and values rather than to a precise calculation of their own material advantage.

But state and local party organizations have interests and preferences of their own, sometimes very different ones. They use the banner of a national party to pursue those local purposes. The national organization is quite willing to let them do so, because it helps keep the wider alliance together.

Through their activities, local organizations advance the aims of powerful people in their communities. And this creates a curious result: although most are concerned mainly with local interests, they end up strengthening the national party as a whole.

That is one of the remarkable features of the American party system.

## 4. Debating on Television

The 1988 presidential election is as busy and noisy as ever. Dukakis and George Bush travel constantly, making speeches and campaigning in one place after another. Every day brings a new event, a new appearance, a new attempt to win votes.

I have already compared their policies. But the real purpose of all these appearances is not simply to teach voters what the candidates believe. America's news media are developed enough that people can find out their positions without attending a rally.

The deeper purpose is to become better known, build an appealing image, create a personal connection, and make the campaign seem strong and energetic.

Many voters no longer think mainly in terms of party platforms. They pay close attention to a candidate's personality, ability, and appearance. One university professor told me he disliked Bush.

“Why?” I asked.

“His face doesn't look trustworthy,” he said. “Dukakis looks much more reliable.”

What sort of political standard is that?

One high point of the election was the televised debate between Bush and Dukakis on the evening of September 25. It lasted ninety minutes and was broadcast across the country. Many people looked forward to it. Professors in the University of Iowa's political science department had been talking about it for days—perhaps because it was their field of study.

Others paid no attention at all. Afterward, television reporters interviewed people in the street who said they had not watched or had hardly noticed it.

The political science professors happened to be having a picnic that afternoon. As the broadcast approached, they got into their cars and went home to watch.

I watched at the home of a professor. His wife, who worked with lasers, sat down to follow the programme too. Their son, a primary-school pupil, also seemed fascinated. He stayed right to the end.

The debate began at seven. Two lecterns stood facing the audience, one for Bush and one for Dukakis. In front of them sat four television journalists, all experienced reporters, who would ask the questions. Several hundred spectators sat farther back in the large hall.

Red and green lights on the journalists' table helped keep track of speaking time. The journalists sat facing the candidates, their backs to the audience. They remained seated while the two men seeking the country's highest office stood before them.

The procedure was simple. A journalist asked one candidate a question. He answered. Then his opponent commented on the answer—or, more accurately, challenged it. The two candidates received roughly equal numbers of questions.

The exchanges were sharp. At one point Bush remarked that he hoped it would not be too friendly an evening. They argued about taxes, the budget deficit, education, drugs, abortion, weapons, and international relations.

Much of what they said repeated positions they had already expressed elsewhere. But facing each other before a live audience, they also needed quick thinking and a sense of humour.

Early on, Dukakis joked that if Bush could deliver everything he promised, he would become the “Joe Isuzu of American politics”—a reference to a television advertising character famous for extravagant sales claims. The audience burst out laughing.

Bush soon found a joke of his own. He said one of Dukakis's answers was “as clear as Boston Harbor.” Boston, in Dukakis's home state of Massachusetts, was notorious for pollution. Bush was saying that the governor's explanation was as murky as the harbour water.

There were more pointed exchanges. Dukakis said he wanted to make absolutely clear that he would never deal with Panama's dictator, Manuel Noriega. Bush replied that seven American administrations had dealt with Noriega, but the Reagan–Bush administration was bringing him to justice.

Dukakis said that many Americans found the idea of “President Quayle” deeply worrying. Dan Quayle was Bush's running mate. Bush defended him as someone who had performed extremely well.

Dukakis also accused Bush of questioning his patriotism. “I love this country,” he insisted, making clear his faith in it. Bush denied questioning Dukakis's love of America. The real question, he argued, was whether Dukakis would take the country too far to the left, away from the American mainstream.

There was applause and laughter at various moments throughout the ninety minutes.

Under pressure, both candidates also made mistakes. Bush once called himself “president” when he meant “vice-president.” Dukakis made a slip that brought laughter from the audience.

Bush was the more practised speaker. At one point, halfway through an answer, he could not remember the name of a defence system. He covered himself by saying that only an iceman never makes mistakes. Later, a moderator miscalculated the time and interrupted Bush before his turn was over. When Bush protested, the moderator apologized and replied, “You said everyone makes mistakes.”

Both men were thinking carefully about every word, even their ways of addressing one another. Bush repeatedly called Dukakis “the governor of Massachusetts,” encouraging viewers to see him as no more than a state-level politician. Dukakis called Bush “Mr. Bush” or simply “George,” avoiding the title of vice-president.

These details often seemed more important than the substance of the policies. Much depended on which candidate could win over voters emotionally.

Appearance mattered too. Newspaper commentators praised both men's speech, voices, and styles. They were experienced politicians, and both knew how to talk. In America, the first requirement for a successful politician seems to be the ability to speak well.

Even their clothes had been planned. Frank Greer, a Dukakis adviser, said the governor brought several shirts and ties to make sure he looked right on camera. He appeared in a blue-grey suit, white shirt, and red tie. Bush wore much the same combination.

Americans do not see a politician's image as a purely personal matter. It affects whether ordinary people feel they can trust that person. Candidates therefore pay enormous attention to it.

As soon as the debate ended, opinions poured in. Some said Bush had won; others said Dukakis. These judgments were often coloured by people's existing loyalties. Members of both campaign teams, interviewed immediately afterward, naturally insisted that their own candidate had performed better.

The television networks themselves disagreed. NBC said it was hard to pick a winner. ABC praised both candidates but thought Dukakis had done slightly better. CBS said neither had offered anything new, although Dukakis might have had a small edge. CNN declared Bush the winner.

Opinion polls were organized almost immediately to see which man had impressed more viewers.

My own impression was that the two had performed about equally well. Bush was more experienced, especially in international affairs and national politics. Dukakis was weaker in those areas. But Dukakis had an advantage of another kind: expectations of Bush were higher. If Dukakis could hold his own against him, he did not have to win a clear victory to satisfy the public.

The whole arrangement is fascinating because it is so open. A contest to choose the highest executive official becomes a public debate that the entire country can watch.

Of course, the candidates arrive with the backing of powerful political parties, and ordinary citizens have little chance of becoming candidates themselves. But citizens can join a party, volunteer for a campaign, canvass for votes, and give money. Anyone who hopes to win must pay attention to public relations.

The American political system pushes parties and political groups toward openness. Without being seen and heard, they cannot get elected.

Two forces drive this openness. One comes from parties competing to win. The other comes from the public. In many societies, demands for greater openness arise mainly among ordinary people. Here, the demands of electoral competition also play a decisive part.

As material life becomes more comfortable, many voters pay less attention to abstract political principles and detailed programmes. They want a direct impression of the person asking to lead them. The election system encourages this tendency. Candidates need votes, and without them they have no path to office. They must therefore make themselves as visible as possible—visiting places, appearing on television, making speeches, and showing the public what they are like.

Some voters worry about Quayle partly because he is young and perhaps inexperienced. Entrusting public affairs to someone like that does not feel reassuring to them.

Americans, in fact, are often less interested in politics as an abstract activity than in the individual person involved. We might call this the *depoliticizing of politics*: politics becomes a judgment about personalities.

Televised debates also help citizens know more about their choices. For a long time, adults have had the right to vote without necessarily knowing who the candidates really were—their character, abilities, strengths, or beliefs. The ballot could be cast, yet the choice might hardly be democratic in any meaningful sense.

An important measure of democracy is whether people can choose with enough knowledge to make their choice count.

That is extraordinarily difficult to achieve. America has not achieved it perfectly either.

## 5. Dreaming of Congress

Elections to the House of Representatives take place at the same time as presidential elections. On election day—November 8 in 1988—Americans would vote for both their president and their representative in Congress. So while the presidential race was gathering speed, candidates for Congress were campaigning just as hard.

I contacted the Republican Party's county headquarters in Iowa City and asked if I could observe a congressional campaign. The people there were very welcoming. They arranged for me to follow their candidate, Don Redfeln, as he campaigned around the city.

Members of the House are elected by district. Districts are drawn according to population, with roughly the same number of people in each, and each district chooses one representative. In this way, each member of the House represents about the same number of people. A district might cover one county or several, though things are different in big cities. Iowa is a farming state with a relatively small population, so its districts cover quite large areas.

Both major parties have campaign offices in every district. Iowa City belongs to the third district, and below the district office there is a county office. These grand-sounding "headquarters" are usually nothing more than rented rooms with one or two people working in them. The Republican office in Iowa City occupied the second floor of a building. It was fairly spacious, with an area large enough for twenty-odd chairs and a speech. Two or three people worked there. One young woman was a regular employee; the others were volunteers. Their job was to arrange appearances for the party's candidate in the county.

One item on Redfeln's schedule was breakfast with local business leaders. These people are the county's elite—something like the leading citizens or local notables of an earlier age. They have a club of their own and meet for breakfast every Tuesday to exchange news, discuss business and listen to invited speakers. They are a powerful local circle. Anyone hoping to win an election here must first try to win their support.

Breakfast began at seven. After everyone had eaten for a few minutes, Redfeln gave a speech. He discussed his views on domestic and foreign affairs, mostly the familiar positions he had given many times before. As a Republican, he agreed with the party's presidential candidate, Bush, on many issues. The businesspeople then asked questions, and Redfeln answered them one by one.

Afterward they played a little game. Each person received a slip of paper and was asked to cast a pretend vote. Bush received eighteen votes; Dukakis received two. Of course, with a Republican candidate speaking that morning, this result was hardly surprising. Had the guest been a Democrat, things might have been very different.

As eight o'clock approached, the businesspeople began leaving. By eight, only four remained. Breakfast was over.

Business leaders have their own organizations. No one can win an election without paying attention to them.

Campaigning is exhausting. Candidates spend their days rushing from one place to another, with something to do at every stop. Around noon, Redfeln drove to Iowa City from a place 250 miles away so that he could attend the afternoon's events.

Our next stop was the University of Iowa's teaching hospital, an institution known throughout the country for its equipment and medical care. Redfeln drove himself. An American professor and I rode with him. There was no entourage, no one rushing ahead to clear the way.

At the hospital we were taken to a meeting room. Only sixteen to eighteen people were there. Five had come with Redfeln, including us; seven were journalists; the rest worked at the hospital. I was surprised by how small the audience was. Redfeln did not seem bothered. He gave a lively speech about his main domestic and foreign-policy positions, took questions, and finished. He then set aside twenty minutes for the reporters, speaking to each of them and answering several questions apiece.

That explained why the small audience did not matter very much. Through newspapers, television and radio, thousands of people who had not been in the room could still hear about him.

In America, finding an audience may be the hardest part. Even visiting professors giving university lectures often struggle to attract listeners. A candidate for Congress seems to have little more drawing power. It is one of the interesting things about American politics: anyone who wants public office must ask something of the voters, not the other way around.

After the hospital, we drove to a student residence. Redfeln's task there was to stand outside the student dining hall, shake hands and exchange a few words. He waited by the entrance and, whenever someone came along, introduced himself: "Hello, I'm Redfeln. I'm running for Congress." He would ask whether the student planned to vote, or say something else to make contact.

It was difficult work. The students seemed almost entirely uninterested. Most answered with a quick "Hi" and walked past. Few wanted to stop and talk; some were positively cold. Watching from the side, I thought how hard it must be to run for Congress. There was no entourage here either. There were just three of us: Redfeln, his assistant and me. This, too, was American politics.

His opponent was Nagel, the district's sitting representative, who was seeking reelection. Nagel had also visited the campus several times and given speeches. Redfeln did not seem to enjoy this particular task. After a short while he decided to move on. "Let's go," he told his assistant, plainly discouraged. "I've got a bad feeling about this." He had reason to feel that way.

The next stop was another residence hall, the Mayflower. The International Writing Program, well known in China, was housed in the same building. Once again, we drove ourselves. A female student welcomed us and led us to a meeting room where nearly twenty students were waiting.

Redfeln spoke for forty minutes about domestic and foreign affairs, placing particular emphasis on education. The students then asked questions about social security, schooling and other matters. The whole visit lasted an hour. Again, the audience was small.

Outside the meeting room was a student recreation area. Quite a few students were playing video games, pool or table tennis. Others were watching television. A congressional candidate was speaking in the building, but none of that seemed to draw them away from what they were doing.

That was the end of the day's events. Redfeln drove me back to the university, and on the way we talked about how candidates for Congress are chosen.

A candidate runs in a particular district. Anyone who believes they are capable of running can put themselves forward, provided they obtain the support of a required number of people. But first they must win a primary election—a vote among people seeking the nomination of the same party. Redfeln had won the Republican primary and become his party's candidate. The party supported his campaign with funds permitted by law and with other forms of help.

He told me that this support mattered enormously. Without a political party behind you, he said, winning was very difficult.

There is real competition in America's primaries. But each party's primary also removes everyone who fails to win its nomination. After that, only the two major-party candidates have a realistic chance of winning the seat. In practice, the election becomes a contest between them.

The two-party system also has a way of sorting through candidates and settling disagreements—a job government itself could never carry out. Because people compete within the parties, different views can be heard without necessarily splitting the organization apart. Those who lose a vote are eliminated through a recognized procedure, rather than left nursing a personal grudge against someone. As one part of the way society manages political competition, this arrangement has its advantages.

Another thing struck me. An election to an important office like Congress does not necessarily excite the public. Most people seem rather indifferent. In one sense, that indifference is a condition for democracy to work, though hardly an ideal one. If everyone felt passionately about every political question, democratic politics might find it difficult to function calmly and steadily. Someone has even suggested that democracy's greatest enemy may not be dictatorship, but democracy itself.

Apart from matters like taxation, politics in America often has little immediate effect on voters' daily lives. It is not hard to see why many people simply shrug their shoulders. But that indifference forces candidates to do everything they can to attract attention: travel, speak, persuade and make themselves known.

It also pushes the parties to put forward candidates who can make a good impression. Appearance, character, experience, speaking ability, knowledge, conduct and a talent for dealing with people all matter. Someone who cannot present themselves convincingly has little hope of winning voters over.

Redfeln, for example, was a lawyer with considerable experience. He looked the part, spoke extremely well, had a strong sense of humour and knew how to get along with people. I imagine Nagel had many of the same qualities. The election system and the party system together help produce capable candidates. Remove either one, and the outcome could become absurd.

Redfeln largely ran his own campaign. Very few people worked for him. He talked about the practical issues that ordinary people cared about, not necessarily because those were the subjects he would have chosen for himself, but because the election forced him to. Yet another force was even more important: the political party. An ordinary person without a party behind them has little chance of winning.

## 6. Election Day

November 8 was election day in 1988. After nearly a year of hard campaigning, the voters were finally about to decide who would be president. On the same day, they would elect members of the House of Representatives, some senators, some state governors, and officials in a number of counties. Holding all these elections together saves the time that separate elections would require.

Before they can vote, Americans must register. Anyone who misses the registration deadline cannot vote in that election. People can register as Republicans or Democrats, or choose not to identify with either party. A special office of the county government runs the election. It keeps voters' names on a computer, sorted by small local voting areas called precincts. The records contain names, Social Security numbers, addresses and other details.

The parties begin encouraging people to register with them well before election day. On university campuses, students who support one party or another often set up tables where passers-by can register. People can also register at party offices or with the election authorities.

Each party's headquarters has a list of people registered with it. As election day approaches, staff become busy checking those lists. Local party workers contact their supporters. They used to go from door to door; now they generally telephone. Volunteers get to work distributing campaign leaflets and doing other tasks.

On election day, every precinct has a polling place. Voters drive there themselves. I watched the process at one of these places and saw a steady stream of people arriving. They seemed to feel a duty to vote. Think what it means to drive ten or fifteen minutes, stand in a queue and then cast a ballot. People would hardly do it without a strong belief that voting matters.

That belief is one of the conditions that allows the American political system to keep working, and to endure for two hundred years. If nobody cared enough to vote, nobody would be showing any interest in keeping the system alive. How could it survive? Of course, material conditions also make participation easier. When nearly every family has a car, getting to a polling place is simple, especially in the countryside.

Even so, voting in America is less convenient in some respects than in certain Western European countries. Voters must register beforehand; elections are not held on a public holiday; and there are requirements concerning how long someone has lived in a place. These difficulties are often cited as reasons for America's relatively low voter turnout.

Inside the polling place I visited, voters first filled out a small form with their name, address and Social Security number. They went to another worker, who located their registration in a computer-printed list. The voter signed in to show that they had arrived, then went to a third person for a ballot.

The ballot was a long strip of paper, perhaps forty centimetres long and fifteen wide, though ballots vary from state to state. Candidates' names were printed on it. At the top were spaces for the Democratic and Republican parties. If someone wanted to support every candidate of one party, a single mark beside that party's name would do the job.

But voters could also choose candidate by candidate. Farther down the ballot were the names of people running for president—including candidates from smaller parties—as well as candidates for the Senate, House and county offices. Someone could choose a few Republicans and a few Democrats, mixing their votes however they wished. The result of the election showed that people did just that: the Republicans won the presidency, while the Democrats won most seats in the House. Those seats mattered even though the party had lost the White House.

After marking the ballot, the voter covered it with a sleeve and put it in a ballot box. The box had an automatic counter to record how many ballots had been deposited. When voting ended at nine in the evening, the ballots were taken to the county election office to be counted by computer. From nine onward, television stations kept reporting the returns from the different states.

Many polling-place workers were volunteers. Rules were posted on the walls: no campaigning inside the polling place, no showing your ballot to another person, and so on.

What were the political parties doing while all this went on? Their campaign workers could not enter the polling places to urge people to vote for their candidates. But each party sent an observer to every polling place, carrying a list of voters. As someone arrived and signed in, election staff would let the observers know. Each observer marked the person's name on their own list.

This served two purposes. First, it helped watch for anyone trying to vote twice. Each party feared cheating by the other. Second, it told the party which of its supporters had still not turned up. Workers could telephone those people, ask whether they needed help, and sometimes arrange practical assistance, such as someone to look after their children. The process was called "pulling the poll"—making sure your supporters actually came out to vote.

Americans do not take the possibility of cheating lightly. They are watchful of one another. Precautions begin with the parties' primaries, which are held at the same time so that a person cannot vote in both. On election day, too, each party posts observers at polling places.

The counting began after nine. Since the mainland United States stretches across several time zones, the eastern states finished voting first. Television began announcing their results immediately, with figures changing as more votes were counted. Viewers could follow every shift.

In this election, the outcome was already clear by around eleven o'clock Central Time: Bush had won. Yet California and several other western states had only just closed their polls and begun to count votes. People in the West were angry. They felt it was unfair that the country already knew its next president before their votes had been counted. It made their votes seem useless. This is one of the weaknesses of the American system, and some people were proposing a law that would require polling places across the country to close at the same moment.

Of course, the result became clear early only because the eastern and central states had voted so decisively. Had the contest there been close, western votes could have made all the difference. California, with its large population and many electoral votes, would have carried particular weight.

The way Americans elect their president deserves closer examination. They use an Electoral College. Each state receives a certain number of electoral votes according to its representation, with larger states having more and smaller states fewer. In the system Wang describes, the number of individual votes does not translate directly into a national presidential result. It determines which party receives the state's electoral votes. If the Republicans win a state by even one vote, all that state's electoral votes go to their candidate. In that sense, the ballots cast for the Democratic candidate in that state count for nothing toward the final electoral tally.

Look at the figures from 1988. Bush received 47 million votes, or 54 percent, while Dukakis received 41 million, or 46 percent. The popular-vote gap was not enormous. But in electoral votes the difference was striking: 426 for Bush, or 79 percent, against 112 for Dukakis, or 21 percent.

Elections depend on the rule of the majority. One extra vote is enough to turn one side into the majority and the other into the minority. It is an old political difficulty that has never been fully solved. Must ten million people obey ten million and one? Yet anyone who accepts democratic elections must also accept that possibility. Lose this time, and the answer is to win that one extra voter—or many more—next time.

When the result became known, both presidential candidates made speeches. Dukakis said he had telephoned Vice President Bush a few minutes earlier to congratulate him on his victory. Bush said he had just received a sincere call from Governor Dukakis, describing it as one of the great traditions of American politics. He added that he hoped to be president even for those who had not voted for him.

Both thanked the workers who had contributed to their campaigns, their families and their running mates. The losing candidate and his supporters recognized the winner as president. There was no talk of refusing to accept the result and setting up a rival camp.

That is where we can see how orderly and well-established this system has become. The process by which the major parties choose their presidential candidates is not open to everyone; it is largely in the parties' hands. But the final election result is real. The losing party cannot simply refuse to recognize it or pursue some alternative plan. Even if somebody wanted to, who would follow them?

A mature political system has a particular quality: people accept that a result produced by the agreed rules must be challenged through those same rules, not overturned by methods outside them. If you want a different result, you must go through the process again. The law decides when you may do so.

People trust rules not simply because they want to restrict themselves, but because they want protection from others. A procedure that one person might use against another can also be used in the opposite direction. The system becomes established as more people understand this, and as society develops the practical conditions needed to maintain it.

## 7. Who Decides the Outcome?

After fighting through one contest after another, Bush became America's forty-first president. Dukakis had fought just as hard, yet he lost. Why did one win and the other fail? The answer tells us something about more than two men. It shows how the American electoral process works.

In a special election issue of *Time* dated November 21, 1988, Richard Stengel identified nine turning points in the presidential campaign. In his account, those moments helped decide the outcome. His article offers a useful starting point for looking more closely at the process.

**1. A last-minute decision in New Hampshire.** Early in the Republican primaries, Bush was behind Senator Bob Dole and Robertson in New Hampshire. He called in Peggy Noonan, one of Reagan's favourite speechwriters, who travelled back to the state with him aboard Air Force Two. Noonan worked through the night to produce a speech that presented a new image of Bush.

Meanwhile, media adviser Roger Ailes wanted Bush to attack Dole. Two other advertising advisers disagreed: they thought it unwise to expose the faults of a fellow Republican. Bush initially sided with them. Then Haig came out for Dole. With only three days to go, opinion polls suggested Bush could not win unless he ran an advertisement attacking his rival.

Bush hesitated. Governor Sununu thought the advertisement should run, and Bush's wife did not see a serious problem with it either. Bush finally agreed. His campaign bought all the available advertising time on Thursday.

Dole's team had met on Wednesday and decided not to run negative advertisements. Their polls showed Dole ahead, so they did not rush to prepare anything new. When they later tried to book time for their existing advertisements, they found it was all taken. Bush won. Had he lost that primary, where would he be today?

**2. An endorsement that backfired.** Dukakis was doing well in the Democratic primaries, but he faced two strong opponents, Al Gore and Jesse Jackson. He believed he needed the support of New York mayor Koch and telephoned him.

Koch wanted to know where Dukakis stood on Jackson. Dukakis was vague. Koch opposed Jackson and was dissatisfied with the answer. Gore, by contrast, behaved respectfully and appealed to Koch, who chose to endorse him. But Koch's feelings toward Jackson were so fierce that, at the event announcing his support for Gore, he launched into an attack on Jackson. The attack hurt Gore rather than helping him. Dukakis came out ahead.

**3. Bush chooses to attack.** Once the nominees were chosen, the race became a direct contest between Bush and Dukakis. Early polls gave Dukakis a lead of ten to twelve percentage points. Bush's aides urged him to focus on his opponent's weaknesses. Sununu proposed attacking Dukakis over the Fuluhi plan, pollution in Boston Harbor and overcrowded prisons.

Bush hesitated, hoping to wait until the Republican national convention. But the polls suggested that by then Dukakis's lead might reach twenty points. Bush made up his mind and went on the offensive.

**4. Dukakis chooses a running mate.** Dukakis's advisers thought he should pick someone from the South to strengthen his ticket, as Kennedy had done. He had several possibilities, but chose Senator Bentsen, whom he regarded as an experienced politician.

**5. A difficult relationship with Jesse Jackson.** Rivalries continued inside the Democratic Party. Dukakis needed the backing of its leading figures, and Jackson was one of them. Jackson wanted to be the vice-presidential nominee, but Dukakis was not considering him. Jackson repeatedly sought a private discussion. Dukakis acted as though he did not understand, and Jackson began creating difficulties.

Dukakis's campaign chairman, Paul Brountas, telephoned Jackson. Jackson complained that Dukakis never sought his advice. Brountas replied that Jackson had never clearly declared his support for Dukakis. Jackson was taken aback: now he was being accused of doing too little. His aides urged him to offer an endorsement.

When Dukakis later called, Jackson's advisers encouraged him to bargain and speak indirectly. Dukakis was left confused and discouraged by the conversation. They met again five days later. Jackson was enthusiastic, but by then the moment had passed.

**6. Bush surprises his own advisers.** Bush had many possible running mates, among them Jack Kemp, Pete Domenici and Dole. He wanted his choice to show that he could make decisions independently. He discussed the matter several times with senior advisers, including James Baker, without reaching a decision.

In the end he chose Quayle. It was entirely his own decision. His staff did not know how to handle the choice or introduce Quayle to the press. They had not even prepared the necessary background material. They were caught off guard.

**7. Dukakis loses time reorganizing his campaign.** Dukakis's campaign lacked clear direction. He had dismissed Sasso, one of his most effective campaign workers, and the two rarely spoke. During the Democratic convention they stayed at the same hotel but never met.

As the situation worsened, Dukakis called Sasso, who recommended a clear campaign theme, more television advertising and a stronger campaign team. Dukakis still could not decide. Only in August, when his polling figures began falling, did he bring Sasso back. Sasso threw himself into the work, but too much time had been lost.

**8. The second television debate goes wrong for Dukakis.** Dukakis had performed respectably in the first debate, but the second was more important. His advisers urged him to fight as fiercely as Rambo in *First Blood*. Dukakis held back at first, then agreed to prepare attacks on Bush over Quayle, the Iran–Contra affair, abortion, patriotism, drugs and Boston Harbor.

On debate day, Dukakis felt ill. He cancelled three hours of practice scheduled for the morning. He was still tired in the afternoon and slept until five, leaving only an hour before the debate. Half an hour before it began, someone telephoned him. He spent twenty minutes on the call. His aides believed this prevented him from concentrating.

Less than a minute into the debate, the questioner raised a murder case. Dukakis had not prepared for that subject. Of the six attacks he had planned, he managed to bring up only one. By the end, the direction of the contest seemed clear.

**9. Bush prepares to fight back.** Bush had been unhappy with his own performance in the first debate. The following day he asked for a daily briefing note on a different issue so that he would be ready for the next one. He was eager to get to work.

He had used more than ten coaches for the first debate; this time he wanted to prepare largely on his own. Once, when a briefing note failed to arrive, he demanded that his aides produce it within an hour. On another occasion, aboard Air Force Two, he argued aloud with an imaginary Dukakis, practising his responses. Just before the debate, a coach chatted with him and made him laugh to ease the tension. When Bush walked onstage and saw how nervous Dukakis looked, he felt he knew what to expect.

That is one writer's account. It may be incomplete or concerned only with the surface of events. But it does reveal several crucial parts of a presidential campaign.

Personal relationships matter enormously. A candidate who mishandles them is unlikely to win. Strategy matters too. Without an effective plan, every stage of a campaign becomes harder, and a single decision can be decisive. The candidate's character and abilities also carry great weight. Many voters do not study complicated political questions; they judge the person standing before them.

This account shows how fierce the struggle can be, both inside the parties during the primaries and between the two nominees afterward. Nobody can afford to hold back.

So who really decides a presidential election? The answer is complicated. Different people have different kinds of influence at different stages and levels.

But we must remember who gets as far as those stages. Only people who have climbed to the top with the support of powerful groups ever reach that point. Ordinary citizens cannot simply enter the presidential contest, however much they might wish to. Once the two parties have chosen their candidates, those men face a vast sea. Each has to fight his way across it. Who sinks and who reaches the far shore depends on many forces acting together.

# Chapter 7: The Pyramid of Power

## 1. Capitol Hill

Congress is generally regarded as the centre of political power in America. In principle, it holds the highest authority. I do not want to spend pages explaining its formal organization and powers. Any textbook can do that. I would rather describe a few things I saw there.

One day in September 1988, shortly before noon, we drove up to the Capitol. It is a magnificent white building. The first thing I noticed on getting out of the car was the line of triangular concrete barriers arranged in a winding pattern along the approach. No vehicle could drive straight at the entrance. I was told the barriers were there to guard against terrorist attacks from the Middle East. Some extremists had threatened to blow up the Capitol, and the police were taking precautions. Armed officers stood outside the entrance.

More security officers waited at the door. Getting into the building involved much the same checks as boarding a plane. Yet the Capitol is open to ordinary visitors. Anyone can come in and look around. When the chambers are not meeting, tourists can even sit in the seats and see what it feels like to be a member of Congress.

Once inside, we first found Professor Sinclair, a well-known specialist on Congress. She had taught political science at the University of California, Berkeley, and was temporarily doing research in Congress, apparently also working as an assistant to the Speaker of the House. After a brief explanation of how Congress was organized, she showed us around.

We began with the corridors—the very place from which lobbyists take their name. Lobbyists try to persuade legislators to pass particular laws or influence legislation in other ways. I described their work earlier, in Chapter 5.

There were plenty of chairs in the corridors, making it quite comfortable for people to wait and talk. To me, this is one of the hardest parts of American political culture to understand. Politics is a serious business, involving the interests of the public. Lobbyists, by contrast, work for particular interests; their activities can seem like something best kept out of sight. Yet in America lobbying is open and lawful. Congress actually provides a comfortable place for it to happen.

Perhaps that reflects a businesslike way of thinking. A company has its own interests and can openly bargain for what it wants. That is considered legitimate. The director of the American Political Science Association's congressional research programme, who accompanied us, joked that China ought to send more people here.

Next we entered the chamber of the House of Representatives. The room looked old-fashioned. Its decoration and layout did not meet modern tastes, perhaps because the traditional appearance was deliberately preserved. The light-brown chairs looked rather rough. The technology, however, was modern.

Members voted electronically. Each had a card with a short magnetic strip carrying an identifying code. To vote, a member inserted the card into a device behind a row of seats and pressed a button. The choice immediately appeared on a huge electronic display at the front of the chamber. Everyone could see it.

Congressional debates were often broadcast live. A dedicated television channel covered political events, including presidential campaigns, Senate debates and votes in the House. Voters could therefore find out how their representatives had voted. It was a useful way of keeping watch over them.

There were several microphones in the chamber. Members wishing to speak walked to the front. But when one member was speaking, many others might not be there at all. This happened even more often in the Senate. Senators who were not interested in a particular debate could leave the chamber for coffee and conversation, or return to their offices. They followed developments on television and rushed back when it was time to vote.

Visit a senator's office at such a moment and you might find the senator suddenly breaking off in mid-conversation: "Excuse me, I have to go and vote!" Then off they run.

It is a curious sight. This is one of the country's highest lawmaking bodies, yet its members are not required to sit solemnly in their places throughout a debate. The attitude is practical: what matters is that Congress can make laws, not whether everyone sits in the chamber while doing so. For an individual member, what matters is having a real vote when the time comes. They do not have to fear some unexpected disaster simply because they are out of the room. Otherwise no one would dare go far from it.

After touring the Capitol, we went to meet a House member whom I will call P, from North Carolina. Members of Congress have their own offices, in separate buildings for the House and Senate. Entering an office building meant another airport-style security check.

Each representative had a large office, with the emblem of their home state displayed outside. Most offices were divided into two rooms: an inner office for the member and an outer one for a secretary and several assistants. Papers were piled everywhere.

We announced ourselves to P's secretary. She telephoned inside and asked us to wait. Then she warmly offered us bags of peanuts. We discovered that P's home area produced a great many peanuts. Even here, he was advertising a local product.

Soon P came out to greet us, and we sat down in his inner office. He was approachable, easy to talk to and good at picking up whatever subject his visitors raised. These are probably essential qualities for a member of Congress. Elections are sharp instruments; they show no mercy. A politician who wants to win must know how to reach voters and persuade them to trust him.

To keep those relationships alive, members of Congress employ quite a few assistants—around ten for a House member and twenty to thirty for a senator. They have offices not only in Washington but also in their home states or districts. House members are elected by district, so they maintain local offices and representatives at the county level to keep in contact with voters.

They also print regular newsletters and send them to constituents, explaining what they have been doing. P showed us one of his, called *David P's Washington Report*. It listed his achievements: working for a law to ease the burden on homeowners; gaining House support for his consumer and lending proposal; securing federal funds for medical and space research at two universities; paying attention to farming concerns; and helping residents of the fourth district through congressional services.

Americans are not afraid to sing their own praises. I had already heard that members of Congress send astonishing amounts of mail. Some of this postage is free to them, so they can send large numbers of letters to maintain contact with voters. During the visit, we saw an employee driving a small electric cart from office to office delivering mail. Outside one office stood more than a dozen cardboard boxes. I looked closer: they were labelled *Envelopes*. There must have been thousands of them.

Politicians do not spend so much time writing and mailing things because they have nothing better to do. The system demands it. To win, they must remain connected to their voters and represent them. Letters and newsletters are only part of the work. They also have to meet people, talk about daily life and make personal connections whenever they can.

The relationship between Congress and scholars is worth mentioning too. Members usually have assistants or advisers who help them understand specialist issues. Many of those assistants are young people studying subjects such as politics or law. Working in Congress gives them a chance to see politics from the inside.

I remembered a senator's visit to Fudan University years earlier. A car had arrived full of young fellows, and we had been surprised by it. But I now saw that this was an important part of American political life. It brings youthful energy into politics and gives young people practical training. It is one of the ways a society teaches a new generation how political life works.

Many political scientists have served as senior advisers. Quite a few will tell you which president or presidential candidate they advised, and on what issue. They also find it relatively easy to enter government institutions to observe and study how they operate.

On the way to the congressional offices, I asked Sinclair whether this was common. Very common, she said. The American Political Science Association even had a programme to encourage such research.

This exchange between universities and politics reflects America's practical spirit. Politicians are happy to seek scholars' advice. Kissinger and Brzezinski are familiar examples. Expert involvement can make policy better informed. It can also make the public feel more confident in a policy, precisely because experts have helped shape it.

The spirit of American politics is rather like the spirit of American business: look for the best product on the market. Many parts of politics have become specialized jobs. What remains at the heart of politics is dealing with disagreements and solving problems. People welcome those who can do that. Of course, they still make choices. The same piece of meat can be cooked in many ways, and not everyone will like every recipe.

## 2. A Union of Fifty States

America is a federal country. Its fifty states have considerable power of their own. The states existed before the federal government, and that history largely explains their position today. One of the founding ideas of the United States was to preserve the powers of the states and limit the authority of the national government. After all, the Constitution was drafted by representatives from those states, and they knew where their interests lay.

Thomas Dye describes five basic features of American federalism: the Constitution gives the national government its powers; it protects the states; it divides responsibilities between the two levels; it provides for the states to form a federal government; and the courts interpret what these constitutional arrangements mean.

Strictly speaking, a state government is not simply a local government. Each state has a high degree of independence and its own constitution and laws. The English word *state* can also mean a country. Chinese usually translates it as "province" or "state," since one country cannot ordinarily contain other countries. But in many respects American politics looks like a union of fifty small countries.

The federal government has grown enormously during the four decades since the Second World War, but the states have not easily surrendered their position. State politics remains extremely important. In many areas the federal government has neither the authority nor the ability to intervene. State leaders decide what happens within those areas.

This relative independence may be one reason American society, its economy and its culture have developed so quickly. Federalism divides the country into fifty smaller units. The full English name, *United States*, stresses that these states are joined together. In Chinese, its meaning might almost be rendered as "United Countries."

There is a limit to how much any government can manage effectively. Beyond that limit, its reach becomes too short. The different parts of the system no longer receive enough attention or support. This is not necessarily a fault in its political design. It is a problem of scale. Even the strongest person has only so much energy. Ask them to do more than they can manage, and strength alone will not save them. Governments are much the same. History offers plenty of examples.

For a large country, finding a suitable arrangement for governing itself can matter more than almost anything else. America's federal system has quietly performed that task. The federal government concentrates on its own responsibilities. The country as a whole is enormous, but each state is much more manageable. Governments at different levels can therefore deal with matters within their reach. Of course, this does not mean every country has the conditions needed for federalism.

States are not all governed in the same way. In one arrangement, called the **weak-governor system**, executive power is divided among many officials who act independently. The state constitution spells out the machinery of government in detail, and boards or commissions oversee different departments. Governors cannot appoint or dismiss the members of many of these bodies. Their own executive authority is limited, and much of the government workforce is outside their direct control. Many states still use some form of this arrangement.

The opposite is the **strong-governor system**. It grew out of reforms that tried to bring business-style management into government. Under this system, the governor has greater authority over the state's administration. Boards and independent agencies have fewer responsibilities, a cabinet is formed around the governor, and the governor prepares and supervises the budget.

Dye regards the governor as the central figure in state politics, and the public expects governors to take responsibility for their states' development. Within a state, the governor can play a role similar to the president's at the national level: chief administrator, an important force in lawmaking and a party leader. The governor coordinates departments, prepares budgets and oversees major government programmes.

The job is also widely seen as a stepping-stone to the presidency or vice-presidency. Reagan had governed California; Dukakis, the Democratic presidential candidate in 1988, governed Massachusetts. That makes the governor's office a prize worth fighting for, and the two parties often compete fiercely in state elections.

State administrations usually include departments dealing with finance, public welfare, highways, taxation, agriculture, industry, conservation, insurance, banking, education, budgets and services for people with disabilities. In weak-governor states, many such offices enjoy considerable independence.

Officials such as the treasurer, attorney general, secretary of state and auditor may be elected directly by the public, rather than appointed by the governor. They answer to voters and are not easily controlled by the governor. This gives specialist departments room to exercise their own authority and meet public needs, while keeping their officials under direct electoral supervision. Under a strong-governor system, such independent officials may not exist, or may work under the governor.

In practice, however, governors of either kind can often find ways to influence departments and expand their own power.

Alongside the administration stands the state legislature, whose main job is to make laws. States generally have a House and a Senate, though they do not all use exactly the same names. Most legislatures are called a *General Assembly*; some use *General Court*. State senates range in size from nineteen to sixty-seven members, while lower houses range from thirty-nine to four hundred. Their organization and procedures resemble those of Congress and are laid down in law.

The importance of state politics shows up in other ways. Interest groups work vigorously at state level, and lobbyists are frequent visitors. More striking still, the state is a central level of organization for the two major political parties.

Party organizations build upward from small precincts. At state level they have state committees and executive committees. But American parties are not tightly controlled from a national centre. National party offices have relatively little direct authority. State parties usually decide their own affairs, and in some states county and city organizations enjoy independence too.

State party committees organize campaigns for governor, US senator and member of the House. County and city committees organize elections for local offices and state legislatures. The national committee becomes most visible every four years, when it organizes the national convention and the choice of a presidential nominee.

Because state parties operate so independently, some people describe each of America's two major parties as a union of fifty parties—or even more. The national committee is more like a meeting place for the state parties than a body that commands them.

States also have detailed laws governing party activity. Rules about organization, nominations, primaries, party officers, membership, responsibilities and meetings are often set by state law, not by the parties themselves. Part of the reason is the trouble party organizations can cause if left unchecked. Another is that no single national party authority controls their work across the country. The states are where much of party life actually happens.

State governments can also influence counties and cities through grants. In turn, the federal government can influence states by giving them money. Yet there are limits to this influence. Most of a state's budget is not simply handed down by Washington.

Two important forms of federal financial support are grants and revenue sharing. States also raise money through income taxes, sales taxes, taxes on gasoline, cigarettes and alcohol, various fees, and earnings from public enterprises. The tax system is so complicated that many ordinary Americans can barely make sense of it. I discussed this earlier in the chapter on taxation.

Federalism gives the states an unusually wide field in which to act. Few other countries have quite the same arrangement. One might even worry that so much state independence would make the whole system unworkable. Yet, broadly speaking, it works fairly well. The reasons lie in several layers of American history, society and culture.

First, the country is economically united. America is one huge market. Hamburger chains, IBM, petrol stations, Kentucky Fried Chicken, Greyhound buses and many other businesses operate across the country without being government organizations.
