Car rental is a good example: rent a car in San Francisco and you can return it to the company's branch in New York. Large hotel groups such as Hilton have properties throughout the country.

Information and transport networks also connect different places. These links hold the country together. Economic development itself becomes a powerful force for national unity, leaving the political system less work to do. Under such conditions, a conflict like the Civil War is difficult to imagine happening again.

Second, Americans know from their history that the states existed before the national government. The states already had working institutions of their own. The federal government was created partly to protect their position, not to destroy or weaken it.

If a central government comes first and local governments are created afterward, the result can be very different. Some countries develop from local governments upward; others build local governments from a central authority downward. These two paths produce different political arrangements.

Finally, long experience has built a culture in which people accept the existing balance of power among the fifty states and the federal government. That acceptance is itself one of the main things keeping the arrangement alive. To defend the system is, in a sense, to defend one's own place within it. Seen from this angle, the federal government is almost an agency through which the fifty states send representatives to coordinate their shared affairs.

## 3. Politics at the County Level

Counties are local governments in America; states, strictly speaking, are not. Counties matter because they deal directly with ordinary people. The state government stands one level farther away, and the federal government another level beyond that.

There are more than three thousand counties across the country, though some states use other names for them. Louisiana has *parishes*; Alaska has *boroughs*. Nearly all American land belongs to one of these county-level areas. The exceptions described in the source include thirty-four independent cities in Virginia, St. Louis, Baltimore, Washington, DC, Yellowstone National Park and nineteen election districts in Alaska. This gives some idea of how much everyday governing happens at county level.

Most counties have their own governments. But these governments are by no means identical. Their structures and responsibilities differ greatly, something rarely seen in countries where local governments at the same level usually follow a standard pattern. Federalism allows more variation.

Not every county has a county government. Around thirty-one are governed through other arrangements. A few have governments combined with those of cities; others are run directly by a large metropolitan authority. Even the number of counties varies sharply from state to state: Delaware has three, while Texas has 254.

Their sizes differ just as dramatically. San Bernardino County in California covers more than twenty thousand square miles—almost twice the size of the Netherlands, according to the comparison given here, and roughly the combined area of Massachusetts, Connecticut, Rhode Island and Delaware. Arlington County in Virginia, by contrast, has only twenty-four square miles.

Economically, many counties are based on farming, with towns inside them serving as the main markets. Others depend on industry. Each has its own pattern of economic life and local organizations.

County politics deserves close attention because it is one of the foundations of American politics. Counties are also important building blocks for the political parties. Some of the most substantial work of politics happens there.

Herbert Sidney Ducombe, author of a book on American county government, wrote that the chair of a county political party is often a central figure in the local political organization. Winning county office can be a step toward state or federal office. Counties are also places where campaign funds are raised, and electoral districts often follow county boundaries. Most political organizations therefore maintain county branches. County party officials may help divide their areas into smaller voting districts and choose people to organize them.

Below the county comes the *precinct*, the small local area in which people vote. In the countryside it may have only a dozen voters; in a city it may contain as many as two thousand.

Party organizations work at this level too. Some precincts elect a local party official, called a *committeeman*; others have a committee. These are party organizations, not government offices. During elections their leaders organize volunteers, contact potential supporters, deliver campaign literature and attend meetings. Much of the direct struggle between the two major parties for individual voters takes place here.

In densely populated counties there may be other party organizations between the precinct and county levels. County party organizations are generally led by a committee or chair. They have no direct power over government, although they may influence government decisions. They must raise their own money and find their own staff and premises. They cannot simply use taxpayers' funds to support party activity.

County government itself comes in many forms. Some counties use a traditional arrangement; others use a system with a professional manager, something resembling a manager, an elected executive, an administrative assistant or a county administrator. Ducombe groups these arrangements into two broad families.

**The traditional system, with several elected officials sharing authority.** Roughly 85 to 90 percent of county governments have some form of elected governing board. It is usually called a *Board of Commissioners*, although names such as *County Court* and *Board of Supervisors* are also used. In some places its members perform only county duties; elsewhere they may also be town supervisors, judges or other officials.

Such boards mainly handle administration. They may appoint certain employees, approve financial documents, adopt county budgets and pass local rules where state law allows them to do so. Many other officials, such as the county treasurer, may also be elected. The division of responsibility among several elected people is a central feature of the system.

**Systems with a stronger executive or professional administrator.** Here one person has wider responsibility for supervising county administration. There are several versions.

Under the county-manager system, the elected county board makes policy and hires a professional manager to run most day-to-day operations. Managers are selected for their knowledge and experience rather than their political affiliation. The board makes decisions; the manager carries them out. The manager may appoint most administrative officers, prepare the county budget and put it into effect.

The quasi-manager, county-administrator and administrative-assistant systems are variations on that idea. In each, the county board appoints a professional to handle administrative work, but that person does not have the full authority to appoint officials or run the government independently.

Another arrangement has a chief executive elected by the voters of the entire county. In that case, the county board has little executive authority and acts more like a local legislature. These different arrangements have roots in the early colonial history of America.

What does a county government actually do? Its usual responsibilities include valuing property for tax purposes, collecting property taxes, keeping property records, maintaining rural roads, assisting poor people, enforcing laws, running elections and carrying out some court-related tasks. State laws define many of these functions.

Counties may also take on other jobs according to what their residents need: fire protection, water supply, parking, airports, public schools, libraries, healthcare, welfare, public safety and transport, among others.

Another important power is the ability to collect property tax. A county may collect this tax and distribute the proceeds to cities, school districts and other local bodies. County revenues generally come from two main sources: property taxes and aid from the state, along with other funds. The balance varies. Some counties rely heavily on their own property taxes; others depend more on money redistributed by the state, while some levy additional taxes as well.

County government occupies an important place in American political life because it is so close to voters. Much of the political system takes visible form at this level.

The economy of many counties has reached a fairly advanced stage. Living standards and public facilities may not be so different from those in large cities. Many young people can go on to higher education; credit and transport are well developed. Even in farming areas, counties often show little of the obvious poverty and isolation associated with backward rural regions. Such conditions help local government work effectively.

In many ways county politics forms the base of American politics. What people experience there shapes the kind of politics they will accept—or refuse to accept—elsewhere. County affairs are more about getting things done than about grand political disputes. Class interests may show themselves more directly in federal and state politics. County officials, by contrast, are usually pressed to deal with residents' immediate needs. Class interests have not disappeared, but they are expressed less directly.

The election system reinforces this. A county is small enough for people to see what their officials are doing. If residents are unhappy, they can refuse to vote for those officials next time. Anyone seeking votes must therefore produce visible results. This is one reason local politics becomes a matter of ordinary, practical concerns rather than lofty slogans.

The widespread use of county managers and other professional arrangements reflects the same trend. Many people treat county government as a technical problem, not primarily a political one. Politics is still present, of course, but it must rest on the ability to accomplish useful things.

America's political system has become orderly and accustomed to established procedures partly because its three thousand or so counties function as effectively as they do. Any society that hopes to build a mature political system, whatever its form, must first make sure its local and grassroots governments can work well.

Local governments deal directly with citizens. They are not as remote as state governments, let alone Washington. When people decide whether government has succeeded or failed, they usually begin with the things closest to their own lives. That is where the political importance of local government lies.

After more than a century of experience, and under the constant pressure of elections, county governments have become reasonably well established. That does not mean they are perfect. It means they provide a stable foundation for the political order as a whole.

Americans often pay more attention to county government, or even smaller local bodies, than to the federal government. Washington can seem distant and uninteresting. Americans are a practical people, and their political practicality often means keeping an eye on the government closest to home. That attitude, in turn, encourages local government—including county government—to keep improving.

## 4. City Politics

Cities are one of the basic units through which American society and politics are run. To a considerable extent, America's success is the success of its cities.

According to figures from 1970, the country had fifty-six large cities, each with more than 250,000 residents. Together they were home to over forty million people. There were also more than two hundred so-called central cities. And smaller places, such as Iowa City, were scattered across the country in countless numbers.

America is a highly urban society. More than 95 percent of its people do not work in agriculture. Most live in towns and cities, which makes the quality of city government enormously important. China is different: 80 percent of its people live in the countryside. For China, the question of how well the countryside is run is therefore central to the country's development.

Managing society, whether we call it social administration or politics, ultimately means dealing with people. If a society hopes to develop, its economy to flourish, and its culture to grow, it must first find good ways of serving and organizing the lives of the people who make up most of its population. Those ways must take account of culture, geography, the economy, religion and politics. A society can develop in a balanced way only when it can deal effectively with the needs of the great majority.

What, then, does a city government actually do?

I suspect cities around the world handle many of the same basic tasks, because people everywhere have much the same essential needs. Each country also has its own arrangements, shaped by its culture. In *City Governments and Urban Problems*, Demetrios Caraley divides municipal responsibilities into three broad kinds:

1. **Public services and welfare:** preventing and fighting fires, supplying water, maintaining streets and highways, providing public healthcare and similar services.
2. **Regulation:** setting and enforcing rules for food safety, tax evasion by businesses, building maintenance, construction, everyday services, transport, air and water pollution, racial discrimination, and urban development.
3. **Taxation:** raising the money needed to support those services and rules. The two main kinds of local tax are property tax and sales tax.

Cities have other ways to bring in money too. When you buy something in America, a local tax is often added to the price. In effect, everyone who buys something helps pay for local government.

Some cities also collect what are called *nuisance taxes*. The Chinese expression might be closer to a fee charged for the privilege of doing something. They can apply to banks, cigarettes, theatre tickets, gasoline, stock transactions, hotels, cars and the rental of business property. Then there are *user fees*: charges for particular services and facilities, such as zoos, recreation areas, museums, bridges, tunnels, buses, subways, public housing and hospitals. Cities charge for issuing licenses as well. Finally, they collect fines.

City budgets can be enormous. American officials often have a surprisingly simple starting point when they face a problem: money. Their basic response is to make a budget. In China, a budget by itself plainly isn't enough. You also have to coordinate relationships between people and find ways to get the materials you need. America's highly developed economy allows officials to think first in financial terms—and, just as importantly, to make that approach work.

The federal Constitution does not prescribe how cities should be governed. In theory, a state creates its cities and assigns them duties. Their powers are generally set out in a *city charter*, something like a city's own governing rulebook. Charters differ from place to place.

States can influence cities in several ways: through the powers granted by state constitutions, through laws delegating authority, through financial assistance, through administrative supervision and through special investigations. According to the figures used here, state assistance accounts for roughly one-third of city spending.

Because state laws differ, city governments rarely look exactly alike. Most have some kind of mayor and city council. In a familiar arrangement, the mayor is elected separately, serves as the city's chief executive, and supervises the enforcement of its laws. The mayor also represents the city at public events. The council makes local laws, draws up plans and raises taxes. Its chair is usually chosen by the council, although in some cities, including Chicago and Atlanta, voters elect the person directly. Some cities, such as New York, have had two lawmaking bodies to check one another's power.

Not all mayors are equally powerful. In a *strong-mayor* system, the mayor has considerable authority. In a *weak-mayor* system, the city council places tighter limits on that authority. The difference becomes especially clear in the appointment of officials, the preparation of plans and the making of laws.

Another arrangement is the *council-manager* system. It is found mainly in cities with fewer than a million people; according to the figures cited here, about half of cities with populations below 500,000 use it. The council appoints a professional city manager to run the administration. There is no separately elected mayor serving as chief executive.

The manager answers to the council and can resign if the arrangement no longer works. The manager appoints department heads and prepares the budget, which the council must approve. Council members generally cannot give orders directly to departments; they have to work through the manager.

Such a city may still have a mayor, chosen either by voters or by the council. But the mayor mainly represents the city. The duties are ceremonial: attending public occasions, signing laws and the like. The manager, not the mayor, runs daily operations.

A third approach is the *commission* system. It survives in a few cities with populations below 500,000. Here a commission of five to seven members holds the city's governing power. Together its members act as the council and make laws; individually, each supervises an administrative department. Some cities elect members for separate posts from the start. Others elect the commission first and divide the responsibilities afterward.

There is no mayor with separate executive powers. One commissioner may carry the title of mayor, chair meetings and represent the city at ceremonies, but has no special authority over the others. That person may be chosen by voters or by fellow commissioners. In some places, the candidate who receives the most votes automatically becomes mayor.

Electoral rules vary just as much. Terms last anywhere from two to four years. Some cities divide themselves into electoral districts; others elect officials citywide. Some elections list candidates' party affiliations, while others do not. Where parties are named, candidates are usually chosen in party primaries. People hoping to stand for election have to collect a required number of signatures from supporters. Other elections also have signature requirements, and some cities ask candidates to pay a deposit.

Cities also have to live alongside other local governments. Most large cities lie geographically inside counties, which have their own administrations. Special districts, towns and other nearby local bodies may also operate in the same area. They are not simply branches of city hall, but their decisions can affect the city. The relationship with county government is particularly important.

Here the American meaning of *county* differs from the Chinese one. In China, a county is generally thought of as rural and does not include large or medium-sized cities. In America, a county is both a geographical area and a unit of government. A large city may lie within a county's borders without being governed by the county in every respect. Counties often maintain their offices in the major city within their boundaries.

County governments may oversee local courts, register marriages and supervise elections. Some help run welfare programs. In some places they operate hospitals and public health services. The exact arrangement varies. Counties may have their own entirely separate governments, or county officials may perform duties within city government, as in the examples Wang gives of New York, Philadelphia, Boston and New Orleans. Elsewhere the city and county form one government. San Francisco is an example of such a *city-county*, organized largely around the city.

Most counties were established long before the cities within them grew to their present size. As towns expanded and their needs changed, the balance between county and city shifted. Some were brought together; others remained separate. The American arrangement often puts a city *inside* a county geographically. The Chinese arrangement more commonly separates cities and counties. Having cities within county areas can help the surrounding countryside develop.

Cities are political centres as well as places where services are provided. Party activity is especially lively in large cities. Party leaders are usually people who hold important public offices or leadership posts in the party itself.

To understand the organization, start with the smallest voting area, the *precinct*. A precinct might contain six hundred to nine hundred people on average, sometimes only two hundred, sometimes as many as two thousand. Its practical purpose is to provide a local polling place.

Supporters of a party in a precinct may choose one or several *committeemen* in a primary. These people do much of the party's work on the ground. They are party workers rather than major party leaders.

Above the precinct is the *ward* or *assembly district*. A district of this kind commonly elects a city council member or state legislator. Its party members also select their own ward leader, either directly in a primary or through a meeting of precinct representatives. Ward leaders may appoint precinct *captains*, although in practice a captain and a committeeman are often the same person.

Ward leaders together form the party's city or county central committee or executive committee. That body then chooses a city or county chair, who leads the local party organization.

As with almost every American political structure, there are exceptions. New York City consists of five counties, each with its own party organization, but no single citywide party committee and chair of the kind just described. Local party officials formally belong to wider state and national organizations, yet in practice they enjoy considerable independence, especially when city offices are at stake.

In the nineteenth century, party bosses could have an enormous influence over politics. That power has weakened greatly, partly because primary elections allow voters to choose candidates. Yet this flexibility may help explain the two-party system's survival. Both parties allow people with differing opinions to remain inside them. Without that room for disagreement, they would be more likely to split apart. Cities remain strategic centres of politics. Even if party organizations have lost some of their old strength, much party activity still takes place there.

With more than 95 percent of Americans living outside agricultural work, the future of cities is bound up with the country's future. Large and medium-sized cities face increasingly serious problems: rising crime, higher living costs, ageing public facilities and growing taxes.

I visited San Francisco, Washington, New York, Philadelphia, Chicago, Boston, Atlanta, Seattle and San Diego, among others. New York's crime rate was enough to make a visitor anxious. Parts of downtown Chicago seemed strangely deserted. There were other troubling sights too.

Even so, in fairness, American city government is among the best and most modern in the world. It has one major advantage: it needs to *regulate* the economy, not *run* it. Private organizations carry out most economic activity. City hall mainly sets the rules. Compared with Chinese city governments, American cities have much narrower responsibilities. They can concentrate their attention on urban development, the quality of everyday life and public facilities.

As a society develops, new problems keep appearing. Each one tests our ability to govern and organize life together. Faced with such challenges, we have two broad choices. We can try to hold the problems down, or we can look for ways to deal with them. Suppressing a problem will not settle it for good. Only by finding an answer can we begin to resolve it.

## 5. Politics at the Grassroots

Political scientists speak of *grassroots politics*. In plain language, this means politics at the most local level, close to people's everyday lives.

But how does government reach people below the county level? In some states there is another layer: the *town* or *township*. Here again we encounter a characteristic feature of American society. Not every state has such governments. They exist chiefly in places where history and local habits have given them a role, especially in the East, the Midwest and some other regions.

Town government is particularly well established in New England. Thomas Jefferson and other political thinkers praised it as an almost ideal form of direct democracy, in which citizens make decisions themselves rather than leaving everything to representatives.

These New England towns date back to colonial times. Early settlers, facing bitter winters and conflict with Indigenous peoples, built small communities surrounded by their own land. A church often stood at the centre of an area that included both houses and farmland.

The arrangement was relatively simple. Eligible voters met together to make decisions and choose town officials. A town meeting was usually held once a year, with additional meetings when necessary. The voters decided basic policy, and a small elected board of three to nine officials carried it out. Other local officers were elected too.

Yet scholars have noticed a change. Fewer and fewer residents come to meetings, and fewer people are willing to serve as town officials. Some towns have therefore created representative meetings: voters choose a hundred or more people to attend on their behalf.

Rousseau argued that the general will—the shared will of a political community—cannot be represented by someone else. If citizens prefer to send representatives rather than make decisions themselves, he believed, the character of the political system changes. Where will town government go from here? It is too early to say. In many places it still plays a major part in public life. I have already described a visit to one such government in Chapter 2, Section 6, “The Roots of American Politics.”

Outside New England, rural townships are found in parts of the Mid-Atlantic states and the Midwest. A township often covers an area of thirty-six square miles. Township governments remain active in states such as Illinois, Indiana, Kansas, Michigan, Minnesota and Wisconsin. Missouri and Washington have a smaller number. Iowa and Oklahoma once had township governments that, in the account given here, no longer functioned.

Townships look after roads, handle some minor legal matters and run elections. They may assess the value of property and collect taxes. Large townships in some states can maintain police and fire services and water supplies. Their purpose is to meet the needs of rural residents.

Their method of government resembles that of towns. Voters may meet each year to set policy, but attendance is generally low unless something important is at stake.

Township government is losing ground in many areas. Rural populations are shrinking. Better roads, communications and economic links bind the countryside more closely to cities, creating needs that a small township may struggle to meet. At the same time, fewer people want to become township officials. Ambitious people often look beyond their home communities for more senior positions.

Oddly enough, some more urbanized townships are growing in importance. With denser populations come more competing needs and interests, which makes organized government more necessary.

Another local arrangement is the *special district*, something many people know little about. A special district is a government body created for a particular purpose. It has its own organization and may have the power to raise taxes. Districts can be set up to manage airports, planning, parking or many other services. Their boundaries may be separate from those of cities and counties, or overlap them. They exist in both urban and rural areas. About 60 percent, according to the figures cited here, were concentrated in ten states, including California and Illinois.

Why create one? Usually because a particular service is easier to provide through a dedicated body, or because that one service is so important in an area that it needs an organization of its own.

The range is wide. School districts are the most numerous and are generally led by boards responsible for education in their areas. Other districts handle sewage and sanitation, electricity, water supplies, libraries, fire protection, forests and more. They grew especially quickly in the 1950s and 1960s. The total number of special districts, as described here, was roughly equal to the number of cities nationwide.

State law determines how these districts are created. Residents establish a local government for a specific need, usually electing a board that can collect taxes, build an organization, buy equipment and provide the service. The people who benefit from a particular service are asked to help pay for it. Such a body becomes useful when ordinary city or county government cannot supply what is needed.

Special districts are not usually concerned with broad political questions. They concentrate on getting a specific job done.

That does not mean everyone approves of them. Critics argue that they can become a windfall for companies selling specialized equipment, since a district exists precisely to purchase and operate things for its chosen purpose. And however practical its aim, a special district cannot escape politics. Interest groups and politicians still try to influence its decisions.

Even so, special districts are a flexible solution. When a special need arises, creating a body to meet it can be an effective response. If state and other local governments cannot provide the service, an organization formed for that purpose may make good sense. This is especially true in a large, populous country where cultures and needs differ greatly from place to place.

There is a wider lesson here. Systems for running society need room to bend. Make them too rigid and they restrict the natural growth of social life. But flexibility can also go too far: without some common structure and clear rules, government becomes disorderly. Good government depends on finding a workable place between those extremes.

Finally, there are *villages*. The American word does not necessarily mean a rural settlement, as the Chinese word often does. It may describe a small urban community more like a town. In legal terms, a village can have the same standing as a city, depending on state law.

Some villages are little more than a cluster of houses around a post office or a shop, with neighbours close enough to hear one another's chickens and dogs. Others are larger. Their residents want roads, streetlights and water supplies, but the place is too small to become a city. State laws provide ways for such communities to apply for village status.

Village government is usually simple, something like a weak-mayor arrangement. A small board of three to nine people makes local rules. A chair or official with another title leads its meetings. A few other officers may be elected. Some villages are independent of the township government and send their own representatives to the county; others remain under the township. Arrangements differ.

At this grassroots level, American politics leaves communities considerable freedom to organize themselves. The main purpose is to provide practical services and conveniences; other political roles are less prominent. Local officials are elected and pay for their work through taxes collected from residents. Because these organizations are small and straightforward, people can watch what they do.

Ordinary citizens may feel they have little say about national politics, or even the government of their state. At the grassroots level, their voice can be much more direct. The decisions happen where they live, before their eyes, and are woven into daily life. This is government concerned above all with community needs and welfare. For many people, useful local services and a politics that does not intrude too much are enough.

## 6. Government in the Open

One striking feature of American politics is how much of its decision-making takes place in public. Meetings and debates in the Senate and House of Representatives are open. Television broadcasts them live, and members of the public can sit in designated galleries to watch. Legislators' votes are public too. Electronic boards display how each member has voted, so the result is visible to everyone.

State legislatures work in much the same way. I saw the visitors' gallery and electronic voting boards in Maryland's legislature. At the local level, open meetings are also an established practice, particularly in bodies that debate and decide policy. Because counties are organized differently from one another, I cannot say they all work in exactly the same way. But I attended an open meeting of the county board in Johnson County, Iowa, and it was revealing.

The meeting took place in a four-storey county administration building, about the size of a cinema. Compared with the offices of a Chinese county government, it was tiny. The two largest offices appeared to be the voter-registration office and the place where people applied for driving licenses.

The meeting room was perhaps fifty or sixty square metres. At the front stood a fixed table with five seats for the five elected county board members. A nameplate identified each one. The county attorney and auditor sat to the right, ready to advise on legal and financial matters. A clerk sat to the left, beside recording equipment.

Facing the board was a small table and chair for people invited to speak. Behind it stood more than twenty chairs for members of the public. Anyone could sit there, listen to the discussion and watch decisions being made. Most people were not especially interested; those directly affected by the day's business were the ones most likely to attend. Visitors could also speak or comment if they first obtained the chair's permission.

There was a great deal on the agenda that day. The meeting began at nine in the morning, with a reporter from the local newspaper present.

Several matters were discussed informally before the official agenda. The first concerned renovating the office of a county agency. The board went into small details: carpets, curtains, air-conditioning, how much the state would contribute and how much the county would have to pay. The agency's representative explained the request, and board members asked careful, serious questions. Even the architect was there. In the end they agreed that the architect should prepare a draft plan and bring it back for further discussion.

Next came a road project, connecting two roads to make travel in the area easier. Those proposing it had to explain their reasons, answer questions and produce documents and drawings. People gathered around the plans to discuss the details before the board reached a view.

These informal discussions were for preparing and examining questions; they did not require votes. The county board is, in some respects, like a local lawmaking body. Its final decisions must be taken by vote, and those votes are public.

At ten o'clock the formal meeting began. The chair struck a small wooden gavel.

The first business concerned three proposed property sales. Two were approved quickly. The third provoked a long argument.

The background was the property-tax law. People who own land or buildings must pay taxes on them. If they do not, the government has the power to seize and sell the property to recover the unpaid tax. It is a strong rule, and one that makes ignoring property taxes difficult.

In this case a man had started building a house but apparently ran out of money before completing it. He could not pay the taxes, so the county had taken possession of the unfinished house and offered it for sale. An old friend wanted to buy it for $2,000 and give it back to him, hoping to rescue him from his difficulties.

But a young couple living next door saw the building as a danger. Children could easily wander into the unfinished structure and get hurt. The couple offered $1,000 so they could buy the building and demolish it.

The owner explained his circumstances. His friend spoke in his support. The young woman argued the couple's case. Each side had a neighbour or two willing to speak as well.

After a lengthy debate, the board voted. The auditor called each member by name. Those in favour said “yes”; those against said “no.” The auditor recorded every response. The young couple won. The county chose $1,000 over $2,000.

Perhaps the board felt that a man who had gone eleven years without paying property tax should not be allowed to regain the property. Accepting the $2,000 bid would have returned the house to him without requiring him to settle the taxes owed over those eleven years.

Next came a proposal from the county's Human Services department. It wanted more office space and a centre for young people. Its director gave the reasons and submitted plans and drawings. The board approved the proposal.

The third item was a letter to the state's economic development department, seeking more support for the county. There was little disagreement, and it passed quickly.

The board then handled several auditing matters and applications for licenses to sell alcohol. In America, you need a license to sell alcoholic drinks. Selling them without one is against the law. I once ate in a Korean restaurant that had no beer because it did not have a liquor license. There were no objections to the applications before the board, and they were approved.

The county attorney then presented a proposal concerning the 1988 school election. He had prepared the documents in advance, and the board passed it.

Another applicant wanted to turn eleven acres of farmland into a residential development and build houses. The board approved that too, without objection. Owning land in America does not mean you can use it however you like. Planning and land-use laws still apply, and construction cannot begin until the necessary approval is granted.

Several more items followed and were quickly passed.

The last lengthy discussion concerned snow removal in winter. With so many cars in America, clearing winter roads is a serious problem. The county needs equipment, a budget and people to do the work. Spend a great deal preparing for snow that never comes, and the money seems wasted. Spend too little and a heavy storm can leave the roads unusable—and the voters complaining. Snow removal therefore becomes an important item of winter government business.

Once the agenda was finished, the commissioners, county attorney and auditor exchanged views. Each could raise further matters to be recorded for later attention.

Finally, the chair asked whether anyone in the audience wished to speak. Only two visitors remained. The other twenty or so had left once their own business was settled.

One man raised his hand. He wanted to know whether, when approving the Human Services proposal for a new office building, the board had considered renting or buying an existing building instead. Why insist on constructing a new one when it would cost so much more? He had no personal connection with the proposal. He was simply concerned about the expense. A commissioner who had investigated the options answered him. Then the chair struck the gavel, and the meeting ended.

Everything had taken place in public. Anyone could attend and, with permission, offer a view. All the commissioners were elected. Afterward one of them told me that the board could make no decision without a public vote.

A large part of political life takes place in local government. The whole system depends on its ability to work properly. Ordinary people often judge politics first by the institutions they encounter closest to home. The Johnson County meeting gave an impression of remarkable openness.

Of course, officials have ways to move things through that they want approved—or things they do not particularly want the audience to understand. Votes must be public, but a board can use highly specialized language and move through the agenda at great speed. Americans call this *railroading*: pushing something through so quickly that people have scarcely understood it before it has passed.

Much of the county board's real work consists of deciding how money should be spent. Few items are purely political, and the board does not directly run the economy. Its business is welfare, roads, taxes, buildings, land and similar practical concerns. Local government therefore tends to look less like a political contest and more like the management of everyday needs.

In 1987, Johnson County's total income was $21 million. About half came from property taxes. More than $2 million came from funds transferred between levels of government. The board's main job was to decide how to use that money.

Party competition certainly exists at county level. Counties provide an important base for political parties. But the day-to-day work of county government is concrete and usually concerns people's livelihoods.

I think local government, in any society, should pursue two goals. First, it must effectively meet the immediate needs of local residents. Second, it should make its discussions and decisions as open as possible.

Local government is the central government's feet: without them, it cannot take a step. It is also the central government's hands: without them, it cannot get things done. The centre should adopt policies that help local government achieve these two goals. When local government works well, the whole political system gains strength and freedom to act.

## 7. Choosing Public Officials

America has a permanent civil service. People entering ordinary administrative posts are generally selected through open competition and examinations. I have been told that the idea ultimately came from China's old imperial examination system and was first adopted in the West by the British. But China's traditional examination essays and the modern civil service are, of course, very different things.

America began establishing a modern civil service around the end of the nineteenth century. Before that, it relied heavily on the *spoils system*: when a party won the presidency, its supporters expected government jobs as rewards. Those who had helped the winner could celebrate their good fortune.

The system had serious faults. It encouraged corruption, favouritism, private deals and poor administration. One famous story tells of a man who wanted a government post in Washington, failed to obtain it, and shot the president. The killing became an important turning point in the movement toward civil-service reform. But the deeper issue was never simply one disappointed man. It was whether the old system could still meet the needs of a changing society.

The central idea of the new system is to separate *political officials* from *career employees*. Political officeholders gain and lose their posts through elections and changes of party. Career public employees, by contrast, are chosen through open examinations, compete for their jobs and are expected to remain politically neutral in carrying out their duties. Many are not officials in any grand sense; they are ordinary office workers.

Someone who wants to enter public service must generally either win election or pass a selection process. The aim is to bring capable people into government, close off opportunities for favouritism, and keep competing political forces in balance.

How does this work in practice? I investigated Iowa City's municipal government.

Iowa City uses the council-manager system. Its highest governing body is a seven-member city council. One member serves as mayor, a largely ceremonial position. At the time of my visit the mayor was John Mc Donald, who ran an optician's shop on a street opposite the university. He could often be found there repairing customers' glasses. He went to city hall mainly when the council met or had official business.

Daily administration was the responsibility of the city manager. Under the manager were departments dealing with finance, housing, public places, fire protection, cooperation, planning, public works, transport and personnel, along with the police. The library director, city clerk and chief legal official did not fall under the manager in the same way.

The city manager employed nearly five hundred permanent staff, including firefighters, police officers and library employees. Anyone working in the public sector was counted. The city also needed three to four hundred temporary workers over the course of a year, for tasks such as clearing winter snow, maintaining roads in summer and cutting grass. Whether someone was a senior officer, a clerk or a manual worker, a public-sector employee belonged within the civil-service arrangements.

So how were all these people recruited?

An assistant in the city's personnel department told me that all vacancies had to be made public and open to anyone. The city was not allowed to discriminate against women, ethnic minorities or disabled people. It was also expected to take active steps to make sure these groups had fair opportunities. This policy is known as *affirmative action*. Anyone willing and qualified to do public work could apply.

But how did the city let people know there was a vacancy? It used local newspapers, employment bulletins, advertisements, notices in city hall and the city's cable television service. Outside the personnel office I saw some such notices under the heading “City of Iowa City Memorandum.”

The vacancies included school crossing guards, relief workers who could cover for sick employees, planning staff, instructors in water-related activities, skiing, science and preschool arts, a library assistant and a police chief. How widely a job was advertised depended on its importance. Ordinary posts were advertised locally. More responsible positions were advertised across the state or farther afield. Senior jobs, such as city manager or police chief, could be advertised nationally.

Every notice was expected to say what the job involved, what duties it carried, what hours and qualifications were required, how much it paid, when it would begin and when applications had to be submitted.

Two examples show the level of detail.

**Community Development Grant Program Assistant.** The successful applicant would help run a community development grant program and solve problems as they arose. The duties included preparing grant applications, writing reports, drafting documents, and conducting research—designing procedures, collecting information and analysing results. Applicants were expected to hold a master's degree in political science, community studies, urban studies or a closely related field, with more than three years of experience in program management, development, urban housing or community development. A master's in public administration could count in place of one year of work experience. Excellent speaking, writing and administrative skills were required, together with the ability to communicate effectively with residents and knowledge of federal, state and local laws. Applicants were also required to hold an Iowa City driving license.

**Chief of Police.** The chief would oversee an annual budget of $3 million and fifty-three employees, serving Iowa City, with a population of about fifty thousand, and surrounding smaller communities. Applicants needed a wide range of skills: budgeting, program design, long-term planning and practical police leadership. They had to respect different views, know how to meet goals and anticipate problems, lead and coordinate staff, advise the city manager and council, deal with the public, communicate with a highly educated community, cooperate with other government bodies and work closely with the university.

The benefits were listed too: fully paid medical, dental and life insurance; one day of annual leave and one day of sick leave earned each month; an additional $200 a year after five years' service under the seniority arrangement; a salary between $39,936 and $57,429; and a car. The notice described the selection stages and gave January 31, 1989, as the application deadline. Applicants had to submit supporting documents. Finalists would be brought in for interviews at the city's expense. A panel of police experts and other interested parties would assess them, and the University of Iowa would administer an examination. Those shortlisted would face further interviews.

When advertisements went out, applicants sent their materials to the personnel department. It collected the applications and, at the appointed time, organized examinations. These might include interviews, written tests and practical demonstrations.

In an interview, a panel questioned and assessed each candidate face to face. Written tests were more complicated. A different examination had to be designed for each kind of job. A vague, one-size-fits-all test would tell the city little. Questions had to examine the knowledge, ability and judgment needed for that particular position.

Some posts had additional requirements. A candidate for police chief might be assessed for mental and physical fitness to make sure the person could cope with the duties. For practical jobs—typist, computer operator or snowplough driver—the examination could simply require applicants to perform the work in front of assessors. The aim was to select the strongest candidate for the actual task.

I examined a test for a senior fire-department post. I remember questions along these lines:

- What do you understand by the development of policy? What does making policy involve?
- Can you prepare long-term plans? What does long-term planning mean to you?
- What would you do if the department you led developed a budget deficit?
- Could you train firefighters? How would you do it?
- What do you think would be the hardest problem in the job, and how would you handle it?
- How do you see the role of women and ethnic minorities in the workplace?

There were about thirty questions altogether. Every candidate for a post with this level of responsibility had to take the test. The results were assessed, and the best-qualified applicant was chosen.

The rules were designed to prevent people from giving jobs to friends or supporters. Every vacancy had to be announced. No individual could simply decide who would get it—not even the mayor or city manager.

The city did have an agreement with its unions. Before offering a job to the general public, it first had to announce it to people already employed by the city. If no suitable person was found, the opening would then be advertised outside. But that first stage also had to be open and fair. Department heads, the mayor and the city manager had no power to appoint someone directly.

What if applicants believed the choice was unfair? They could take the matter to court, or complain to the federal Equal Employment Opportunity Commission. Unions also had a role in protecting the fairness of appointments. Together, these arrangements were meant to keep the hiring process honest.

Public bodies are supported by taxpayers. They must therefore answer to the public, and taxpayers ought to have an equal opportunity to compete for public employment. In selecting applicants, officials were not supposed to investigate marital status, children, religious belief, racial identity or personal history unrelated to the job. They could examine only what mattered for the position. None of those personal characteristics was supposed to be a reason to exclude someone.

That is the theory. In practice there are always abuses, and there is plenty of box-ticking and pretence. Every set of rules faces the problem of people finding ways around it. The civil service is no exception.

We need not try to calculate exactly what percentage of these ideals is achieved. In principle, the system offers several important advantages.

**First, it gives administration stability.** Public service becomes a skilled occupation. Career employees do not have to come and go with political parties. When one party replaces another, these workers can continue running the many parts of society for which they are responsible.

**Second, it makes party competition easier to sustain.** Day-to-day administration is like the enormous part of an iceberg hidden underwater; party rivalry is the smaller part visible above the surface. If every political contest threw society into disorder, party competition could not continue effectively. The permanent civil-service machinery forms one of the foundations of American politics.

**Third, it brings specialized skills into government.** Modern societies require people with increasingly precise knowledge. Government staff cannot afford to be less capable than the engineers and technicians working in factories. Many public problems are practical or technical ones. If every official is caught up in political struggles, it becomes impossible to build a stable body of trained specialists.

**Fourth, it usually puts capable people into the jobs.** Not necessarily the most capable people: some of them may avoid government because private business pays better. But open competition, examinations and selection make it more likely that those appointed can do the work.

Successful government depends in part on turning a large share of its work into stable, skilled professions. People need the time and security to study the areas they oversee and improve how they are run. At the same time, public positions must be open to anyone willing and able to compete. That is how a society finds stronger candidates and filters out weaker ones.

The wider the search, the more talent it can discover. A radar sweeping a full 360 degrees will find more targets than one scanning only 180. But to open government jobs widely, the work itself must be sufficiently professional and based on clear skills. Openness and professional standards reinforce one another.

American politics has a double character. It draws talent from across society into a permanent civil service, yet places that service under the authority of political forces. In this way, the same system that recruits capable administrators also helps preserve the ruling class's power.

## 8. A Congressman's Local Office

America's political pyramid is large and complicated from top to bottom. Its parts are not held together particularly tightly, yet the whole structure runs with surprising steadiness. The different levels do not constantly clash, and the system manages to endure. There must be threads running through it, connecting one part with another. One such thread is the relationship between members of Congress and the people who elect them.

Members of the House of Representatives are elected from districts, one representative for each district. Iowa's Third Congressional District was represented, at the time of my visit, by a man named Nagel. The Chinese expression for *congressman*, incidentally, could be translated word for word as “a person of Congress.”

A representative's job is to stay in touch with voters, listen to their concerns and help the district deal with practical problems, sometimes by changing the law.

In reality, much of a representative's attention is directed not toward the nation as a whole but toward home. Naturally, representatives must take positions on national bills when Congress votes on them. Yet their political future depends on whether the people of their own district are satisfied. If voters are unhappy, the representative may not be elected again.

Here the electoral system has a real effect. People may have only a small number of candidates to choose from, but anyone who hopes to enter the halls of Congress must still win enough votes. That pressure makes representatives pay close attention to local concerns.

A representative has an office in Washington, usually with fewer than ten staff members. Different people handle different areas: foreign affairs and national politics, welfare programs, business and economic questions, agriculture, industry and so on.

But Washington is far from the district. The office there cannot easily stay in touch with everyone back home. Representatives therefore establish district offices—local places where voters can reach them. The number depends on the district's size. Because Iowa's Third District was relatively large, Nagel maintained four. One was in Iowa City, serving Johnson County. The University of Iowa's importance to the area was one reason for giving the county an office of its own.

What does one of these offices look like? I visited the one in Iowa City. It occupied two or three spacious rooms above a bank in the city centre. Two people worked there. The representative's four district offices employed roughly ten people altogether. Add the Washington staff, and a member of the House might have about twenty people working on the member's behalf.

The local staff acted in the representative's name. Most letters went out under the congressman's signature, even when the people in the office had made the practical decision themselves.

The office was, above all, a place where voters could bring problems. Anyone with a complaint, a difficulty or a question could ask for help. These offices were not government departments in their own right, but they could contact government departments and use the representative's standing to encourage officials to act.

Much of the work concerned Social Security, benefits for veterans, support for disabled children, immigration, agricultural development and similar matters. The idea was simple: help constituents get through the difficulties of daily life.

Part of this was a social service. Part of it was also a way of winning public support for the congressman.

One staff member described how she had found assistance for children from federal, state and other funds. She also helped foreigners who wanted to remain in the United States work through immigration questions. At the time, she was handling the case of a member of Poland's Solidarity trade union who wanted to immigrate.

By using a representative's influence to coordinate with agencies, explain cases and press for action, these offices could often solve problems that individuals could not solve alone. In one respect they reminded me of the Chinese offices where people go to petition the authorities about grievances.

The staff were paid through congressional funds, not by the administrative agencies they contacted. Their budgets came from Congress. That gave them a degree of independence from the government offices they dealt with. The representative was their boss.

Representatives frequently travelled between Washington and their districts, sometimes every week, and spent a good deal of time at home. If a representative lost an election, the local offices closed and their employees had to find other work. The newly elected member would establish new district offices, naturally often choosing people who had worked hard for the winning campaign.

Taken together, congressional district offices form an important network linking representatives to voters. A member of Congress does not merely travel to Washington to debate great national questions. There are small household problems to deal with every day.

That arrangement can help ease conflicts and frustrations in society. When citizens cannot make headway with an administrative department, they have another place to turn: the office of someone in the legislature. Its staff can then deal with the department on the citizen's behalf. They must still work within the law, of course, unless the representative succeeds in getting the law changed.

There is another, perhaps even more important, function. These local offices form a nationwide system for gathering information.

How can a legislature know which laws to make? This is a central problem in practical politics. Lawmakers need enough information to understand what people face, decide which problems are urgent and write laws suited to real conditions. Without a reliable flow of information from across the country, how could individual representatives—or Congress as a whole—judge what ought to be done?

District offices, combined with modern transport and communications, make that flow possible. They are an important part of the way American society and politics are organized.

Representatives depend on this network, but they are also held to account by it. Does a member of Congress do the job properly? Listen? Help people? Produce results? The work of the district offices gives voters ways to find out.

People often establish something to achieve one purpose, only to discover that it serves another as well. District offices were built to help representatives hear what voters think. At the same time, they make representatives answerable to those very voters.

# Chapter 8: Governing Without Giving Orders

## 1. The Driver's License

There are two broad ways to run a society. One relies openly on political authority. The other relies more on practical systems that make everyday life work. We might call the second approach *soft governance*.

By this I mean the arrangements a society develops to guide people's behaviour through money, culture, habits, laws and routine procedures. Direct political control relies more visibly on power and compulsion. It also places greater demands on government, because officials must take responsibility for so many things themselves.

If political institutions can hand over much of that work to other parts of society, while still making sure necessary tasks get done, the whole system has reached a more mature stage. It may also face fewer of the disputes that arise when government tries to manage everything directly.

America is a capitalist society built on private ownership. Through a mixture of deliberate arrangements and the power of capital itself, it has developed many forms of this softer kind of government. They are among the reasons for its stability.

Consider something as ordinary as a driving license.

Americans do not have a special national identity card issued to every citizen. The document they most often use to prove who they are is a driving license. Most American adults have one. Those who do not drive can usually obtain a similar card from the licensing office. It does not allow them to drive, but it serves as proof of identity. They can show it when they need to identify themselves—for example, when writing a cheque. Public offices and private businesses alike accept it by common agreement.

I applied for such a card myself, simply so that I would have a convenient form of identification. I telephoned first. They told me it was possible and that I should bring my passport.

When I arrived, I found that the office belonged to the state Department of Transportation. This kind of service is generally handled by state government. Quite a few people were applying for licenses or cards. I waited in line for about ten minutes.

When my turn came, the employee asked no searching questions. I opened my passport. She copied down my name, asked my height and weight, looked at the colour of my eyes and recorded my date of birth. That took perhaps two minutes.

At the next desk someone checked the information. At another, an employee entered it into a computer and collected five dollars. Perhaps a minute passed. The next person handed me a printed form showing my sex, eye colour, weight, height, date of issue, date of birth, address and identification number, and asked me to sign it.

Then I sat in a chair to have my photograph taken. Another minute or two. A third employee told me to sit and wait while the photograph was processed. Two minutes later he returned with the finished card. My picture and details had been printed together and sealed in plastic.

The whole process, once I reached the counter, took only five to eight minutes. I had an identity card I could use throughout the United States. No one asked why I wanted it. No one said a foreigner could not have one.

I discussed this with a professor of political science at the University of Iowa. He said it came down to tradition. Americans dislike having government interfere in their lives. They would never willingly accept a government scheme that simply assigned everyone an identity number.

Yet there is an obvious contradiction here. Americans applying for jobs—and even foreigners who do so—already have Social Security numbers used in federal administration. Different political cultures lead countries to handle identification differently. In France, for example, the government assigns a number to a child at birth.

Americans, the professor explained, want identity documents because they need them for ordinary purposes: banking, paying for things or driving. Why let the transport department issue the cards? Because a car is almost essential to living in America for any length of time.

But Americans, he said, would never accept the police issuing their identity documents. That would give the police too much power.

In practice, however, American computer systems are advanced enough for the police to consult information held by transport departments and other agencies. Almost anywhere in the country, an officer could use the number on a license to look up a person's details.

I remembered something a Chinese scholar who had studied in America told me after returning home a couple of years earlier. While visiting another state, he was taken to a county police department and shown its computer system. The police chief asked for his driving license and entered its number. The screen immediately displayed a detailed record: age, nationality, date of birth, occupation, whether he had a criminal record, and more. He was astonished.

So there is something intriguing about having the transport department, rather than the police, issue these cards. It reveals a deep wariness of agencies that can use force. The head of a county law-enforcement agency, too, may be elected rather than appointed. Both arrangements express a desire to restrain the power of the police.

The transport office did not ask me much. Applicants had to show suitable documents proving their identity and that they were at least eighteen. Those younger than eighteen needed a parent's permission. The office accepted documents such as licenses from other states, original birth certificates or copies, passports, immigration papers and insurance records. Its stated purpose was simply to establish the applicant's full name and date of birth—“Showing your full name and birth date,” as the instructions put it.

Americans constantly seek a feeling of security about their freedom from government interference. As we have seen, that security may be partly psychological. The police can still consult information stored in computers. But this need to *feel* protected from official power is itself an enduring force in political life. The real question is how to give people confidence while allowing government to work efficiently.

Look again at the process of obtaining a license or identity card. What matters most is not which office issues it. What matters is that people carry a widely accepted identification document and that government computers hold information about them. When necessary, other government agencies can use the same information. So although there is no obvious official standing over everyone, society remains subject to a far-reaching system of government oversight.

The government does not present this network as a police operation. It presents the card as a service to the public. One document serves as both a driving license and proof of identity. It makes life easier. And because people need identification for so many everyday activities, they willingly apply for the card and join the system. They do so because it really is useful.

The arrangement has another advantage: one card can replace several separate documents. That makes ordinary procedures simpler. On the surface, the system of proving identity looks non-political. In practice, it can perform many of the same functions as a more openly political identity-card system.

## 2. The Rules of the Factory

I knew very little about American factories, and I wanted to see one for myself. Ezra Vogel, a professor at Harvard, had written a book called *Japan as Number One*, comparing American and Japanese ways of managing businesses. He was trying to understand the extraordinary economic success Japan had achieved since the war. Now, with Jack Newman, the former manager of a factory, accompanying us, we went to have a look.

It was the Iowa City plant of Procter & Gamble, a company that had grown into an international giant. It began in Ohio in 1837 as a small manufacturer of candles and soap. There is little need to make candles now. Its best-known products are household cleaners, including Tide washing powder and detergent, and Crest toothpaste. I had seen Crest advertised everywhere in Singapore and Hong Kong. The company also produces food and medicines.

Procter & Gamble puts enormous effort into developing new products. More recently it has entered the rapidly growing market for disposable nappies. Like most businesses, it is constantly looking for new products and new ways to increase its profits. Across the United States it has more than fifty factories and research and development centres. The Iowa City plant is just one of them. It also operates in forty-five countries and territories.

This kind of international organization is typical of American business, and reflects an important part of its business spirit. If an American company sees an opportunity to open another operation, at home or abroad, it is likely to take it. Companies then keep close track of the numbers their new ventures produce. They compete over those figures, too. Yet American business thinking is concerned with more than making money. Reputation matters. Americans are quite willing to spend money to acquire prestige, and that desire influences much of what they do.

The company's growth is striking. Between 1850 and 1859, it employed around eighty people and recorded sales of one million dollars. By 1930, sales had reached two million dollars. It had taken roughly seventy years to double. But in 1956 sales reached one billion dollars, showing how quickly the company had expanded after the war. By the end of the 1970s it employed fifty-nine thousand people and had sales of ten billion dollars.

Such growth is astonishing, but it also illustrates a familiar pattern. Economic development often starts slowly. Once an enterprise reaches a certain size, it can move forward in great leaps. This is true of factories and businesses, and of countries and regions. Japan offers perhaps the clearest national example; many newly industrialized places have followed a similar path.

The Iowa City factory employs about five hundred people. For a highly automated plant, that is not a small number. It makes toothpaste, mouthwash, shampoo and other products.

The new manager welcomed us. He had joined the company more than twenty years earlier, after earning a master's degree. He had worked at several of its factories to gain experience, and had eventually been sent to manage a plant in Saudi Arabia. Only after that long preparation had he been appointed manager here.

At this factory, a university master's degree—preferably in chemistry—is a basic requirement for entering middle management. Candidates then undergo repeated tests in the course of their work, and those with the greatest promise are chosen for leadership. Vogel described Japanese management as promoting people slowly and American management as promoting them quickly. This manager's career suggested almost the opposite. The long process meant that someone who rose to the top understood both the work and how to manage people. Neither education nor practical experience could take the place of the other.

We were interested in the way the factory was run. On the wall of the reception room hung a framed declaration. Its contents were so revealing that I copied them down. It began:

> Every one of us is an owner of this company. We will follow these principles:
>
> - We will work safely at all times and provide a safe working environment.
> - We will act with honesty and integrity.
> - We will treat each person as an individual, with care, respect and trust.
> - We will take responsibility for ourselves and our work, and strive for excellence.
> - We will work together toward shared goals and long-term progress.
> - We will recognize that the needs of the business and of individuals both matter, and work for both.
> - We will understand our business and continue to support it.
> - We will use people's abilities wisely, developing and maintaining the expertise and flexibility we need.
> - We will provide effective training for the work.
> - We will encourage and support personal growth, and look for opportunities to help it happen.
> - We will reward people fairly and recognize each individual's contribution.
> - We will communicate effectively, so that people receive the information they need.
> - Decisions will be made by the people who have the best information about each situation.
> - We will encourage new ideas and knowledge, and learn from success and failure.
> - We will be responsible members of our community.
>
> *Iowa City Plant, November 1986*

This is a remarkable statement of what a business wants to be. Written in November 1986, it hardly resembles the old image of a factory governed by orders alone. It appeals to people as members of one enterprise, rather like the spirit associated with Japanese companies. At the same time, it insists on something deeply rooted in American tradition: the importance of the individual.

Seen historically, the declaration also reveals how far employers and workers have come in finding ways to live with their conflicting interests. Fifty years ago, such a statement might have been unimaginable in a Western factory. These newer changes in capitalism deserve our attention.

There is another side to the story. As science and technology advance, many of the old rules governing production become less important: arriving on time, not leaving early, producing a fixed number of items, checking that each item meets a standard. We could call these *hard rules*, because they directly control the work being done. In a society with less advanced production, they are indispensable. Marx could describe many such examples in *Capital* because they were part of the industrial world he observed.

In a technologically advanced society, however, automated machinery, electricity and electronics make many of those rules unnecessary. What matters increasingly are *soft rules*: arrangements that guide workers' feelings, attitudes and state of mind.

An automated production line forces every worker to keep pace with it. We saw several lines moving so quickly that it was difficult to follow them with our eyes. The employees had an enormous amount to do. Here the pressure does not come chiefly from a supervisor standing over someone. It comes from the machinery itself. We might call this *impersonal compulsion*.

Psychologically, people often find it easier to accept pressure from a machine than pressure from another person. Herbert Marcuse's analysis of modern capitalism is worth studying in this connection, especially his account of how industrial production changes the way people think and feel. Keeping people's inner lives in balance has become an important management problem. The factory's declaration clearly reflects some of the ideas associated with Abraham Maslow's psychology.

Automation also makes management simpler. So does the company's power to dismiss employees. The former manager described some of the people who had been fired. One had stolen things from another worker's locker. Another had taken goods from a self-service shop without paying. These were not disputes about the amount or quality of work performed. They were offences of a different kind.

Technology has removed many of the points at which owners and workers might otherwise clash over the production process. That makes it easier for businesses to expand and for some social tensions to ease. The undemocratic side of American factory management has, in effect, become automated. In Marcuse's terms, it has been made to appear rational.

What remains is the harder task of managing people's inner lives. And that may be the one thing managers cannot easily manage. When arrangements that are unreasonable come to look perfectly reasonable, they can quietly weigh on the human spirit. As technology develops further, that pressure may grow stronger. Western society will have to live with this problem for a long time.

## 3. Businesses Aren't Democracies

I was visiting a friend, and we were having dumplings for dinner. In America, this felt like a real treat.

After weeks of being surrounded by hamburgers, hot dogs, and French fries, you begin to appreciate the simple pleasure of Chinese dumplings or a little pickled mustard. Many Chinese people never quite get used to Western food, even after living in America for years. Perhaps that is one of our more stubborn national habits.

Over dinner, the conversation turned to work.

My friend's wife had taken jobs in several places. She had to work to make a living, and she had found it exhausting.

"Working here wears you out," she told me. "I've cried from sheer exhaustion more than once. Sometimes I just couldn't go on."

What interested me was not simply how hard the work was. I wanted to understand how American workplaces were run. What kind of system could push people to work so hard that they ended up in tears?

Her answer was revealing.

Management, she explained, was extremely strict. In factories and businesses, the boss had almost absolute authority. Some supervisors were so fierce that everybody was afraid of them.

She was now working as a cleaner at a university. Her shift began at five in the afternoon and ended at one in the morning, when the buildings were mostly empty.

Every task was carefully laid out. The supervisor told each worker exactly what needed cleaning and how thoroughly it had to be done. Nothing was left to guesswork.

Then came the inspection.

If the work was not up to standard, the supervisor issued a written notice ordering the worker to do it again. If the problem continued, the worker could be dismissed.

And you never knew when the supervisor might appear.

Someone could be watching at any moment. If a worker was caught resting or chatting, they would be called in and warned. A second offence could mean losing the job.

When the supervisor gave an order, you obeyed.
