There were several levels of management. Above the supervisor were more senior managers. Below that level, a contractor had taken responsibility for cleaning the entire building and divided the work among individual employees.

Each worker had to finish whatever they had been assigned.

If you thought the distribution of work was unfair, you could, in theory, complain to your supervisor. If that got you nowhere, you could take the matter to someone higher up.

But while your complaint was being considered, you still had to finish the work.

Refuse, and you might be fired.

The result was a remarkably strict way of organizing economic activity. It helps explain why American workplaces can be so productive. It also helps explain how so much of American society can function without the government having to manage every detail.

Modern technology makes this kind of management even easier.

My friend's wife told me that supervisors carried walkie-talkies, allowing them to stay in constant contact with their own managers. They could patrol the building while receiving instructions from above.

Some buildings also had cameras that allowed management to watch what employees were doing.

But the most powerful means of control was not the camera or the walkie-talkie.

It was money.

If you lost your job, finding another one could be difficult. A prospective employer might ask why you had left your previous position, then telephone your former boss to check the story.

And in a society like America, life becomes very hard when you cannot find work.

The need to earn a living, the power of money, and the authority of management all push workers to give their best and avoid mistakes.

If you are going to do a job, you have to work at it. There is little room for pretending.

This strict management of everyday working life is one of the things that keeps the wider society running.

Private companies organize most of America's economic activity. Public institutions use much the same methods. And behind it all lies the power of money.

That power can be enormous. But money does not have the same effect in every circumstance. The amount matters.

Consider the wages available to ordinary workers. Someone washing dishes might earn between three and six dollars an hour. Ten hours of work could bring in around forty dollars. At the official exchange rate, that was roughly 160 yuan in Chinese currency.

Of course, simply comparing wages between countries tells us very little. We also have to consider what people must spend to live, and how rising prices affect the value of their earnings.

Even so, the point remains: many jobs in America offer a meaningful financial reward.

If a worker earned only a dollar an hour, the pressure to keep that particular job might not feel nearly so strong.

But when a job provides a decent income, losing it becomes a serious matter. People therefore work hard to hold on to their positions.

This creates a powerful way of keeping economic life organized, without the government having to direct people's daily work.

Government usually steps in only when disputes between employers and workers, or wider social tensions, become too serious to leave alone.

In other words, money does much of the work that government might otherwise have to do.

There is little need to spend time teaching workers what they ought to believe or persuading them that hard work is a moral duty.

You could certainly argue that this system allows business owners to get as much work as possible out of their employees. After all, businesses exist to make profits, and strict management helps them do exactly that.

Yet the same approach can be found well beyond private business. Government offices and public services also rely on clear rules, close supervision, and careful control of their employees.

Together, these arrangements produce a striking result: economic life is managed with great discipline and efficiency.

And I would argue that this is one of the conditions that makes American political democracy possible.

At first, that might sound strange. How can strict management at work help support democracy in politics?

The answer lies in the division of responsibilities.

Because private businesses and other organizations manage so much of everyday life, the government does not have to involve itself directly in everything. Its responsibilities are narrower, and ordinary economic activity can continue even when politics becomes unsettled.

In countries where power and decision-making are concentrated in the hands of government, a change in political leadership or national policy can affect almost every part of society.

America is different. Much of its daily economic life operates beyond the direct control of politicians.

This is a major reason why the system can remain stable.

American management also differs sharply from traditional Chinese approaches.

Americans tend to favour strict rules that are applied consistently. Chinese management has traditionally been more flexible, allowing people to adjust their decisions to particular circumstances and personal relationships.

To Americans, their approach seems perfectly natural. To Chinese people, the more flexible approach often feels just as natural—and more humane.

A Taiwanese man who held the second-highest position in an American company once told me how he dealt with this difference.

He explained to his American colleagues that he was Chinese and preferred to manage people in the Chinese way.

"But if you gentlemen won't cooperate," he told them, "I'll have no choice but to manage you the American way. It's up to you."

The story captures something important.

For Chinese people, accepting American-style management can be psychologically difficult. It may feel rigid, impersonal, and unfair. It can also make personal relationships harder to maintain.

But this raises a larger question for China.

How should we organize everyday economic and social life so that it provides a sound foundation for political development?

That is something we need to think about very carefully.

## 4. Human Services

*Social services*, sometimes called *human services*, have become a central part of welfare policy in Western countries. I have long felt that public welfare is an important source of stability in these societies. It has helped ease conflicts that once seemed impossible to resolve.

Marx, Engels and Lenin described and criticized the inhuman conditions they saw in capitalist society. Today, governments have taken responsibility for dealing with many of those conditions. Of course, this did not happen out of kindness alone. These arrangements grew out of conflicts that had already become severe. But once established, they began to play an unexpectedly important part in keeping the social system going.

I visited a county human services office. Several people were waiting to be seen. They appeared poor. Some were pregnant women; others were mothers with children. The director, whom I shall call F., received us.

Organizations of this kind exist in every state. Iowa provides a useful example of how they work. At the state level there is a Department of Human Services. Below it are eight regions, each containing several counties. The county we visited belongs to the Cedar Rapids region.

The state department is headed by a commissioner who answers to the governor, and there is also a Human Services Council. At the state level, its work includes a veterans' home, mental health and social services. These offices develop policies, organize programmes and provide assistance.

Other branches handle particular tasks. Community services, under a deputy commissioner, deals with refugees, volunteers, regional offices and county offices. Another deputy commissioner supervises planning and organization, including communication, programme development and coordination. An administration and budget branch deals with finance, legal affairs, personnel, training and evaluation.

The Human Services Council sets policy and advises the department. The community services branch oversees the eight regional offices and 146 local offices. What is the whole organization trying to achieve?

Its own documents say that it provides help to people in Iowa who face personal, financial or social problems. Its main responsibility is to help individuals and families support themselves and to improve the well-being of the people of Iowa.

The thinking behind that goal is worth examining. It begins with the belief that a healthy family provides the foundation for personal growth and a happy life. The department therefore seeks to strengthen families and help people develop. It also says that services should respect privacy, protect personal dignity and allow people to keep control over their own lives.

These are unmistakably American ideals. The services are designed not simply to rescue people in difficulty, but also to support and preserve the values of American society.

What do human services actually provide? Every society must take some responsibility for people who cannot care for themselves and for those with serious mental illnesses. The more revealing aspect of this system is its attention to problems created by society itself.

One major programme distributes food stamps to people without work or income, helping them meet their most basic needs. People may need them after losing a job, or because of an early pregnancy or other hardship. The office also helps pregnant young women and single mothers, encouraging them to return to school and finish their education.

Then there is the protection of abused children. America may be highly developed, but accounts of child abuse are disturbingly common. F. told us that in the previous year the county had confirmed one hundred cases. Two of the children had suffered permanent disabilities. Many of these injuries, she explained, were caused by beatings.

The office also helps adults who have been abused, most of them women. Violence against wives is hardly unknown in America. Medical assistance is another part of its work.

Such services provide a kind of shock absorber. Pressures that might otherwise shake the social order are reduced before they grow into a larger crisis. Their wider purpose is plain enough.

Social services can accomplish things that laws and politics alone cannot. Legal and political arrangements work when people can still respond to reason. But someone who is close to despair may no longer care about the normal rules. Social services can help keep people from being driven to that point.

Because they perform this important function, governments spend a great deal on them. And because social problems have become so serious, governments have little choice but to spend the money.

Consider the Cedar Rapids region's report for the 1987 financial year. It recorded spending of $168 million on aid to children, $108 million on food stamps and $412 million on medical assistance. The region had a population of about 430,000. The scale of that spending is not hard to grasp.

We can look still more closely at the county we visited, with a population of around 16,000. Compared with counties in China, it would seem almost deserted. Its reported spending for the same financial year included:

- Nearly $320,000 in assistance to children.
- $63,000 for families with unemployed parents.
- $270,000 in food stamps.
- More than $140,000 in medical assistance.
- $60,000 in additional state assistance.
- $76,000 in temporary emergency food assistance.
- $200,000 for adoption services.
- $100,000 to purchase services.
- $160,000 in child benefits.
- $140,000 for administration, salaries and related costs.
- $9,500 in administrative expenses.

The report gave the total for the various items as $2.7 million. For Iowa as a whole, the figure was $839 million. These are astonishing sums. In one sense, they tell us how deeply rooted the underlying social problems have become.

Fifty-seven per cent of the funds came from the federal government and forty-three per cent from the state. Spending on this scale is essential to social stability. Imagine what might happen if all these programmes were suddenly withdrawn. At the same time, the cost is becoming a growing burden on taxpayers, and the arguments over it will only become more intense.

F. traced the idea behind this assistance to England in the sixteenth century. Around the year 1500, she said, people in England had begun setting aside land to help the poor. That tradition then crossed the Atlantic aboard the *Mayflower*.

Perhaps so. But I think a more important question is why poverty existed in the first place. When the *Mayflower* arrived, the settlers did not yet have the deep divisions between rich and poor that came later. A social system can create people who have reason to oppose it. Those in power must then find ways to change or contain that opposition. The history of America since the Second World War makes the point clear enough.

Many people strongly favour cutting welfare spending. Yet when the time comes to act, nobody is eager to make drastic reductions. Human services have become part of the system itself. Remove them, and the balance could be lost.

The deeper difficulty is that American society has not stopped producing the conditions that make people reject it. In some ways it seems to be producing more of them. Human services can soften the consequences, but cannot remove their causes. If the process continues, there may come a day when even this system reaches its limits. For the moment, however, America has little choice. The alternative might be serious political unrest.

There is a lesson here for every country. No society can solve every problem faced by every individual. But a society that wants lasting stability must build ways of meeting as many of those needs as it can.

Sometimes the source of a crisis is not a grand political principle or an elaborate system of government. It is something very ordinary: whether people have enough to eat and enough clothes to keep warm.

## 5. Coca-Cola Headquarters

Coca-Cola must be one of the most famous companies in the world. In recent years it has also found an enormous market in China, where practically everyone knows the name. When people in China debate the idea of spending beyond our means, Coca-Cola often comes up as an example. Perhaps the real symbol of excess is the aluminium can rather than the drink inside it. In any case, the world seems to be taking to Coca-Cola. I wanted to see what this celebrated company might reveal about the way American society is organized.

Its headquarters are in Atlanta. For such a famous company, the office buildings are surprisingly modest: an older fifteen-storey building and a newer one beside it. Amid Atlanta's skyscrapers, they are hardly conspicuous. Yet from this relatively unremarkable site, people direct a business that reaches across the world.

The opening page of the company's 1987 annual report declared, in effect:

> Coca-Cola's mission is to sell drinks to a thirsty world. Every day, more than five hundred million people enjoy Coca-Cola ...

There was no mention of money. Yet the company exists, of course, to make money by selling drinks.

Its 1987 figures show just how successful it had become. Total business amounted to more than $7.6 billion, probably more than the entire national output of quite a few small countries. Operating income was more than $1.3 billion, and net income exceeded $900 million.

Coca-Cola had grown from a small, rough workshop into a company operating on five continents. Other great American businesses had followed much the same road. By this time it had reached 155 countries and accounted for 44 per cent of worldwide sales of soft drinks. The company itself employed 17,000 people. Across the global Coca-Cola system, the workforce was far larger: 500,000 full-time and 500,000 part-time employees. Through its operations, the company was directing the activities of roughly a million people.

Think about that for a moment. Does it have political significance? Or perhaps a meaning that goes beyond politics?

We were shown around the headquarters by a woman from public relations, who appeared to have some connection with the family behind the company's early history. The offices contained valuable paintings, both modern and classical. Collecting art was part of company policy. In a few years, the pictures might be worth even more.

In the entrance hall hung flags representing the 155 countries and territories where Coca-Cola was sold. Nearby, an electronic display automatically showed the price of the company's shares on the New York stock market. Business was good, the share price was rising, and the outlook seemed favourable. Many employees bought shares in their own company.

The corridors also displayed advertisements from the 1930s and 1940s. Several featured an attractive young woman in different poses—perhaps a well-known singer or dancer of the time. Professor Shi Xiaochong remembered seeing those advertisements in Shanghai before the founding of the People's Republic of China. There were also gifts sent by Coca-Cola factories around the world. Among them we found one from the company's plant in Xiamen, China.

Our guide then took us into the former chairman's office, which, we were told, was not ordinarily open to visitors. There were all kinds of gifts, but the signed photographs of several American presidents hanging on the walls were enough to convey the company's standing. Americans place great value on such honours. Having a famous person's photograph on your wall becomes a source of pride. Perhaps this weakness belongs to human beings everywhere.

Once Coca-Cola became immensely rich, it did more than expand production. It also gave substantial amounts of money to universities and research institutions. That was what interested me most.

Emory University, near Atlanta, had received considerable support from the company and had developed rapidly in recent years. During our visit, its president told us that Coca-Cola's donations had made an enormous difference. The evidence was there to see: handsome new buildings were rising across the campus. With more money, the university could offer high salaries to distinguished professors and scholarships to promising students.

Large American companies have a tradition of making substantial gifts to universities, research institutions and foundations. The tax system also helps encourage such giving. Consider the Brookings Institution, which I discuss further in Chapter 10.

Its 1987 accounts show how a research institute can support itself. Government funding made up just 3 per cent of its income; private donations accounted for 39 per cent; membership fees and sales of books and periodicals, 31 per cent; and grants, 27 per cent. In dollars, the sums were approximately:

| Source of income | Amount |
| --- | ---: |
| Government funding | $450,000 |
| Private donations | $6,000,000 |
| Membership fees and publications | $4,850,000 |
| Grants | $4,100,000 |
| **Total** | **$15,400,000** |

More than fifteen million dollars for an institution devoted to research and ideas. With support like this, it is hardly surprising that American research institutes flourish.

Imagine instead that every institute had to be financed by the government. Problems would soon appear. To begin with, a government that pays for research may also try to influence what researchers think and say. And there are limits to how many institutes the public purse can afford. If only one or two survived, there would be little real competition between ideas. It would be difficult to create the lively intellectual world that such competition makes possible.

All this leads back to my earlier question about the political significance of enormous companies.

Coca-Cola organizes the work of nearly a million people and oversees hundreds of factories inside and outside the United States. In a practical sense, it governs the working lives of those people. Add together the workers directed by America's private companies, and the political importance of the arrangement becomes clear. If all these factories and businesses belonged to the government, their daily management would become a vast public responsibility. No government has the capacity to handle all that work directly.

Under capitalism, private companies pursue their own aims, using whatever methods they can to organize their activities. Government, for its part, sets limits and resolves problems between them. The responsibility for managing people is divided in two.

One part belongs to society's ability to organize itself through businesses and other institutions. The other belongs to the political system. Government supervises the first from above, but is not caught up in its daily operations. As a result, its burden is lighter.

The organizations outside government develop their own procedures, rules and ways of working. Their activities can continue steadily even when political leaders or policies change. While pursuing their private goals, they also help keep the whole social system moving. This division of labour is one of the foundations of America's political arrangements.

The large companies make both sides of it possible. Their contribution is not merely economic, though they themselves may not recognize the wider political effect. If they consciously set out to exercise that kind of political power, the result might be very different—and much less stable.

Coca-Cola began in a little workshop. So did many other large companies. As enterprises like these grow and their networks become established, the political and social order may also become more stable. This is one way of understanding the relationship between economic life and politics.

## 6. God Among Us

Religion—or, more precisely, religious life—is one of the basic parts of American society. For many people, it is inseparable from daily living. You cannot understand America without trying to understand its churches.

Enter almost any community, whether a city or a village, and you will find churches of all shapes and sizes. Sometimes there seem to be an astonishing number of them.

Not all Americans are religious. Indeed, not everyone who goes to church is a believer. There is a considerable difference between having religious faith and attending a service. Yet people go, for all kinds of personal and social reasons, and their presence gives the churches an important place in American life.

Christmas is an especially revealing example. It commemorates the birth of Jesus Christ, yet it has long since become a popular public holiday as well as a religious one. Churches arrange special activities, and so do organizations with no religious connection.

On the eve of Christmas, I attended a special concert given by a city symphony orchestra. Concerts of this kind are held each year, often with much the same music: hymns praising the Saviour. This time the programme included Handel's *Messiah*. When the choir reached the *Hallelujah* chorus, the entire audience stood, following a custom associated with King George II. The words praise the Lord and proclaim that the kingdoms of the world will become the kingdom of God and Christ, and that the King of Kings and Lord of Lords will reign forever.

Many Americans draw emotional strength from religion or religious organizations. That helps explain why these institutions retain such influence.

But religious belief takes different forms. Some people are deeply devout. They approach God with genuine reverence, attend church each Sunday and take an active part in church activities. Their religious habits continue at home.

I once had dinner with a prominent man in finance. Before we ate, everyone joined hands and his daughter said a prayer in Latin. For that family, prayer was still a normal part of sitting down to a meal.

Other families go to church every Sunday even though they are not especially religious. It is simply what they do. Missing a service leaves them feeling that something is absent.

I discussed this with a professor of theology. In his view, relatively few of the people attending church were firm believers. Many came because church offered them a way of life. It was a place to meet friends and neighbours, talk, and enjoy a sense of belonging. Others maintained close ties with churches because of the help they provided. Churches do a great deal of charitable work, and people who are poor or in trouble often depend on it.

The church door, then, is open to people whose beliefs, feelings and purposes may be very different.

One important reason for religion's influence is that it has become closely woven into ordinary life. Much of the mystery associated with religion has faded. American religious practice looks very different from that of the Middle Ages. Many large new churches are strikingly modern. Instead of pointed towers and old-fashioned stonework, they have contemporary designs. Some have caused arguments precisely because they look so unlike traditional churches. Inside, they may be splendidly furnished and decorated with fresh greenery. I doubt God or Christ would have imagined anything quite like them.

Churches also use the newest methods of communication. Several major television stations broadcast Sunday worship into homes across the country. Well-known preachers lead the services, but much of what they discuss concerns ordinary worries and hopes. They may talk about how to succeed, or why people should live in peace with one another. Whatever the subject, they connect it with a passage from the Bible.

During the Olympic Games in Seoul, a famous American preacher held a service there and had it broadcast live to the United States. The theme was a biblical saying: “Before you ask, I will answer.” He claimed that the fine weather during the Games showed that God had granted people's wish even before they had expressed it.

Modern broadcasting carries religious culture far beyond the reach of missionaries in the seventeenth or eighteenth century. People absorb it simply by seeing and hearing it again and again, sometimes without realizing it.

Another reason for religion's strength is charity. Religious organizations have their own nonprofit services, supported by donations from believers. Many churches are wealthy. People give large sums to demonstrate their faith or to seek peace for their souls, and the churches can spend these tax-free gifts on their activities.

One church, for instance, provides a free lunch every Thursday. Anyone can come and eat. During Thanksgiving or Christmas, churches arrange for foreign students to spend a holiday with American families. They also organize inexpensive trips, with travellers sometimes staying in the homes of fellow believers. These are only a few examples.

In a country built around private ownership, government does not take responsibility for every kind of personal hardship. Some people who have nowhere else to turn seek help from churches. Along with other voluntary organizations, religious groups bring such people into a network of care and support. That is a significant part of America's social organization.

The sheer variety of religions is remarkable. Most Americans do not seem terribly concerned about the precise differences between one church and another. They may not know what separates their own beliefs from someone else's. Being part of one religious community is enough.

Religious groups are so numerous that even a handful of people may declare themselves followers of a distinct faith. The Constitution protects freedom of religion. If somebody were to announce tomorrow that they had founded a religion in which bees were the angels of the world, no one could forbid the belief. Other people could simply refuse to share it.

There are dangers, too. Some religious groups have caused terrible tragedies. Jim Jones founded the Peoples Temple, who forced more than nine hundred followers into mass suicide in Guyana. The tragedy shocked the world.
Religion, then, cannot be understood solely as a matter of personal faith. Religious organizations have a much wider social role. They connect thousands upon thousands of people, bring them together and guide their behaviour. That gives them considerable influence over public life.

Robin W. Lovin, writing about religion and American public life, describes three important relationships between religion and society.

First, religion can help preserve order. It supports established authority and teaches people that some obedience is necessary for organized life. Without religion, he suggests, a society may come close to disorder. Religious teaching calls on people to respect both God and the order of the world.

Second, religious life can protect freedom. Lovin sees this as one of religion's greatest contributions to American public life. Religion developed differently in America from the way it had in Europe. Many settlers arrived seeking freedom from religious persecution. Once established, their religious communities defended their own liberty—and, in doing so, also defended freedom more generally. Religious freedom cannot survive without some measure of freedom for society as a whole. It encourages people to value liberty in public affairs, too.

Third, religion can advance social justice. By spreading religious teachings about equality, it can influence public life and political culture. Lovin regards these three roles—order, freedom and justice—as the basic connections between religion and public life.

I do not think his account tells the whole story. Religion plainly has useful social functions, but its problems are plain as well. We should not pretend otherwise. Its effects differ according to the society and culture in which it operates.

Looking at religion from the standpoint of how society is organized and governed, I would identify three main functions:

- It can create a powerful network of organizations, capable of guiding members according to rules of their own.
- It can provide a set of moral beliefs and values that helps guide people's conduct.
- It can spread its activities and ideas widely through society.

But these functions benefit society only under certain conditions. Otherwise religion can do great harm. In many places it has been a major cause of unrest. Two conditions seem especially important if it is to play a positive role.

The first is that religion must not become an instrument of politics or a ruler over politics. In medieval Europe, religion's dominance could take the form of ignorant tyranny. In some modern countries, political rulers turn religion into a tool of power, producing a tyranny that keeps people ignorant.

The second is that religion must not become superstition. Once it does, we encounter absurd and cruel acts: burning Giordano Bruno, sacrificing children to a river god, or searching for magical substances that promise immortality.

Religion and superstition are not the same. Religion, at its best, helps people develop moral character, self-restraint and a willingness to give. Superstition demands submission to an idea without asking people to think. It is driven by blind belief and fear. Where the two have not been separated, religion cannot easily make a positive contribution to society.

A society may benefit from religious communities that bring people together and help set standards of conduct. Thinkers such as Plato, Aristotle and Rousseau all discussed the place religion might have in an ideal society. Without religious life, society loses one of the ways it can organize itself, and more responsibility falls directly on government. Where many religious organizations exist, the political system must keep them within shared rules rather than try to run their affairs.

At first it seems strange that religion should flourish in a country so advanced in science and technology. It looks like a puzzle about human reason. Yet Americans often approach religion in much the same practical, rational spirit that they bring to technology. In such surroundings, it is difficult for a religion to develop entirely into superstition. In other countries, where modern scientific knowledge is less widespread, superstition may find more room to grow.

There is a curious possibility here. The further knowledge advances, the less dangerous religious life may become. And as religion becomes less dangerous, it may become more active.

Of course, people can use anything for their own ends, including religion. American newspapers and television have recently been full of a scandal involving a television preacher. He had an affair with a woman and diverted large amounts of money donated by believers for his own benefit. The affair became a national scandal.

It is a reminder that religion can serve society or harm it. Every society in which religion plays a part must face that problem again and again.

# Chapter 9: How a Society Passes Itself On

## 1. The Education System

For any society, the hardest task is not to keep producing goods. It is to ensure that its way of life can survive from one generation to the next.

A political and social system is most secure when people accept it as their own. Will the next generation accept it? Will their children? Those questions are central to the system's future. Education is one of the main ways a society passes on the beliefs and values that sustain it. America has developed a particularly extensive means of doing this.

American education is highly developed. People sometimes say that America is a paradise for children, a battlefield for the young and a hell for the elderly. Part of what makes childhood a supposed paradise is the chance to receive a good education.

Education also serves another purpose. America has no deeply rooted aristocratic tradition like Europe's. The old divisions of rank are less powerful, and people can, at least in principle, move from one social level to another. Society still has its groups and ranks, with people at the top and others at the bottom. But in a country organized around advanced technology, higher education is often the ticket to a better position.

You can see this in the jobs people do. Cleaning and other physically demanding work is frequently left to those with less education. Staff in large companies, technicians and managers generally need good university training. In such a society, education becomes almost an article of faith.

I call it a faith because it settles deeply into the way people think. Charles R. Adrian remarked that parents nearly always want their children to have a good education, and a better one than they had themselves. Americans may not feel quite the same intense pressure to raise a brilliant child that Chinese families often do. But no parent hopes to raise a child who achieves nothing. Sending a son or daughter to a famous university is one of the great ambitions of the American dream.

What is unusual is the way American education is organized. There is no single national system directing every school. Each state has its own arrangements.

State laws establish school districts with authority over public education. They provide for local school boards and specify how members are chosen—usually by election. That alone shows how important education is considered. School boards can raise taxes, borrow money, build schools, hire staff and decide local school policies.

The precise powers differ from state to state. Laws may specify which taxes boards can collect and at what rates, how much they can borrow, how many years children must attend school, the minimum pay and qualifications of teachers, and the broad content of the curriculum.

Many states choose textbooks, establish teaching plans, set examinations for the whole state and specify minimum staffing requirements. Some have even made teaching communism or evolution unlawful. State governments can therefore exercise close control over education, sometimes through very detailed rules. Iowa, for example, requires secondary students to study American history and government. In practice, those subjects also teach American beliefs.

Each state has a department of education, led by a state education official. In some states the official is elected; elsewhere the person may be appointed by a school board or the governor. The department works to bring schools up to the standards set by the state. It oversees teaching, provides technical help and information, sets minimum curriculum requirements and approves teachers. States can also influence local decisions through the money they provide.

Yet despite the powers of the state, much of everyday school policy remains in the hands of locally elected boards. A school district is a special public body, separate from the ordinary machinery of local government. It can therefore make many decisions for itself, so long as it stays within state law.

Elections to school boards can be fiercely contested. The outcome helps decide what children will learn, and parents naturally care about that. Education is one of the fields in which disagreements between conservatives and progressives, tradition and modern life, religion and unbelief come most clearly into view. The beliefs of board members can shape the minds of the next generation.

This is a distinctive American arrangement. People elect local representatives to decide what kind of education their children will receive. The federal government has little direct power over the content of local education.

We might call it *education that is not run as a single national system*. In Japan, France, the Soviet Union, China and many other countries, education has been organized much more directly by the state.

People once joked that France's education minister could sit in an office, look at the clock and tell a visitor exactly which lesson children of a particular age were studying in every primary school in the country—even which part of the lesson their teachers had reached. The joke may be exaggerated, but it makes the contrast clear. Local American school boards can make their own choices.

I met one school board member whose views were decidedly old-fashioned. He disliked the way young people now dressed and behaved. He would have preferred everybody to look as they had before the 1930s: men in suits, women in skirts, everyone neatly turned out.

What, then, does the federal government do in education? In principle, not very much beyond helping pay for it. But growing federal funding has itself become controversial. With money comes influence over policy, and that can weaken both state governments and local school boards.

There are also many private schools, including Catholic schools concerned about interference with their religious character. Race, culture, property ownership and taxation all complicate the question of who should control education.

School boards are highly political bodies. Their members are often prominent local people: business owners, people with property, managers and others of standing. Teachers do not always trust them. Some teachers argue that educational policy should be left to educators and kept free of politics. But that hope is probably unrealistic. It is difficult to imagine education entirely without politics—or politics entirely without education.

States also maintain their own universities, commonly two or three in each state. These universities are overseen and funded by state governments, with university presidents appointed by governors. They do not depend on the federal government for their regular budgets. It is an arrangement worth considering. If Washington had to pay for every university in the country, the burden might be far beyond its means.

American schools can be equipped to an impressive standard. I visited a primary school in Iowa City with modern rooms, generous space and excellent facilities. But fine equipment does not guarantee a good education.

There are serious problems. In some places, the quality of teaching is shockingly poor. Racial divisions trouble schools: some white parents resist sending their children to schools with large numbers of Black pupils. Government efforts to draw attendance boundaries and require children to attend different schools have created difficulties of their own. Drug use among young people is another grave concern. Unless such problems are addressed, education itself could become a source of crisis.

Even so, the overall education system is extensive and firmly established. Spending on it may be second only to military spending. No investment is more important to a society's progress—or more likely to repay it over the long term—than education.

A modern society needs more than modern machines. It needs people who can invent those machines, understand them and use them. The development of people is perhaps the most important measure of modernization.

But developing people is not a task that can be finished once and for all. It must begin in childhood and continue through every generation. Who will carry out this immense, never-ending work? The education system.

Human beings have one profound limitation: the knowledge and moral habits gained by one generation cannot simply be inherited by the next. Every new generation must learn them afresh. That simple fact makes education indispensable.

## 2. MIT

MIT is the Massachusetts Institute of Technology, one of America's best-known universities. Its established Chinese name is usually translated as the Massachusetts Institute of Technology, though a more literal modern rendering would be something like the Massachusetts College of Technology. It stands in Cambridge, Massachusetts, beside Boston.

Professor Lucian Pye, president of the American Political Science Association, welcomed us. He teaches political science at MIT. That might sound odd at a university known for science and engineering. Yet its political science department is a distinguished one, just as the Naval Academy also has a political science department. Pye himself is a well-known scholar. I might add that in many places I have been struck by how closely the development of American political science and American political institutions have gone together.

MIT is, in many respects, a typical American university. It was established in 1861 and began admitting students in 1865. Its growth accelerated during the Second World War and the years that followed, when much of its research was connected with wartime needs.

Today it has five schools, covering agriculture and planning, humanities and social sciences, engineering, management, and science. Science, engineering and the natural sciences form its centre of gravity, but it also offers a wide range of subjects in the humanities and social sciences. Because fewer students enter those fields, the standard can be especially high.

MIT now has around ten thousand students, including approximately 4,500 undergraduates and 5,000 graduate students. About half study engineering and another fifth study science. Competition for places is exceptionally fierce. A student without outstanding ability is unlikely to get in.

The budget is just as remarkable. One of the professors receiving us said the university spent around a billion dollars a year—roughly the same as Coca-Cola's annual net income.

Another professor, who directed MIT's Centre for International Studies, spoke almost apologetically about his own institution's finances.

“Our centre has a very small budget,” he said. “It's pitiful compared with the other schools. We have only two million dollars a year.”

*Only* two million dollars? The word “only” would mean something quite different to a professor in China. Of course, compared with MIT's total budget, the centre's share really was small.

American universities, especially the most famous ones, have extraordinary resources. That is one reason they have been so important in spreading modern ways of thinking. In a sense, American modernization has grown out of the country's thousands of universities.

Young people first encounter much of the modern world at university. They study the techniques of modern life, but more important still, they develop a *modern way of thinking*. Without that outlook, a generation can enjoy the benefits of modernization without being able to create anything modern itself.

Universities with enough money become laboratories for new ideas and methods. Students entering them are immersed in an atmosphere of change and discovery. By the time they leave, that way of thinking has become part of them.

This, to me, is the real importance of higher education. Its greatest purpose is not simply to produce a handful of gifted people. It is to give an entire generation—an entire generation—a modern outlook.

Who pays for all this? MIT is a private university. Much of its money comes from donations and support outside the university. Here again we can see the importance of the ways American society supports institutions without leaving every expense to the government.

MIT is typical in another respect. Its professors call it a *research university*. What does that mean?

They offered a simple distinction. European universities have traditionally concentrated on passing down existing knowledge. American universities, they said, put greater emphasis on discovering new knowledge. That is the central idea of a research university.

MIT is organized around laboratories and research. It has more than a thousand professors, who teach and conduct research, supported by more than a thousand other staff.

To encourage discovery, the university tries to make teachers and students partners. Their relationship should not be limited to one person passing information to another. They should learn and develop together, working toward things that neither yet knows. The aim is not only to repeat the past but to prepare for the future.

That outlook is a product of America's wider culture. It is difficult to calculate how much universities have contributed to the country's progress, because the contribution is so great. Generation after generation, they encourage young people to think of something new, respect new ideas and find ways to put them into practice. This is one of the main engines of social progress.

Education is particularly vulnerable to an understandable mistake: teachers may see their task as explaining things they know to students who do not know them. There is nothing absurd about that. But there is a better possibility as well. Why not encourage teachers and students to discover things neither side knows yet?

That, I think, is what drives human progress forward.

MIT's results suggest that the approach works. I was told that 70 per cent of companies in the Boston area had been founded by its students, most of them in advanced technology. More than four thousand people associated with the institute were teaching at universities around the world. Students came from every part of the globe.

Walking across the campus, I saw magnificent buildings rising above green lawns and people of many different backgrounds coming out of them. There were especially large numbers of Asian students. This was the power of knowledge made visible.

The institute also had an explicit policy against discrimination. It stated that students of every race, colour, sex or national origin should enjoy the same rights, privileges and programmes available to other MIT students. That, too, is part of the power of knowledge.

MIT's influence extends well beyond American society. It spreads not just knowledge but also something of the American spirit. Without advanced education, a people cannot have a lasting influence on others or establish itself fully among the nations of the world.

Education cannot provide everything that industry, agriculture and commerce produce. Yet it offers something that none of those activities can supply.

Of course, America's universities have problems—so many that some professors are deeply worried about their future. But the strongest students still find their way through. At an institution like MIT, the competition is so intense that inferior work has little chance of passing unnoticed.

## 3. Harvard's Kennedy School of Government

A beautiful river runs past MIT: the Charles. In the evening, walking slowly beside it, watching happy couples and birds overhead, you feel an extraordinary sense of ease. Harvard University shares the river with MIT. It, too, is known throughout the world. We went to Harvard to visit the John F. Kennedy School of Government.

The school occupies an attractive modern building. A professor welcomed us and explained that it was not simply another university political science department. It had its own aims and its own way of teaching. Its main task was to train people for work in the public sector. Hence the name: a school of government.

The Kennedy family had given it money, and the school was named in recognition of that gift. Given its purpose, the school's connection with political life could hardly be closer. Its graduates would go on to work in public institutions, where their decisions would directly affect government and the way the system operated.

The school defines the public sector broadly. It includes government, the news media and other areas of public life, though not private enterprises. Nor is it quite like a traditional school of public administration. Such schools usually emphasize administrative theory and basic knowledge. Kennedy emphasizes public policy. It wants people with a broad understanding, rather than experts trained in a single narrow field. Practical problems matter more here than theory for its own sake.

I asked how this differed from political science. The professor said that political science tries to describe political behaviour accurately, while the school concentrates on analysing real situations. They were also making a distinction between two ideas: *public administration* and *public management*. Public administration asks how to get people and organizations to obey more effectively; public management asks how to innovate.

They wanted their students to develop two kinds of ability:

- **Policy analysis:** the ability to examine public problems, evaluate programmes, plan action and work with economic and statistical information.
- **Political understanding:** knowledge of political systems and international politics.

The point of their research was to discover how much room remained for developing and improving political institutions. Students therefore needed ideas, creativity, sensitivity, imagination and the ability to bring different things together. The professor summed up the approach neatly: its aims were political, but its way of anticipating events was scientific.

Put plainly, this was an American school for training government officials.

One American strength is the willingness to treat almost anything as a field that can be explored and developed. Politics is no exception. Americans are unusually willing to study political problems as if they were studying particles or the movement of heavenly bodies. I doubt even Europeans carry the approach quite so far. European tradition tends to see politics as an art. Americans are inclined to believe it can also be a technique.

Kennedy is not alone. Other institutions of this kind exist, and many university political science departments do similar work. Even the idea of private universities training public officials is striking. Turning politics into a technical discipline has advantages and disadvantages. But when it comes to training civil servants and people who design public policy, it does produce results. It helps give the social system a firm foundation.

America has gone a long way toward making administration a technical profession. Without higher education and special training, a person can hardly work in government. At the same time, the political system forces officials to deal with ever more complicated social problems: if they do not, they will not win votes. As a result, research into particular problems becomes steadily deeper and more exact.

Two important effects follow.

First, the civil service gains a pool of well-qualified people. Strict training gives them the knowledge and skills needed to serve those in political power in whatever ways may be required. That helps political rule endure.

Second, government decision-making begins to resemble a science. Officials trained in these methods naturally use them when drawing up policies. If they ignore the accepted standards, their proposals may be rejected not only by other government departments but also by society. Every day, thousands of people examine the policies already in force, exposing their strengths and weaknesses. Those preparing to enter government are taught to look for new areas where policy is needed. That, too, pushes decision-making forward.

A government that can make sensible policies and solve social problems has a better chance of surviving. People have argued for years about why capitalism, seemingly on the point of collapse, goes on living and refuses to decay away. Perhaps part of the answer lies here: whether public policy becomes something people can systematically study and improve.

The Kennedy School has great confidence in its methods. One of its programmes offers a particularly clear example. It is designed to train officials from developing countries in the analysis and management of public policy. Its name is the **Edward S. Mason Program in Public Policy and Management**.

The school argues that modern countries face challenges unlike anything they have known before. Every one of them must manage rapid economic and social change, and the public sector has a crucial part to play. Officials therefore need the skills to analyse policies, make decisions and carry them out.

The Mason programme recruits about fifty senior officials from developing countries each year. Its aim is to give them three qualities needed for excellent public service: sound analytical skills, management ability and a sense of professional ethics. All three matter.

Participants come from Asia, Africa and Latin America. Their average age is thirty-seven, and they have twelve years of work experience behind them. They study English, mathematics, microeconomics, economics, computers, business and government, crime, energy and the environment, health, housing and social development, human resources, international development, international security, journalism, politics, public policy, science and technology, transport, urban economic development, agriculture and central banking, among other subjects.

Teaching relies heavily on case studies. The different policy fields are divided into considerable detail. Students also make field visits, attend lectures by politicians and scholars, and travel abroad—to places such as Mexico, Cuba, South Korea, Egypt, Morocco and Argentina—to broaden their experience.

It is an impressive programme. The cost, though, is enough to take your breath away: **$35,012 per person**, not including overseas study visits. The programme has no funds to cover these costs, so students must find the money themselves. But the Kennedy School's reputation brings in students every year. The school makes a tidy sum as a result.

American education in public management has plenty of problems. Yet it also has enough successes to make it attractive. Much of that attraction comes not from the public sector's achievements but from the country's economic success. People can be shortsighted: they sometimes imagine that economic success proves everything else works equally well.

Even so, the public sector's contribution to American society cannot be overlooked. Still less can we ignore the contribution made by the institutions that teach people how to run it.

## 4. The Talent Factories

The Maxwell School at Syracuse University in New York State is one of America's leading institutions for training public administrators. Its graduates enter the federal government, state governments and other public bodies, often rising to important positions. I was told that more than twenty American ambassadors had studied there. The former ambassador to Pakistan, who had recently died in the same plane crash as President Zia-ul-Haq, was also a graduate. In Washington, you find Maxwell alumni everywhere. They work not just across the United States but in many developing countries as well.

The Maxwell School of Citizenship and Public Affairs was founded in 1924. It is described as America's first school of public administration, established to educate and train civil servants. After more than sixty years, it has become a considerable *people factory*, steadily supplying government institutions with trained staff.

The school brings together departments of anthropology, economics, geography, history, political science, public affairs and sociology. But it does not offer an undergraduate public-administration programme. The reasoning is that undergraduates usually lack the experience and judgement needed for this kind of work. Instead, the programme concentrates on master's students and people already in employment.

It takes around six doctoral students a year. Their work is more academic, and they generally go into teaching or research rather than the daily business of government. The master's programme, by contrast, is designed to prepare people for a profession: government service.

Federal and state agencies regularly send their employees to Maxwell for further study. When I was talking with the well-known professor Rosenbloom, several older students came in to hand over assignments. At first I found this puzzling. He explained that they had been sent by the federal government. After a year's training, they would return to their jobs. Then he joked, “You had better be polite to them. They're all from the tax department.”

Schools like Maxwell supply skilled people to agencies that make policy and carry out public work. They help government operate efficiently. Although they are educational institutions, they are also an unofficial—and indispensable—part of the political and administrative system.

What do they teach their students? What kind of knowledge do they believe a public official needs? Maxwell offers its master's students in public administration courses such as these:

- **Computers and the computerized public sector.** Basic computer knowledge, the use of computers in public administration and practical computer work.
- **Introduction to public administration.** The central questions facing public administration, the problems officials must tackle and methods of analysing policy.
- **Organization theory.** How public organizations operate and change; how they differ from private organizations; ways of thinking about organizations; individual and group decisions; organizational design and relations with the surrounding environment.
- **Public administration and democracy.** The place of public policy and administration in a constitutional democracy, especially the relationship between administrative priorities—such as efficiency and economy—and constitutional values such as equality, liberty and due process. How can administration reconcile them?
- **National planning and management capacity.** The main difficulties of governing, the design and execution of long-term plans, and the roles of planning offices, forecasts, research institutes, multinational banks and companies, politicians and senior administrators. The social and political conditions and consequences of national planning.
- **Statistics.** Descriptive statistics, the analysis of trends and data, sampling, evaluation and the testing of predictions.
- **Research and development policy.** The institutions and people involved in research and development; innovation, invention, the transfer of technology, budgets and taxation policy.
- **Health policy.** Major problems in health policy, including private funding, reform, AIDS and federal regulation of the health-care industry.
- **Development economics.** The principal problems of development in the Third World, including accounts and theories of development, population, employment, agriculture, industrialization, education and food. It also considers international trade, investment, aid and finance.
- **Managerial economics for public administration.** Using microeconomics to understand complicated public-policy problems.
- **Public development of science and technology.** The public sector's role in technological progress, specific technology policies and relations between government and industry.
- **Energy principles, problems and choices.** Energy policy and related questions.
- **Computer applications for public managers.** Using computers in large public organizations to process information, plan operations and build administrative and information systems.
- **Public personnel and collective bargaining.** Civil-service reform, the growth and decline of the public sector, salaries, performance reviews, equal employment opportunities and personnel law.
- **Public finance.** Budgets, financial planning, financial evaluation, flexibility and implementation, along with the economic character of budgets, tax relations among levels of government, accounting and financial reform.
- **Government and the mass media.** Power and dependence among government, newspapers, television and cultural leaders; conflicts over information and culture in a democracy; policies affecting journalism, communication, the arts, entertainment and leisure.
- **Defence policy: military, economic and cultural.** The military, economic, political and cultural sides of national defence.
- **Quantitative methods for public-policy analysis.** Methods including cost–benefit analysis, linear programming and decision theory.
- **Public management and policymaking.** The ideas, practice and techniques of policymaking: organizational design, government reform, communication, information management, evaluation, leadership and planning.
- **Health-service management.** The administration and organization of health services.
- **Marginal economics in public administration.** Applying microeconomics to public policy, including supply and demand, company behaviour and market balance.
- **Development administration.** Lessons from the management of development programmes over the previous twenty-five years; the people and organizations involved, implementation and evaluation.
- **Public personnel management.** The structure and environment of public employment systems, personnel procedures and the duties of personnel managers.
- **Local-government finance in developing countries.** What local governments do, why they are needed, where their money comes from and how higher levels of government affect them.
- **Organizational development.** How organizations and the people in them behave: structures, processes, individual and group behaviour, identifying problems, making suitable changes, management strategies and ways to improve effectiveness.

These are only some of the subjects available to master's students. Most classes are taught through discussion. Before joining in, students must read a great deal, so that they know the existing research and arguments as fully as possible.

Consider the course on national planning and management capacity. Its reading list includes:

- Lodge and Vogel, *Ideology and National Competitiveness*.
- *The Global 2000 Report and Its Critics*.
- Reich, *The Power of Public Ideas*.
- Ascher, *Forecasting*.
- Devons, *Planning in Practice*.
- Hayek, *The Road to Serfdom*.
- Hirsch, *Social Limits to Growth*.
- Palmer, *The Character of the Reagan Years*.
- Pahrendorf, *Governing Modern Democracies*.
- Page, *Protection and Economic Efficiency*.
- Baumal, *Productivity Growth*.
- Grieco, *Between Dependence and Autonomy*.
- Wildavsky, *Searching for Safety*.
- Tussman, *Government and the Mind*.
- Auletta, *The Underclass*.
- Urban, *Conversations with George Kennan*.
- Dahl, *Controlling Nuclear Weapons*.

That is the reading for just one course. Add all the courses together and students receive a remarkably broad and thorough professional education. The demands placed on them help ensure their quality. This is one reason Maxwell's programme succeeds and why its graduates are able to take on substantial responsibilities in government.

How well a society is run depends closely on the ability of its officials. If they lack knowledge and judgement, an efficient government cannot be built: the necessary foundation is missing. A body of carefully trained officials, by contrast, gives good administration a chance.

Public administration cannot be separated from the civil service, and the civil service cannot be separated from the training of people for government. The aim is not simply to recruit the most brilliant people in society. It is to find those best able to manage public affairs. An examination alone cannot guarantee efficiency. Nobody is born knowing how to run a government. That ability must be developed through serious education and training.

The training of officials therefore affects the quality of politics and administration, and through them the wider political and economic development of society. That is why I call institutions devoted to preparing government personnel an unofficial part of the government system.

Maxwell's courses are plainly directed toward professional work. They concentrate on what administrators actually have to do. Modern government faces responsibilities of every kind: the economy, culture, education, transport, scientific development, foreign trade, health, old age, the environment—and much else besides. Officials need knowledge and skills they can use in particular fields.

Training that is too abstract or academic does little for people whose job is to administer public affairs. They need knowledge that is practical, workable and usable. Unless education for public administration moves in that direction, it will be hard for it to contribute much to society's development.

## 5. The United States Naval Academy

The United States Naval Academy stands in Annapolis, about an hour's drive from Washington. Annapolis is also the capital of Maryland, where the state government and legislature meet. The legislative building is said to be the oldest still used by a state legislature. It was here that General Washington put aside his military uniform and returned to civilian life. The room where he did so has been preserved for visitors. There is much to think about in that story. But what interested me on this occasion was something else: what we discovered at the Naval Academy.

Our programme included lunch with professors of political science. That surprised us. Did a naval academy really have an entire political science department?

It did. The department offered a full programme of political science, just as an ordinary university would, with undergraduate and graduate courses. I do not know whether West Point and the Air Force Academy have departments of this kind. A professor sitting beside me said that the Naval Academy's political science department had around four or five hundred students. Its professors were civilians, like professors elsewhere.

I found myself wondering what a graduate in political science would do at a naval academy. The answer was simple: “Serve in the US Navy as an officer.” The professor added that the academy had to prepare students not only for their immediate military careers but also for what they would do after leaving the service. That was another reason many of them chose political science.

Military colleges in many countries teach political classes without maintaining a full political science department. In America, education in politics is an important part of the process through which military officers learn the basic principles and habits of their society.

In many countries, the armed forces are a source of political instability. Military intervention in politics is common, especially in parts of Africa and Latin America. American military academies teach the basic principles and spirit of the American political system. The importance of that teaching should not be underestimated.

In America, I doubt that society would accept military interference in politics, or that a soldier could carry it out. It would be too much to claim that nobody ever thinks about doing so. The important point is that those who entertain such ideas cannot put them into practice. Teaching a society's basic political principles to its armed forces is a long-term measure of great importance. The same is true of democracy. Developing countries need to spread democratic ideas among military personnel early on. Even a developed country like America cannot dispense with this work. How much less can other countries afford to neglect it?

The academy claims to be the world's first naval academy and to have the world's largest student dormitory. The man called the father of the American Navy is buried beneath its chapel. The campus faces the water, with a broad sweep of blue stretching into the distance.

I asked a woman professor in political science whether young Americans wanted to come here. “A great many apply,” she said. “Tens of thousands every year, but we can take only a little over a thousand. The students are very good. Girls even want to marry them.”

The cadets must wear uniforms. We saw them in brilliant white clothes and white caps, with their hair cut close and their faces clean-shaven. They looked exceptionally smart and vigorous—no doubt partly the result of careful selection. The academy's head was a naval vice admiral. Military officers ran the institution, while the teaching side was civilian. The two responsibilities were clearly separated. That, too, was unusual.

That afternoon the Naval Academy football team was playing another university. Our hosts invited us along, and we accepted.

Football is America's national sport. If you cannot talk about it, people will sometimes find it difficult to talk with you—though that is perhaps an exaggeration. The stadium was packed, and the atmosphere was electric. Opposite us sat the visiting university's supporters, cheerleaders and band. On our side were the naval cadets, a great expanse of white. Below them were fifteen hundred first-year students.

Before the game, the band played the national anthem. Everybody stood, and many sang. Two men then came forward to speak about the honour of the Navy and the need to remember those who had served it. They named six naval officers killed in Vietnam. The crowd stood in silence as six ceremonial shots rang out. After each shot, a banner bearing one officer's name was displayed beneath the platform. Years had passed since the Vietnam War, yet those men were still remembered at important gatherings. The purpose was to cultivate a sense of honour.

Then the game began. Both groups of cheerleaders made a tremendous noise. The first-year cadets formed two long lines on the grass to welcome their players. On the field, the opposing teams fought fiercely for every advantage.

Football seemed a simple game to me. I saw little of the subtle tactics found in some other sports. Strength and speed appeared to matter most: could you seize an opponent and bring him down? The players were large and powerful, and they charged into one another. People who really understood the game assured me that its tactics were far more complicated than they looked.

Three things struck me particularly strongly.

The first was the **importance Americans attach to honour**. Both sets of supporters cared passionately about scoring. Whenever Navy scored, the cadets applauded and roared. Hundreds of first-year students would rush around the edge of the field and do push-ups, as though ordinary cheering could not contain their excitement. When the visitors scored, cheers rose from the opposite stands and their band struck up a tune. The cheerleaders never stopped urging their teams on. Everything was filled with a powerful sense of shared honour.

The second was **the American respect for strength**. Football has tactics, certainly, but the basic contest seems to depend on physical force. I doubt any other sport has so high a ratio of players in the squad to players on the field. The players drive straight ahead, trying to force their way across the goal line. In a sense it embodies an American habit: use strength to reach a goal quickly. You can see something similar in American military affairs, politics and economic life.

The third was **American directness**. I noticed it during the break. The visiting university's band came out to perform, and at first the Navy students seemed unimpressed. Twice they jeered. Then the visiting conductor announced that they would play a special piece in honour of the Navy.

It was a stirring tune, perhaps a naval marching song. Everyone in the Navy stands rose to their feet. The opposing supporters stood too. The scene was moving. When the music ended, both sides applauded and shouted enthusiastically. A moment earlier the two groups had been ready to tear one another apart. Now they were warm and friendly.

The Naval Academy is a military college, but it does far more than teach military skills. It takes pains to pass on a political spirit. Through activities such as this game, it cultivates in young people a sense of honour and immerses them in an established national spirit.

The academy does not treat its students merely as future officers who need military training. Above all, it seeks to make them qualified citizens. Whether young Americans attend an ordinary university or a military college, they are expected to absorb something of the American spirit. That aim comes first; others come after it. Seeing this, we begin to understand how America keeps producing new generations who will preserve its institutions.

## 6. Education for Export

People often ask why so many people around the world want to move to America, especially people from developing countries. Travelling in the United States, I regularly met people from other countries discussing how to get a green card and settle here. Helping them do so has become a business, and a profitable one. Newspaper advertisements sometimes promise: “Our law firm specializes in green cards. If we cannot get you one, we will refund your money.”

American culture—or, more precisely, the American way of life, since many people find it hard to say exactly what American culture is—has enormous influence in many countries.

A way of life is more than food, clothing, housing, travel and personal habits. It includes economic behaviour, methods of management and political values. How does a society spread its dominant way of life to others?

History offers many methods. There is military conquest: the Roman Empire taking control of provinces or fascist states invading their neighbours. There are political relationships, such as those between colonial powers and their colonies. There is economic integration, including the growth of today's world economy, international trade and finance. And there is education, as in the spread of Greek culture in the Hellenistic world or theology in medieval Europe.

Military conquest depends on force. Political domination depends on power. Neither can guarantee that a society's beliefs will take root in people's minds. They can compel people to follow a set of ideas, but they cannot make people genuinely believe in them.

In today's world, the last two forces are especially important.

One is economic strength. Economic development appeals to some of the most basic desires in human nature, which makes its influence easy to accept. America's economic growth after the Second World War became a major means of spreading its way of life.

The other is education. Education has a remarkable power to transmit a society's leading beliefs almost without people noticing. It works that way within a society, and it works the same way between societies.

Foreign students are everywhere on American campuses. The United States has one of the world's largest populations of students from abroad. Education has become a broad channel through which the American way of life travels around the world. So when we ask why people want to come to America, we must first ask how America has influenced their societies and how it spreads its values beyond its borders.

Many foreign students are supported by one organization or another. America has all kinds of bodies whose purpose is to encourage and help people from other countries study here. Few countries can match the amount of money invested in this work. Yet in spreading the American way of life and supporting America's international position, these institutions do things that the Seventh Fleet and the space shuttle cannot accomplish.

Consider the country's largest non-profit international education organization, the **Institute of International Education**. Founded in 1919, its main purpose is to help people from other countries obtain an education in America. It carries out several kinds of work:

- It manages scholarships, grants and other financial support so that people can study, conduct research and receive training outside their home countries.
- It organizes international research projects and publishes reports, findings and guides.
- It provides information about international study through offices in New York, Atlanta, Chicago, Denver, San Francisco, Washington, China, Hong Kong, Indonesia, Mexico and Thailand, among other places.
- It arranges meetings and discussions on international education and its development.
- It provides money and staff support to agricultural and health centres around the world.

The organization has substantial resources. In 1986 it spent more than **$100 million**. By 1987 it was running **247 programmes**, supported by **145 funding bodies**, including governments, joint organizations, international institutions, foundations, universities and companies. Its programmes reached **152 countries and territories**.

From the point of view of training people, it helped approximately **10,000 individuals**—roughly the entire student body of a medium-sized university. Of these, **5,000** were foreign students pursuing degrees in America, while **2,700** were taking courses that did not lead to degrees or receiving professional training. Apart from about **200 people**, nearly all of this second group were in the United States. Another **1,200** teaching assistants from various countries worked in international centres, and **800** distinguished people from around the world came as visiting scholars.

The institute also supported publishing, research, discussions, lectures and the development of academic fields. **562 colleges and universities** had received its assistance.

If a single organization can have this much influence, imagine the combined effect of all the institutions involved in exporting American education.

A considerable share of the institute's funding came from the **Fulbright Program**, established with government support. Education abroad, then, is not merely an idea promoted by educational organizations. It is also an aim of government.
