Seen in the long term, this is a far-sighted political choice, both at home and internationally. The benefits of exporting education may be far greater than the immediate gains available through economic activity.

The institute runs many different programmes. Among them are Fulbright graduate scholarships for foreign students, scholarships for Latin America and the Caribbean, and scholarships for students from Africa, Asia and Europe.

One programme connected with China was the **Committee on International Relations Studies with the People's Republic of China**, funded by the Ford, Rockefeller and MacArthur foundations and others. In 1986 it supported **twenty-six Chinese students and seven visiting scholars**.

The institute's work touches almost everything: art, music, science and technology, architecture, business management, human rights, agriculture and natural resources. Through these varied activities, students from abroad gradually absorb America's dominant way of life and its core values.

The influence goes well beyond what students know. It affects how they think, what they feel drawn toward, their habits of mind, the values they choose and the standards by which they act. Many people educated here become part of their own countries' leading groups. Some occupy important positions in politics, business, culture, education, science and the arts.

Exporting education therefore has two basic purposes. The first is to spread knowledge. The second is to build lasting feelings of goodwill. The latter creates a kind of political capital that no country can afford to dismiss.

That capital can work in two ways.

**At home**, any political or administrative system works more easily when it faces less pressure. Pressure can come from within or from outside. If outside countries understand and approve of a government's actions, some of the pressure on it disappears. Their approval may also counterbalance criticism at home. Strong opposition or incomprehension abroad, on the other hand, can increase domestic pressure and create difficulties for the political system.

**Abroad**, exporting education helps other countries understand one's foreign policy. This creates a favourable setting for international action, something that military or economic strength alone cannot supply. Foreign policy does not succeed only because it is sensible. It also has to be understood and accepted, in an atmosphere of international opinion that allows it to work. Some countries have failed in foreign affairs not because the policies themselves were necessarily wrong, but because the surrounding cultural climate was unfavourable.

That is where education has its effect. It expands the circle of people prepared to understand a country. Once they have accepted its way of life and central values, they begin to judge events by those standards. From the standpoint of long-term political development, few investments can be more valuable.

## 7. Where Technology Is Made

Chicago is one of America's great cities. I drove there with friends, a journey of about five hours. We took Interstate 80, then Interstate 55, which runs straight through the city. Joining the stream of cars hurtling along the busy expressway, I felt the same distinctive pace of modern life that I had experienced in New York.

Many Chinese people feel uneasy the first time they travel on an American expressway. There is a strange nervousness about it. Chinese people, like people in many developing countries, are used to a slower pace. Suddenly finding themselves in a world that moves much faster can be difficult, mentally and culturally, and sometimes even physically.

I call this a **modernization stress reaction**.

Yet in America you cannot get around without using expressways. Their speed is not a choice. It forces people to adjust, and before long the nervousness fades. What would happen if there were no such pressure to adapt?

Can an entire nation experience a similar stress reaction as it modernizes? What effects might follow? And how can a developing country help its people overcome the strain? These are questions worth asking.

Our first stop was a marine museum, a little world of the sea filled with remarkable fish and marine creatures. Visitors could learn a great deal about life in the oceans. We then went to the **Field Museum**, which combined natural history and human history in a way I had seldom seen. It held thousands upon thousands of animal and plant specimens, alongside objects from American history and the histories of other countries.

Its collections relating to Indigenous peoples were especially extensive. Several enormous totem poles rose in the entrance hall, perhaps objects of worship from an Indigenous community somewhere in the Americas. There were even a few Chinese objects from the Qing dynasty. As a museum of nature and society, it seemed to me among the finest in the world.

But the most fascinating place was the **Museum of Science and Industry**.

The building was enormous, the number of exhibits astonishing—and entry was free. As soon as I stepped inside, I noticed more children and teenagers than adults. That was entirely in keeping with its purpose. It was designed to place the young in a great furnace of science and technology, where their interest in science and their scientific outlook could be formed.

The museum was one of Chicago's main attractions, drawing around **four million visitors a year**. It had **seventy-five exhibition halls** and more than **two thousand exhibit groups**, explaining scientific principles, technological progress and industrial applications.

Its guiding principle was participation. Unlike a museum where people simply look at things, this one invited visitors to press buttons, pull levers and take part. The experience could be unforgettable, especially for children.

Press a button, and a voice would speak through a telephone; press another, and a television programme would start. You could operate a machine tool, walk inside an enormous model of the human heart and see how it worked, or sit in a driving simulator and try your hand at a car. Elsewhere you could descend beneath the surface of the earth and explore the structure of its crust.

The museum was founded by **Julius Rosenwald** and opened in **1933**. It stood beside the beautiful waters of Jackson Lake, welcoming thousands of visitors every day.

A short list gives some idea of the range of its displays: aircraft from different periods, old typewriters, news, telephones and telegraphs, agricultural machinery, city construction, computers, petroleum, geological layers, sound and vision, basic science, industry, cars, chemistry, cinema, medicine, bicycles, postal services, electricity, food, photography, money, energy, libraries, biology, the human body, railways, the sea and solar power.

There seemed to be everything, from the earliest developments in science and technology to the space shuttle and the computer. The museum even displayed the German submarine **U-505**, captured by American forces during the Second World War.

Walking through the halls was like entering a temple of science. There was so much to see that you hardly knew where to look. It truly was a furnace in which an interest in science could be formed.

Parents brought their children, who seemed entirely at home and reluctant to leave. Light, electricity and moving exhibits caught their attention at every turn. What effect would such powerful impressions have on young minds? It was not hard to imagine.

American society devotes considerable resources to the science and technology needed for its continuing development. But for progress to last, the young must grow up prepared to carry it forward. Society seems willing to spend freely on them. From primary school through high school, children often have excellent conditions for learning. That is one reason some people call America a **children's paradise**.

This way of supporting the next generation is an important part of the country's continuing prosperity. It deserves close study. In many societies, people's attention and resources are concentrated on the middle and later stages of life. That may make sense if the aim is personal comfort. But if the aim is social progress, is it not rather late?

America is a society of goods and money, a place where almost everything has a price. Yet when it comes to scientific and technical education, Americans seem to understand how spending can produce the greatest return.

The Museum of Science and Industry offers a clear example. Many museums charge admission. This one was free, open every day, and even its large car park cost nothing. Education works in a similar way. University fees can be astonishingly high, but schooling through high school is free. Many Americans regard museums as educational institutions too.

Here is an interesting contrast. In some societies that have not traditionally been organized around markets, more and more activities are beginning to be measured by money. Yet in America, one of the world's most commercial societies, people try to keep certain basic educational activities outside the market.

This is not merely a matter of preference. It is a policy that a commercial economy may actually need. Without such protection, activities that do not produce a direct financial return could be squeezed out. Societies moving toward a market economy should think carefully about that.

At dusk we climbed the **Sears Tower**, then described as the tallest building in the world. Looking out over the vast blue lake and down at the buildings of different heights clustered below, I felt again the almost miraculous power of science and technology.

A society's progress depends on the young creating something new. But they cannot create what comes next unless they know what has already been achieved. How can someone go a step further without understanding the steps others have taken? How could anyone design a taller building without knowing what a building is? Only because something tall already exists can someone imagine building something taller. The principle is simple.

The achievements of modernization should therefore be opened fully to the public. Society itself should become a great furnace of science and technology, forming the spirit of modernization in everyone. When a society hides its modern achievements away, what it ultimately shuts away is the human mind.

The passing on of modern life, and the work of education, reproduce a social system at two levels. Education encourages people to believe its values are justified. The spread of science and technology gives it a material foundation. Education and science do not always produce goods directly, but they can produce the future.

# Chapter 10: The Power of Ideas

## 1. Idea Factories

*Factories of ideas* is my own expression. The usual term is *think tanks*. America has so many different kinds of them that they have become one of the country's distinctive features. In a sense, Americans are happy to treat ideas as products: examine the quality, compare what is available and choose what suits you. Looking at the development of modern society, it is hard to miss the remarkable influence these organizations have had. Their importance is especially clear when we think about how a society preserves itself while continuing to change.

In San Francisco I visited Stanford University. The famous **Hoover Institution** is located on its campus, though it is not itself a part of the university. Stanford's buildings show a strong Spanish influence, reflecting the history of the region. The Hoover Institution has a different appearance. It is known for its conservative ideas. Some professors jokingly call it “reactionary.” Many of the ideas behind the Reagan administration came from Hoover. But it has also attracted distinguished scholars, including the well-known political scientist **Lipset**.

The Hoover Institution was founded in **1919**. According to a story I heard, its early collection of materials about the Soviet Union came from the Soviet government. President Hoover, so the story went, had provided aid to the Soviet Union. The Soviets could not pay, so the Americans asked for documents and other materials instead. I do not know whether the story is true. If it is, it shows considerable foresight.

After the Second World War, the institution acquired a great many materials from Japan, and later from China. Its holdings in these areas became particularly rich. Gradually the institution grew on the strength of these collections.

The **University of California at Berkeley** is another of America's finest universities, comparable in quality with an old institution like Harvard. There I visited the Institute of Governmental Studies. Its director was **Polsby**, another well-known political scientist. He wanted to make the institute influential in national policy. Previously it had concentrated mainly on problems facing California's state government. Now he intended to extend its reach across the country. In other words, it would become a think tank of national importance.

These visits left me convinced that the contribution of think tanks to American development should not be underestimated. They also reminded me of an article by **R. Kent Weaver** of the Brookings Institution, “The Changing World of Think Tanks.” It offers a useful account of how these organizations have developed.

**1. What is a think tank?** There is no definition everyone accepts. Generally, think tanks are independent, non-profit organizations. People also like to joke about them. Peter Kelley, for example, described a think tank as an arrangement in which corporations, governments or eccentric multimillionaires voluntarily put up millions of dollars to support people who spend much of their time trying to get their names into print.

In translating that remark into Chinese, I used a term that suggested formal printing. To a Chinese reader, printed work may imply official publication. In America, of course, both formal and informal work can appear in print.

Think tanks have also been called **universities without students** and **non-profit contractors that carry out research for government**. Their numbers have exploded. The Washington, DC telephone directory listed **124 “institutes” in 1988**, and that did not even include some of the best-known think tanks.

**2. What do think tanks do?** That question is also open to debate. Broadly, they perform four jobs.

First, they produce policy ideas. An important part of their work is finding and spreading ideas that may not become government policy for a long time, allowing decision-makers gradually to become familiar with them.

Second, they propose and assess particular policies. They study an idea, argue for or against it, and publish books and articles explaining their conclusions. This is why think tanks care so much about publication. For a university without students, books and reports are among its chief products. Some institutes also prepare material for legislation in Congress.

Third, they evaluate programmes the government is already carrying out, asking whether the programmes work and whether they meet their stated aims.

Fourth, they supply trained people to government. A researcher who has spent years studying a problem may eventually be appointed to run the department responsible for it. Such a person arrives with a deep knowledge of the subject.

**3. How are think tanks run?** Several questions have to be considered.

One concerns **reputation and identity**. Traditionally, think tanks try to appear non-partisan: they investigate problems and reach conclusions rather than searching for arguments to defend a conclusion already chosen. That does not mean they have no political outlook. Brookings is often described as a liberal, Democratic-leaning institution, while the **American Enterprise Institute (AEI)** is associated with conservative Republicans. Yet the picture is not so simple. People move between the two. The current head of Brookings, for example, has been trying to strengthen its reputation for neutrality.

Some institutions make no attempt to disguise their convictions. An official of the **Heritage Foundation** described his colleagues as “the intellectual shock troops of the conservative revolution.”

Another question concerns **research staff**. Should an institution employ its own specialists, or commission scholars elsewhere? Both approaches have advantages and drawbacks. How should university-trained scholars learn to study practical policy questions, which are not the same as academic problems? Someone may be excellent at research within a university yet much less capable when asked to deal with policy. Should an institution choose people with advanced academic training or people with rich practical experience? Again, both have strengths and weaknesses.

Then there is **money**. Think tanks may be non-profit institutions, but finances are vital. Without dependable income and sensible financial management, they cannot survive.

There is also the problem of **setting an agenda**. Research cannot be a collection of unrelated activities. Which policy fields should an organization investigate? One or two, or a wide range? How should its different projects connect? The answers determine the direction of its work.

Finally, there is **managing the results**: getting studies published and making sure they reach the people who might use them.

Whether they mean to or not, think tanks act as **doctors for society**. They continually search for problems, diagnose them and propose remedies. Their own survival depends partly on doing so. To obtain support, they must offer a fresh approach or discover something others have overlooked. That pressure encourages them to look for new policy problems.

Whatever their motives, once those problems have been identified, somebody can investigate them. If the findings eventually become government policy, they may help society improve. Many think tanks probably have an explicit commitment to preserving the existing political order. Yet the work they do has precisely that effect, even when it begins as a criticism.

Like doctors, they examine society, identify symptoms, seek underlying principles, anticipate consequences and prescribe possible treatments. Their social function is clear enough. Many American think tanks began developing in the 1920s and 1930s, then expanded greatly after the Second World War.

That was also a period when some of the tensions within American capitalism eased and the economy grew rapidly. Is there a connection? We cannot simply say yes, but neither can we dismiss the possibility.

I still think the Chinese term usually used for *think tank* is unsatisfactory. A “tank” or “storehouse” suggests somewhere things are kept. But these institutions do not merely store ideas. They produce them. That is why I prefer **factories of ideas**.

And their factories are full of active minds.

## 2. The Brookings Institution

The **Brookings Institution** occupies a classical-style building. I had long known it was among America's most important think tanks, standing alongside the RAND Corporation and the Hoover Institution.

Brookings was established in **1927** through the merger of several organizations. One was the **Institute for Government Research**, founded in **1916**, described as the first national private institution to study public-policy questions. Another was the **Institute of Economics**, founded in **1922**. The third was the **Robert Brookings Graduate School of Economics and Government**, founded in **1924**, an early experiment in training people for public service.

The combined institution took its name from **Robert Somers Brookings (1850–1932)**, a businessman from St. Louis.

Brookings is a private, non-profit organization. It receives support from foundations, companies and individuals. It also earns income through grants, conference registration fees, sales of publications and computer-use charges. It sometimes accepts government contracts to research particular questions, while retaining the right to publish its findings.

A board oversees the institution and protects its independence. The chair of the board is described as its principal administrator, proposing and coordinating policies, planning work and choosing staff. The role also involves constant discussion with government officials, members of Congress, business leaders, foundation officers and scholars.

Projects are selected according to their importance, timeliness, availability of information, possible methods of investigation, staff and funding, and their fit with Brookings's purposes. Every study is supposed to be rigorous enough for scholarly publication. The institution itself claims to have no official policy position.

What, then, is Brookings trying to achieve?

Its **1987 annual report** opened with a statement attributed to Robert S. Brookings: if people can be encouraged to think about the problems of law, government, economics and social relationships, the contribution to humanity will be greater than any act of charity.

Brookings concentrates on public policy. Its work falls into three broad areas: defining policy problems fully and accurately; drawing public attention to issues that are beginning to emerge; and examining the successes and failures of earlier policies so that people can learn from experience. Research must establish the facts, offer worthwhile judgements and propose measures that can actually be carried out.

At Brookings we discussed the relationship between research and policymaking with **James D. Carroll**, a programme director. He said the institution did not measure its success by whether it immediately influenced a government decision. Nor did it concentrate on problems that demanded instant answers. Government specialists could deal with those.

Brookings preferred long-term research, especially the study of broad social trends. It also valued the educational effect of research: drawing the whole society's attention to an important development and encouraging people to examine it. That wider understanding would eventually help produce sensible policies.

“Much of our work is of no immediate use,” Carroll explained in effect. “But one day people will need it. By then, Brookings will already have provided it.”

Taking a long view and looking at society as a whole are important reasons for Brookings's success. If every researcher rushed to investigate only what the government wanted to do that day, both scholarship and social progress would probably suffer.

Brookings divides its research into several programmes.

**1. Economic studies.** These investigate the problems of American economic development: raising productivity, encouraging saving and productive investment, cooperation with other countries over money and taxes, opening world markets to free trade, supporting development in the Third World, and improving public services such as health and education. All of this is considered in relation to keeping America among the world's leading countries.

**2. Foreign-policy studies.** These consider changes in the world, growing international connections, economic development, advances in science and technology, the movement of investment, international debt and competition among nations. Brookings specifically points to China's growing place in the world economy. It argues that such developments demand new goals and major adjustments in American foreign policy.

Brookings has a strong reputation in this field. We met **Harry Harding**, a China specialist of the younger American generation, perhaps around forty years old. He had written a new book called *China's Second Revolution*, an account of China's reforms that provided readers with a systematic background for understanding the changes taking place. The programme's work ranged widely across the Soviet Union, China, Japan, the Middle East and other regions.

**3. Government studies.** This programme examines how political institutions respond to urgent social problems. The American Constitution is two hundred years old. Political arrangements have changed relatively little over those two centuries, while society has changed almost beyond recognition. How should the political system deal with that? Researchers look at government efficiency, structure and responsibilities, election laws, presidential nomination procedures, and relations between the president and Congress.

Brookings also has a computer centre that gathers and processes information. Its public-policy education centre holds discussions and conferences, bringing together leaders from government and private organizations. In the **1987 financial year**, around **three thousand senior figures** took part in its activities. They discussed everything from America's aviation programmes and electronics industry to international terrorism.

That last activity reflects another feature of American society. Senior government officials and leading people in business are generally willing to take part in public meetings and give speeches. It has become an accepted custom, perhaps encouraged by the political system itself. Political leaders are often forced to compete for support. If they do not, their careers may be short.

Officials also seem willing to explain their policies. Such explanations create a valuable channel of communication. People live in different circumstances and face different problems, so they do not automatically understand a policy. Yet policies are far more likely to work when the public understands them.

Americans approach this in a practical spirit. It is rather like selling a product: provide detailed instructions and explain how it works. In other societies, political habits may encourage officials to avoid explanations altogether. Under certain circumstances, that too is treated as a kind of political art.

Publishing is another major Brookings activity. It produces a journal called **The Brookings Review**, as well as many academic books. In the **1987 financial year**, it published **twenty-nine books and six collections of essays**.

Brookings publications are generally of a high scholarly standard, sometimes demanding for ordinary readers. In 1987, three of its books were selected by *Choice* magazine as outstanding academic works of **1986–87**, and its publications regularly won other awards. We met several scholars there, each of whom had written a number of books. At Brookings, writing books is one of a scholar's principal occupations.

The growth of think tanks is one of the most remarkable developments in twentieth-century American society. I would say America has carried the idea further than any other Western country.

The American habit is to treat ideas as things that can be examined, improved and revised, then offered for people to choose among. That follows naturally from a culture shaped by practical thinking and business. European traditions tend to regard ideas as expressions of personal convictions, not things that can be selected or altered at will.

**Tocqueville**, the nineteenth-century French historian who studied American politics, never witnessed the rise of the modern think tank. Yet anyone trying to understand American politics today must take this development into account.

Commercial life encourages people to compare competing products. Ideas in America compete in much the same way. The two main parties, Democrats and Republicans, share a broad opposition to socialism and communism, though they differ considerably in many other beliefs and values. That limited agreement does not extend to every political party; the American Communist Party, for example, plainly stands outside it.

Because the two main parties generally work within the same broad system, both face the practical problem of responding to crises and conflicts. They are therefore willing to select useful ideas wherever they find them. This competition helps government act more sensibly.

Individual think tanks sometimes sharply criticize the policies of the day. But taken together, they help keep the political system stable.

## 3. The Carter Center

Since the war, a tradition has developed in America: when a president leaves office, a centre bearing his name is established. It holds the documents and records of his presidency, along with objects of special interest—gifts he received, for example, or things connected with important events. It also presents an account of his achievements in office.

A presidential centre, sometimes called a presidential library, can cost several million dollars, or even more than ten million. Money comes from government funds, sometimes supplemented by private donations. Because President Carter was from Georgia, his centre was built in Atlanta.

**The Carter Center** is a substantial modern building that cost **$25 million**. I was told that Carter had raised quite a lot of the money himself, in addition to the government contribution. The gardens have a distinctly Eastern feeling.

The complex houses several organizations.

**1. The Carter Center itself.** This is a partly commercial institution whose stated aim is to reduce social conflict, relieve suffering and deepen understanding among people through research and practical efforts. It also claims to be the only institution in the world in which a former president takes part in the daily work. Jimmy Carter has an office there.

**2. The Emory University Carter Center.** Sharing the building with the main centre, this organization conducts research, holds discussions and publishes articles. Its subjects include Latin America and the Caribbean, the Middle East, Soviet–American relations, Africa, health, human rights and conflict. Its researchers work closely with Emory University, and graduate students come to the centre to carry out their research. Most members are professors in Emory's political science department.

There are distinguished scholars among them, including **Karl W. Deutsch**. The centre's research on the Soviet Union is well known throughout America. Emory has one of the country's leading satellite television reception systems, which receives Soviet television programmes twenty-four hours a day for analysis. I was told that the work of **Ellen Mickiewicz**, one of the professors, has had considerable influence on American foreign-policy decisions.

**3. Global 2000.** This organization aims to improve health and agriculture in developing countries.

**4. Save the Children.** Its main concern is the care of children. According to the information I received, it is funded by the World Health Organization, World Bank, United Nations Development Programme and Rockefeller Foundation.

**5. The Jimmy Carter Library and Museum.** The library holds approximately **27 million documents**, available to anyone researching the Carter presidency. The museum is intended to be open to the public and to deepen people's understanding of the presidency. This part is administered by the National Archives.

That last statement is especially important: **the museum exists to help the public understand the presidency.**

It is one of a great many institutions through which Americans learn the beliefs, values, political principles and preferences that shape their society. Its purpose is not simply to convey information. It helps people learn to think and feel about politics in a particular way.

How does it do this?

The first thing the museum offers is a film about the Constitution and the presidency, with footage from Carter's years in office. It begins with the War of Independence, explains how the Constitution was made, describes Washington's great achievements and introduces America's political traditions. It is rather like a lesson on the American system of government. Anyone who visits can watch it, free of charge.

Inside, the main exhibition hall is divided into areas devoted to foreign affairs, the environment, health, finance and other subjects. Large photographs and written explanations fill each area, along with one or two television sets. The sets have buttons labelled with topics such as *human rights*, *war* and *the environment*. Press one, and Carter appears on the screen to explain the background of a policy and the principles that guided it.

Most interesting of all is a special cartoon for children, called **“If You Want to Be President of the United States.”** It explains the presidential system through lively pictures and an entertaining story.

This is quite striking. Americans take care to pass on their country's political beliefs at every stage of a citizen's growth. Any society that wants its institutions to remain stable needs to do something of the kind.

America has developed this process to a high degree. It is one of the less visible reasons for the stability of American society and its political system. Every society must find ways to pass on its leading values. Without a sound and effective means of doing so, neither stable institutions nor the transmission of political ideals is possible.

The basic purpose of a presidential library or centre is therefore not merely to preserve archives, important though those archives are for historians and future generations. It is **to spread values and beliefs**. Visitors keep coming. Americans make use of every possible channel for this kind of political education, and they seem to know the business very well.

Americans often say that they believe in their political system. I think it would be more accurate to say that they believe in the principles behind it.

In political life, they seem more concerned with defending and passing on those principles than with preserving any one set of arrangements. The Constitution has endured because it expresses American values and beliefs. As a system of government, it is far from perfect. But beliefs matter more than the written arrangements, because those arrangements are abstract until people give them life. Any institution whose principles fail to take root in the minds of the people who operate it is fragile.

That is why a presidential centre is about **politics, not simply history**.

Consider Ronald Reagan. Even before he left office, he was choosing a site for a centre of his own. I heard that he was interested in Stanford University in California. Stanford was not enthusiastic. As an academic institution, it wished to remain politically neutral, while a Reagan centre would inevitably have a political tendency. In the end, it was not built on Stanford's campus.

The Carter Center differs from some other presidential libraries because it includes a research centre operated with Emory University's political science department. In effect, this is a think tank.

Many senior American officials move into think tanks after leaving their positions in Washington. Their experience of government and their continuing ties to people in office help bring research and political ideas into the policymaking process. This, in turn, can help society develop.

The Carter Center has gathered a small group of scholars, most of them political scientists. That tells us something about its purpose. Its research reaches across both domestic and international politics. The plans are ambitious, and the intention is plain enough.

Researchers here enjoy excellent conditions. The centre pays all their expenses. When an institution brings together the experience and connections of former political leaders, money from private business, and the active minds of scholars, it can do things that an ordinary think tank cannot easily match.

## 4. A Forum for Defence Secretaries

Atlanta lies in the American South. During the Civil War, it was a battleground between the Northern and Southern armies. The South is, relatively speaking, less prosperous than the North, and the memory of that war still seems to trouble many Southerners.

The subject comes up again and again. Northerners know this too. When people learned that I was going to Atlanta, they warned me that I would certainly hear about the Civil War. They were right. A number of people told me that the war had destroyed Atlanta and that the city I saw had been built almost entirely anew.

While there, we visited the **Southern Center for International Studies**, one of the few centres in the South devoted to international affairs. It had made a name for itself by organizing some remarkable events.

The centre occupied a fairly small house, recently purchased and still being renovated. Its director, whom I shall call W., described its history.

“We have been here for nearly twenty years,” he said. “Our main work is to hold discussions on foreign policy. We have brought hundreds of experts and distinguished people together with local business leaders, politicians, scholars and the public. We began as the Southern Council on International and Public Affairs in 1967 and were reorganized in 1977 as a non-profit educational organization.”

I was curious about the word *educational*. What exactly did it mean here?

W. explained:

“The South is a very important region, but for various reasons it has not been as open to the world as other parts of the country. Modern technology forces Americans to take their place in a world where everyone depends on everyone else. You cannot make decisions without understanding international society as it is today.

“Our job is to help our members and the public understand that new setting. They can hear talks about the international economy, foreign policy and other cultures for themselves. They can listen to international experts and world leaders, and begin to understand the world community. The centre itself has no particular policy position.”

So he said. In reality, no institution is without values or preferences. The difference lies in how it expresses them.

The centre organizes educational seminars and has invited an impressive range of speakers. Among those who had appeared were **Deng Xiaoping**, former French president **Valéry Giscard d'Estaing**, former British prime minister **Edward Heath**, **King Hussein of Jordan**, Saudi prince **Faisal**, and ambassadors from many countries. The list shows just how much influence this institution possesses.

Every year it brings together former secretaries of state and secretaries of defence for a discussion. The **CBS** television network broadcasts the event nationwide, while a government information service sends it overseas by satellite. The centre also sets up working groups to study individual countries, regions and global issues; runs continuing programmes on international business; and arranges dinners with internationally prominent guests. It publishes two periodicals, **Global Review** and **International Affairs Documents**—the English renderings of the titles in the Chinese source.

A few recent events show the range of its work:

- **18 December 1987:** The fifth annual gathering of former secretaries of state, attended by **Rusk, Rogers, Kissinger, Vance, Muskie and Haig**, discussed “The Making of American Foreign Policy.”
- **19 January 1988:** **Han Xu**, China's ambassador to the United States, gave a speech.
- **6 May 1988:** The annual Asia-Pacific discussion considered “Washington—Beijing—Taiwan: Ten Years After Normalization.”
- **30 September 1988:** Eight former secretaries of defence—**McNamara, Clifford, Laird, Richardson, Schlesinger, Rumsfeld, Brown and Weinberger**—met to offer “Recommendations to the Next Administration.”

An organization capable of bringing together such people plainly has influence. Yet how many employees does it have? Only about **ten regular staff members**. Its reference room has two people, who also prepare the publication of documents, largely a useful collection of photocopied articles.

Where does the money come from? That is the crucial question.

The centre receives donations, grants from foundations and membership fees. Its membership is divided into several levels:

| Membership level | Contribution |
| --- | ---: |
| International group | $25,000 or more |
| Business owner | $10,000 |
| Sponsor | $5,000 |
| Supporter | $500 |
| Patron | $250 |
| Ordinary member | $100 |
| Special member | $50 |

The special category includes students, people working in education, members of the news media and retired people.

Naturally, fees alone cannot meet the costs. Most of the money comes from foundations and companies. W. told us that a centre should not depend on a single source. If one person or organization supplied all the money, that donor might begin to control it.

The interesting thing is that so many people are willing to contribute. A centre like this, widely known and well regarded, can attract support from many directions. The willingness to fund such institutions is an integral part of American culture. I have already mentioned that Brookings receives much of its money from foundations and other outside supporters.

Another important reason for the centre's success is the willingness of present and former senior officials to take part. Former officials in particular seem to feel that they have a responsibility to express their views on domestic and foreign policy.

Americans are also willing to hear government officials and leaders from other countries. Every country must form policy from its own standpoint, but listening to different opinions helps it understand both itself and others. Everyone involved can learn something.

One ordinary measure of a policy is how many people oppose it. The better policymakers understand different points of view, the more likely they are to devise a policy that speaks to a wider range of people.

How does the centre look after its distinguished guests? Must it pay them? Usually not. Most are happy to participate, although some require a fee. The greater challenge is arranging their travel and schedules.

From the moment a guest leaves home until the moment he returns, every step has to be planned carefully. When people of such standing are involved, a mistake can have serious consequences. Sometimes the centre must provide a private aircraft. And those immense luxury cars—**limousines**, with three doors along each side—are practically indispensable.

Think about what all this means. The Southern Center is a private institution, yet it can bring former secretaries of state and defence to Atlanta to lecture and debate. It can put them before the public to discuss both domestic and international affairs.

Ideas begin to circulate. Knowledge becomes available to the society and its political institutions.

This also shows the remarkably down-to-earth American attitude to politics. Americans are inclined to strip political office of its mystery. People who once held great power do not feel above such public activities. They actively support them.

One could put it less kindly: they cannot bear to disappear from the public eye. But precisely because they are unwilling to disappear, a centre like this can achieve so much.

Nor is this the only such organization. Whatever their individual successes, centres of this kind play an important social role. One of their stated aims is public education. Through their many events, they pass American values and beliefs to a wider audience and help people learn the political culture of their society. Government policies reach the public by these routes and may win broader acceptance.

At the same time, these centres carry the views of different groups back to policymakers. Imagine the effect when a discussion is televised across the whole country.

The issue is not simply whether a policy has been well designed, although that is essential. It also matters whether the public accepts the ideas on which the policy rests. Without that acceptance, even a sound policy may be hard to put into practice. Some policies fail not because they are wrong in themselves, but because people do not understand or support them.

These centres are not formal organs of government. Yet in political life they can accomplish things that government offices cannot.

## 5. A Centre for Regional Affairs

Much of America's overall development consists of the development of its towns and neighbourhoods. A society does not become modern or prosperous out of thin air. Progress depends on the growth of the countless smaller communities that make it up.

It is not enough to talk about development on a grand scale. Some countries in the developing world make plans around ambitious national targets without breaking them down into specific, carefully chosen smaller goals. Yet those small goals are the ones that touch ordinary people's lives.

Achieving them does more than contribute to national progress. It also helps create the confidence and habits that allow development to remain balanced and continue over time.

One feature of American society is that its development is relatively even. Neighbourhoods and communities differ, of course, but the gaps often seem less dramatic than in many other countries. This is one of the most important signs of a developed society.

There are poor people here, including some who are poor beyond belief. Yet the society as a whole has reached a high level of development. Strong local government is one of the conditions that allows communities to develop together.

Local development depends on many things: federal policy, state policy, the attitudes of residents, the structure of the community and more. The question is how to encourage it.

I spent three days visiting **Miami University in Ohio**, in the Midwest. Professors there told me that people in the centre of the country tended to be much more conservative than people on the East and West Coasts. New ideas, they suggested, usually begin on one coast or the other and then travel inland, losing some of their force along the way.

It is an interesting way to explain differences in American culture. But American society is certainly not all of one mind. Older traditions and newer ideas, conservatives and radicals, argue with one another fiercely. To understand America, we must never imagine it as a single solid block.

America has the good fortune to possess two long coasts. The East Coast faces Europe across the Atlantic; the West Coast faces Asia across the Pacific. Its strongest economic and cultural forces are concentrated in those coastal regions.

China's coastal areas are also the most developed. If China had a western coast on the other side of the country, perhaps it would not be the China it is today.

At Miami University I came across an interesting institution: the **Center for Public Management and Regional Affairs**. Its principal aim is to study local government and help improve local administration. I do not know whether other places have similar centres, but here was a good example of university research being put directly to work for local political development.

The state government supports the centre and provides most of its funding. Clearly, the state hopes to improve local administration through research, education and analysis, while giving local authorities access to practical expertise.

The centre helps county, city, village and town governments with management methods, strengthens their ability to operate, encourages local economic development, evaluates public programmes and studies policy questions.

This arrangement brings advanced knowledge together with very practical needs.

Local governments often lack specialists and cannot properly assess the problems they face. Universities have knowledge and technology, but that expertise does not automatically find its way into local government. To a political scientist looking at national affairs, the ordinary work of a small town council may seem hardly worth noticing.

But organize the relationship, give it some encouragement, and valuable work can follow.

Usually the centre goes to a local government after receiving an invitation. Sometimes its own staff identify a problem, develop a project and offer their services without being asked.

The centre has only **two permanent staff members**, but it has computers, laser printers and other modern research equipment. It works closely with the university's political science department. Much of the research is done by professors in that department, often with help from students.

A few of its projects show just how practical the work is:

- Preparing a handbook of economic statistics for **Butler County**.
- Surveying residents to help plan parking in **Monroe**.
- Drawing up a tree-planting plan for **West Carrollton**.
- Preparing personnel forms and conducting a work survey for **Fairfield**.
- Developing a comprehensive economic plan for **Hamilton**.
- Analysing the costs and benefits of **Monroe's emergency medical services**.
- Surveying public attitudes toward administration in **Carlisle**.
- Editing and distributing a reference handbook for town managers.
- Studying how federal budgets and tax policies affect rural communities in Ohio.
- Holding public-service seminars on problems shared by local governments.
- Preparing handbooks on economic and management questions.

And so on. The projects are detailed and specific. They do not address the great questions of national policy, but they can be extremely valuable to local governments.

Take one recent study, the centre's **Economic Development Report for Preble County**. Its purpose was to gather information about the county's society, economy, geography, culture and population, so that it could attract businesses and investment.

The report began with a short history of the county, then described its location, its towns and its highways. It gave driving distances to many major cities: **496 miles to Atlanta**, **835 to Boston**, **250 to Chicago** and **223 to Detroit**, among dozens of others.

It also described the airports, from nearby international airports to the smaller fields within the county. There were substantial sections on population, government, public facilities, housing, sanitation, medical care, libraries and education, including detailed accounts of **six schools**.

Such facts matter to businesses deciding where to invest and to people deciding where to work. The county had **eight towns**, and the report supplied detailed figures on their populations, budgets, facilities, transport and economic conditions.

Because the study came from a university research centre, it carried some authority. Preble County could distribute it to potential investors. At the same time, the county government could use the information to assess its own position and set development goals. One piece of research served two purposes.

This centre points toward an important question: **how can a society guide its best knowledge and technology toward places that are relatively underdeveloped?** How can it help the whole society advance more evenly?

Ohio's state government has chosen to use money as the means of encouragement. It funds a specialist non-profit centre whose work is to help local governments develop.

This takes us back to the point with which I began. The small achievements of individual communities are the foundation of a nation's larger achievements.

A society may have space shuttles, satellites and enormous computers. That does not necessarily mean it has developed evenly. In a very real sense, modernization is not measured mainly by such achievements. It is measured by whether ordinary people can live better lives.

This is a question of how we understand progress. Without that understanding, we will design a very different kind of development.

## 6. Reservoirs of Knowledge

The development of a society is inseparable from the spread of knowledge, especially the most advanced knowledge available. Looking back over human history, we find that the discovery of a new idea does not necessarily mean progress. Progress comes when that idea spreads widely enough to affect people's lives. Without a way to share new knowledge, even the finest discoveries cannot become a powerful force for change.

Ancient China produced ideas that were outstanding in their time. Yet many of them failed to become forces that moved society forward. That is a great pity.

Chinese thought and culture occupy a distinctive place in world history, comparable in historical importance to Western civilization. But they did not lead Chinese society along the same path of development as the West. One important reason, I believe, was the absence of effective ways to spread new knowledge.

Western development, particularly the growth of advanced technology after the Second World War, has been closely connected with the movement of knowledge and ideas. We should not underestimate the part played by the spread of information.

Today, **university libraries** are among the main institutions through which knowledge moves across society.

I visited libraries at **Harvard, MIT, Stanford, the University of California at Berkeley, the University of Missouri at Columbia, the University of Iowa, Miami University in Ohio, Yale, Princeton, Syracuse, the University of Washington in Seattle, the University of California at San Diego, Emory, the University of Michigan**, and other universities.

These visits left me with a powerful sense of what libraries do. Thousands of university libraries, taken together, form an important part of the way American society is organized and run. They also form a vast network through which knowledge can reach almost everywhere.

A library is like a **reservoir of knowledge**. It holds the many kinds of understanding and wisdom that humanity has created over the centuries. No single university library can hold everything, of course. But libraries work closely together, using modern technology to connect their collections.

They preserve knowledge so that it will not disappear. Anyone who thirsts for learning can come and drink.

University libraries do their best to collect books, journals, newspapers and other materials, both from America and from abroad. One library cannot possibly collect every foreign publication, so different institutions develop special strengths. Some concentrate on India, others on the Soviet Union or Eastern Europe.

Yale's East Asian Library holds a large amount of material in Chinese, including publications that were never formally issued. Many American university libraries have kept remarkably complete collections of the small newspapers produced by Red Guards during China's Cultural Revolution. These now give scholars valuable material for studying that period.

I once asked the head of a library what principle guided its collecting.

“Collect as much as we can,” he replied. “We don't know whether something is useful today. Perhaps it isn't. But it may be very useful to people studying history in the future.”

A library, then, is not simply a place that lends books. It gathers and preserves the world's accumulated knowledge so that people can find what they need.

Libraries also resemble **a network of irrigation channels**.

If you cannot find a book or document at your own university, the library can borrow it from another. You can ask for a copy of an article from a journal held somewhere else. Computers link the catalogues of many institutions, making it possible to search collections far beyond your own campus.

Many libraries are connected electronically to the **Library of Congress**. They can obtain material directly, including records of debates in Congress, and print it almost immediately.

The network joins not only libraries to one another, but people to knowledge. It makes the widest possible circulation of information practical. In a sense, the libraries work as one great system, gaining advantages from their combined size.

These reservoirs are also **open to the public**. A society's progress depends on whether people can obtain and use the knowledge that has been built up around them. The easier and faster it is for everyone to reach ideas—both those inherited from history and those being created today—the greater the possibilities for progress.

Libraries of different kinds, not only university libraries, generally welcome anyone. Visitors can use books, newspapers, reference works, microfilm and other material. The shelves are open, and people can consult the collections. Local residents and university members can borrow books with little difficulty. Rare volumes are handled separately, but even they can be consulted. Libraries also keep special collections of government documents, open to readers.

This openness is essential. Without it, the reservoir would become a stagnant pool. A reservoir is built not merely to store water, but to irrigate the fields. A reservoir of knowledge must do the same.

Libraries do more than spread what people know today. They carry knowledge from one generation to the next. They preserve what previous generations have learned and make it available to those who follow.

American university libraries have reached a high level of equipment and management. Computer catalogues, microfilm readers and photocopiers make research much easier. Their guiding principle is service to the reader. Everything is arranged, as far as possible, to help people find the material they seek.

What does such a system contribute to the way society is governed? It is a question worth thinking about.

Much of governing a society is really a matter of how knowledge is handled. Political, administrative, economic and technical management all come down, in the end, to dealing with people. But people are not machines. They think and form opinions of their own.

Their actions are guided first by their ideas, by the culture and beliefs they have absorbed, and by the new ideas they develop from them. Many of society's advances and conflicts can therefore be traced to the way knowledge is shared and used.

History tells us that one of the great responsibilities of political systems is to deal with knowledge. The knowledge available in a society helps determine what its political life can become. The way knowledge is organized may encourage political progress or contribute to decline. Every great change in politics, every replacement of an older political order by a new one, is connected in some way with the growth—or the failure to grow—of knowledge.

How a society handles knowledge thus helps explain how that society works. Spreading knowledge, or particular kinds of knowledge, is not only a task for education. It is also a major political responsibility, and sometimes a heavy one.

America has chosen a relatively **decentralized** way of doing this. No system for spreading knowledge can be entirely separate from politics. But in America the process is largely outside the direct control of government administration.

The government does not formally have to carry the whole burden, while the developed library network makes it possible for knowledge to circulate widely on its own.

Where such independent networks are weak, government offices must shoulder an enormous responsibility for teaching and informing society—not only to spread knowledge, but to create a firm foundation for their own authority.

When a society succeeds in organizing the spread of knowledge effectively and bringing that system to a high standard, the benefits for both public life and political government are plain to see.

## 7. The City Library

**Iowa City** is a small place. Its resident population, excluding students, was said to be around **15,000**. The university had approximately **35,000 students**. The population was described to me as roughly **60,000** in all.

Small towns like this are scattered all over America. Many people prefer to live in them rather than in great cities such as New York or Chicago. Life is quieter, more relaxed, and often more straightforward. People in large cities may look down on small-town residents, but small-town residents do not all envy them.

Why is that?

In many developing countries, everyone seems determined to move to the big cities. A town of fifty or sixty thousand people would find it hard to keep talented people there. Yet the University of Iowa has many distinguished professors who enjoy the atmosphere of Iowa City and have no desire to leave.

Personal preferences matter, of course. But so do **living conditions**. In a small American city, many of the basic services and opportunities are not fundamentally different from those available in a large one. The city library offers a good example.

Iowa City's public library is impressive. It has the equipment one would expect in a much larger library. Its book collection is considerable, although naturally it cannot compare with a university library or the Library of Congress.

There is a good audio and video collection. Residents can borrow records, videotapes and even films. They can watch or listen at the library using its equipment, or take things home. It is rather like a restaurant offering both meals on the premises and takeaway service.

There are computers for public use, along with photocopiers and other equipment.

A city library has a different purpose from a university library. It is not mainly there for academic research. Instead, it serves local families, with many books for children. Groups of children are a common sight. The library keeps newspapers and journals, a respectable selection even if it is not enormous, along with reference books for looking up university addresses, telephone numbers, information about high schools and much more.

Staff also cut out and organize newspaper articles, so readers can easily find a collection of reports about a particular subject.

The library offers another useful service: it gathers job advertisements in one place for people looking for work.

Most surprising of all, it has decorative pictures, oil paintings and reproductions of famous paintings, beautifully framed—and **you can borrow them**. With a library card, you can take a picture home for two or three months, hang it on your wall, then return it and choose another.

What a clever idea. This is not merely a library of books; it is a library of pictures as well.

The entire library uses computers to manage its collections. A central computer connects the terminals inside the building. To find a book, a reader simply consults one of them.

But the terminals have been designed especially carefully for the public. There is **no keyboard**, only a screen. All the commands appear at the bottom of the display. Touch the one you want with your finger, and the computer carries it out. It seems to use a touch-sensitive system.

This is particularly useful for children, who might have trouble remembering typed commands. When instructions can be seen and touched, the machine becomes easy to use.

In some places, computers fail to become useful because most people do not know how to operate them. Here the aim is not to force people to adapt to computers. **The computers have been adapted to people.**

This convenient library is free to residents. Anyone living in Iowa City can obtain a library card without charge. People from elsewhere can use it by paying a fee. Getting a card is simple: give your address, show some identification, and you can receive one on the spot.

The library is only one of the services available in this small city. In many American towns, people do not feel that they are living in a backward place. They can enjoy most of the benefits of modern life.

This is an important foundation of social stability.

One of the most difficult problems faced by developing countries is how to deal with uneven political and economic development between regions. Those differences produce conflicts. They are not simply the result of bad decisions by particular people; at certain stages of development, some are almost unavoidable.

The important question is whether governments recognize the problem and make more even development a central concern of political planning and social cooperation.

Uneven development creates struggles over resources, makes population movements harder to manage and draws too many talented people into a handful of great cities. In some developing countries, a coup may even represent forces from poorer regions seizing control of the country's wealthiest areas. Perhaps this is a question for political geography.

Iowa City has several shopping districts. Almost everything sold in shops in New York or Washington can be found here too. There is little reason to travel all the way to New York or Chicago just to buy something.

Nor do you often see people making a living by buying goods in one place and reselling them at a higher price elsewhere—unless the goods are things such as illegal drugs. Housing, public facilities and service businesses are well developed. Everyday life is convenient.

As a result, most residents seem content to stay. Young people may long to go out and see the world, but many others feel little urge to move. Some are almost **too** settled: they have never left Iowa in their lives and know little of New York or San Francisco.

Why can a small city reach this standard?

**First, a highly developed market economy.** Most shops and service businesses are privately run. The market draws businesses to small towns wherever there are customers. Local shops sell not only American goods but products from all over the world—Japanese electrical equipment, Chinese textiles, and household goods from Taiwan and Hong Kong.

That is the power of a market economy. The desire to make money pushes merchants to extend their sales networks as far as possible. If some force prevented people from seeking a profit, small towns would not develop in the same way.

**Second, a highly developed transport system.** Most towns are linked to the national highway network, making travel across the country practical. Businesses can invest far from where they began. The highways, built by federal or state governments, provide essential conditions for both business and ordinary life.

This also shows where public investment can be most valuable. If government had spent enormous sums directly building shops in towns, I doubt the result would be what we see today.

**Third, enormous productive capacity.** Without high productivity, goods could not flow so freely. America also benefits from the international position of the dollar, which draws products from around the world into its market. Advanced technology contributes to prosperity too: America exports high technology and imports great quantities of well-made everyday goods.

**Fourth, an effective system of local government.** American development has taken place gradually, through many years of accumulated work. Local government generally rests on public elections. If officials want to be re-elected or leave a good reputation, they must achieve something useful. Improvements accumulate over time. Elections can also help keep capable people in office and remove those who perform badly.

The true foundation of American modernization lies not in great cities like New York, but in **thousands of small towns**. The great cities are only the peaks of the mountains.

# Chapter 11: Warning Signs Beneath the Surface

## 1. The Meaning of Family

People brought up in an Eastern culture generally find American ideas about the family difficult to accept, and often difficult even to admire. Those ideas have changed greatly over the past few decades.

There are, of course, millions of different families in America. No two are quite alike. I am speaking here of what seems to me the typical pattern, the one that represents the mainstream.

A family usually begins when a man and woman come together. Yet for many American men and women, marriage is not supposed to interfere with either person's **privacy**. Husband and wife may treat one another with great courtesy, while carefully avoiding interference in each other's private world.

This kind of relationship would be hard to accept in China or Japan. Couples discuss and negotiate all sorts of things, sometimes sounding like two people who hardly know one another. Naturally, there are warm and harmonious families too.

I think this pattern of marriage is the long-term result of America's pursuit of individualism. Americans are taught from childhood to value personal independence above almost everything else.

But after years of such training, some seem less able to get along with others, or even to share a life with another person. I believe this will become one of America's future difficulties. Marriage does not necessarily break down the fortress each person has built around the self.

Younger couples seem especially determined to keep those walls standing. Older husbands and wives often appear to depend more on one another and to care less about defending their inner boundaries.

Where do those boundaries come from? We have to look at the way Americans grow up.

I find that many young parents are extremely individualistic, sometimes selfish, and give less attention to bringing up and educating their children than Eastern parents generally do. They do not always devote themselves to their children's success in the way many Chinese parents do, or make the sacrifices associated with older Western family traditions.

Very young children, sometimes not yet a year old, are commonly put in a room of their own. The idea is that a child should begin to have a private space, a territory of his or her own. At the same time, the parents preserve their own privacy.

This is the first lesson in independence. Americans place a high value on being self-reliant, and parents teach that value to their children. Yet in doing so, parents are also protecting themselves. They do not want the arrival of a child to take away their independence.

Perhaps some of these motives operate without their being fully aware of them. They encourage children to “strive to be number one” and to “stand on their own feet.” The social effects may well be positive. Children learn early that they must make their own decisions and take responsibility for what they do. Parents also free themselves, comparatively early, from the children's constant demands.

Children begin to earn money at a young age, and parents give them some money too. Many have bank accounts while they are still small. A child of **nine or ten** may deliver newspapers to neighbours. Teenagers of **thirteen to eighteen** may earn money by babysitting.

This is a society organized around money. Parents know that if their children are to become independent, they must learn how to handle it.

By the ages of **eighteen to twenty-one**, parents usually encourage their children to leave home and make their own lives. Most young people are expected to support themselves. From a Chinese point of view, the relationship with the family can become remarkably distant.

I have often heard stories of adult children calling their parents to say they will arrive at a certain hour and leave at another—and then leaving exactly as arranged.

For a young American, entering society can feel like entering a battlefield. Making a decent living requires a tremendous effort. The pressure leaves many with little time to look after their parents.

I know some people who love their parents deeply but cannot visit them often. I also know others who seem quite indifferent to the elderly. Many older people live in retirement homes or similar institutions, with little practical involvement from their children. In either case, it can be extremely difficult for grown children to support their parents.

What do these arrangements teach younger people? What impressions do they leave on the next generation?

Parents generally leave marriage decisions to their children as well. A son or daughter brings a boyfriend or girlfriend home to meet the family, but the choice belongs to the young person. Wealthy and prominent families may be stricter, but most American parents are relaxed about these matters. After all, many of them went through much the same experience themselves.

Young people often begin dating while they are still at school, and attitudes toward sex are relatively open. Parents do not necessarily object when their children of fifteen or sixteen have boyfriends or girlfriends.

One teacher I knew brought her **fifteen-year-old son** and his girlfriend into a classroom and introduced them to everybody. I asked what she thought of the relationship.

She said she was pleased. The girlfriend was two years older than her son, had a car of her own and would not let him drink or smoke.

To many Americans, this is entirely ordinary. They grew up in much the same way. Having a boyfriend or girlfriend does not mean that marriage will follow, although of course it may.

There are many reasons for the American attitude toward children. I think a great many parents love their children very much. But ordinary families must encourage independence early because they cannot afford to support grown children indefinitely. In that sense, they are unable to express their love through continued support.

The reverse is also true. Children may love their parents, but cannot provide for them in old age. The costs are too great. They too may find themselves unable to express affection through the kind of care that Chinese families regard as natural.

This has far-reaching consequences for society.

Older Americans must depend on social insurance or welfare rather than on their children. They have to make a life of their own.

Chinese culture has long contained the belief that one raises children partly to be cared for in old age. Respect for parents is one of its basic principles. Perhaps Americans do not lack the desire for such relationships so much as the ability to maintain them.

A friend offered an important point: culture matters, but so do material conditions. Depending on one's children in old age is a product of agricultural society. Under the pressure of industrial life, that tradition is bound to weaken.

**The lives of today's elderly are the future awaiting today's young.** What young parents see around them affects how they raise their own children. From the moment a child is born, parents know that they probably will not be able to depend on that child later. Raising a child becomes largely a one-way responsibility, with little expectation of repayment.

What does that do to the parents?

For them to accept fully the responsibility of bringing up a child requires overcoming the individualism they themselves have learned since childhood. I do not mean *individualism* here simply as an insult, but as a cultural habit. Such habits are passed from generation to generation. Beginning again with different expectations is very difficult. It may become one of the problems of the future.

More than two thousand years ago, **Aristotle** described the family as the basic unit of society. But in the decades since the Second World War, that unit has been breaking apart in America.

On the surface, the family remains a basic social unit. In reality, the **individual** increasingly fills that role. Changes in the organization and meaning of family life are speeding up this process.

A family that clings too tightly to tradition can restrict the individual's development. But does a family held together too loosely really help society progress?

Family relationships once settled many personal disagreements and tensions. As those relationships weaken, the problems are passed on to society at large.

Government takes on a heavy burden: everything from children's education to care for the elderly. This becomes a considerable cost to economic and social development. As families grow less closely connected, the burden may only increase.

And government can provide mainly **material help**. Who will look after people's emotional needs? The weakening of family life removes some human warmth from society, which cannot be good for a harmonious community.

Many Americans are worried about these changes. The rise of **new conservatism** is one sign of that concern. Conservatives emphasize the importance of the family. They hope it will continue passing on religion, morality and the basic rules by which people live together. But given the way American society is organized, how easily can it do so?

Should society be built around individuals or families? How should a family be formed? Different cultures answer these questions differently.

America still treats the family as a basic unit in its formal arrangements. But much of the family's inner meaning is being emptied out. At the same time, society has not yet developed a complete way to meet the needs of individuals living on their own.

Two questions remain:

- Are human beings better suited to a life with the emotional bonds of family, or without them?
- Is individualism something people freely choose because it meets their emotional needs, or something they have little choice but to accept?

Does economic development inevitably break apart the traditional family? The American experience seems to suggest that it does. Here is one of the most difficult tensions between modernization and older social arrangements.

I felt the difficulty clearly when I visited **Singapore**. It is a predominantly Chinese society and a newly industrialized country. After achieving rapid economic growth, one of its foremost concerns became the danger of family breakdown. Singapore has tried to soften modernization's effects by drawing on Confucian culture.

Changes in family life also bring to mind **Herbert Marcuse's** picture of the *one-dimensional man*. Marcuse asked whether a civilization centred on human desire and love could replace one centred on technology.

My question is a little different: **beyond sexual love, what other kinds of affection must human society preserve?**

## 2. An Ignorant Generation?

A crisis in the education of young Americans has become a serious public worry. University education is of a fairly high standard. One reason is that many young people have a chance to enter university: the selection of talent takes place during university study, rather than entirely before it.

But secondary education is another matter. Standards in high schools can be alarmingly low. Naturally, the situation differs from place to place. The problem appears particularly severe in large cities and in areas where many Black Americans live.

On the evening of **4 October**, television broadcast a special edition of **Burning Questions**, asking: **“Why Are Our Children Failing?”** What I saw was astonishing. It is hard to imagine standards this poor in one of the world's most developed countries.

The programme had three parts, dealing with students, teachers and parents.

First came the students. Their basic knowledge seemed very limited. The programme reported the results of several tests.

On American history and government:

- **81%** knew that the attack on Pearl Harbor had brought the United States into the Second World War.
- Only **64%** knew that the American government was divided into legislative, judicial and executive branches, which check one another's powers.
- **42%** knew about the Monroe Doctrine.
- Only **20%** knew that the Supreme Court had ruled racial segregation unconstitutional.
