The results on world affairs were worse. Many students could not identify major political leaders abroad, though they knew a great deal about famous singers such as **Michael Jackson** and **Madonna**. Only **25%** knew George Orwell's novel *1984*.

Americans regarded this as a serious problem, although their concern also reflected particular political beliefs.

The mathematics results were just as surprising. Only **60%** could work out **87% of 10**. Geography produced another record: I heard that **half the students** could not locate Washington, D.C., on a map.

A Taiwanese student I knew told me that an American student once asked him where he came from. When he answered “Taiwan,” the other student asked an astonishing question: “What part of Europe is Taiwan in?”

A student from Panama told me something similar. Many American college students seemed to think Panama was merely a canal with houses along both sides. They did not even know it was a country.

Americans have discussed such failures for years. They are worried about people who cannot write or do basic arithmetic.

Someone told me about a shopkeeper he knew who offered a discount on Coca-Cola. One bottle cost **fifty cents**, but six together cost **two dollars**. The shopkeeper explained to a customer that buying six was cheaper. The customer simply could not follow the arithmetic and insisted on buying the bottles one at a time.

That is an extreme case, of course. But such difficulties are beginning to affect the economy.

On **3 October**, the *Chicago Tribune* reported that **IBM** had established an **adult literacy centre** in the area. It offered classes three days a week to students ranging from people in their twenties to those approaching retirement. This was the first programme of its kind undertaken by a major local company.

IBM had a practical reason. It expected that by the 1990s, the region might not have enough of the kinds of workers the company needed.

**Dave Cox**, the manager of the literacy centre, explained the thinking: “We believe adult illiteracy is a major economic problem. If we want to expand the economy and compete, we must have workers who are trained and who can be trained.”

According to figures from **1980**, officials in Illinois estimated that approximately **two million people** in the state were functionally illiterate. The estimate for Chicago alone was **750,000**. One can imagine how large the problem might be across the country.

If the education system cannot produce people with the skills society needs, America's future development will be difficult. One important reason for Japan's economic success after the war has been the strictness of its educational system. Japanese education remains highly effective, and perhaps represents the greatest challenge now facing America.

Yet education in basic knowledge is not the only concern. There are equally serious worries about morality and values.

Many people feel that young Americans have lost a sense of belief and ethical responsibility. Conservatives are particularly troubled by this. The television programme summed up the difficulty in three words: **values, discipline and goals**. Children, it concluded, lacked all three.

Why has this happened?

Teachers said that their pay was too low and their conditions too poor. High schools could not keep good teachers, because people were always looking for something better. Parents said they worked too much and had no time to look after their children. They were busy enough simply earning a living.

Would more money from the federal government solve the problem? It might help with part of it, but not all of it. Americans often reach for more funding as their first answer.

I watched the programme in the home of a fairly ordinary middle-class family. The couple had a daughter in high school, and one of her classmates was visiting. Afterwards, we discussed what we had seen.

The daughter said: “These things certainly happen in America, but the situation at my school isn't nearly so serious. Our students do much better. Perhaps one reason students know so little geography is that life here is too comfortable. They don't need to go to another country to have a good life. As for values, discipline and goals, different people make different choices.”

Her classmate added: “It's especially bad in the big cities and poor areas. Many people can hardly read or write. They think that studying won't bring in money right away, while dealing drugs or doing something else might. One important reason is that more education doesn't offer an immediate reward.”

This was a high-school student who had given the matter some real thought.

In a market economy, money has enormous power. Without another force to guide it, people can become concerned with profit above everything else. Eventually that creates grave social problems.

Many developing countries face the same difficulty. To get economic growth started, they first use financial rewards to direct people's energy toward production. Later, when the economy has developed and needs advanced technology and highly trained workers, they discover a shortage of such people.

Money may have encouraged people to choose work that produces quick earnings rather than the difficult study required for more demanding work. Developing countries should learn from this danger.

A remark made in the family stayed with me:

> “Anything money can solve, intelligence can also solve. But whatever intelligence cannot solve, money certainly cannot solve.”

That is too absolute, of course. Problems are best solved when knowledge and money work together. The main difficulty in developing countries is not necessarily an absolute shortage of either one, but the failure to bring them together.

The father of the family put the seriousness of the situation even more sharply: “Young people lack values, discipline and goals mainly because their parents lack them too.”

This expresses the anxiety of educated Americans. The present generation was shaped by the one before it, and the next generation will be shaped by this one. It is not difficult to imagine where the process could lead.

If even basic knowledge is failing to pass from one generation to the next, **how will a society pass on its central beliefs and values?** How will children learn the political habits of their society?

This is not only one of America's greatest economic challenges. It is one of its greatest political challenges as well. The rise of new conservatism shows how deeply the anxiety has spread.

## 3. Young People Adrift

What effect are changing attitudes toward the family, and the loosening of family ties, having on American society? Do they help society develop, or do they work against it? This question deserves serious study. As individualism grows stronger, one begins to wonder who will take family responsibilities seriously. For young unmarried people, the question of how to meet those responsibilities is already a difficult one.

In small and medium-sized towns and in the countryside, I have found that family ties remain fairly strong. People with a university education also tend to have a clear sense of their obligations to their families. American society teaches parents quite plainly that they cannot depend on their children in old age. Even so, parents with a sense of responsibility continue to fulfil their duties and bring up their children until they are adults.

Yet individualism and the insistence on personal privacy run so deep that they shape life inside the family as well. I think this spirit—the wish to be independent and to keep one's own life private—makes family relationships harder to manage. Tension within the household can become quite intense. Parents may find it difficult to guide older children even when their advice is sound. Parents who are determined to protect their own private lives sometimes fail to take their children's wishes fully into account. The same conflict can arise between husbands and wives.

America has a high divorce rate. In many cases, I suspect, the reasons are not mainly social or financial, but lie in people's emotional lives. The Oscar-winning film *Kramer vs. Kramer* captures some of the difficulties that arise from this state of mind. What deserves attention is not so much Joanna's reasons for leaving, which are never entirely clear, as the father's attitude toward his child. These attitudes within the family have brought a number of problems: divorce, single-parent households, and young people who leave home and drift from place to place. Let us look more closely at the last of these.

The term I am using for these young people comes from Singapore. It is much like the Chinese expressions for children who stay out all night, including an old Beijing expression for wandering the streets after dark. Family problems often fall first on the young. Children and teenagers leave home because of conflicts with their parents, and their flight has become a serious social problem.

Why does this matter so much to me? Because young people are a society's future. Lose a generation of young people and you lose the future itself. The kind of society that will exist tomorrow depends on how today's children grow up. Americans understand this too. In 1976, Senator **Birch Bayh**, chairman of a Senate committee investigating juvenile delinquency, said that young people were the nation's most precious resource. A society may produce plenty of outstanding people. But it needs ordinary people as much as it needs a talented few. If the majority become a 'lost generation', how can society move forward?

I have begun with the family and arrived at children who run away because I want to trace the problem back to what families are supposed to do. I have with me a book by **Arnold P. Rubin**, published in 1976, on juvenile delinquency and runaway young people in America. Although it appeared twelve years ago, it still has much to tell us.

The scale of the problem is alarming. An estimate for **1975** put the number of young people running away each year at **one million**, and that was considered conservative. In a country of a little over two hundred million people, this is hardly a small figure. In **1973**, police took **265,000** runaway young people into custody. The term refers to minors who leave home without their parents' consent. Some are children of only ten or so. Rubin describes what drove them away and what happened to them. Here are a few of his examples.

- **Terry, seventeen:** 'The main reason I left was the tension at home.' She had no desire to go back: the atmosphere was too strained. Nor did she want to return to school, where she felt she learned nothing useful.
- **Randy, seventeen, from Florida:** 'My father put me on a bus and told me, “Don't let me see you again.”' His explanation was that his parents wanted to be rid of a burden. 'They think there's something wrong with my head.' He and his sister were adopted.
- **Kathy, from San Diego:** She had been on the streets since she was thirteen. She could not live with her stepfather, who drank heavily and beat her. 'One day I got home an hour late after walking back with a friend. He wanted to hit my friend, but my mother stopped him. Then he beat me with a belt, demanding to know why I was late. He brought out a knife, and my mother stopped him again.'
- **Rick, twelve:** His parents lived apart, and he had not seen his mother for seven years. He lived with his father and grandparents. All three adults drank heavily. His father beat him when he was drunk.
- **Gise:** She came from what appeared to be a very comfortable home. 'My mother's health was very bad, and home was a mess. She wanted attention, and sometimes she hit me to get it.' After Gise ran away twice and returned, her father told her she could no longer live there. She left again and lived on the streets for about a month.
- **Baxter, fifteen:** She was murdered in New York. The police said her killer was one of her 'customers'. Earlier she had been arrested for prostitution, but persuaded the police that she was nineteen and that it was her first offence. They released her. She was killed the following night.
- **Barkley:** After running away, she was arrested for prostitution and was later killed. Her father had disappeared without warning ten years earlier, leaving the family poor and dependent on public assistance. She had wanted to escape that life.

These are only a handful of the many cases Rubin describes. Again and again, the immediate cause is tension at home, whatever may have produced it. Of course some children have run away because their parents refused to buy them a high-fidelity music system or something of the kind. But the deeper issue is that many families cannot achieve any real harmony within the home.

If everyone insists on independence and a private space that no one else may enter, how is a family to remain close? Individualism and privacy are central American values. But are they beneficial at every possible level? Even something good will turn against its original purpose if it is carried too far. In American workplaces, people generally pay little attention to one another's private lives. Should the family operate by the same rule? Or is some balance needed?

At the very least, young people's flight from home shows that their families have lost the power to hold them. It points to two wider problems for society.

**First, the family is becoming less able to perform its social role.** It is one of the chief places where the next generation is educated. It is also where many of society's tensions can be settled before they spread. As family relationships change, more of these difficulties are passed on to society at large. Government spends **tens of millions of dollars each year** dealing with runaway young people alone. It has set up shelters and other services for them. The result is more pressure on society, higher public spending, and greater tension between people.

**Second, we must ask what kind of outlook young people develop when they grow up in such conditions.** Will they acquire the attitudes and emotional strength needed for a healthy society? We should not force young people to follow a single path, but neither should we simply leave them to drift. When such large numbers grow up in this atmosphere, the consequences for society cannot be good. Many runaway children become involved in crime or fall victim to it, and many lose their chance of an education.

Japan's rapid development is closely connected, I believe, with its comprehensive way of bringing up and educating the young. From the standpoint of Western culture, some of Japan's methods may seem unacceptable. Yet Japan's success should remind us that following familiar paths can also lead to failure. Every society must find suitable ways to raise its next generation. Will the next few generations of Americans be able to carry the country forward? Many Americans themselves have doubts.

A society's development certainly depends on its political institutions, businesses, banks, science, technology and culture. But it also depends on a more basic institution: **the family**. The difficult question is how to bring changing social attitudes into harmony with family life.

The answer will help determine how well the whole society can hold together.

## 4. America and Its Drugs

In today's world, one can hardly speak of America without speaking of drugs. That is why I have given this section a title which suggests that drugs have become inseparable from the country. Drug abuse is one of America's most stubborn social diseases. The Reagan administration has recently launched a powerful campaign against trafficking and drug use, but how effective it will be remains to be seen.

Criminal organizations have built themselves around the drug trade. They use every means at their disposal to stay ahead of the authorities. Drugs enter the country through many different routes. In some cities, buying a packet is easy: people offer them for sale on streets and in alleyways. Many users become addicted and cannot escape. Once dependent on drugs, some find themselves under the control of criminal gangs. Young women may even be forced to sell sex in exchange for drugs.

Drugs are eating away at America. Their power may be greater than that of many of the forces that have shaken the country in the past. Their spread challenges the way American society works. Whether the country can contain and reduce drug use is a test not only of its institutions, but also of its moral beliefs.

Young people are among the first to suffer. Drug use among them is extraordinarily serious. I saw a film about a secondary-school principal trying to restore order to a school that had become dirty, chaotic and badly managed. His main opponent was a Black student with a following of his own. The student openly sold drugs in the school toilets. He and his supporters arranged to beat the principal and tore apart his motorcycle. Eventually several of them came with guns to kill him. The principal was almost beaten to death before he was unexpectedly rescued. As the police took the student away, he stared at the principal with a look that suggested he had not given up. It was chilling.

Of course a film contains artistic invention. But I fear that the story may not be far removed from what happens in some of the worst schools. Drug organizations have reached into every corner of society, especially places where young people gather. Small towns may be less badly affected. In the large cities, the problem is much more severe.

Consider an article called 'Children and Drugs', published in the **May 1987** issue of *Ladies' Home Journal*. It began by quoting a letter President Reagan had written to the magazine. He warned that drugs threatened every American family and the American way of life. They deprived young people of the education they needed, left **eighteen out of every thousand Americans** unable to work, contributed to crime, destroyed property, raised insurance costs and placed a heavy burden on the police. The financial damage, he said, could be measured in hundreds of millions of dollars. But how could anyone measure the loss suffered by a nation whose people's lives were being ruined? The letter makes clear the seriousness of the challenge.

What is happening to young drug users? The magazine interviewed several. One of them, **Tim Osiki**, described his experience:

> 'I started taking drugs when I left elementary school for junior high. I found that there were lots of drugs at the new school, perhaps because I got to know some older children and children from the city centre. Some people I knew used drugs. At first I wasn't interested. Later I started smoking marijuana. After a while that stopped doing anything for me. Then I met some children from very wealthy families. They introduced me to expensive drugs, including cocaine. I tried it, and from then on cocaine became an inseparable part of my life.'

Once young people pick up the habit at school, in the neighbourhood or elsewhere, they may find themselves trapped. There are many kinds of drugs. Some are medicines that can be useful in treatment, but can become addictive and harmful when misused. Among the substances discussed are **heroin, morphine, codeine, methadone, cocaine, barbiturates, amphetamines, LSD, DMT, mescaline, psilocybin and alcohol**.

Countless young people become involved in crime through drugs. The subject has inspired a great many films, television programmes, novels and stories. In one film, a group of school students obtain a small case full of drugs and decide to sell it. They arrange to meet buyers, but discover that the buyers are carrying weapons and intend to rob and kill them. The students draw guns of their own and escape.

Then two other students learn about the case and decide to steal it. They arrive armed, only to find another group attempting the same robbery. The three groups exchange fire. Two people die. One of the two would-be robbers is shot dead. The other, fleeing, runs over and kills the younger brother of the leader of the group with the drugs. He is sent to a juvenile detention centre.

The dead boy's older brother is determined to take revenge. He deliberately rapes the detained boy's girlfriend and is himself sent to the same institution. The two carry on their feud there, sometimes openly, sometimes in secret. One night the older brother knocks a guard unconscious, breaks into his enemy's room and begins a desperate fight. Both are badly injured. All the people involved are minors.

Naturally, drug use is not confined to teenagers. There are plenty of adult users as well. The trouble is that many began using drugs when they were young. Addiction can have devastating physical and emotional effects. It may leave a person listless, exhausted and depressed, eventually destroy a person's normal state of mind, and sometimes lead to death.

Not everyone agrees on how society should deal with drugs. **Thomas S. Szasz**, for example, wrote an article challenging the usual argument. Most people condemn drug abuse and favour compulsory controls because drugs are dangerous. But Szasz argues that the freedom to injure or even kill oneself is a basic part of personal freedom. Self-destruction may be regarded as wrong, and society may discourage it through informal means. Yet it should not automatically be treated as a crime or a mental illness to be controlled by the police.

On this view, it is absurd to prevent an adult from possessing drugs. Guns and sharp weapons can be even more dangerous, yet they are not completely prohibited. Possession is a matter of rights. Therefore adults should not be deprived of the right to possess and use drugs, even though most people will never choose to do so. This is a striking argument from the opposite side of the debate.

It is not the dominant view. Most Americans now want the government to act effectively against the sale and use of drugs. Still, Szasz's argument cannot simply be dismissed. A gun can kill someone with one shot. A packet of drugs cannot kill another person in the same way unless that person takes it. Americans speak of gun ownership as a citizen's right. Why, then, is possession of drugs different? They have not yet found an answer that resolves the contradiction.

This is really a question about the limits of individual rights. **How much freedom can a society give each person?** It is not realistic to say that anyone can have unlimited freedom. Locke and Rousseau discussed such questions without settling how they would work in practice. People today have not settled them either. Perhaps we need to think again about human nature itself.

This is what makes drugs such a serious challenge for America. Americans believe that each person has an inalienable right to decide their own fate. Over time, especially since the Second World War, claims to freedom have expanded. Political voting rights, racial equality and equality between women and men were followed by claims to sexual freedom and the freedom to choose one's own way of life, including the life of the hippies. These changes have gradually gained acceptance.

Now comes a claim to the freedom to take drugs, and Americans do not accept it. To accept it, they fear, might bring decline to the nation, or at least to a substantial part of it. Does the American system possess a firm philosophical basis for prohibiting drugs? It is difficult to say. Philosophical arguments in favour of the opposite position certainly exist. At present most Americans support prohibition. That is a choice based on public opinion, experience and feeling. But what will happen later? Was sexual freedom not once condemned as immoral too?

The challenge goes far beyond philosophy, though few people are interested in such abstract questions until circumstances force them to confront them. There is a more immediate problem: **why do people begin taking drugs?** Many users say that they are bored, empty, worried or depressed, and seek excitement or relief. One psychiatrist has suggested that a third of Americans have some kind of psychological difficulty. What produces such conditions?

To answer, we have to look much more widely—at families, schools, workplaces, culture, education, politics and the law. Does the environment created by these institutions help produce drug users, or help prevent drug use? The number of users keeps increasing. Police action and prohibition can only deal with the results. They cannot, by themselves, remove the causes. And how hard it is to change the causes.

We Chinese know what drugs can do to a nation. After the middle of the nineteenth century, great quantities of opium poured into China. Their damaging effects can still be felt, and their consequences will continue to shape our history for a long time.

Most Americans who oppose drugs are worried about their own children. A smaller number are concerned about the development of society as a whole. A few look even further ahead. That longer view is the hardest to make people accept.

## 5. Organized Crime

Organized crime has considerable power in American society. Its groups are tightly run, and their activities extend far beyond isolated criminal acts. Anyone who has seen *The Godfather* will have some idea of the Mafia and the ruthless struggles between its rival groups. *The Honor of the Family* also portrays its methods. Books, films and other works constantly return to this subject.

Of course, these are artistic portrayals, often heightened for dramatic effect. But the problem itself is real. Criminal organizations have become one of the most difficult problems facing this wealthy society. They threaten its security and challenge its institutions.

An article in *The New York Times* on **25 November** described two drug-trafficking organizations. The account was frightening. They were called the **Bloods** and the **Crips**, and their bases were in California. Taking advantage of America's extensive interstate highway system, they had built organized networks of violence and drug distribution across the country. Their activities stretched from California on the West Coast to Baltimore and Washington in the East, and from large cities to small ones.

Their main business was selling cocaine. According to the report, the two organizations had spread into almost every state, with **eight hundred operating locations**. They had worked patiently to build what was, in its own way, a well-managed business.

They were not merely growing larger. They were also heavily armed. Sometimes drug-enforcement officers found themselves facing better weapons than their opponents and had to retreat. The organizations possessed both light and heavy firearms and were ready to use them whenever they encountered resistance.

They did not necessarily enter a new town quietly. Often they made a show of arriving, driving expensive luxury cars and wearing costly, dazzling clothes.

Their representatives frequently carried no identification and did not personally sell drugs on street corners. They had greater ambitions. Their purpose was to establish local distribution networks and make agreements, behaving almost as though they were arranging perfectly ordinary commercial deals.

Two offenders recently arrested in Missouri were found with **1,100 pounds of cocaine**, worth an enormous amount of money.

No one seems to know the exact size of these organizations. One figure for Los Angeles County put their numbers at about **seven hundred**, most of them Black. Another estimate for the city of Los Angeles gave around **27,000 active members**. Their presence was substantial elsewhere too: about **seven hundred in Denver**, and some **sixty in Oklahoma City**, in addition to more than thirty who had already been arrested. There were also many in Kansas City. Wherever they established operations, they gathered large groups of people around them.

Their discipline was strict, and their behaviour was often brazen. They sold drugs throughout neighbourhoods and threatened residents who might report them to the police. In **Omaha**, one residential area had acquired the name **'Vietnam'**, because gunfights occurred there so frequently.

Another recently published book offers an even closer look at organized crime: *Mafia Enforcer: A True Story of Life and Death in the Mob*, by **Thomas Renner** and **Cecil Kirby**. Renner is a well-known reporter who specializes in investigating the criminal underworld. He has won various awards and is regarded as an expert on organized crime. Kirby was once a leading member of a criminal organization but later turned against it. The organization reportedly offered a reward of **$100,000** for his death. He lives under another name and receives strict police protection.

Kirby told his story, and Renner wrote it down. The book's account of the criminal underworld is terrifying.

Consider just the organizational charts near the beginning. One kind of criminal group is built around a family, like the Mafia. At the top is a commission. Beneath it are the boss and underboss; below them come the **caporegimes**, or group leaders, and then the **soldiers**. The soldiers enforce discipline through threats, attacks and murder. They also carry out orders from above.

These groups maintain extensive connections with people and organizations outside their immediate membership. Their work falls into two broad categories. On the legal side, their interests may include **banks, bars, clubs, food production, labour unions, insurance, property, restaurants and entertainment**. On the illegal side are activities such as **illicit alcohol dealings, violent attacks, bombings, bribery, unlawful exports, kidnapping, murder, prostitution and theft**.

A second form is not based on a family. The book calls these **motorcycle gangs**. They have national leaders—presidents, vice-presidents and treasurers. Below them are officials with titles such as **sergeant-at-arms, war lord and road captain**. Their duties include keeping members in line and controlling outsiders, again through beatings, intimidation and sometimes murder. They, too, have contacts throughout society.

Their lawful businesses can include **car dealerships, bars, entertainment venues, clubs, food businesses, motorcycle sales, property, restaurants, transport, construction and antiques**. Their unlawful activities are much like those of the Mafia.

Such organizations have strict internal rules and extraordinary reach. They exercise power over a significant part of American life. The White House governs the United States, but in some places criminal groups also exercise a kind of government. The territory they rule is a dark one.

The book describes what happens to young women who become trapped in this world. One passage from Chapter 5, about women forced into prostitution, is especially disturbing:

> 'They often killed women to protect themselves because they thought the women might become informers. That is one reason so many girls disappeared. Some were tortured or murdered. Their bodies were mutilated, nailed to crosses or abandoned in wild places for animals to consume, leaving no trace. Girls disappeared for other reasons too. Some worked as prostitutes or dancers in low-class bars, and by sixteen or eighteen were considered too old and no longer useful. Some wanted to escape the clubs that controlled them. Some had failed to hand over the required money. Others simply had not earned enough.'

After reading accounts like this, one cannot help asking: **Is this really America?** Yes, it is. This is the other side of the coin.

The government acknowledges the threat posed by organized crime. According to **FBI figures from 1985**, the four largest criminal organizations had **3,800 members**, and that did not include some **eight hundred smaller groups**. Since then, the influence of the Mafia and other organizations has grown further. They have become one of society's most persistent problems.

Their existence challenges the system itself. Organized crime on this scale suggests that there are openings in the way society is governed. Of course no system can eliminate crime altogether. But when criminal groups become large, nationwide organizations, something deserves closer examination. By an 'opening', I mean simply a gap through which something can pass. I am not suggesting that it was deliberately left there. Two features of American political life are especially important.

**First, Americans believe that people should be prosecuted for crimes they have committed, not for acts they have not committed.** The law is there to be enforced when someone breaks it. This may be an important principle of government: people cannot be punished or forbidden to act in advance merely because their conduct may later cause harm. Society waits to see what they actually do.

But organized criminal groups can become powerful before the authorities are able to act effectively against them. By the time their crimes are exposed and punished, they may already be too strong to control. America's experience illustrates the danger.

**Second, Americans believe that people have the right to form organizations of their own.** This is another basic political principle. Political parties themselves operate because of this right. But if people are free to join together, that freedom is available to everyone. Criminals can organize too. Society may punish particular offenders while finding it much harder to deal with the organizations behind them. And because these groups mix lawful business with unlawful activity, officials often face legal and practical obstacles when they try to dismantle them.

These are potential weaknesses in the American political system. Organized crime has brought them into the open. Every society faces questions of this kind when it designs its institutions. A freedom granted for one purpose may also protect conduct society wants to stop. Something we would rather withhold may have to be allowed because of a more basic freedom we have chosen to recognize.

If the American system has a lasting difficulty, perhaps it lies here. It has been remarkably successful at **granting freedoms and permitting people to act**. It has been less successful at **preventing harmful conduct and placing limits on it**. Americans want to prohibit many things, yet sometimes their efforts have the opposite effect. The more determined they are to ban something, the more of it seems to appear.

## 6. A Kingdom of Beggars

Victor Hugo's immortal novel *The Hunchback of Notre-Dame* describes the suffering and daily lives of beggars in the Paris of an earlier age. Its characters, Quasimodo and Esmeralda, are unforgettable. Film adaptations have made Quasimodo seem almost real. His image is so vivid that people have come to see him as a symbol of the poor and downtrodden.

What is strange is that, walking through the United States—the richest great power of the twentieth century—I often come across people who remind me of him. Their clothes are filthy and torn, their movements slow, their eyes dull. Their faces have a look of suffering that is difficult to forget. There are so many that one could almost speak of a **kingdom of beggars** in America in the 1980s. Their presence is one of the country's major social problems.

The newspapers usually call these people **'the homeless'**. As their numbers have grown, both government and the public have paid more attention. In recent weeks the issue has come up repeatedly in newspapers and official statements. In cities large and small, homeless people are everywhere: sitting silently on the pavement, asking passers-by for money, sleeping curled up in doorways and corners, or moving slowly along with piles of worn-out possessions on their backs.

A few experiences of my own may help show what this means.

- **Washington, August 1988.** A British political scholar invited me to dinner at a Lebanese restaurant. We agreed to walk there from the Washington Hilton, where we were staying, so that we could see the city at night. We set off at about seven in the evening and reached one of the busiest streets downtown. A Black man stopped us and asked whether we had any change. It was my first encounter with a homeless person in America. On the walk to dinner and back, we met no fewer than **ten** people who were homeless or asking us for money. That seemed an extraordinary number for a single evening.
- **New York, December 1988.** People in such circumstances seemed particularly concentrated here. One night some friends and I drove into the city, intending to stay with an acquaintance. We did not know the streets well and accidentally drove into **Harlem**, an area with a very high crime rate. It was already past midnight. Groups of people stood at nearly every intersection, some shouting, others drinking. We felt increasingly uneasy. Only the next day did we learn just where we had driven, and the thought sent a chill through us.
- **New York, December 1988.** On another occasion I approached a friend's apartment building and noticed someone sitting on the steps, eating. The person was badly dressed, with a heap of battered belongings nearby. I became wary and hesitated before going inside. Then the person looked up. It was an elderly woman. 'Please don't hurt me,' she said. 'I'm homeless. I'm only sitting here to eat. I'm not going to do anything else.' I felt a sudden, overwhelming pity for her.
- **Berkeley, January 1988.** While visiting the Institute of East Asian Studies at the University of California, Berkeley, I walked along a shopping street every lunchtime. There were many people begging. For several days we noticed an empty doorway where someone had spread old newspapers, cardboard and scraps of paper on the ground. A person evidently slept there each night beneath a ragged cover. We never saw the sleeper, but it was easy to imagine the circumstances.

Such scenes are common. Almost anyone who spends time in America is likely to encounter them. With the arrival of winter, homeless people have gathered in considerable numbers in West Coast cities. Groups can be seen outside San Francisco's City Hall. Seattle has many in its streets and alleyways. On the colder East Coast they gather in railway stations and public buildings. In the toilets at New York's railway stations, groups ask for money, making other people afraid to use them. The Greyhound bus stations are much the same.

On **20 January 1989**, George Bush was sworn in as the forty-first president of the United States. While television carried his stirring inaugural address, it cut to a different picture: a homeless person sleeping on the ground beneath a heap of miscellaneous coverings. The contrast showed how serious the problem had become.

A front-page article in *The New York Times* on **16 January 1989** examined the tensions caused by homelessness in a small village called **Elmsford**, which had become something of a 'capital' for the homeless in **Westchester County**. The village had only **3,300 permanent residents**, yet **378 homeless people** were living in four local motels.

New York State law required that anyone in need of emergency accommodation should be offered it. The county welfare department therefore had to find places for these people. Only **nine of the 378** were from the village. The others had come from elsewhere, including Boston, Louisiana and Oregon.

In **1988**, Westchester County spent **$54 million** arranging accommodation for homeless people. It planned to spend **$64 million** the following year. The concentration of homeless people in Elmsford made local residents anxious. They complained that their quality of life had declined, crime had increased and fights were more common. They feared the loss of the peace they had once enjoyed. Some were considering moving away.

This small village shows how homelessness can create wider conflicts. If a community of only a few thousand people struggles with the arrival of so many homeless people, what happens when we consider the problem across the whole country?

Why do people become homeless? There is no simple answer.

Some Americans say homeless people have chosen that way of life. Many, they point out, appear young and physically strong. They would rather beg than look for jobs, and those who take this view have little sympathy for them.

Others believe that a major cause was the decision to release some people with mental illnesses who had not behaved violently. They may be unable to hold regular jobs, while their families and relatives may be unwilling to care for them. Many end up on the streets.

In American cities, one frequently encounters people behaving in unusual or disturbed ways. Another explanation looks back to the Vietnam War. Some homeless people, it is said, are veterans who became dependent on drugs while trying to endure the war. The damage to their physical and mental health made it difficult for them to work afterwards. Unable to return to ordinary life, they too became homeless. There are other explanations as well.

Whatever the cause, the phenomenon is deeply puzzling. It challenges both America's institutions and its guiding beliefs. On one side stands an extraordinary abundance of goods and wealth. On the other are large numbers of people living in extreme poverty.

America is not like some developing countries, where there are simply too few resources to provide for everyone's basic needs, however fairly those resources are shared. There is enough wealth here for every homeless person to live a decent life **if it were distributed differently**. The difficulty is that a different way of distributing it is hard to create within the existing system. It does not fit easily with the system's character.

Someone might argue that homelessness has nothing to do with the political or economic system. Even then, we would have to admit that American culture and the American spirit have not supplied an answer. Government agencies and private organizations are certainly making serious efforts to help. But solving the problem is far from easy.

Whether a particular person becomes homeless mainly through personal circumstances or through wider social forces, the existence of so many homeless people challenges the society around them. These difficulties will not cause the world to regard America as poor or backward. It will still be seen as the leading developed country. But the difficulties do help us see the real America more clearly.

No society can be perfect, and no system of government can foresee and handle every problem. The measure of a good political and administrative system is not whether it was designed without a single flaw from the beginning. It is **whether it can respond to new problems, develop its strengths and improve itself**.

That requires the ability to adapt. And the changes it makes must be possible within the way the system itself works.

## 7. The Black Challenge

By the 'Black challenge', or what I might call the 'Black storm', I mean the problems surrounding the position of Black Americans. These have become a source of deep social and political difficulty, and a serious challenge to the country. Some people describe the issue as America's greatest social problem and believe it could one day prove fatal to the existing order. Travelling through the United States, one can see why they say this.

I visited the **Martin Luther King Jr. Center** in Atlanta. Compared with the Carter Center, it was small and rather worn, partly because it had been established much earlier. King was the famous leader of the Black civil-rights movement. Racial discrimination remained severe into the 1960s. There were schools reserved for white children, buses and hotels divided by race, and residential areas where Black people could not live. Black Americans had little political standing or financial strength.

The struggle to end segregation brought large demonstrations, unrest and violence. The federal government even had to send troops to protect Black students entering formerly white schools. Although the courts ruled racial segregation unlawful, discrimination remained deeply rooted in everyday life. More than twenty years have passed since those struggles. Black people's conditions have improved in some respects, yet the underlying problems seem to have grown still more serious.

A Chinese student at **Emory University** told me: 'In the South, Black people were freed after the Civil War, but their social standing is still very low.' The Civil War was fought over whether slavery would continue or be abolished. *Uncle Tom's Cabin* describes the position of Black people in that period. One of President Lincoln's great achievements was the emancipation of enslaved people. Yet more than a century later, Black Americans, though no longer slaves, still do not enjoy the same social standing as white Americans.

The student told me about a county near Atlanta where discrimination was especially severe. According to him, the county still opposed Black people living there, and there were reportedly none among its residents. When Black people tried to settle there, they were forced out. Organizations opposing racial discrimination had repeatedly encouraged courageous Black families to move in, only to see them driven away.

The previous year, one such organization arranged a march through the county. Local residents attacked the marchers. When newspapers reported the incident, it provoked anger around the country. Supporters travelled from many places to join another march. The police were anxious, but the local opponents of integration, seeing the great numbers assembled against them, did not dare attack. Yet the protesters could do little more than march. They had achieved no lasting change. The county, the student said, continued to discriminate.

In most places, explicit rules discriminating against Black Americans have now disappeared. Indeed, some regulations aim to produce the opposite result. Government agencies and other employers may be required to hire Black people. Some white Americans see this as discrimination turned upside down. They argue that a white applicant may sometimes be better qualified for a particular job but still lose it because an employer is required to hire a Black applicant.

But such complaints concern only the surface of the problem. Many white Americans still harbour strong prejudices. In certain largely white residential districts, if a Black family moves in, white residents begin to leave. Property prices may fall because other white families refuse to buy there. The result is that Black and white residents largely live in separate areas.

Most large cities have districts where Black residents are concentrated. These are often among the poorer and more neglected neighbourhoods. Even a Black person who becomes a millionaire may still find it impossible to enter the highest circles of white society. Americans can present an admirable public appearance of equality. Look beneath it and racial prejudice remains deeply entrenched.

In general, Black Americans occupy a lower social and economic position and have fewer educational opportunities. In the streets, I have encountered many people begging, and it has seemed to me that a large proportion are Black. In Washington the number is especially striking, at odds with the city's wealth and splendour. Many street performers I have seen are Black. So are many of the people waiting outside employment offices and welfare offices, many of those living without shelter, and many of those wearing worn and ragged clothes. There are white people in desperate circumstances too, though I have encountered fewer of them. A visitor seeing such conditions is naturally uneasy.

The neighbourhoods where Black people live offer the clearest picture of their difficulties. I have visited such areas in **San Francisco, New York, New Haven, Philadelphia, Washington and Atlanta**. My impressions were very poor. Generally these districts looked dirtier, more disorderly and more neglected than white neighbourhoods. They were visibly poor. People sat listlessly outside houses, and groups of young men stood on street corners. I found the atmosphere unsettling.

New York's Black neighbourhoods struck me as especially troubled by crime. A friend once told me about an American who drove him through one of these areas. Halfway in, a police car began following them closely. They thought they had broken a traffic rule. When they stopped, the officer asked whether they knew what district lay ahead. They said they did. He advised them to be very careful. They drove straight through without getting out of the car. My friend wondered what might have happened if the car had broken down.

With so few economic opportunities, I have been told that some people turn to crime when they see no other way out. America already has a very high crime rate, and the problem is particularly serious in these poor districts. I have heard numerous stories of Chinese people being robbed by Black offenders. One friend said he was accompanying a colleague to a restaurant when two Black men confronted them on a staircase, held knives and demanded money.

A Chinese student told me that crime was rampant on **Forty-Second Street** in New York, where he said the Chinese consulate was located. According to him, criminals deliberately targeted Chinese people for money. He claimed the New York police had even placed an officer outside the consulate because ordinary patrols were not enough. I should add that I did **not** myself see an officer posted there. Such incidents, people told me, could take place in broad daylight.

Outstanding Black Americans continue to emerge and become national idols: track athletes, singers, basketball and softball players, boxers and American football players. Yet the overall position of Black Americans, in my view, has not changed enough.

The difficulties have become a cycle that is hard to break. I see Black communities as having, on average, fewer educational and financial resources. Families are often large, while the welfare system provides assistance for children. The Black birth rate is higher than the white birth rate. Many children grow up without a comfortable home or a good education. When parents themselves have had little education, they may find it difficult to pass on the habits and beliefs that children need.

Growing up in a disadvantaged neighbourhood can leave young people uncertain of their place in society. They may reach adulthood without the education or skills needed for more demanding jobs. Some grow up with only one parent, usually their mother. Others are born to what are sometimes called **'child mothers'**—girls who become pregnant at fourteen or fifteen. How is a girl of that age to provide a child with all the guidance and education it needs? Such situations, I have been told, are not uncommon.

As these children grow up, they often find that only the lowest-paid work is available to them. A few struggle their way out, only to meet new barriers when they try to enter white society. The pattern continues from one generation to the next, and the problem grows like a rolling snowball.

This is the challenge facing American society and its institutions. So far, the system has seemed either too weak or unable to solve it. When a problem appears impossible to resolve, hostility toward the people associated with it may grow. The new conservatives' complaints about 'reverse discrimination' carry something of that danger.

Legal segregation has become history, but the deeper racial challenge has not disappeared. The age when the **Ku Klux Klan** could act with open confidence may be past, yet we cannot assume that such attitudes will never return. If society fails to find a fundamental way to improve Black Americans' circumstances, the result could be an even stronger reaction against them. There is a weakness in human nature here: when people cannot solve a problem, they may find it easier simply to turn against those involved.

Almost every society has some version of this difficulty: a group of people regarded by others as 'inferior'. The reasons vary. They may lie in cultural differences, historical events, customs or institutions. In America, I believe, the chief cause is **the way society is organized**. That is why the situation of Black Americans poses such a serious challenge to the American system itself.

## 8. The Situation of Native Americans

One important fact about America's colonial history is that the land was already inhabited when Europeans crossed the ocean to reach the New World. The people living there were the **Native Americans**, whom Europeans called **Indians**. Evidence of their earliest presence reaches far into the past.

In **1925**, a Black cowboy named **George McJunkin** discovered a heap of partly burned animal bones in New Mexico. Scientists later concluded that the animals had been killed by hunters **ten thousand years earlier**. Until discoveries of this kind, people had generally assumed that the Indigenous peoples' history on the continent extended back only a few thousand years. Who were these ancient people? Where had they come from?

Research after 1925 led some scientists to believe that people had been living in the Americas for at least **twenty thousand years**. But there was no agreement about how they arrived. One theory suggested that during the Ice Age the **Bering Strait** had been dry land, allowing people to cross from Asia. The earliest inhabitants of America may therefore have come from Asia. The physical resemblance some observers saw between Native Americans and Mongolian peoples seemed to support this explanation.

Not all scholars accepted it. Some Native American scholars were especially opposed, believing that the theory weakened their claim to the land by suggesting that they too were immigrants from somewhere else. Where America's earliest inhabitants came from remains, perhaps, a difficult question to answer.

The arrival of Europeans brought two cultures into conflict. Europeans tended to dismiss Native American culture as inferior. A book called *America: People and Values* compares the two ways of life. It describes several elements found in any culture: ways of obtaining food, clothing and shelter; religious practices; artistic creation; forms of group organization; and ways of passing on culture. Native American societies possessed all of these, in many distinctive forms.

The book then draws a distinction between **culture** and what it calls **civilization**. Civilization, in this account, includes advanced technologies such as metalworking; highly developed forms of settlement such as cities; bodies of knowledge expressed through calendars and writing; and a division of labour. According to this comparison, the Native American societies being discussed lacked these later features, whereas Europeans possessed them.

From a European standpoint, it was therefore easy to look down on Native Americans. Only after the 1960s did greater respect for other cultures become widespread. But showing respect is not necessarily the same as genuinely valuing a culture. Many Americans today seem to take this attitude toward unfamiliar ways of life. They are courteous and speak respectfully of different cultures, while inwardly regarding them as less worthy—perhaps even when they think about Japanese culture.

In fact, the Americas produced remarkable cultural achievements, including those of the **Maya, Aztecs and Incas**. Why these traditions did not develop into modern industrial civilization is another question that is not easy to settle.

The very name **'Indian'** reflects European prejudice and error. When Columbus arrived in the Caribbean in **1492**, he mistakenly believed the islands were part of India. He therefore called their inhabitants Indians. Even after the mistake became clear, Europeans kept the name. Had they mistaken England for India, I suspect they would certainly have corrected themselves.

Conflict followed European settlement. Europeans also brought diseases previously unknown to Native American communities. Europeans had developed ways of treating many diseases, while Indigenous peoples had not encountered some of these illnesses before. The death rates in some villages were said to reach **80 or 90 per cent**.

A writer whose name is given in the source as **Limirich** recalls a remark attributed to **Alexis de Tocqueville** in **1831**: 'Heaven has not made them civilized; they are destined to die.' White Americans today are generally less willing to express such prejudice openly. But another attitude remains:

**Heaven has not made them rich; they are destined to be poor.**

It is a powerful assumption.

At first, relations between Europeans and Native Americans could be friendly. But these were often Europeans who had come to trade rather than to settle, much as the first Europeans to do business in China were different from those who later came with other aims. Europeans supplied horses and guns to some Native American groups, helping them expand farther inland. The new goods and weapons changed the organization of Indigenous societies and the balance of power among them.

Native Americans, too, initially welcomed the Europeans. At that stage the newcomers needed them for food and other necessities. Native Americans did not depend in the same way on the Europeans.

Over time this changed, and trade was one important reason. Communities that had previously been largely self-sufficient became dependent on goods brought from outside, including alcohol. To obtain those goods, they had to work or trade on European terms. Debt bound them ever more tightly.

**Thomas Jefferson** is quoted as saying in **1820**: 'We have the wolf by the ears; we can neither hold him nor safely let him go.' In the account before me, the wolf is taken to mean Native Americans. Whatever the precise history of the remark, the attitude it expresses is hardly the one Americans openly profess today.

Then came a long period when European settlers seized Native American land, drove its inhabitants away and killed them. The prolonged wars between settlers and Indigenous peoples began. American films and historical writing contain countless portrayals of those struggles. In the end, the government created **reservations** and confined Native Americans within designated areas—rather as people set aside nature reserves for wild animals.

White Americans have historically treated Native Americans differently from Black Americans. After the Civil War, constitutional amendments recognized the rights of formerly enslaved Black people. Before emancipation, an enslaved person had counted as **three-fifths of a person** for the purpose of calculating representation in the House of Representatives.

The amendments affirmed equal rights for Black Americans but did not extend the same protections to Native Americans. For a long time, Native Americans could not testify in court, own property, vote or leave their reservations.

The difference in treatment is revealing. White Americans long denied Black people access to institutions that they themselves enjoyed: schools, churches, shops, restaurants and other public places. With Native Americans, the policy often went in another direction. The law tried to make them adopt white institutions. Native American children were compelled to attend schools, and churches were established on reservations. White Americans wanted Indigenous peoples to accept their way of life. The effort sometimes resembled the training of a wild animal.

Why the difference? Native Americans were born on the land and had been its owners. Black Americans had been brought to the continent from elsewhere. Although both groups were denied a full place in society, Native Americans were in a different position: **they were the original Americans**. The difficulty for European settlers was how to make the original inhabitants 'identify with their country'—a country the newcomers had established over them.

The problem has not disappeared. At the time of my visit, there were said to be about **half a million Native Americans** in the United States. Most lived in conditions far below the national average. Disappointment and hopelessness had contributed to serious alcohol abuse. According to the figures I have seen, the suicide rate among Native American young people was **ten times the average**. **Half** were unemployed, while **80 per cent** depended on government assistance.

Many Native Americans still did not identify with Anglo-Saxon or mainstream American culture. They had their own languages and traditions. Educational opportunities were often poor, making it difficult to compete for work outside the reservations. Some moved to cities but later returned home. Their sense of belonging remained tied to their own people and culture.

Because Native Americans are far fewer in number than Black Americans, the immediate challenge they pose to American society may be smaller. But they pose a challenge to **American civilization's own understanding of itself**. Why, after so many years, have the descendants of European settlers been unable to assimilate the continent's original peoples?

I do not think the answer is mainly technical. It is a matter of identity and feeling. In many parts of the world, one can see American culture plainly on display, while opposition to America remains intense. The situation of Native Americans raises a similar question about what it means to belong.

## 9. A Crisis of Belief

Is American society facing a crisis in its beliefs and values? Some people never think to ask. When an economy is so prosperous, it is easy to overlook what may be happening beneath the surface. For visitors from outside, it is even harder to see beyond the wealth and spectacle. There is already so much to take in. One success can hide a hundred shortcomings, especially in a society that has achieved such astonishing material progress.

But among those who live here, some are more willing to examine what lies behind that progress. They are often older people, or people accustomed to thinking seriously about society. What is happening to Americans' inner lives while their material world grows richer? A number of thinkers described as conservatives believe that **America is facing a spiritual and moral crisis**.

One of the most controversial recent books is **Allan Bloom's** *The Closing of the American Mind: How Higher Education Has Failed Democracy and Impoverished the Souls of Today's Students*. Bloom teaches on the **Committee on Social Thought** at the University of Chicago and is associated with the **John M. Olin Center for Inquiry into the Theory and Practice of Democracy**. He has translated Plato's *Republic* and Rousseau's *Émile*.

The book caused a fierce argument when it appeared. Many people saw it as a declaration of conservative ideas. Supporters of the Black civil-rights movement and feminists reacted especially strongly, because some of Bloom's positions ran directly against theirs. Others welcomed it enthusiastically. President Reagan reportedly thought it a good book. Several university professors I spoke with agreed.

What is Bloom's argument?

He believes that modern university education is failing to give students an understanding of the traditional values on which Western society was built. As universities have developed, they have increasingly embraced **cultural relativism** and an attitude of openness toward every idea. Cultural relativism asks us to keep our minds open. But it also contains another assumption: that there are no absolute values, and that any belief or practice may be accepted or rejected. Openness itself becomes a virtue.

Bloom argues that every system of education has a moral purpose. It tries to shape a particular kind of person, and its courses are meant to help achieve that end.

In the 1950s, he believes, American education still pursued such a purpose. Cultural relativism later succeeded in challenging the belief that Western culture was the centre of everything. At the same time, however, it weakened the standing of Western culture itself.

Under the influence of this new outlook, university students have lost their sense of the past and have little idea of the future. Universities responsible for higher education no longer introduce them adequately to the great history of Western philosophy and literature. Without that knowledge, they find it harder to understand the order of nature and humanity's place within it. Universities fail to give students the self-awareness that serious study should develop.

What is the result?

Young people know surprisingly little about the classic works of Western history. Bloom describes asking a class which books had left the deepest impression on them. Not one student could name a great work of literature. One mentioned **the Bible**, but the Bible was not taught at the university.

Students sometimes watch films such as *Gandhi* or *A Man for All Seasons*, about Thomas More. But Bloom regards such films as artistic reconstructions rather than substitutes for the original works and experiences. Students turn to stories like *Kramer vs. Kramer* to understand divorce and sexual relationships, while few consider *Anna Karenina* or *The Red and the Black* an essential part of their own lives. Bloom portrays feminists as opponents of the traditional literary classics—a position that helps explain why feminists object to his book.

Bloom argues that the struggles against elitism and racism in the 1960s and 1970s did not at first do much to change students' relationship with books. The activists seldom attacked the classics directly. But literature reflects differences between men and women, and the great writers of the past—**Socrates, Plato, Shakespeare, Rousseau, Tolstoy and Stendhal**—could hardly be expected to hold the ideas of today's feminists. Under the pressure of these competing movements, the classics have gradually come to seem like relics of yesterday.

The finest achievements of Western music are meeting much the same fate. Young people are increasingly indifferent to them. Even when they hear such music, Bloom believes, they often lack the understanding and feeling needed to respond to it.

The twentieth century could be called the century of music. Young people are passionately devoted to it, but their enthusiasm is directed mainly toward rock and other modern styles. Classical music seems to be dying among the young. **Beethoven, Chopin, Brahms, Mozart and Wagner** receive less and less attention.

Western education has traditionally attached great importance to music as a way of shaping the character. Socrates and Plato both considered its place in education. What will it mean if that part of education disappears?

Bloom believes that the younger generation is also losing qualities needed to live as fully developed human beings. Modern society has given them new habits suited to its way of life, but some of those habits point toward a crisis. Young people have become increasingly **self-centred**. It would be unfair to call students bad, yet Bloom finds it equally difficult to call them noble or high-minded. Individualism taken too far weakens the family and encourages people to put themselves at the centre of everything. One of the books that still interests students is Camus's *The Stranger*. What that suggests hardly needs spelling out.

The sexual revolution and feminism have raised endless new questions about human life. Sexual freedom challenged the Puritan tradition. But, as Bloom sees it, an immediate consequence was that people began to understand happiness chiefly as physical satisfaction and to assume that sexual desire was harmless. These two movements shook traditional Western values.

They also encouraged a new kind of separation between people. Each individual became more detached from everyone else. Bloom calls such people **'social solitaries'**. Divorce is perhaps the most visible sign. A high divorce rate suggests that growing numbers of Americans no longer want to live together.

Love itself, Bloom argues, has become unfamiliar to students. Many begin having sexual relationships at a young age, and finding a sexual partner has become comparatively easy. But genuine affection, that valuable bond between people, has grown weaker. Human relationships increasingly lack it.

Bloom offers many examples to support what he sees as the closing of the American mind. His own position is clearly in favour of preserving and strengthening the cultural traditions of the West. He admires the intellectual achievements of **Plato, Socrates, Locke, Rousseau, Kant, Shakespeare and Bacon**, and wants this inheritance to carry Western civilization forward.

He discusses basic questions about the state of nature and life in society, the self, creativity, culture and values. Most of his arguments and evidence come from the great writers of Western history. He has a particular affection for French culture, perhaps because he translated *Émile*. His enthusiasm for French thought appears repeatedly in the book.

Bloom calls the overall tendency **nihilism**: the belief that no firm values remain. Nihilism, he suggests, has become part of the American way of life, and is dealing a potentially fatal blow to American culture and the nation's guiding spirit. As shared values weaken, the whole democratic system comes under pressure.

He draws support from **Tocqueville**, who attached great importance to education in the working of American democracy. Tocqueville believed that one of democracy's greatest dangers was the tyranny of public opinion. Democracy requires individuals to form their own judgments. It frees people from traditional political coercion and religious dogma, but freedom is not enough by itself. People must also be able to decide what they believe.

If the traditional Western system of values falls apart, Bloom argues, democracy itself will eventually suffer. Society will have no shared standards to help individuals make decisions, and universities will no longer provide them. Ideas and social development are closely connected. When the ideas that support a way of life come to an end, the institutions and habits built upon them may come to an end too.

Bloom's position is unmistakably conservative. Those who favour social progress may reject it. After all, societies must advance, and old ideas inevitably give way to new ones.

Yet **the questions Bloom raises deserve attention**, whatever we think of his answers. America's guiding beliefs face serious challenges. The younger generation knows too little about traditional Western values. To what extent will these changes in people's inner lives affect society's future development and its ability to govern itself?

No social or political system works simply because a law says that it should. People must first believe in the fundamental values behind the system, and those values must help guide their behaviour. **If the shared system of values collapses, what will keep the institutions standing?**

In many societies, the deepest problem lies not in the formal institutions but in the decline of the beliefs that sustain them. Without a core of shared values, a society faces enormous difficulties in maintaining order and bringing people together.

Here we encounter a dilemma. On one hand, progress requires new values. A society must be able to break free from older beliefs that hold it back. On the other, harmony and institutional stability require that some central values survive. If everything is cast aside, the framework of belief that holds society together may collapse, leaving confusion and a moral crisis.

And that brings us to a second dilemma.

If society is simply left to develop on its own, traditional values will be difficult to preserve. Development constantly replaces older ways with new ones. Every new generation may know less about the past. **Without education, there can be no continuity.**

In a democracy, people choose for themselves. But each new generation is likely to feel less attached to the values of earlier generations. If the decision is left entirely to individual choice, the result may be predictable.

Then who will do the work of keeping a society's essential values alive?

Anyone who thinks seriously about a society's stability and future development must, I believe, begin by thinking about that question.

## 10. The Empire of the Sun

A film called *Empire of the Sun* has recently appeared. Set in Shanghai during the Second World War, it tells the story of a group of Westerners and the Japanese troops occupying the city. The Westerners are confined to an internment camp, guarded by Japanese soldiers. They suffer hunger and humiliation, and are treated in ways that strip them of their dignity.

At a time when Japan's economy is sweeping into America and markets around the world, an American director has chosen to make a film like this. It is difficult to know exactly what he intended. But at the very least, the film seems to warn its audience that the Japanese and Westerners hold different values. It portrays a fierce and cruel side of the Japanese, people who, in the film's account, fail to recognize the basic worth and beliefs of other human beings. Does it also suggest something about why Japan is winning against the West on another battlefield—the economy? That is for viewers to consider.

Japan's economic success is widely called a miracle, and the Japanese themselves have every reason to feel proud. They lost the Second World War, and their country was occupied. Yet in a remarkably short period, measured against the long course of human history, Japan rose to become one of the world's leading economic powers.

Of course, it cannot yet claim the same standing as a political or military power. Some Japanese would like their country to have that position too. For now it remains beyond reach, but that does not mean it will always be so.

The strength of a great power has generally rested on three things: military force, economic strength, and skill in politics. Japan is hardly lacking in the last two. Its military strength is constrained by its constitution. But a constitution is, in the end, a document written by human beings. What one generation believes, the next may not. If the circumstances change, the situation could look very different.

**The Empire of the Sun is putting America under pressure.** Japan's economic strength has forced Americans to look at it with new respect.

Just before the Second World War ended, the distinguished cultural anthropologist **Ruth Benedict** wrote a book that caused a sensation: *The Chrysanthemum and the Sword*. Her examination of Japanese culture was penetrating and often insightful. Yet there was also something of the attitude of a supposedly superior people studying an inferior one. It was rather like a zoologist describing the habits of a gorilla or a golden snub-nosed monkey: meticulous, perhaps accurate, but without the slightest trace of admiration.

Decades later, another American, **Ezra Vogel**, a Harvard professor, wrote *Japan as Number One*. The zoologist's attitude was nowhere to be found. Vogel wrote with praise, and even envy. The change is striking, almost dramatic.

On November 28, 1988, an anonymous Japanese buyer paid more than **$38 million** at an auction house in London for a Picasso painting made in 1905. According to the report, it set a record for a twentieth-century work of art and was the third-highest price ever paid for a painting. That gives some idea of the financial strength Japan now commands.

Japan's economic invasion of America has gone far beyond the destruction wrought at Pearl Harbor. Look along an American street and Japanese cars are everywhere: **Toyota, Honda, Nissan**. Step into an electrical-goods store and the Japanese presence is even more obvious. Japanese products fill the shelves, of every kind one could imagine.

Japanese buyers are also purchasing American property on a grand scale. San Francisco seems full of Japanese visitors and investors. Honolulu, I am told, has practically become their domain. They can buy houses with cash. Their arrival pushes up property prices, leaving Americans without the money to compete looking on helplessly.

An IBM office tower in Atlanta has passed into Japanese ownership. In St. Louis, I visited one of the finest gardens in the country, only to find that much of what I saw was a Japanese garden. Chicago's Sears Tower, then the tallest building in the world, has recently been put up for sale. Some people worry that it, too, will end up in Japanese hands.

A friend told me that certain Japanese buyers do not even ask a property's price first. They drive through a city, point to a building they like, and have someone arrange to buy it.

Japanese investment in America has grown enormously. And many Japanese products sold here are now manufactured on American soil. The shadow of the Empire of the Sun is spreading across the United States.

Let us look at some figures.

In **1985**, Japan had a population of **120 million**, compared with **239 million** in the United States. Japan's land area was a little more than **377,000 square kilometres**, while America's was more than **9.372 million square kilometres**. In both land and population, Japan was far smaller.

Yet the gap between their economies was not nearly so great. In **1987**, Japan's gross national product stood at **$2.385 trillion**, compared with **$4.488 trillion** for the United States. Their total trade was **$379 billion** and **$677 billion** respectively. Set against the difference in population and territory, those figures are remarkable.

In 1987, exports to the United States accounted for **36.5 percent** of Japan's total exports, while imports from America accounted for **21 percent** of its total imports. For the United States, the corresponding 1986 figures were **11.9 percent** of exports going to Japan and **22.4 percent** of imports coming from Japan.

Japanese investment is also high, with factories now operating in many American states. And measured by gross national product per person, Japan has already passed America. The figures for **1985** were **$17,244** in Japan and **$16,709** in the United States. Between **1981 and 1985**, America's gross national product had a reported growth rate of **2.4 percent**, compared with **4.1 percent** in Japan. The trade imbalance between the two countries continues to widen.

During the Second World War, Americans came to know Japan through the experience of war. Today they know Japan through the goods that make their lives more comfortable. The Japanese economic advance is visible in how thoroughly Japanese products have entered American homes.

The Empire of the Sun is growing in strength with the force of nuclear fusion. In 1985, one American dollar bought **240 yen**. Now it buys only about **130**.

Writing in *The New York Times* on **November 28, 1988**, **Susan Chira** described several consequences of Japan's rising strength:
