- Japanese companies can outbid their rivals and purchase American businesses or property with relative ease.
- Japanese investors have lent enormous sums to the United States, much of it through purchases of **US Treasury securities**. Their decisions could shake financial markets.
- Japan is becoming a dominant economic force in other places, too. Currencies elsewhere have not risen as rapidly as the yen, and heavy Japanese investment in Taiwan and South Korea gives Japan great influence in those economies.
- Japan is becoming wealthier than America by some measures. Its contributions to developing countries give it new power to influence international economic policy.

Many Americans worry that Japan now has the means to influence the American economy itself. America has borrowed heavily from Japan and, in doing so, has handed its creditors a measure of control. In the first seven months of **1988**, Japanese buyers spent **$9 billion** acquiring companies overseas, including several in America. American markets and firms appear, one after another, to be falling into Japanese hands.

The challenge is serious. That does not mean Japan has replaced America, or that it will do so in the near future. But the point of Japan's sword is already aimed directly at the United States.

There is an irony here. Japan grew to strength under America's protection. The American occupation, the Korean War, and the Vietnam War all brought advantages to Japan. Even the broad outline of Japan's present system was shaped by Americans.

And yet today the defeated country seems to have more financial confidence than the victor. Japanese people make little effort to hide their satisfaction. They enjoy showing how thoroughly their fortunes have changed, sometimes almost as if they were putting on a demonstration for Americans. Americans can do little but look on.

Under the rules of a free economy, they cannot simply shut Japanese buyers out. Besides, the American economy has troubles of its own and increasingly depends on help from abroad. In their hearts, many Americans do not particularly like the Japanese.

How did things come to this?

Differences in management and institutions matter, of course. But **the difference between the two cultures** is also a major reason. Institutions have effects that do not depend on what people want them to do. Culture can have effects that are even harder to escape.

The outcome of the economic competition between Japan and America grows in part from the different habits and beliefs of their societies. To a considerable extent, this is a question of culture, not merely a question of institutions. Some people say that, in the contest between America and Japan, Eastern culture is gaining the upper hand.

What qualities are making the difference? There are too many to list, but three seem especially important.

**First, Japanese culture places the group first, while American culture places the individual first.**

Modern large-scale production depends on people dividing tasks and working together. Japan's habit of putting the group ahead of the individual makes this kind of cooperation easier. American individualism, by contrast, can work against large-scale cooperation or the willingness to give oneself completely to a common effort.

Americans want recognition for their own place in the world. They tend to think about individual success. Japanese workers are more often encouraged to think about the success of the group. The shared sense of purpose found in large Japanese companies is difficult to find in America.

Someone in Japan may devote a lifetime to a single company, performing one small part of its work. It is hard to imagine many Americans doing the same.

Group loyalty has advantages and disadvantages for a society's development. Still, the power it can bring people together to exercise is plainly greater than the power of individualism alone.

**Second, Japanese culture values personal dedication, while American culture values personal enjoyment.**

There is a strong pleasure-seeking side to American life. People work so that they can enjoy themselves. They do not generally believe in making unnecessary sacrifices. If they have money, they spend it. Americans with large savings seem almost like rare animals. Many people do not know where tomorrow's money will come from.

They borrow to buy houses and cars, to pay for university, and for countless other things. Enjoy life now; worry about the bill later.

Japanese culture gives the individual a less prominent place. Instead of stressing personal enjoyment, it encourages people to devote themselves to their work. Many Japanese men remain at work until midnight, willingly, without expecting overtime pay. Someone told me that if a Japanese man never stays late at the office, people may assume that he has no future there—that the company does not consider him important.

Americans probably enjoy one of the highest standards of living in the world. Their housing, transport, education, food and surroundings are in many ways better than Japan's, even though Japan's income per person is higher.

A culture built around enjoyment leads people to spend large sums on welfare and consumption instead of investment. The Japanese, by contrast, are often described as 'economic animals': intensely concerned with business and investment, and less inclined to spend on personal pleasures.

Enjoyment is becoming more fashionable in Japan too, especially in recent years. But it still runs against much of the traditional spirit of Japanese culture.

**Third, Japanese culture accepts close control, while American culture prefers to leave people free.**

In Japan, people are accustomed to strict management. That makes it possible to organize the whole economic and social system tightly, so that its many parts work together. When such a system is directed toward economic growth, it can release tremendous energy.

Americans could hardly accept that kind of management. They are more easygoing, more informal, and less willing to follow orders simply because someone higher up has given them. The machinery of American society runs in a more democratic way.

In Japan, people may be directed from above to pursue a national or economic goal. They must do it; they cannot simply refuse. In America, people are more likely to pursue success for their own reasons. They can question an instruction from above, even reject it through democratic means, and those giving the instruction may accept that. Such things rarely happen in Japan, if they happen at all.

These are only a few of the differences.

But they raise a question larger than the comparison between two cultures. **They ask us to reconsider the strengths and weaknesses of two ways of organizing society.**

On the whole, America's system rests on individualism, the pursuit of enjoyment, and democracy. Yet in this economic contest it appears to be losing to a system built more firmly on group loyalty, self-sacrifice, and authority.

Perhaps Americans would rather lose their economic lead than give up their way of life. That system protects the values they believe in, while also providing a considerable measure of prosperity. But the world as it now stands suggests that it may not be enough to guarantee America's position as the most advanced country.

Societies often face a difficult choice: **keep their established values, or adopt a more effective system that conflicts with those values.** Sometimes the difficulty is even greater. Can a culture allow people to choose institutions fundamentally different from the ones it has shaped? Often it cannot.

And Americans cannot simply take on Japanese culture. Many show little interest in it. Some regard Japanese society as culturally backward, despite its economic success. Beneath their outward respect, there is often a feeling of superiority.

That psychological barrier may help ensure that America's development does not outpace Japan's. It also helps explain why Americans are unlikely ever to embrace the Empire of the Sun wholeheartedly.

America now faces a powerful challenge from Japan, and **a large part of the reason lies in the way America's own institutions, culture, and values work against America itself.**

After the Second World War, America enjoyed an extraordinary combination of favourable circumstances. It had the resources, the position, and the people to develop at astonishing speed. Yet only thirty or forty years later, its standing faces a serious challenge.

Japan may be only the first country to challenge it in this way. In the coming century, other peoples will surely do the same. Perhaps only then will Americans begin to examine their political system, economy, and culture with new seriousness.

Of course, Japan cannot match America's natural resources or its territory. Those differences remain decisive advantages for the United States.

The danger lies elsewhere. If the conflicting forces already at work in American society continue along their present course, they may do more than prevent the country from making full use of its strengths. **They may create an underlying current of crisis that becomes impossible to stop.**
